Orientation — Scope, Version and Method

0.1 Scope and audience0.2 Version pin: PostgreSQL 180.3 Server architecture and process model0.4 Core concepts and vocabulary0.5 Extensions and tooling0.6 Conventions, notation and reading plans

This section fixes the ground on which the remaining six sections stand. It states what the guide assumes of the reader, pins every behavioural claim to PostgreSQL 18, maps the server’s process and memory architecture, defines the vocabulary used without redefinition, lists the extensions the material depends on, and states the notation and command-line conventions used throughout. The argument is that most operational disagreement about PostgreSQL is a disagreement about version, lock semantics, or vocabulary, and that all three are made explicit here so that later chapters can be read as specification rather than opinion.

0.1 Scope and audience

The guide is written for engineers who administer, extend, or build data-intensive services on PostgreSQL, and it deliberately excludes application-framework and reporting-tool material.

The intended reader already operates PostgreSQL in production. That means the guide assumes fluency with ps, with transactions and their failure modes, with the shape of a SEL statement, and with at least one deployment under load. It does not explain what a relational database is, nor how to install a package, nor how to write a basic query. Where a concept is introduced, it is introduced because the reader needs its exact semantics, not because the concept is new.

Scope is deliberately narrow in one direction and broad in another. Broad: type design, DDL semantics, index access methods, the planner, MVCC and locking, query tuning, and operational index management — the areas in which precise knowledge is what separates a working system from a fragile one. Narrow: everything that belongs to a surrounding toolchain. Object-relational mapping, connection pooling libraries, replication orchestration, and continuous integration pipeline design are out of scope, appearing only where they constrain a database-side decision, for example when a driver’s implicit transaction mode changes the meaning of a lock.

The guide makes three recurring arguments. First, that a database is a specification of behaviour, not a data container: a column type, a constraint, and an index each make promises that the engine will enforce, and those promises are the actual product. Second, that cost is never intrinsic — an operation is cheap or expensive only relative to a distribution of inputs, a hardware configuration, and a concurrency level, and every plan decision is a statement about that distribution. Third, that operational safety comes from reversible, observable, bounded changes; the destructive options in PostgreSQL are numerous and they are consistently documented, which is itself a hazard.

-- The session preamble this guide assumes for any interactive or service session.
-- Every one of these is a SET, not a postgresql.conf entry, and every one of them
-- changes a behaviour the rest of the document depends on.
BEGIN;
SET statement_timeout           = '30s';      -- bound a runaway statement
SET lock_timeout               = '5s';       -- bound waiting for any lock
SET idle_in_transaction_session_timeout = '60s';
SET default_transaction_isolation = 'read committed';
SET default_transaction_read_only  = on;   -- for reporting sessions
-- TimeZone and DateStyle change the text form of every value the session
-- returns, so pin them rather than inheriting the server default.
SET TimeZone  = 'UTC';
SET DateStyle = 'ISO, YMD';
COMMIT;
-- Verify rather than assume: sourcefile NULL means it was never configured.
SELECT name, setting, source, sourcefile, pending_restart
  FROM pg_settings
 WHERE sourcefile IS NOT NULL OR pending_restart
 ORDER BY name;

0.2 Version pin: PostgreSQL 18

Every behavioural claim is stated for PostgreSQL 18, and the release deltas that materially change earlier guidance are enumerated so that advice written for older majors can be identified and corrected.

PostgreSQL follows a yearly major release cadence. Each major release adds features, changes defaults, and occasionally deprecates behaviour that had been relied upon for years. Advice therefore decays, sometimes silently. A tuning recommendation that was correct for version 12 may be counterproductive on 18 because the underlying implementation changed; a data type choice that was necessary in 9 may now be dominated by an alternative. The following table records the deltas that change guidance found in older material.

ReleaseChange that invalidates older advicePractical consequence
18Asynchronous I/O framework; new GUCs io_m, io_max_co, io_wo, io_combiSequential scans, bitmap heap scans and vacuum no longer block on a single outstanding read; random_p and effective_io now describe a genuinely asynchronous engine
18B-tree skip scans over low-cardinality leading columnsThe classic multicolumn ordering heuristic in § 3.3 and § 6.6 is weaker than it was; verify with EXP rather than assuming
18GENERATED ALWAYS AS (...) VIRTUALGENERATED ALWAYS AS (...) VIRTUAL columnsDerived values need not occupy heap storage; the immutability requirement is unchanged and still absolute
18Temporal constraints: UNI and PRIMARY KEYPRIMARY KEY over a range column with WITHOUT OVERLAPSWITHOUT OVERLAPSScheduling and validity-interval tables no longer need an application-level exclusion trigger; the range GIN/GiST machinery is still required
18Core uuidv7()uuidv7() and uuidv4()uuidv4(); NOT NULL ... NOT VALIDNOT NULL ... NOT VALID for existing rowsTime-ordered identifiers remove the write-amplification objection to UUID primary keys; large tables can gain a NOT NULLNOT NULL guarantee without a full table rewrite under ACCESS EXCLUSIVEACCESS EXCLUSIVE
17Incremental backup (pg_basebackup --incrementalpg_basebackup --incremental, pg_combi); MERGE ... RETURNINGMERGE ... RETURNING; asynchronous append and prefetchBackup duration and RPO stop being a function of database size; prefetch makes append-only indexes behave differently under EXP
16pg_st; parallel VAC, ANA, CREATE INDEXCREATE INDEX, DROP INDEXDROP INDEX, CLU; expanded per-relation statisticsMaintenance wall-clock is divided by worker count; I/O attribution must come from pg_st rather than inferred from pg_stat_ block counters
15ME; NULLS NOT DISTINCTNULLS NOT DISTINCT unique constraints; ICU as the default collation provider where availableUpsert logic can move server-side, but concurrency semantics of ME differ from INSERT ... ON CONFLICTINSERT ... ON CONFLICT; see § 2.5

Two defaults in this area deserve explicit statement because they silently change results. In PostgreSQL 15 and later, NULLS DISTINCTNULLS DISTINCT is the default for unique constraints, so a unique index permits any number of rows whose key columns are all null. Setting NULLS NOT DISTINCTNULLS NOT DISTINCT restores the SQL-standard behaviour in which null keys collide with each other. Separately, where a suitable ICU library is present the default collation provider is ICU, which changes sort order relative to the historical li provider; existing indexes built under a different provider are not transparently rebuilt, and queries against them will not use the new collation’s ordering unless the index is recreated.

-- Confirm the exact server build and the parameters this guide assumes.
SELECT version(),
       current_setting('server_version_num')::int   AS ver_num,
       current_setting('server_encoding'),
       current_setting('lc_collate'),
       current_setting('default_text_search_config');
-- 18xxxx is the PostgreSQL 18 series; anything below 180000 needs the caveats above.

-- Collation providers actually present, and which one the database defaults to.
SELECT collprovider, collname, collversion, collisdeterministic
  FROM pg_collation
 WHERE collname IN ('C', 'POSIX', 'en_US.utf8', 'en-US-x-icu', 'und-x-icu')
    OR collprovider = 'i'
 ORDER BY 1,2;

-- Before a major-version upgrade, record what the cluster depends on. These
-- three queries are the minimum pre-flight evidence.
SELECT extname, extversion FROM pg_extension ORDER BY 1;
SELECT e.extname, count(*) AS dependent_indexes
  FROM pg_index i
  JOIN pg_depend d ON d.objid = i.indexrelid AND d.deptype = 'e'
  JOIN pg_extension e ON e.oid = d.refobjid
 GROUP BY 1 ORDER BY 1;
SELECT conrelid::regclass AS on_table, pg_get_constraintdef(oid) AS def
  FROM pg_constraint
 WHERE contype = 'x' ORDER BY 1;   -- exclusion constraints
--   pg_upgrade --check, then the real run with --clone where the filesystem
--   supports reflinks: the difference between minutes and hours.

Version-sensitive tuning. Parameters such as random_p, effective_io, and effective_ encode a model of storage behaviour. On PostgreSQL 18 with the asynchronous I/O subsystem enabled, that model is materially different from the model assumed when those defaults were first chosen. Copying a tuning recipe from a guide written for version 12 or 13 without re-measuring is a common source of regressions that present as a sudden loss of index usage after a major-version upgrade.

0.3 Server architecture and process model

PostgreSQL is a client/server system of cooperating single-threaded processes sharing one address space, and almost every locking or durability question reduces to which process takes which lock and when it releases it.

The postmaster process owns the data directory and is the only process permitted to signal, spawn, or terminate the others. It starts a fixed set of background workers and then accepts client connections, forking a backend for each. Backends are not pooled at the operating-system level: max_con is a hard ceiling on simultaneously running backends, each of which is a process with its own private memory for working buffers, sort state, hash tables, and per-tuple state. This is why work is a per-operation setting inside a session rather than a server-wide allocation, and why a single query can exceed it several times over.

Shared state lives either in the main shared memory segment created at postm start or in dynamic shared memory segments created at runtime. The main segment holds the buffer pool, the lock manager’s shared structures, predicate lock manager state, and the transaction status snapshot array. Dynamic shared memory backs parallel query workers, parallel index builds, parallel vacuum, and parallel CREATE STATISTICSCREATE STATISTICS on PostgreSQL 16 and later. min_dynamic_ and max_worker jointly bound the parallel path, and a parallel plan that cannot obtain workers degrades to a serial plan at execution time rather than failing.

backenRoleRelevant parameters
client backendOne per connection; parses, plans, executes; the only process that takes AccessExc on behalf of a sessionmax_con, work, statemen
autovacuum launcherSchedules vacuum and analyse for many tables in one coordinator processautovacuu (default 60s)
autovacuum workerRuns one vacuum, analyse, or CREATE INDEX CONCURRENTLYCREATE INDEX CONCURRENTLY; takes ShareUpdateEautovacuum_, autovacuum_vac
checkpointerWrites dirty buffers and the redo pointer to durable storage at checkpoint boundariesmax_wa, checkpoin, shared_
background writerWrites dirty buffers opportunistically to keep the checkpoint’s write burst smallbgwrite, bgwriter_l
walwriterStreams committed WAL to the configured wal_w bufferswal_writ, commit
logical replication launcher and workersApplies or streams changes for subscriptions; workers are per-subscription and per-tablemax_logical_rep
checkpointer, archiver, statsWAL archiving and cumulative statistics; the standalone stats collector was removed in 14 and its work folded into backendsarchiv, track_
 
-- One row per running process type: the fastest way to see the real configuration.
SELECT backend_type,
       count(*)        AS processes,
       max(backend_start)   AS newest_start
  FROM pg_stat_activity
 GROUP BY 1
 ORDER BY 2 DESC, 1;

-- Slots are the unit of replication memory and of WAL retention. Exceeding
-- max_replication_slots does not fail the workload; it stalls WAL removal
-- and the effect appears much later as unbounded pg_wal growth.
SELECT slot_name, slot_type, active, restart_lsn,
       pg_size_pretty(pg_wal_lsn_diff(pg_current_wal_lsn(), restart_lsn)) AS retained_wal,
       wal_status, safe_wal_size
  FROM pg_replication_slots;

-- Prepared transactions survive restart and hold locks; they are invisible to
-- pg_locks joins unless you remember to look at pg_prepared_xacts.
SELECT gid, prepared, owner, database,
       age(transaction_timestamp, prepared_at) AS age
  FROM pg_prepared_xacts
 ORDER BY prepared;

-- Shared memory sizing. The main segment is allocated at postmaster start and
-- cannot grow without a restart, so its components must be known in advance.
--   SHOW shared_buffers;          -- 8kB blocks, the buffer pool proper
--   SHOW max_connections;        -- per-backend fixed overhead: ~2-3 MB each
--   SHOW max_locks_per_transaction; -- lock table entries per transaction
--   SHOW max_pred_locks_per_transaction; -- SSI predicate locks per txn
--   SHOW max_worker_processes; SHOW max_parallel_workers;
-- Dynamic shared memory backs parallel query, parallel maintenance and the 18
-- asynchronous I/O workers; min_dynamic_shared_memory is its floor.
SELECT name, setting, unit,
       CASE WHEN unit = '8kB' THEN pg_size_pretty(setting::bigint * 8192)
            ELSE setting END AS value
  FROM pg_settings
 WHERE name IN ('shared_buffers', 'max_connections',
                  'max_worker_processes', 'max_parallel_workers',
                  'min_dynamic_shared_memory', 'max_locks_per_transaction')
 ORDER BY name;

-- The background side of the process model. Every entry here is a limit that is
-- reached by ordinary operation, and each has a different symptom.
SELECT name, setting, unit FROM pg_settings
 WHERE name IN ('max_worker_processes', 'max_parallel_workers',
                  'max_parallel_workers_per_gather', 'max_parallel_maintenance_workers',
                  'autovacuum_max_workers', 'autovacuum_naptime',
                  'max_logical_replication_workers', 'max_wal_senders',
                  'max_replication_slots', 'max_prepared_transactions',
                  'max_locks_per_transaction', 'max_pred_locks_per_transaction')
 ORDER BY name;
-- Exceeding any of these degrades rather than fails:
--   max_worker_processes        parallel query silently becomes serial
--   autovacuum_max_workers     tables queue for vacuum and bloat grows unseen
--   max_wal_senders            replication clients are refused or delayed
--   max_replication_slots      WAL accumulates in pg_wal without bound
--   max_locks_per_transaction  "out of shared memory" on a wide transaction
SELECT count(*) FILTER (WHERE backend_type LIKE 'parallel%')  AS parallel_workers,
       count(*) FILTER (WHERE backend_type = 'autovacuum worker') AS autovacuum_workers
  FROM pg_stat_activity;

Read the process list before the configuration. When diagnosing resource exhaustion, SELECT ... FROM pg_stat_activity GROUP BY backend_typeSELECT ... FROM pg_stat_activity GROUP BY backend_type answers questions that a reading of postgre cannot: it shows the autovacuum worker count actually running, whether parallel workers are being granted, and whether a pooler is masking a connection problem. Configuration states intent; the process table states fact.

0.4 Core concepts and vocabulary

The terms used precisely throughout the guide — relation, page, tuple, snapshot, transaction identifier, catalog, toast — are defined here once, with the implementation detail that gives each term its operational meaning.

A relation is anything with rel in the catalog: an ordinary table (rr), an index (ii), a view (vv), a materialised view (mm), a sequence (SS), a composite type (cc), a partitioned table (pp), or a foreign table (ff). A page is the 8 kB unit of I/O and the unit of locking within a heap; PostgreSQL does not support a page size other than 8 kB within a cluster. A tuple is a row version: an immutable on-page structure with a 24-byte header carrying xm, xm, cm, cm, and a ct pointing at the physical location. Row updates create a new tuple version; they never modify one in place, which is the foundation of § 4.1.

A transaction identifier is a monotonically increasing 32-bit value assigned to write transactions, plus separate 32-bit epoch space used to reinterpret the wraparound horizon. Read-only transactions consume a virtual transaction identifier that never appears on disk. A snapshot is a backend-local structure recording xm, xm, and the list of in-flight identifiers between them; visibility of any given tuple version is decided by comparison against the snapshot, which is why a long transaction pins the oldest possible horizon for the whole cluster.

Catalogs are relations themselves: pg_c describes relations, pg_att describes columns and their storage, pg_ and pg_col describe types and collations, pg_i and pg describe indexes and access methods. Everything the planner and the executor know is derivable from catalogs, which is why ANA and accurate catalog contents are prerequisites for every performance discussion in § 3 and § 5. The toast mechanism transparently moves out-of-line values of variable-length types into a companion relation when a row exceeds roughly 2 kB, storing a 18-byte pointer in place; SELECT ... FROM pg_toast.pg_toast_<oid>SELECT ... FROM pg_toast.pg_toast_<oid> gives the actual detoast cost, and the threshold is why large js or te values behave so differently from small ones.

-- The five catalog queries that answer most operational questions.
SELECT c.oid::regclass              AS relation,
       c.relkind,
       c.relpersistence,                       -- 'p' permanent, 'u' unlogged, 't' temp
       c.reloptions,
       pg_size_pretty(pg_total_relation_size(c.oid)) AS total,
       pg_size_pretty(pg_relation_size(c.oid))     AS heap,
       pg_size_pretty(pg_indexes_size(c.oid))      AS indexes
  FROM pg_class c
 WHERE c.oid = 'orders'::regclass;

-- Columns, their storage, and the statistics the planner holds about them.
SELECT attname, atttypid::regtype AS type, attlen,
       attnotnull, attstorage, attstattarget,
       null_frac, n_distinct, correlation,
       most_common_vals
  FROM pg_stats
 WHERE schemaname = 'public' AND tablename = 'orders'
 ORDER BY attname;

-- Every index with its access method, definition and size. pg_get_indexdef
-- prints the definition the server would accept if you replayed it.
SELECT i.indexrelid::regclass AS index_name,
       am.amname,
       pg_get_indexdef(i.indexrelid) AS definition,
       pg_relation_size(i.indexrelid) AS bytes,
       i.indisunique, i.indisprimary, i.indnkeyatts, i.indnatts
  FROM pg_index i
  JOIN pg_class c ON c.oid = i.indexrelid
  JOIN pg_am am ON am.oid = c.relam
 WHERE i.indrelid = 'orders'::regclass
 ORDER BY 4 DESC;

-- The page and tuple layer underneath all of it. The tuple header is 24 bytes
-- and the item identifier is 4 more, so 28 bytes of every 8kB page are spent
-- before any user column is stored.
--   CREATE EXTENSION pageinspect;
SELECT lp, lp_off, lp_len, lp_flags, t_xmin, t_xmax, t_ctid
  FROM heap_page_items(get_raw_page('orders', 0))
 LIMIT 4;
SELECT pg_relation_size('orders') / current_setting('block_size')::int AS pages,
       pg_size_pretty(pg_relation_size('orders'))                   AS heap,
       (SELECT n_live_tup FROM pg_stat_user_tables WHERE relid = 'orders'::regclass) AS live_tuples,
       round(pg_column_size(t.*))                                                       AS avg_row_bytes
  FROM (SELECT * FROM orders LIMIT 1000) t
 LIMIT 1;

-- Resolving a name to its catalog identity, and reading the type system behind
-- a declared column. This is how every other catalog query in the guide is
-- written: by name on input, by oid internally.
SELECT 'orders'::regclass          AS relation_oid,
       to_regclass('public.orders')     AS same_by_schema_qualification,
       'text'::regtype::oid              AS type_oid,
       format_type(t.oid, t.typmod)                            AS declared_format,
       array_type = CASE WHEN t.typelem <> 0 THEN true ELSE false END
  FROM pg_type t
 WHERE t.oid = 'text'::regtype::oid;
SELECT t.typname, t.typelem::regtype AS element_type, t.typlen, t.typbyval
  FROM pg_type t WHERE t.typelem <> 0 AND t.typlen = -1
 ORDER BY 1 LIMIT 8;
-- A type with typelem <> 0 and typlen = -1 is an array type whose element type
-- is named by typelem. There is no generic "anyarray" column type, which is
-- the structural point made in section 1.5.

Object identifiers are not durable identities. An o is a 32-bit catalog key that is stable only for the life of a cluster; after pg_ and restore, or a VACUUM FULLVACUUM FULL that rebuilds a heap, values change. Never store an o in an application table, and never rely on o ordering. The stable identifier for an object is its qualified name, which is also what ::regclass::regclass input accepts.

0.5 Extensions and tooling

The capabilities this guide assumes are either core or installed from con; the exact creation, upgrade, and preload requirements for each are stated so the examples are reproducible.

Extensions are the unit of distribution for everything outside core. CREATE EXTENSIONCREATE EXTENSION executes a control file and a set of scripts; the object is then an ordinary database object and participates in ordinary dumps, ownership, and privileges. Extensions that register access methods, operators, or types must be created before the index or column type that depends on them, and extensions that need shared memory or background workers must be named in shared_prelo before the postmaster starts, not merely installed.

ExtensionProvidesInstall-time requirement
pg_stat_sPer-statement time, rows, shared-block and temp-block I/O, plus pg_stat_statements_reset()pg_stat_statements_reset()shared_prelo; CREATE EXTENSIONCREATE EXTENSION in each database that is to be observed
pg_st (core, 16+)Per-backend-type I/O counters by backend object type, read/write/extend, with op_bNone; requires track_i for the timing columns
auto_eLogs the plan of statements exceeding auto_explain.l, including nested ones by defaultMay live in session_prel; no superuser bypass needed to plan own queries
hypCreates hypothetical indexes to answer "would this index be used" without building itNone; must not be left in place in production planning paths
pagei, pgstapgsta, pgstatg, pgsta, pgstattup — the only supported way to measure bloatSuperuser; run during a quiet period since both hold AccessS and read every page
pg_visInspects the visibility map, which determines whether an index-only scan can avoid the heapNone
pg_prpg_prewarm()pg_prewarm() to prefault relation blocks, pg_buff view for residencyNone; needs pg_read_b to read cache contents
btre, btreeScalar operators in GIN and GiST, which is what makes EXC constraints over mixed column types possibleCreate before the constraint
 
-- Extension lifecycle. IF NOT EXISTS is safe but hides a version mismatch, so the
-- installed version is checked explicitly after creation.
CREATE EXTENSION IF NOT EXISTS pg_stat_statements;
SELECT extname, extversion, nspname AS schema
  FROM pg_extension e
  JOIN pg_namespace n ON n.oid = e.extnamespace
 ORDER BY 1;

-- pg_stat_statements: the workload table. Normalising is what makes this
-- useful; a wall of distinct literal values hides the actual hot query.
SELECT calls,
       round(total_exec_time::numeric, 1)                          AS total_ms,
       round(mean_exec_time::numeric, 2)                           AS mean_ms,
       rows,
       shared_blks_hit, shared_blks_read,
       temp_blks_written,
       left(query, 110) AS query
  FROM pg_stat_statements
 WHERE query NOT LIKE '%pg_stat%'
 ORDER BY total_exec_time DESC
 LIMIT 20;

-- Extension ownership and dependency: what breaks if an extension is dropped,
-- and what must exist before an index or constraint that uses it can be made.
SELECT d.deptype,
       e.extname,
       c.relname,
       pg_describe_object(d.classid, d.objid, d.objsubid) AS dependent_object
  FROM pg_depend d
  LEFT JOIN pg_extension e ON e.oid = d.refobjid
  LEFT JOIN pg_class c ON c.oid = d.objid
 WHERE d.refclassid = 'pg_extension'::regclass
 ORDER BY e.extname, 3;
-- DROP EXTENSION CASCADE removes exactly these objects. Read this list
-- before running the drop, not after the constraint disappeared.
-- Extensions that must be preloaded cannot be added to a running cluster:
--   SHOW shared_preload_libraries;   -- requires a postmaster restart
--   SHOW session_preload_libraries;  -- per session, no restart required

Extension versions are not additive. An extension that is present but not upgraded still exposes the older function signatures, and upgrade scripts can rewrite objects — including indexes on long-lived tables. After any ALTER EXTENSION name UPDATEALTER EXTENSION name UPDATE, diff pg_get_indexdef()pg_get_indexdef() across the whole database before assuming the upgrade was behaviour-preserving; a changed operator class in an index definition is a full rebuild, not a metadata edit.

0.6 Conventions, notation and reading plans

Notation for types, identifiers, settings, plans, and shell commands is fixed here, including the exact formatting of EXP output used in every later example.

SQL is written in upper case for keywords, lower case for identifiers, with snake_case for schema object names. Types are given with both the SQL standard name and the internal name where they differ, for example big (in), because the internal name is what appears in pg_, in informati output for some views, and in third-party documentation. Identifiers that are actual settings appear in their exact G spelling; there is no shorthand. Path names, file names, and status values appear verbatim in monospacemonospace.

Plan output is the primary evidence in this guide, so its format is fixed throughout. The standard invocation is shown below; it is deliberately maximal, because the two most commonly omitted options — BUF and SETT — are the two that distinguish a plan that is fast because the work was cheap from a plan that is fast because the work was already in memory.

-- The invocation used for every plan in this guide. SETTINGS is what prevents
-- "why is the estimate wrong" arguments when the answer is a GUC override.
EXPLAIN (ANALYZE, BUFFERS, VERBOSE, SETTINGS, SUMMARY, TIMING, FORMAT TEXT)
SELECT o.id, o.placed_at, o.total_cents, c.code
  FROM orders o
  JOIN customers c ON c.id = o.customer_id
 WHERE o.placed_at >= '2026-01-01T00:00:00Z'::timestamptz
   AND c.country_code = 'DE'
 ORDER BY o.placed_at DESC
 LIMIT 50;

Reading rules for that output are stated once here. The tree is printed with the deepest node first and the root last, so read it bottom-up and treat each parent as a consumer of its children. rows=rows= is the planner’s estimate for one loop of that node; the ro on the following line is measured and already multiplied by loopsloops. A Bat value greater than 1 on a Ha node means the hash table did not fit in work and spilled to temporary files. Heap Fetches: 0Heap Fetches: 0 under an Index Only ScanIndex Only Scan is the only proof that the covering index paid for itself. temp readtemp read and temp writtentemp written above zero on a So or Ha node indicate a mis-sized memory setting, not a slow disk.

Never tune against a plan taken without ANALYZE on a cold cache. EXP without ANA costs nothing and touches no data, so it is the right tool for checking whether an index is even considered. EXPLAIN (ANALYZE)EXPLAIN (ANALYZE) executes the statement and is the right tool for measuring. Two consecutive EXPLAIN (ANALYZE)EXPLAIN (ANALYZE) runs answer different questions, because the second one runs against a warm cache; if a plan flips between runs, the answer is about the cache, not about the planner, and the fix is to force a realistic working-set measurement with pg_pr or a restart rather than to add an index.

Exercises 0

  1. Establish a baseline for a lab cluster: capture version()version(), the postgre file hash, SHOW ALLSHOW ALL output, the pg_stat_ process census, and the pg_se rows whose sourc is not NULL. Deliverable: a single text file plus a query that diffs the current configuration against that baseline and reports only the changed settings.
  2. Map every extension in pg_ext to its required GUC and preload status, then verify each claim: for each extension present, confirm whether it appears in shared_prelo and whether the postmaster has been restarted since installation. Deliverable: a table with a verdict column of veri or inco per extension, with the catalog query used as evidence.
  3. Prove the process model empirically. Hold an open transaction in one session, read xm from pg_stat_ in a second, then attempt a VAC in a third and capture the exact lock it waits for from pg_lockspg_locks. Deliverable: a transcript with the lock mode named and the conflicting relation identified.
  4. Measure the storage overhead of the system you are documenting. For the ten largest relations, break pg_total_re into heap, toast, free space, and index components using pgstattuplepgstattuple. Deliverable: a table showing the free-space fraction per relation and a written judgement of which relations are candidates for VACUUM FULLVACUUM FULL and which for pg_r instead.
  5. Instrument a workload with pg_stat_s and pg_st, then answer three questions: which statement class dominates total time, which dominates shared-block reads, and which dominates temp-block writes. Deliverable: three ranked lists with the exact EXPLAIN (ANALYZE, BUFFERS)EXPLAIN (ANALYZE, BUFFERS) of the top entry in each list, and a note on which list disagrees with the others and why.

1 · Data Types, Collation and Type Design

1.1 Numeric types and money1.2 Text, collations and Unicode1.3 Collation providers, ICU and sort semantics1.4 JSON, JSONB and semi-structured data1.5 Arrays and the absence of a native element type1.6 Ranges, multiranges and canonicalisation

This section establishes the type system as an engineering contract rather than a convenience. It covers the exact storage, precision, and performance behaviour of every built-in numeric family; the text types and the collation machinery that governs their ordering; the two JSON representations and why the choice is effectively permanent once data exists; the array and range types, including the structural gaps that make each of them a specialist rather than a general-purpose tool; and the design discipline that follows from all of it. The argument is that type selection is the cheapest performance and correctness decision available, because a column’s declared type determines its storage, its comparison semantics, its indexability, and its error behaviour for the entire life of the data.

1.1 Numeric types and money

The numeric family is not a hierarchy of sizes; it is two unrelated families, one binary and one decimal, and confusing them is the origin of most numeric defects in PostgreSQL schemas.

PostgreSQL provides two unrelated implementations of "number". The binary family — smal (in, 2 bytes, −32768 to 32767), int (in, 4 bytes, ±2 147 483 647), big (in, 8 bytes, ±9 223 372 036 854 775 807), re (flo, 4 bytes), and double precisiondouble precision (flo, 8 bytes) — stores values in a fixed-width machine representation and uses hardware arithmetic. The decimal family consists of exactly one type, num (also spelled dec), which stores a base-10000 digit array with a sign, a weight, and a scale. There is no numeric(8,2)numeric(8,2) storage class; declaring precision and scale on num constrains the declared result, and a value that violates it is rounded rather than rejected unless the scale is negative in the constraint context.

The precision table that follows is exact and worth memorising, because the failure mode of num is silent over-scale rounding at the boundary of its declared domain.

TypeStorageExact digitsArithmeticUse for
smal / in2 bytes, fixed4 to 5 decimal digitsInteger, overflow is an errorCounters, small codes, enum-like integers
int / in4 bytes, fixed9 to 10 digitsInteger, overflow is an errorAlmost every surrogate key and every count
big / in8 bytes, fixed18 to 19 digitsInteger, overflow is an errorIdentifiers wider than 2^63, epoch microseconds
numeric(p, s)numeric(p, s)Variable, up to ~1 310 bytes on diskUp to 131 072 digits before the point, 16 383 afterExact, arbitrary precision, with a result scale ruleMoney, rates, anything that must reconcile
re / flo4 bytesAbout 6 significant decimal digitsApproximate; N, InfiRarely; see the warning below
double precisiondouble precision / flo8 bytesAbout 15 to 16 significant digitsApproximate, hardware FPU, not rep-orderableScientific values, geometry, statistics
mo8 bytes, int64 minor unitsExact to 2 fraction digitsExact within a fixed scaleEffectively never; see below
 
-- Exact numeric: the accumulator pattern that makes reconciliation possible.
CREATE TABLE ledger_entry (
  id            bigint-- Exact numeric: the accumulator pattern that makes reconciliation possible.
CREATE TABLE ledger_entry (
  id            bigint GENERATED ALWAYS AS IDENTITY PRIMARY KEY,
  account_id    integerinteger     NOT NULL,
  amount_cents  bigintbigint      NOT NULL,   -- integer minor units
  tax_rate      numeric(9,6)numeric(9,6) NOT NULL DEFAULT 0.190000,
  recorded_at   timestamptztimestamptz  NOT NULL DEFAULT now()
);
-- The tax is a rate with six fraction digits, not a rounded amount. Storing the
-- rounded amount here and the rate there is how penny drift becomes real.
SELECT amount_cents,
       round(amount_cents * tax_rate)                 AS tax_cents,
       amount_cents - round(amount_cents * tax_rate)   AS net_cents
  FROM ledger_entry
 ORDER BY id LIMIT 3;

-- binary floats are not equal to their decimal literals, even for 0.1.
SELECT 0.1::float8 = 0.1::numeric      AS differs,
       0.1::float4 AS f4,
       0.1::float8 AS f8,
       (0.1::float8)::text AS shortest_repr,
       1.0 / 3.0::float8 AS third;

num division is where most teams first encounter PostgreSQL’s exactness. Division produces a result whose scale is derived from the operand scales, capped at numeri — but addition, subtraction, and multiplication of two num values produce the minimum scale needed to represent the result exactly, with a floor determined by the result of the operation, not the declared scale of the target column. The consequence is that num division is not associative with respect to rounding, and an intermediate expression of the wrong type silently truncates a financial result. The rule is to carry num end to end and to force a scale explicitly at the point of presentation, never at the point of storage.

mo deserves a separate verdict. Its internal representation is an in of minor units, which is fast and exact, and it is comparable in binary. Its output is produced by mone, which formats a value using the locale’s currency symbol and decimal conventions from lc_monetarylc_monetary. The result is that a mo value rendered on a German session prints as 1.234,56 €1.234,56 € and on an American session as $1,234.56$1,234.56, and a plain text export of such a column is unparseable. It has no defined scale other than 2, no explicit currency column, and casting to num depends on the locale. It is retained largely for backward compatibility with very old systems and is not recommended for new work.

-- money is locale-dependent on the way out, which is why it is a display hazard.
SELECT (1234.56::money)::text               AS in_default_locale,
       (1234.56::money)::numeric            AS cast_to_numeric;
SET lc_monetary = 'C';
SELECT (1234.56::money)::text               AS with_c_monetary;
RESET lc_monetary;

-- Storage class is a catalog fact, not a guess.
SELECT t.typname, t.typlen, t.typbyval, t.typstorage,
       t.typdefault, t.typcategory
  FROM pg_type t
 WHERE t.typname IN ('int2','int4','int8','numeric',
                 'float4','float8','money','text','uuid')
 ORDER BY t.typlen, t.typname;

Do not use flo for money, and do not use flo for anything you must sum. A flo has about six significant decimal digits, so a total above 100 000 is meaningless; the error is invisible because the type never signals it. Even flo at 15 to 16 digits accumulates error across an aggregate in an order that depends on the plan, so two runs of the same aggregate over the same rows can differ in the last digit. Additionally, flo columns cannot be b-tree range-scanned and re-sorted as reliably as num, because comparison has no total order for N and the sort is not order-stable with respect to equal values.

Integer money beats both. For most systems the most robust representation of a monetary amount is an in of minor units with a separate char(3)char(3) or te currency column, or a numeric(20,4)numeric(20,4) with the currency implied by the account. Both give exact arithmetic, a fixed and documented scale, a stable text representation, and index behaviour identical to any other fixed-width type. The choice between integer minor units and num is a decision about which component of the system owns rounding: with integers, every consumer must remember the scale; with num, the scale is self-describing.

1.2 Text, collations and Unicode

The three character types differ in semantics rather than in storage, and the correct choice among them follows from what the application must enforce, not from what the data happens to be today.

te is the default and should be the default. varchar(n)varchar(n) enforces a maximum length in characters, not bytes, and since PostgreSQL 9.2 it is stored exactly as te — there is no per-row storage penalty for a short value in a long declaration. The remaining costs are real but small: an ATTR length check per value on input, and the loss of a guarantee that a length assertion in application code matches the schema. A varchar(n)varchar(n) can also mask a modelling error, because a column declared varchar(50)varchar(50) will silently truncate on a cast and will reject a value on direct insert, so the same logical bug presents differently depending on the code path that reaches the database.

char(n)char(n) is the type that is almost always wrong. It is blank-padded on storage and trailing blanks are ignored in comparison, so 'ab''ab', 'ab ''ab ', and 'ab ''ab ' are the same value. Two consequences follow. First, char(n)char(n) comparisons and lookups are not equivalent to te comparisons unless the right-hand side is also char(n)char(n), and a cast of a te value to char(n)char(n) in a join condition is a silent correctness hazard. Second, because trailing space is significant to length()length() but not to ==, round-tripping through the type produces values that look padded in exports. Its historical advantage — comparisons could stop at the first difference without a detoast — was removed with the introduction of short var headers in 9.1, so nothing is gained by using it today. Treat char(n)char(n) as a legacy type to be migrated away from, converting with ALTER TABLE ... ALTER COLUMN ... TYPE text USING rtrim(col)::textALTER TABLE ... ALTER COLUMN ... TYPE text USING rtrim(col)::text.

Propertytevarchar(n)varchar(n)char(n)char(n)
Storage1-byte or 4-byte varlena header, TOAST above ~2 kBIdentical to teIdentical to te, but value is blank-padded to nn
Length enforcementNoneError on insert or update exceeding nn charactersError on insert exceeding nn; short values padded
Comparison semanticsByte-wise under the column collationIdentical to teTrailing blanks ignored; 'ab''ab' = 'ab ''ab '
Index behaviourDefault opclass follows the collationIdentical to teIdentical, but ordering differs for values shorter than nn
VerdictDefault choiceUse only to mirror an external fixed-length contractAvoid; migrate to te

Every var and te value is stored in the database encoding, which is a cluster-wide setting fixed at ini time and changeable only by recreating the cluster. UTF-8 is the only sane choice, and the server setting client_ converts on the wire, never on disk. Character counting for length()length() and char_length()char_length() is in characters, not bytes; octet_length()octet_length() is in bytes, and the two differ for any non-ASCII text, which matters when enforcing a wire-format limit that is stated in bytes.

-- Fixed-length comparison semantics, demonstrated rather than described.
SELECT 'ab'::char(6)          = 'ab'::text        AS char_equals_text,
       length('ab'::char(6))          AS char_length,
       'ab'::char(6) = 'ab'::char(6)       AS char_equals_char,
       ('ab'::char(6))::text = 'ab'::text        AS after_cast;

-- varlena header is 1 byte while the value is short; above 2 kB the toast
-- pointer is only 18 bytes, and the value is stored out of line.
SELECT pg_column_size('short')                  AS small_text,
       pg_column_size(repeat('x', 500))            AS medium_text,
       pg_column_size(repeat('x', 20000))          AS toasted_text,
       pg_column_size(repeat('x', 20000) || '!')  AS toasted_plus1;

-- Character count versus byte count for a non-ASCII value.
SELECT 'café'                    AS value,
       length('café')                AS characters,
       octet_length('café')            AS bytes,
       char_length('café')            AS char_length,
       'café'::bytea                AS utf8_bytes;

The remaining character-related types are narrow but have no substitute. by stores an arbitrary byte string with no encoding and no collation; its textual input and output are governed by bytea_, whose esc setting produces a legacy backslash form and whose h setting produces \x\x-prefixed hexadecimal. Binary data in te is always wrong because the server will attempt to interpret and validate it under the client encoding. cit, from the cit extension, is a case-insensitive te; it is implemented as a domain-like type with its own operators, and it cannot be compared efficiently against a plain te column in an index scan without an explicit cast, which is why a stored generated column normalised to lower case is often the better design.

-- bytea representation is a session setting; round-tripping through text is lossy
-- for any binary payload, so store and compare in bytea.
SELECT '\x48656c6c6f'::bytea           AS hex_form,
       length('\x48656c6c6f'::bytea)      AS five_bytes,
       '\x48656c6c6f'::bytea::text    AS default_output;
SET bytea_output = 'escape';
SELECT '\x0001ff'::bytea::text           AS escape_form;
RESET bytea_output;

-- Digest and length over binary data, using core functions only.
SELECT encode(sha256('payload'::bytea), 'hex') AS sha256_hex,
       md5('payload'::bytea)                  AS md5_raw,
       get_byte('ABC'::bytea, 0)              AS first_byte;

1.3 Collation providers, ICU and sort semantics

Collation is where text stops being a simple type, because it injects locale dependence into comparison, ordering, indexing, uniqueness, and pattern matching simultaneously.

A collation in PostgreSQL is a named object in pg_col carrying a provider, a collation name within that provider, a version string, and a determinism flag. Comparison of a collatable type — te, var, ch — resolves at plan time to the operator class of the chosen collation, which selects a comparison function. The default collation for a database is the one named by lc_co at creation time, recorded in pg_da; a column may override it with a COL clause, and that override propagates into every index built on the column.

There are two providers in general use plus one recent addition. The li provider delegates to the operating system’s str, which is locale-dependent, may be slow, and on some platforms is not even reentrant in a multi-threaded client. The i provider delegates to the International Components for Unicode library and is deterministic, reproducible across machines with the same ICU version, and significantly faster than the libc collations that perform locale-sensitive multi-level comparison. The bui provider added in PostgreSQL 17 uses ICU when a suitable library is present without requiring a full ICU installation.

CollationProviderOrderingIndex consequence
"C""C"libc, built inByte-wise on the UTF-8 representation; uppercase before lowercase, no linguistic rulesIndex-usable for LIKE 'prefix%'LIKE 'prefix%'; never reindexed by an ICU upgrade; the only collation that is guaranteed stable across upgrades
POSIX"POSIX"libcByte-wise, locale-independentSame as "C""C" for practical purposes
en_USlibc where glibc supports it, otherwise ICU by name translationLinguistic: case, accents, punctuationIndex order depends on the C library or ICU version; upgrading the library can silently invalidate index ordering
und- or en-USICULinguistic, versionedMust be recorded; a different ICU major version can produce a different order and requires a reindex
Nondeterministic collationICU, created with deterministic = falsedeterministic = falseCase- and accent-insensitiveEnables a case-insensitive unique index; not usable for pattern matching or for LI prefix acceleration
 
-- A case-insensitive, accent-tolerant unique key. Requires a nondeterministic
-- collation, which itself requires ICU and a C library with full Unicode
-- support; both are checked here before the constraint is created.
CREATE COLLATION IF NOT EXISTS ci_en (provider = icu, locale = 'und-u-ks-level2',
                        deterministic = false);
CREATE TABLE account_alias (
  account_id  integer

The performance consequence of collation choice is sharper than it first appears and is covered in detail in § 3.1. In a nondeterministic collation, the b-tree comparison function may consider two values equal that are not byte-equal, so the index stores the value once and cannot supply the bytes for a key lookup; the planner will not use such an index to support LI prefix searches, because prefix semantics are defined on the raw representation. Where a case-insensitive lookup is required, the reliable design is a stored generated column holding the normalised value, indexed normally, and used for equality while the original column remains the source of truth.

-- The generated-column alternative: a real index on normalised text, with the
-- original value preserved. Both columns are in the index, so the index
-- is a covering index for the common lookup-and-display query.
CREATE TABLE customer (
  id      bigint

Pick one collation per column and record it. Mixing collations across the columns of a join predicate forces the planner to insert a sort or a hash on a function call rather than on a memcmp, which routinely turns a hash join into a nested loop with a sort. If two columns must be compared, give them the same collation explicitly at both sites, including in the foreign key and in any check constraint, so that an index on one of them remains usable for a lookup from the other.

1.4 JSON, JSONB and semi-structured data

The choice between js and js is effectively permanent, because the difference is a physical representation difference and every future feature — indexing, containment, path extraction, statistics — favours one of them exclusively.

js preserves the exact text of the input: whitespace, key order, number formatting, duplicate keys. It is validated as well-formed JSON on input and stored as given, which means the same logical document has many physical representations and no canonical form. js parses the input into a binary tree of typed nodes, discards whitespace, removes duplicate keys keeping the last occurrence, normalises numbers, and stores the result with no original ordering. The consequence is that js is faster to query because no reparsing is required, supports structural comparison, supports @>@> containment with an index, and has statistics — but it is slower to write, it loses key order, and it loses the distinction between 11 and 1.01.0 unless num is used as the type modifier.

Propertyjsjs
StorageOriginal text, up to 255 bytes of leading whitespace and key order preservedBinary node tree; keys deduplicated with last-one-wins; no order, no whitespace
Input validationWell-formedness onlyWell-formedness plus normalisation of numbers and escapes
Equality and ORDER BYORDER BYText comparison of the serialised form; two equal documents may compare unequalStructural comparison; == and << are semantically meaningful
IndexingNone; no operator class exists for jsGIN with json (default) or jsonb_p; b-tree on an extracted expression
StatisticsNoneANA records key frequencies in pg_sta via js statistics target
VerdictOnly when the exact bytes are the payload, for example a signed document bodyDefault for any queried or filtered document
 
-- Observable differences between the two representations.
SELECT '{"a":1,"a":2,"b":3}'::json  = '{"a":1,"a":2,"b":3}'::jsonb  AS always_equal,
       ('{"a":1,"a":2,"b":3}'::jsonb)#>>'a'          AS last_duplicate_wins,
       ('{ "a" : 1 }'::jsonb)::text                        AS normalised,
       ('{ "a" : 1 }'::json)::text                         AS preserved,
       ('1.0'::jsonb) = ('1'::jsonb)                  AS numbers_normalised,
       ('1.0'::jsonb) <> ('1.0'::jsonb::numeric)  AS numeric_modifier_differs;

-- Extraction and containment, the two operations that justify jsonb.
SELECT doc FROM shipment
 WHERE payload @> '{"status":"delivered"}'::jsonb;
SELECT payload #>> '{carrier, tracking}' AS tracking_no,
       payload #> '{lines,0,sku}'              AS first_sku,
       jsonb_path_query_first(payload, '$.lines[*].qty ? (@ > 100)') AS large_lines,
       jsonb_path_exists(payload, '$.lines[*] ? (@.qty > 100)')     AS has_large_line
  FROM shipment
 WHERE id = 4711;

Indexing a document is a two-part decision: which operator class, and whether the query is a containment test or an extraction. The default json class indexes every key and every value in the document, which supports @>@>, ??, ?|?|, ?&?&, and @?@? at the cost of index size. jsonb_p indexes only a hash of each whole path-and-value pair; it supports @>@> and @?@? only, and is dramatically smaller and faster to build on large documents, because it stores one entry per distinct path rather than one per distinct key and value. The rule is to use jsonb_p unless the query uses the key-existence operators, which it cannot answer from the hashed representation.

-- GIN index that answers the common containment predicate.
CREATE INDEX shipment_payload_gin ON shipment
  USING gin (payload jsonb_path_ops);

-- Expression index for extraction, which is a b-tree and is far more
-- selective when exactly one key is filtered on.
CREATE INDEX shipment_status_btree ON shipment ((payload ->> 'status'));
CREATE INDEX shipment_placed_cover ON shipment ((payload ->> 'status'))
  INCLUDE (id, customer_id, placed_at);

-- jsonb_set and #- remove keys; both are functional and immutable, so both
-- are indexable and can appear in a generated column.
SELECT jsonb_set(payload, '{status}', '"delivered"'::jsonb) AS updated,
       payload #- '{internal_notes}'                                AS stripped,
       jsonb_object_keys('{"a":1,"b":2}'::jsonb)                  AS key
  FROM shipment WHERE id = 4711;

A js column that is never queried is a te column with extra cost. Normalisation is a per-write cost proportional to document size, and the resulting binary is larger than a compact text representation for many small documents, because the node tree carries type tags and offsets. Before introducing js, state the predicate that will use it. If no predicate is anticipated, store js — or better, model the document as columns, which gives constraints, statistics, narrower indexes, and a planner that can reason about the values.

1.5 Arrays and the absence of a native element type

Arrays are the most misused type in PostgreSQL, chiefly because the type system has no notion of an array’s element domain beyond its OID, and so cannot enforce element-level constraints, statistics, or referential integrity.

An array type exists for every base, composite, domain, range, and enum type; the array type of int is integer[]integer[] and has its own OID in pg_typepg_type. What does not exist is a generic array type with a per-element type parameter. An integer[]integer[] and a text[]text[] are unrelated types, there is no array-of-array covariance, and an anya pseudo-type exists only in function and operator signatures, never as a stored column type. This is the structural reason arrays are a specialist tool: the engine can validate that every element is an int, and can do nothing else about them.

Three consequences follow. Element-level NOT NULLNOT NULL and CH constraints cannot be attached to a column array; only the whole array can be constrained, for example with a check that every element is positive. Element values carry no pg_sta entry, so the planner has no histogram and no most-common-values list for the contents and estimates array predicates by selectivity heuristics only. And there is no foreign key from an array element to another table, because referential integrity in PostgreSQL is defined on columns, not on elements.

CapabilityColumn arrayChild table
Per-element NOT NULLNOT NULL / CHNot expressibleYes, per column
Foreign key per elementNot expressibleYes
Planner statistics on contentsNone; estimates are heuristicFull pg_sta support
IndexingGIN with the default array opclass, or inta for integer setsOrdinary b-tree or partial index
Row size growthLinear in element count; four bytes of overhead per element plus the element itselfConstant per row plus index entries
VerdictGood for short, bounded, rarely-queried lists such as tagsDefault for anything whose elements are addressed individually
 
-- Declaration forms, multi-dimensional shape, and slice semantics.
CREATE TABLE article (
  id          bigint-- Declaration forms, multi-dimensional shape, and slice semantics.
CREATE TABLE article (
  id          bigint GENERATED ALWAYS AS IDENTITY PRIMARY KEY,
  title       texttext     NOT NULL,
  tags        texttext[]   NOT NULL DEFAULT '{}',
  grid        numericnumeric[][],
  CONSTRAINT tags_bounded CHECK (cardinality(tags) <= 12)
);
INSERT INTO article (title, tags, grid)
VALUES ('On MVCC', ARRAY['db','internals'], '{{1,2},{3,4}}'::numeric[][]);
SELECT tags[1]            AS first_tag,
       tags[1:2]         AS slice,
       array_length(tags, 1)  AS dim1,
       cardinality(tags)    AS total,
       array_dims(grid)     AS shape,
       array_agg(t ORDER BY t) AS sorted FROM unnest(tags) AS t;

-- The comparison operators a GIN array index can accelerate.
SELECT id, title FROM article
 WHERE tags @> ARRAY['db']::text[];              -- contains, overlap
SELECT id, title FROM article
 WHERE tags && ARRAY['db','perf']::text[];        -- overlaps any
SELECT id, title FROM article
 WHERE 'db' = ANY (tags);                            -- scalar = ANY, GIN or scan

-- Dimensional shape in one line. Note: an empty array and a NULL array are
-- different states, and NULL elements inside a non-NULL array are legal.
SELECT '{}'::text[]                       AS empty,
       '{a,NULL,b}'::text[]            AS with_null_element,
       array_position('{a,b,c}'::text[], 'b')  AS pos_b,
       array_remove('{a,b,a}'::text[], 'a')  AS without_a,
       array_fill(0, ARRAY[3,3])               AS filled,
       array_cat('{a}'::text[], '{b}'::text[])    AS concatenated;

-- UNNEST in the select list, in FROM, and as a set-returning function in
-- a lateral join. The last form is the one that keeps the other columns.
SELECT a.id, a.title, t.tag
  FROM article a
  CROSS JOIN LATERAL unnest(a.tags) AS t(tag)
 ORDER BY a.id, t.tag;

-- Aggregating child rows into a column array at write time is the
-- acceptable use: the array is a denormalised projection, not the truth.
SELECT customer_id,
       array_agg(sku ORDER BY placed_at) AS skus
  FROM order_line
 GROUP BY customer_id;

Nulls inside arrays are a design smell. array_, unn, and array_p all treat a NULL element as an element, so a list with holes propagates NULLs into every aggregate built from it. Where "no value" must be distinguishable from "absent", either filter with unnest(...)unnest(...) plus a WHERE element IS NOT NULLWHERE element IS NOT NULL, or model the relationship as a child table. The same reasoning applies to the distinction between an empty array and a NULL array: choose one representation of "no entries" and enforce it with NOT NULL DEFAULT '{}'NOT NULL DEFAULT '{}'.

1.6 Ranges, multiranges and canonicalisation

Range types are the one place where PostgreSQL’s type system encodes a domain rule — that a range is half-open and canonical — and that rule is enforced by the type rather than by convention.

The built-in range types are int4, int8, numr, tsr, tstz, and date, each paired with a multirange type — int4mul and so on — since PostgreSQL 14. A range is defined by a lower bound, an upper bound, and a pair of flags recording whether each bound is inclusive. A range may also be empty, or unbounded at either end, and the type distinguishes these states rather than encoding them with sentinels. A discrete range is normalised so that an inclusive lower bound is incremented and an exclusive upper bound is decremented, which makes int4range(1, 5, '[)')int4range(1, 5, '[)') and int4range(1, 6, '[]')int4range(1, 6, '[]') the same value.

Every range value is stored in canonical form, and the canonical form is half-open: a non-empty range is [)[). The functions lower()lower(), upper()upper(), lower_inc()lower_inc(), upper_inc()upper_inc(), lower_inf()lower_inf(), upper_inf()upper_inf(), and isempty()isempty() are the only reliable way to read a range back, because the stored flags are already adjusted. A canonical range has either an inclusive lower bound or an exclusive upper bound, and any input that would violate canonical form is converted on input, not rejected.

Constructor formCanonical resultMeaning
numrange(1.0, 5.0)numrange(1.0, 5.0)[1.0,5.0)[1.0,5.0)Default '[)''[)'; covers 1.0 up to but not including 5.0
numrange(1.0, 5.0, '[]')numrange(1.0, 5.0, '[]')[1.0,5.0][1.0,5.0]Closed; since numr is continuous, inclusive upper bounds are kept
int4range(1, 6, '[]')int4range(1, 6, '[]')[1,6)[1,6)Discrete type converts the inclusive upper bound by decrementing
tsrange(a, NULL)tsrange(a, NULL)[a,)[a,)Unbounded above
tsrange(a, b, '()')tsrange(a, b, '()')(a,b)(a,b)Open; valid, and both bounds are exclusive
'empty'::tstzrange’empty'::tstzrangeemDegenerate; not equal to any point, and excluded from every containment result
range_merge(a, b)range_merge(a, b)canonical unionMay coalesce adjacent discrete ranges into one
 
-- Canonicalisation is observable and total: two different constructors produce
-- identical stored values for a discrete type.
SELECT int4range(1, 6, '[]') = int4range(1, 5, '[)')  AS same_value,
       int4range(1, 6, '[]')::text                                  AS canonical_text,
       lower(int4range(1,6,'[]')), upper(int4range(1,6,'[]')),
       lower_inc(int4range(1,6,'[]')), upper_inc(int4range(1,6,'[]')),
       numrange(1.0,5.0,'[]')::text                                AS continuous_closed,
       'empty'::int4range @> 3                                          AS empty_contains_nothing;

-- Exclusive-constraint idiom: no two bookings for the same room overlap.
CREATE TABLE room_booking (
  room_id  integer

Multiranges exist because a set of non-contiguous intervals of the same type is a first-class value that arises constantly — a customer’s active subscription periods, the maintenance windows assigned to a host. A multirange is always canonical and non-overlapping, and the operations that matter are rang to build one from scattered rows, @>@> for point containment, &&&& for overlap, and the multirange GiST opclass, which is the index type to choose when the value being indexed is itself a set of intervals.

-- Build a multirange from scattered validity rows, then query it as a unit.
-- range_agg coalesces adjacent and overlapping intervals automatically.
SELECT customer_id,
       range_agg(during)                                  AS active_periods,
       range_agg(during) @> now()                        AS is_active_now,
       cardinality(range_agg(during))                      AS disjoint_spans
  FROM subscription_period
 GROUP BY customer_id
HAVING range_agg(during) @> now();

-- The index that makes the above answerable per customer.
CREATE INDEX subscription_periods_gist ON subscription_period
  USING gist (customer_id, during);

-- PostgreSQL 18 temporal constraint: the same invariant expressed as a
-- primary key, enforced by the server, with no application trigger.
CREATE TABLE price_book (
  product_id integer

Adopt date or tstz for any validity interval. The pattern valid_from datevalid_from date NOT NULL, valid_to da NOT NULL, CHECK (valid_to > valid_from) is a strict subset of what a range type gives for free: unbounded open-ended validity, canonical comparison in a single operator, and an exclusion constraint that the database itself enforces. The two-column form also permits the pathological state where two rows for the same key have intervals that overlap, which no constraint prevents.

Ranges do not serialise automatically, and empty ranges surprise callers. A range column written through an ORM or a JSON API will arrive as a string such as ["2026-01-01T00:00:00+00","2026-04-01T00:00:00+00")["2026-01-01T00:00:00+00","2026-04-01T00:00:00+00"), which is the internal text form and not a portable interchange format. On the reading side, ise is a real state distinct from both null and a zero-width range; a query that uses @>@> on an empty range silently returns false, and an application that does not check isempty()isempty() will report a record that covers nothing as if it covered everything.

Exercises 1

  1. Build a money-typed ledger and prove the difference between the three representations. Create one table with numeric(19,4)numeric(19,4), one with in minor units, one with double precisiondouble precision, insert 1 000 000 rows of 0.1 plus a compensating negative, and compare the final balances and the per-table sizes. Deliverable: a table of balance, total relation size, and elapsed insert time, plus a one-paragraph judgement naming which representation you would deploy and why.
  2. Characterise the collation of a text column end to end. Create a table under en_US, index a te column, then recreate the index under und- and under "C""C", running the same ORDER BYORDER BY and LIKE 'a%'LIKE 'a%' query against each. Deliverable: the three EXPLAIN (ANALYZE, BUFFERS)EXPLAIN (ANALYZE, BUFFERS) outputs and the three sorted result sets, with an explicit statement of which collation you would pin and what would break if ICU were upgraded.
  3. Convert a js schema to js without data loss and quantify the cost. Measure the size and write latency of both representations for 100 000 representative documents, then add a json and a jsonb_p GIN index and compare index size and query time for a containment predicate. Deliverable: a size and latency table for five variants with a recommendation justified by the query patterns in your own workload.
  4. Replace an array column with a child table and measure the trade. Model tags both ways, run the same four access patterns — contains, overlaps, count, top-N by tag — and record the plan and latency for each. Deliverable: a matrix of access pattern against representation showing which plans changed, and a written rule for when you will use an array in future schemas.
  5. Implement validity periods twice and compare enforcement. Build the valid/vali version and the range version with an exclusion constraint, then attempt ten overlapping inserts against each from two concurrent sessions. Deliverable: the exact error text for each failure, a demonstration that the two-column version silently accepted the overlap, and the EXP of the range query that finds all periods overlapping a given instant.

2 · DDL, Constraints and Referential Integrity

2.1 Creating and altering relations2.2 Constraints and their exact semantics2.3 Defaults and the fast-default mechanism2.4 Generated and identity columns2.5 Foreign keys and referential integrity2.6 Table inheritance and partitioning compared

This section treats data definition as a locking and durability contract rather than as schema administration. It establishes which DDL statements take which table lock, why that matters on a live system, and how the fast-default mechanism removed the most disruptive common operation. It defines the exact truth tables of every constraint class, including the SQL three-valued logic that makes CH behave counter-intuitively, and explains the difference between validating a constraint now and validating it later. It covers defaults, stored and virtual generated columns, and identity columns as the three mechanisms for populating a column automatically, with the operational cost of each. Finally it covers foreign keys in depth — the exact actions, the locks they take, the performance of the check triggers, and the safe ways to load bulk data — and closes by contrasting table inheritance with declarative partitioning, which are frequently confused.

2.1 Creating and altering relations

Almost every ALTER TABLEALTER TABLE statement takes ACCESS EXCLUSIVEACCESS EXCLUSIVE, and the interesting engineering question is which ones rewrite the table, which are metadata-only, and which can be deferred.

CREATE TABLECREATE TABLE is not interesting in isolation; what matters is that it creates a relation with a storage layer, a toast relation if any variable-length column exists, a free space map, a visibility map, and a set of catalog rows, and that it takes an ACCESS EXCLUSIVEACCESS EXCLUSIVE lock on the new name. ALTER TABLEALTER TABLE is where the operational risk lives. The command always requires ACCESS EXCLUSIVEACCESS EXCLUSIVE on the table, so it waits for every concurrent reader and writer to finish, and every concurrent session then waits for it; on a busy table this is where a maintenance window comes from. The only lever available is therefore to choose an operation whose lock is weaker — which is exactly what NOT VALIDNOT VALID constraints, ALTER TABLE ... SET STATISTICSALTER TABLE ... SET STATISTICS, and the concurrent index build exist to provide.

OperationLock on the relationTable rewriteBlocks readsBlocks writes
ADD COLUMNADD COLUMN, no default, non-volatileACCESS EXCLUSIVEACCESS EXCLUSIVENoYesYes
ADD COLUMNADD COLUMN with a volatile default such as now()now()ACCESS EXCLUSIVEACCESS EXCLUSIVEYes, fullYesYes
ADD COLUMNADD COLUMN with an immutable or stable defaultACCESS EXCLUSIVEACCESS EXCLUSIVENo, missing-value mechanismYesYes
ADD CONSTRAINT ... CHECK ... NOT VALIDADD CONSTRAINT ... CHECK ... NOT VALIDSHARE ROW EXCLUSIVESHARE ROW EXCLUSIVENoNoNo
ADD CONSTRAINT ... FOREIGN KEYADD CONSTRAINT ... FOREIGN KEYSHARE ROW EXCLUSIVESHARE ROW EXCLUSIVE on both tablesNoNoNo
VALIDATE CONSTRAINTVALIDATE CONSTRAINTSHARE UPDATE EXCLUSIVESHARE UPDATE EXCLUSIVENoNoNo
DROP CONSTRAINTDROP CONSTRAINT, SET DEFAULTSET DEFAULT, ALTER COLUMN TYPEALTER COLUMN TYPEACCESS EXCLUSIVEACCESS EXCLUSIVEOnly for type changeYesYes
ALTER COLUMN ... SET STATISTICSALTER COLUMN ... SET STATISTICSSHARE UPDATE EXCLUSIVESHARE UPDATE EXCLUSIVENoNoNo
TRUNACCESS EXCLUSIVEACCESS EXCLUSIVEYesYesYes
CREATE INDEX CONCURRENTLYCREATE INDEX CONCURRENTLYSHARE UPDATE EXCLUSIVESHARE UPDATE EXCLUSIVE, twice per phaseNoNoNo
 
-- A table with the storage parameters that matter in practice. fillfactor leaves
-- room on each page for HOT updates; autovacuum thresholds are lowered
-- because an append-mostly table reaches its threshold much sooner.
CREATE TABLE order_line (
  id            bigint

Unlogged tables are not a free performance option. A table declared UNLO is not written to the write-ahead log, so it is truncated to empty after a crash and is not replicated. It is rewritten in place rather than into a new file, which makes TRUN and index maintenance dramatically faster, and this is the legitimate use: a staging table rebuilt on every load. Any table holding data that must survive restart belongs in the transaction log, regardless of whether it is currently interesting.

-- Safe, near-zero-downtime column addition. PG 11 and later do not rewrite the
-- table for an immutable or STABLE default: the attribute is appended with
-- a "missing value" marker and materialised lazily on first read.
ALTER TABLE order_line
  ADD COLUMN discount_cents bigint

2.2 Constraints and their exact semantics

A constraint is an invariant the server refuses to let the data violate, and the exact semantics of each one — particularly around nulls, deferral, and validation — determine whether it protects the data or merely decorates the schema.

NOT NULLNOT NULL is the only constraint with no three-valued subtlety: a null value in a NOT NULLNOT NULL column is an error, and since PostgreSQL 18 the same column may carry a NOT NULLNOT NULL constraint that is declared NOT VALIDNOT VALID and therefore applies only to rows written after the statement, while the historical attno flag is set to satisfy the optimizer immediately. This makes it possible to add a NOT NULLNOT NULL guarantee to a large table without a full validation scan or a long exclusive lock, at the price of a period during which the existing data does not satisfy the guarantee.

CH is the constraint most often misread. Its predicate is evaluated as a boolean expression, and the constraint is violated only when the expression evaluates to falsefalse. When it evaluates to null — which happens whenever any referenced column is null and the operator is not STRICTSTRICT-neutral — the constraint is satisfied. The SQL standard’s three-valued logic is implemented literally, so CHECK (qty > 0)CHECK (qty > 0) permits a row with a null qtyqty. The remedy is to pair the check with NOT NULLNOT NULL, not to write CHECK (qty IS NOT NULL AND qty > 0)CHECK (qty IS NOT NULL AND qty > 0) everywhere, which works but duplicates a guarantee the column declaration can state directly.

ConstraintNull handlingDeferrableEnforced duringBacking structure
NOT NULLNOT NULLNull is a violationNoInsert and updateAttribute flag; no index
CHNull result passes; only fa failsNoInsert and update, except when the row is a null-extended placeholderExpression in the catalog; no index
UNIAny number of rows with an all-null key unless NULLS NOT DISTINCTNULLS NOT DISTINCTYes, if primaryInsert and update, immediately or at commitUnique index; can be INCLUDEINCLUDE-based
PRIMARY KEYPRIMARY KEYNever null; implies NOT NULLNOT NULL on every columnYes, if declaredSame as uniqueUnique index plus a dependency on it
EXCNull elements are ignored by the excluded operatorsYesSame as uniqueIndex supporting the excluded operators, GiST typically
FOREIGN KEYFOREIGN KEYNull is always allowed; MATCH FULLMATCH FULL requires all-or-none nullYes, and commonly used for cyclic graphsInsert, update of the key, delete or key update of the parentNo index; the referencing columns need one for the delete cascade lookup
 
-- CHECK and three-valued logic, demonstrated.
CREATE TABLE t (a integer-- CHECK and three-valued logic, demonstrated.
CREATE TABLE t (a integer, b integerinteger,
                CONSTRAINT c1 CHECK (a > 0));
INSERT INTO t VALUES (NULL);   -- accepted: a > 0 is UNKNOWN, not FALSE
INSERT INTO t VALUES (0);     -- rejected: FALSE violates the check
INSERT INTO t VALUES (NULL, NULL);
SELECT a, a > 0 IS TRUE AS strictly_true FROM t;

-- PostgreSQL 15 and later: a unique constraint whose nulls collide.
ALTER TABLE t ADD COLUMN ext_ref texttext;
ALTER TABLE t ADD CONSTRAINT t_ext_uniq
       UNIQUE NULLS NOT DISTINCT (ext_ref);
-- Two rows with NULL ext_ref are now a conflict. The index is b-tree on the
-- columns plus a null placeholder, and it is the same structure regardless.

-- The three-way add: attach, skip validation, then validate under a weaker lock.
ALTER TABLE t ADD CONSTRAINT t_b_positive
       CHECK (b > 0) NOT VALID;          -- SHARE ROW EXCLUSIVE, instant
ALTER TABLE t VALIDATE CONSTRAINT t_b_positive;          -- SHARE UPDATE EXCLUSIVE, full scan

Deferrability is the second half of the constraint story. A unique, primary key, exclusion, or foreign key constraint may be declared DEFERRABLE INITIALLY DEFERREDDEFERRABLE INITIALLY DEFERRED, in which case the check is queued rather than performed at statement end, and performed when the outermost transaction commits. This is what makes cyclic and mutually referential inserts possible in a single statement batch, and it is also a genuine hazard: the check moves from the statement to the commit, so an error that would have been reported at row 3 is reported at commit, and long-running transactions accumulate deferred trigger events in pg_tr queues. SET CONSTRAINTS ALL DEFERREDSET CONSTRAINTS ALL DEFERRED changes the mode for the rest of the transaction, and it is a per-transaction override that an application can legitimately set once at the top of a batch import.

-- A genuinely cyclic schema, loaded in one transaction with deferred checks.
CREATE TABLE author (
  id        integer-- A genuinely cyclic schema, loaded in one transaction with deferred checks.
CREATE TABLE author (
  id        integer GENERATED ALWAYS AS IDENTITY PRIMARY KEY,
  name      texttext    NOT NULL,
  flagship_book_id integerinteger
);
CREATE TABLE book (
  id       integerinteger GENERATED ALWAYS AS IDENTITY PRIMARY KEY,
  title    texttext    NOT NULL,
  author_id integerinteger REFERENCES author(id)
      DEFERRABLE INITIALLY DEFERRED
);
ALTER TABLE author ADD CONSTRAINT author_flagship_fk
       FOREIGN KEY (flagship_book_id) REFERENCES book(id)
       DEFERRABLE INITIALLY DEFERRED;

BEGIN;
INSERT INTO author (name) VALUES ('Le Guin') RETURNING id;
INSERT INTO book (title, author_id)
     SELECT 'The Dispossessed', id FROM author WHERE name = 'Le Guin';
UPDATE author SET flagship_book_id =
       (SELECT id FROM book WHERE title = 'The Dispossessed');
-- Both foreign keys are checked now, at COMMIT, and both are satisfied.
COMMIT;

-- Override the mode for one transaction. IMMEDIATE makes every subsequent
-- check fire at statement end, which is what an interactive client wants.
SET CONSTRAINTS ALL IMMEDIATE;

Deferring a unique constraint does not defer the index. The uniqueness is still enforced by a unique index, and inserting a duplicate at commit time produces 2350523505 after all the work has been done. Where a constraint is declared deferrable, the NOT DEFERRABLENOT DEFERRABLE default is the safer setting, and deferral should be enabled for the specific statements that need it rather than for the table. The general rule is to declare a constraint DEFERRABLE INITIALLY IMMEDIATEDEFERRABLE INITIALLY IMMEDIATE if it may ever need deferral, which preserves statement-level error reporting while remaining available to an explicit SET CONSTRAINTS ... DEFERREDSET CONSTRAINTS ... DEFERRED.

2.3 Defaults and the fast-default mechanism

A default is an expression evaluated at write time, and since PostgreSQL 11 the decision of whether that evaluation is visible to existing rows depends entirely on the volatility of the expression.

The default mechanism has three properties worth being precise about. First, the default is not a constraint: it is applied when a column is omitted from an INS and is absent from an explicit NULLNULL. A column with a default can still be null if the caller supplies null, unless NOT NULLNOT NULL is also declared. Second, the expression is evaluated in the context of the inserting statement, so a default may reference other columns of the same row, and may reference anything the inserting user can see, but may not contain a subquery. Third, and most consequentially, defaults of any volatility are permitted, which is correct but expensive.

The fast-default mechanism, introduced in PostgreSQL 11, makes adding a column with a default non-blocking for existing data. The new attribute is appended to the tuple descriptor with a attmis recorded in pg_attributepg_attribute. Existing tuples physically lack the column. A reader that encounters such a tuple substitutes the default expression’s value; a writer that updates such a tuple materialises the real value. The mechanism applies when the default expression is immutable or stable, because those expressions are guaranteed to produce the same value on every evaluation. A volatile default such as now()now() or random()random() cannot be represented this way, because a row’s value must be the value computed at its insert time, so ALTER TABLE ... ADD COLUMN ... DEFAULT now()ALTER TABLE ... ADD COLUMN ... DEFAULT now() rewrites every row under an exclusive lock.

-- Immutable and stable defaults take the missing-value path: no rewrite, no scan.
ALTER TABLE order_line ADD COLUMN source text-- Immutable and stable defaults take the missing-value path: no rewrite, no scan.
ALTER TABLE order_line ADD COLUMN source text NOT NULL DEFAULT 'web';
ALTER TABLE order_line ADD COLUMN received_on datedate DEFAULT ('2000-01-01'::date);
-- now() is STABLE, so it is covered. clock_timestamp() is VOLATILE and is not:
ALTER TABLE order_line ADD COLUMN loaded_at timestamptztimestamptz
       DEFAULT now();                              -- no rewrite
ALTER TABLE order_line ADD COLUMN stamped_at timestamptztimestamptz
       DEFAULT clock_timestamp();                  -- full rewrite

-- Prove which path was taken: attmissingval IS NOT NULL means fast default.
SELECT attname, atttypid::regtype AS type,
       attnotnull,
       CASE WHEN attmissingval IS NOT NULL THEN 'fast default'
            ELSE 'materialised' END AS storage_path
  FROM pg_attribute
 WHERE attrelid = 'order_line'::regclass
   AND attnum > 0 AND NOT attisdropped
 ORDER BY attnum;

-- The volatility that decides, straight from the catalog.
SELECT p.proname, p.provolatile,
       CASE p.provolatile WHEN 'i' THEN 'immutable'
                          WHEN 's' THEN 'stable'
                          WHEN 'v' THEN 'volatile' END AS volatility
  FROM pg_proc p
 WHERE p.proname IN ('now','clock_timestamp','statement_timestamp',
                 'gen_random_uuid','lower','length','random')
 ORDER BY 1;

Defaults are evaluated once per statement, not once per row, only for stable functions. A STA function such as now()now() is guaranteed to return the same value for every row within a single statement, which is what makes it a legal default and a legal index expression candidate under some conditions. A VOLA function such as random()random() is re-evaluated per row, so a multi-row INSERT ... SELECTINSERT ... SELECT produces distinct values. The distinction also governs INSERT ... ON CONFLICTINSERT ... ON CONFLICT, where a stable default is evaluated on the proposed row before the conflict test.

2.4 Generated and identity columns

PostgreSQL offers two automatic-column mechanisms with different costs — stored generated columns occupy storage and must be immutable, virtual generated columns occupy nothing and are computed on every read — plus the identity syntax that replaces serial types.

A stored generated column is computed at write time and persisted in the heap, so it is readable without evaluation, is indexable exactly like a normal column, and may be used as a partition key or in a foreign key.
