The expression must be IMMU, because the stored value must be reproducible for the life of the row; a stable function such as now()now() is rejected with ERROR: generation expression is not immutableERROR: generation expression is not immutable. A virtual generated column, new in PostgreSQL 18, stores no data and is computed on every read. It accepts a broader set of expressions — still immutable, but nothing that must be evaluated once — costs nothing per row on disk, and cannot be indexed directly, since there is no storage to index. It must therefore be paired with an expression index, which is the normal pattern for a computed search key.

Identity columns exist to replace ser and bigs, which are pseudo-types that expand to a sequence, a default, and an ownership dependency in a single undocumented step. GENERATED ALWAYS AS IDENTITYGENERATED ALWAYS AS IDENTITY produces the same sequence behaviour but records the intent in the column’s attid and makes the ownership explicit, and it makes the "always" semantics meaningful: an explicit value is rejected unless the statement carries OVERRIDING SYSTEM VALUEOVERRIDING SYSTEM VALUE.

PropertySTO generatedVIR generated (18+)ser / identity
Value computedAt write timeAt read time, every timeAt write time, from a sequence
Occupies heap storageYes, per rowNo, only a catalog expressionYes, the value itself
Expression volatilityMust be IMMUMust be IMMUSequence nex, volatile by nature
Directly indexableYesNo; requires an expression index on the same expressionYes
Read costIncluded in the tupleCPU per row, per reference, not memoisedIncluded in the tuple
Explicit value on insertRejected unless OVERRRejected unless OVERRser accepts; ALW identity rejects
 
-- Stored generated column: a durable search key derived from two base columns.
CREATE TABLE invoice (
  id             bigint-- Stored generated column: a durable search key derived from two base columns.
CREATE TABLE invoice (
  id             bigint GENERATED ALWAYS AS IDENTITY PRIMARY KEY,
  customer_id    bigintbigint  NOT NULL REFERENCES customer(id),
  net_cents      bigintbigint  NOT NULL,
  vat_rate       numeric(5,4)numeric(5,4) NOT NULL DEFAULT 0.1900,
  total_cents    bigintbigint GENERATED ALWAYS AS
                       (net_cents + round(net_cents * vat_rate)::bigint) STORED,
  CONSTRAINT total_positive CHECK (total_cents > 0)
);
CREATE INDEX invoice_total_idx ON invoice (total_cents);

-- Virtual generated column: zero storage, still immutable, not indexable
-- on its own. The expression index is what makes it searchable.
CREATE TABLE event (
  id         bigintbigint GENERATED ALWAYS AS IDENTITY PRIMARY KEY,
  happened_at timestamptztimestamptz NOT NULL,
  kind       texttext    NOT NULL,
  day_bucket datedate GENERATED ALWAYS AS (happened_at::date) VIRTUAL
);
CREATE INDEX event_day_idx ON event ((happened_at::date));

-- identity with explicit sequence options and the ALWAYS/BY DEFAULT choice.
CREATE TABLE ticket (
  id        bigintbigint GENERATED BY DEFAULT AS IDENTITY
              (START WITH 1000000 INCREMENT BY 1
               NO MAXVALUE CACHE 50) PRIMARY KEY,
  title     texttext    NOT NULL
);
INSERT INTO ticket (id, title) VALUES (7, 'legacy import');
-- BY DEFAULT permits this. ALWAYS would raise:  OVERRIDING SYSTEM VALUE required.

Prefer identity columns, and keep the sequence out of band for bulk loads. The sequence advances for every attempted insert including those that later fail or roll back, so a big identity column is monotonic but never gapless — a fact worth stating explicitly to anyone who expects gaplessness. For a bulk load, generate keys with set against the owning sequence and insert explicit values under OVERRIDING SYSTEM VALUEOVERRIDING SYSTEM VALUE, or use CO with an explicit first column, which is far faster than row-by-row insertion and avoids per-row sequence calls entirely.

-- Finding the sequence behind a column, and the supported way to reseed it.
SELECT attname, attidentity,
       pg_get_serial_sequence('ticket', attname) AS owned_sequence
  FROM pg_attribute
 WHERE attrelid = 'ticket'::regclass AND attnum > 0;

-- After COPY with explicit ids, align the sequence. last_value must exceed the
-- maximum present in the table or the next insert collides immediately.
SELECT setval(pg_get_serial_sequence('ticket', 'id'),
       (SELECT max(id) FROM ticket));

-- Converting an inherited serial column to an identity column in place. This
-- is a metadata-only change: the existing sequence is retained and owned.
ALTER TABLE ticket
  ALTER COLUMN id ADD GENERATED BY DEFAULT AS IDENTITY;

-- Removing the automatic behaviour entirely, keeping the plain column.
ALTER TABLE ticket ALTER COLUMN id DROP IDENTITY;
ALTER TABLE ticket ALTER COLUMN id DROP DEFAULT;

2.5 Foreign keys and referential integrity

A foreign key is implemented as a pair of internal triggers and a scan of the referencing table, and understanding that implementation explains both its locking behaviour and its cost on bulk operations.

Adding a foreign key to an existing column requires the referenced columns to be covered by a unique constraint or a non-partial unique index; PostgreSQL will not accept a foreign key pointing at a merely non-unique column, and the error names the missing index explicitly. The referenced side is then protected by a trigger that takes a FOR KEY SHAREFOR KEY SHARE row lock on the parent row when a child row is inserted, updated, or deleted, and by a second trigger on the parent that takes FOR UPDATEFOR UPDATE-class row locks before any delete or key-modifying update. That is why a hot parent row serialises concurrent child inserts, and why an ON DELETE CASCADEON DELETE CASCADE on a large parent acquires locks proportional to the number of children before it deletes anything.

The match rules are MATCH SIMPLEMATCH SIMPLE, the default, under which a child row is permitted to be null in any referencing column, and a row with a null in any column is skipped entirely; MATCH FULLMATCH FULL, under which the columns must be either all non-null and valid, or all null; and MATCH PARTIALMATCH PARTIAL, which PostgreSQL does not implement and which is parsed only for compatibility. The referential actions — NO ACTIONNO ACTION, REST, CAS, SET NULLSET NULL, SET DEFAULTSET DEFAULT — differ from each other in one respect that matters under concurrency: NO ACTIONNO ACTION is checked at the end of the statement and permits other work to proceed first, whereas REST is checked immediately and refuses to let the statement reach the point where the check happens. REST is the correct choice when the intent is to forbid the operation outright.

ActionEffect on the child rowWhen checkedPractical note
NO ACTIONNO ACTIONNone; error if orphans would resultEnd of statementDefault; allows concurrent work to be overtaken before the check
RESTNone; errorImmediatelyUse when the delete must be refused even if a later statement would resolve the dependency
CASDeleted or key updatedDuring the parent operationCascade on delete is one statement; large cascades hold locks and generate WAL proportional to the child count
SET NULLSET NULLReferencing columns set to nullDuring the parent operationRequires the columns to be nullable
SET DEFAULTSET DEFAULTReferencing columns reset to their defaultDuring the parent operationRequires the columns to have a default
 
-- The full foreign key, attached without validation and then validated under a
-- weaker lock. The child index is not optional: it is what makes the parent-side
-- delete check a range scan instead of a sequential scan of the whole child table.
ALTER TABLE order_line ADD CONSTRAINT order_line_order_fk
       FOREIGN KEY (order_id) REFERENCES "order"(id)
       ON DELETE CASCADE ON UPDATE RESTRICT MATCH SIMPLE
       DEFERRABLE INITIALLY IMMEDIATE NOT VALID;
CREATE INDEX CONCURRENTLY order_line_order_idx ON order_line (order_id);
ALTER TABLE order_line VALIDATE CONSTRAINT order_line_order_fk;

-- Inspect the triggers the constraint created, with their definitions.
SELECT conname, contype, condeferrable, condeferred, convalidated,
       conrelid::regclass AS on_table,
       pg_get_constraintdef(oid) AS definition
  FROM pg_constraint
 WHERE conrelid IN ('order_line'::regclass, 'order'::regclass)
 ORDER BY conname;

-- Verify the invariant holds today, with the same logic the trigger uses.
SELECT count(*) AS orphan_lines
  FROM order_line l
  LEFT JOIN "order" o ON o.id = l.order_id
 WHERE o.id IS NULL;

-- Bulk-loading into a referenced table: disable the check for the duration of the
-- load, then prove the data is clean before re-enabling. This requires
-- superuser, and the ALTER must be outside an explicit transaction block.
SET session_replication_role = 'replica';
-- COPY supplies the GENERATED ALWAYS identity column verbatim and does not advance
-- the sequence, which is exactly why the setval below is mandatory afterwards.
COPY order_line (id, order_id, sku, qty, unit_cents) FROM '/tmp/order_line.csv' WITH (FORMAT csv);
SET session_replication_role = 'origin';
SELECT setval(pg_get_serial_sequence('order_line', 'id'),
       (SELECT max(id) FROM order_line));

-- The check that must run before re-enabling, and the constraint validation
-- that replaces the guarantee the trigger would have provided.
SELECT count(*) AS orphan_lines
  FROM order_line l LEFT JOIN "order" o ON o.id = l.order_id
 WHERE o.id IS NULL;
BEGIN;
DELETE FROM order_line l
 USING "order" o
 WHERE o.id = l.order_id AND o.id NOT IN (SELECT id FROM "order");
ALTER TABLE order_line VALIDATE CONSTRAINT order_line_order_fk;
COMMIT;

-- The supported check for a child table lacking an index on the key columns:
-- this is the cost that the cascade trigger pays on every parent delete.
EXPLAIN (ANALYZE, BUFFERS)
SELECT 1 FROM order_line WHERE order_id = 42;

Disabling triggers silently removes constraints, and the re-enabling step is where data is lost. With session_replication_role = 'replica’session_replication_role = 'replica', foreign key triggers do not fire in either direction, and a load that was meant to be temporary can leave orphans that are only discovered when the first real delete arrives. The safe sequence is: disable, load, re-enable, count orphans, delete orphans, then validate — in that order, with each step verified, and never in a session where the setting could leak into a pooled connection. SET LOCALSET LOCAL does not help here because the setting requires superuser and persists until explicitly reset.

2.6 Table inheritance and partitioning compared

Inheritance is a query-time union of physical tables; partitioning is a query-time restriction of one logical table, and confusing the two produces designs that scale badly in either direction.

In table inheritance, a child table declares its parent with INHE, and a query against the parent includes the children by default. The behaviour has been deliberately minimised over successive releases: since PostgreSQL 10 the system no longer creates indexes on children automatically for a parent’s unique or primary key, so inheritance cannot be used to satisfy a uniqueness constraint across the hierarchy; and since the same release a query on the parent no longer guarantees child ordering unless ORDER BYORDER BY is given, because the plan may merge children in any order. Constraints added to a parent are checked against the parent only. The ON keyword excludes children from a single query, and ALTER TABLE ... NO INHERITALTER TABLE ... NO INHERIT detaches a child at the catalog level without moving data.

Declarative partitioning, available since PostgreSQL 10 and mature since 11, is the opposite design. There is one table with one set of constraints, one sequence, one primary key, and one set of statistics. The partitions hold the data, each has its own indexes, and the planner prunes partitions that cannot contain qualifying rows. The two are not alternatives at the same granularity: inheritance answers "one of several dissimilar tables with a common supertype", partitioning answers "one table that is too large to scan".

PropertyTable inheritanceDeclarative partitioning
Single primary key across the setNot enforced; child indexes are not maintained automaticallyYes; the partitioned index is enforced on every partition
One sequence for the setNo; each child has its ownYes; the identity sequence belongs to the parent
Query against the parentAppends children by UNION ALLUNION ALL semanticsOne relation, with partitions pruned or executed
Child rows visible with ONNoNo; a single partition may be excluded
Vacuum and statisticsIndependent per child; no coordinationAutovacuum treats the parent as one partitioned table, but each partition is vacuumed independently
Legitimate useHeterogeneous extensions of a common shape, for example a per-tenant tableTime series, very large tables, retention by detaching partitions
 
-- Inheritance: the classic per-tenant shape, and the ONLY clause that makes it
-- a single relation for the parent's own data.
CREATE TABLE party (
  id      bigint-- Inheritance: the classic per-tenant shape, and the ONLY clause that makes it
-- a single relation for the parent's own data.
CREATE TABLE party (
  id      bigint GENERATED ALWAYS AS IDENTITY PRIMARY KEY,
  kind    texttext    NOT NULL
);
CREATE TABLE person (bio texttext) INHERITS (party);
CREATE TABLE company (reg_no texttext) INHERITS (party);

SELECT tableoid::regclass AS stored_in, kind,
       CASE WHEN tableoid = 'person'::regclass THEN bio END AS person_bio
  FROM party
 ORDER BY id;                                -- includes both children
SELECT FROM ONLY party ORDER BY id;         -- parent rows only

-- Partitioning: one logical table, one key, and a partition per month.
CREATE TABLE measurement (
  sensor_id integerinteger   NOT NULL,
  taken_at  timestamptztimestamptz NOT NULL,
  value     double precisiondouble precision NOT NULL,
  PRIMARY KEY (sensor_id, taken_at)
) PARTITION BY RANGE (taken_at);

CREATE TABLE measurement_2026_03 PARTITION OF measurement
       FOR VALUES FROM ('2026-03-01') TO ('2026-04-01');
CREATE TABLE measurement_2026_04 PARTITION OF measurement
       FOR VALUES FROM ('2026-04-01') TO ('2026-05-01')
       PARTITION BY RANGE (taken_at);   -- sub-partitioning
CREATE TABLE measurement_2026_04_01 PARTITION OF measurement_2026_04
       FOR VALUES FROM ('2026-04-01') TO ('2026-04-02');

-- Attaching and detaching without blocking, and the plan proof that pruning works.
CREATE TABLE measurement_2026_05 (LIKE measurement);
BEGIN;
ALTER TABLE measurement ATTACH PARTITION measurement_2026_05
       FOR VALUES FROM ('2026-05-01') TO ('2026-06-01');
COMMIT;
ALTER TABLE measurement DETACH PARTITION measurement_2026_05 CONCURRENTLY;

-- Attach fails unless the existing data satisfies the partition bound, and the
-- error names the offending row, which is the fastest way to find bad data.
--   ERROR:  row in partition measurement_2026_03 contains value that violates
--          partition constraint

-- Maintenance that does not block readers: drop and rebuild a partition's
-- index concurrently, then the partition index is matched to the parent index.
CREATE INDEX CONCURRENTLY measurement_2026_05_taken_idx
       ON measurement_2026_05 (taken_at);
ALTER INDEX measurement_taken_idx ATTACH PARTITION measurement_2026_05_taken_idx;

-- Confirm pruning is actually happening; Subplans Removed is the evidence.
EXPLAIN (ANALYZE, BUFFERS)
SELECT count(*) FROM measurement
 WHERE taken_at >= '2026-03-01' AND taken_at < '2026-03-02';

Inheritance gives no constraint across the hierarchy, which is the deciding factor. If a child can contain a duplicate of a parent key, and no cross-table index exists, then any query that must prove uniqueness must scan every child, and no amount of indexing fixes it. Where a hierarchy is a genuine modelling need, prefer a single table with a discriminating column, or a single table with a type-specific side table joined on the identifier, because both keep the constraints, the statistics, and the join machinery in the engine’s hands.

Exercises 2

  1. Build a lock-behaviour matrix for your schema. For each of fifteen DDL statements you actually use in deployments, run it against a table under concurrent read and write load and record mo from pg_l for both the DDL and the blocked session. Deliverable: a table of statement, lock mode, seconds blocked, and whether the operation rewrote the table, verified by comparing pg_relat and the table’s xm before and after.
  2. Prove the fast-default mechanism. On a 5-million-row table, time ADD COLUMNADD COLUMN with an immutable default, with now()now(), and with random()random(), and read attmis for each. Deliverable: three timings, three catalog readings, and a written rule for which defaults are safe to add to a hot table and what to do about the ones that are not.
  3. Characterise CH exhaustively. Build a table with a nullable column and a CH, then insert every combination of null, boundary, and out-of-range values, recording the exact SQLSTATE for each rejection. Deliverable: a truth table of inserted or rejected with the SQLSTATE code, and an explicit list of the checks in your production schemas that are currently permitting nulls unintentionally.
  4. Load a million rows into a table with a self-referencing foreign key and measure the cost of the constraint. Time the load with the constraint present, then with triggers disabled and a validating scan afterwards. Deliverable: the two timings, the buffer counts from the validation scan, and a statement of the maximum acceptable child-table size for which the per-row check overhead stays below your stated budget.
  5. Convert a set of time-series tables to a partitioned parent and prove equivalence. Keep the same primary key, add 24 monthly partitions, migrate the data with a batched INSERT ... SELECTINSERT ... SELECT, and compare row counts, constraint violations, and the EXP of the four most common queries. Deliverable: a before-and-after plan table for each query, a note on which queries improved, and a measurement of the ATTACH PARTITIONATTACH PARTITION downtime you incurred.

3 · Indexes, Index Types and the Planner

3.1 The B-tree and what it actually accelerates3.2 Hash, GiST, SP-GiST, GIN and BRIN compared3.3 Multicolumn indexes and column order3.4 Partial indexes and the predicate requirement3.5 Reading EXPLAIN node by node3.6 Statistics, extended statistics and ANALYZE

This section is about the correspondence between an index’s internal structure and the set of queries it can serve, and about the evidence needed to know whether a given index is serving its purpose. It covers the B-tree in detail, including the recent skip-scan capability that weakens the traditional column-ordering rule; it places the five general-purpose access methods side by side by the data type each suits and the exact reason, distinguishing lossless from lossy recheck; it derives the multicolumn ordering rule from how a B-tree search descends; it explains the predicate-matching requirement that makes a partial index either usable or invisible; and it develops a disciplined method for reading a plan and for correcting the estimates that produce a bad one. The argument is that index design is search-structure design, and that the planner will only use what the structure can answer.

3.1 The B-tree and what it actually accelerates

A B-tree index answers a strictly limited family of questions about a single ordered sequence, and every operational claim about indexing follows from the boundary of that family.

The default access method is a balanced B-tree over a sorted key, with one key space per column and a comparison function selected by the operator class. It accelerates equality and ordering comparisons on the indexed column: ==, <><>, <<, <=<=, >>, >=>=, BET, I (which the planner expands into a set of index conditions and, since 12, into an array scan of a single descent), and IS NULLIS NULL / IS NOT NULLIS NOT NULL. It also provides the ordering for ORDER BYORDER BY without a sort, and the uniqueness enforcement for UNI and PRIMARY KEYPRIMARY KEY. It does not accelerate substring search, case-insensitive search under a linguistic collation, LIKE '%x’LIKE '%x' in any collation, or any predicate on an expression that is not itself indexed.

Prefix search is the important boundary case. LIKE 'abc%'LIKE 'abc%' can be served by a B-tree only when the operator class supports pattern matching: text_pat for te and varchar_p for var, or the CC and PO collations, whose operator classes are byte-ordered. A regular text class under en_US sorts by linguistic rules, so 'Aardvark''Aardvark', 'aardwolf''aardwolf', and 'Zebra''Zebra' are not adjacent and the scan cannot stop at the first non-match. This is why the collation decision in § 1.3 is an indexing decision and not only a display decision.

Query shapeB-tree usableWhat is required
WHERE k = 42WHERE k = 42YesNothing beyond the default opclass
WHERE k > now() - interval '7 days’WHERE k > now() - interval '7 days'YesNothing; the bound is evaluated once per statement
WHERE k IN (1,2,3)WHERE k IN (1,2,3)YesMultiple index conditions, or a single array descent
ORDER BY k LIMIT 10ORDER BY k LIMIT 10YesForward scan; the plan stops after ten rows
WHERE k LIKE 'abc%'WHERE k LIKE 'abc%'Only with text_pat or COLLATE "C"COLLATE "C"An opclass whose ordering is byte-wise
WHERE k ILIKE 'abc%'WHERE k ILIKE 'abc%'Nopg_ GIN or GiST, or a normalised generated column
WHERE k LIKE '%abc’WHERE k LIKE '%abc'Nopg_, or a functional index on reverse(k)reverse(k) for the suffix case
WHERE lower(k) = 'x’WHERE lower(k) = 'x'Only as an expression indexExactly the same expression text as the query, see § 6.3
 
-- A B-tree tuned for a mostly-append, update-heavy table. fillfactor 80 leaves room
-- for page splits and HOT updates; deduplication_items is on by default since 13.
CREATE INDEX CONCURRENTLY order_line_order_qty_idx
       ON order_line USING btree (order_id, qty DESC)
       WITH (fillfactor = 80, deduplicate_items = on);

-- Separate opclass for prefix search, and the proof that the plan changed.
CREATE INDEX customer_email_pattern_idx ON customer (email text_pattern_ops);
EXPLAIN (ANALYZE, BUFFERS)
SELECT id FROM customer WHERE email LIKE 'ops%';
--  Index Scan using customer_email_pattern_idx on customer
--    Index Cond: (email ~~ 'ops%'::text)
-- With only the default opclass present, the same query is a sequential scan.

-- What the index really contains: operator classes, access method, options,
-- and which columns are key columns versus INCLUDE payload.
SELECT i.indexrelid::regclass        AS idx,
       am.amname,
       ARRAY[SELECT opc.opcname FROM unnest(i.indclass::oid[]) WITH ORDINALITY AS u(oid, o)
                     JOIN pg_opclass opc ON opc.oid = u.oid ORDER BY u.o] AS opclasses,
       ARRAY[SELECT attname FROM unnest(i.indcollation::oid[]) WITH ORDINALITY AS u(oid, o)
                     JOIN pg_attribute a ON a.attrelid = i.indexrelid AND a.attnum = u.o] AS collations,
       i.indnkeyatts, i.indnatts,
       (i.indoption::int2[])[1]      AS first_key_flags,
  FROM pg_index i
  JOIN pg_class c ON c.oid = i.indexrelid
  JOIN pg_am am ON am.oid = c.relam
 WHERE i.indexrelid = 'order_line_order_qty_idx'::regclass;

Three structural refinements in recent releases are worth knowing because they change both the storage layout and the strategy choice. Deduplication, on by default since 13, allows many index entries that differ only in a trailing suffix to be stored once with a tuple count, which is a large win for indexes over low-cardinality prefixes and for composite keys with repeated leading values. Bottom-up page deletion, since 14, makes VAC delete index pages by moving entries up rather than by the previous page-shifting mechanism, which halves the write amplification of index vacuuming and largely removes the historical reason to run REI routinely. Skip scans, new in 18, allow a multicolumn B-tree search to proceed when the leading column has few distinct values, by descending on the second column for each distinct value of the first; the traditional "equality columns first" rule in § 3.3 is therefore a heuristic rather than a law, and must be verified with EXPLAINEXPLAIN.

-- Skip scan is visible in the plan only through its effect; the node is still an
-- Index Scan. Confirm the shape of the data that enables it.
SELECT tenant_id, count(DISTINCT order_state) AS states,
       count(*) AS rows
  FROM order_line
 GROUP BY tenant_id
 ORDER BY states
 LIMIT 10;

-- Ask the server directly whether an index is structurally sound and how
-- full its pages are. amcheck is a contrib extension; it reads the whole
-- index, so run it off-peak.
CREATE EXTENSION IF NOT EXISTS amcheck;
SELECT bt_index_check(index AS regclass, heapallindexed AS boolean)
  FROM generate_series('order_line_order_qty_idx'::regclass) index;
--   bt_index_check        | heapallindexed
-- ------------------------+---------------
--  order_line_order_qty_idx | t

-- Index bloat, from pageinspect and pgstattuple. Average free space and
-- dead tuple counts are the only supported bloat measurement.
CREATE EXTENSION IF NOT EXISTS pageinspect;
CREATE EXTENSION IF NOT EXISTS pgstattuple;
SELECT indexrelid::regclass AS idx,
       pg_size_pretty(pg_relation_size(indexrelid)) AS size,
       round(pgstatindex(indexrelid)::numeric, 2) AS btree_stats;

Index every foreign key that the parent may delete or re-key. PostgreSQL does not create these indexes for you, and their absence is invisible until the first large delete of a parent row, at which point the referential action trigger performs a sequential scan of the entire child table while holding a row lock on the parent. A single CREATE INDEX CONCURRENTLYCREATE INDEX CONCURRENTLY at design time removes an entire class of production incident. The same argument applies to the referencing side of a self-referential hierarchy and to any column used in a WH clause that will be combined with a join.

3.2 Hash, GiST, SP-GiST, GIN and BRIN compared

Five access methods ship with the server, and each exists because a particular data type has an internal structure that admits a search method other than ordered comparison; choosing by data type rather than by query shape is the correct heuristic.

Hash supports only equality, equality in multiple columns, and ININ. Since PostgreSQL 10 it is crash-safe and write-ahead logged, and since 12 a multicolumn hash index applies the hash to the concatenation of the columns, so a query filtering on all of them can use it. It is appropriate only for equality-only lookups on wide or high-cardinality keys where a B-tree’s page splits and comparisons are the dominant cost — integer and uu keys on very large tables being the realistic case. GiST is a general search tree over an internal "signature" supplied by an operator class: it handles scalar ranges through rang, in through inet, tsve through tsvect, b, po and pol for geometry, and cu, lt, hst, and jsonb_p from extensions. SP-GiST is a partitioning search tree offering more specialised operator classes, notably poin, box, poly, rang, inet, and text, and it is single-column only.

Access methodData type it suitsOperators acceleratedRecheckTypical use
btAny orderable scalar== <><> << <=<= >> >=>= I BET IS NULLIS NULL prefix LINoDefault; keys, ranges, ordering, uniqueness
haint, big, uu, wide fixed-width==, I, multicolumn equalityNoEquality-only lookups on very large tables
giRange, in, tsve, geometry, js with path&&&& @>@> <<<< @@@@ &<&< &>&> -|--|-Usually, unless the opclass is declared lossGeospatial, exclusion constraints, text search, containment
spgpo, b, pol, in, te, rangeSame family as GiST with more specific classesConfigurable per opclassPoint and network proximity where a quadtree decomposition beats a GiST
gtsve, js, arrays, hst@>@> ?? ?|?| ?&?& &&&& @@@@NoInverted indexes: multi-valued containment and keyword search
brAny type, but only for values physically correlated with heap orderRange predicates on the indexed columnAlways, because block summarisation is lossyAppend-only time series, at a tiny fraction of a B-tree’s size
 
-- The catalog view of what is installed, and of what each opclass claims.
SELECT am.amname,
       count(DISTINCT opc.opcname)  AS opclasses,
       string_agg(DISTINCT t.typname, ', ' ORDER BY t.typname) AS input_types
  FROM pg_am am
  LEFT JOIN pg_opclass opc ON opc.opcmethod = am.oid
  LEFT JOIN pg_type t ON t.oid = opc.opcintype
 GROUP BY 1
 ORDER BY 1;

-- GIN with a trigram index is the general answer to LIKE '%x%' and ILIKE.
CREATE EXTENSION IF NOT EXISTS pg_trgm;
CREATE INDEX product_name_trgm ON product
       USING gin (name gin_trgm_ops);
EXPLAIN (ANALYZE, BUFFERS)
SELECT id FROM product WHERE name ILIKE '%stainless%';
--  Bitmap Index Scan on product_name_trgm  (cost=.. rows=.. width=..)
--        Recheck Cond: (name ~~* '%stainless%'::text)
--  Note: the recheck is a real heap recheck, not a lossy-block recheck.

-- GiST for full text with similarity ordering, which no GIN opclass provides.
CREATE INDEX doc_body_gin ON document USING gin (to_tsvector('english', body));
CREATE INDEX doc_body_gist ON document USING gist (to_tsvector('english', body));
-- GIST wins for  ts_rank  ordering and for  %  similarity; GIN wins for raw hits.

BRIN is the correct answer for append-only data and the wrong answer for everything else. A BRIN index stores one summary tuple per pages_p heap blocks, recording the minimum and maximum value found in that range of pages. A query can therefore exclude a whole range of blocks without reading them, which is why a BRIN index on a monotonically increasing timestamp is often three orders of magnitude smaller than a B-tree. But the structure can only say "the values in this block range lie between these bounds", so every candidate block is rechecked against the heap. Where values are not physically correlated with insertion order, every block range overlaps every query range, the index returns everything, and the plan degrades to a sequential scan with extra work. The correlation is a property of physical layout, not of the data, and it must be measured.

Lossy versus lossless recheck is an opclass property, and getting it wrong produces a wrong answer, not just a slow one. A lossless opclass guarantees that its index predicate implies the query predicate, so the recheck is redundant and is omitted from the plan. A lossy opclass returns candidates, and the executor re-tests the real condition on the heap tuple; that is correct but slow. The failures that are actually reported come from third-party opclasses whose losslessness declaration is wrong, and from GiST indexes built on an expression whose underlying collation was later changed, which silently invalidates the recheck’s assumption. Never assume an index gives exactly the rows the predicate names; read the Recheck CondRecheck Cond line and the Rows Removed by Index RecheckRows Removed by Index Recheck counter.

3.3 Multicolumn indexes and column order

A multicolumn B-tree is a search tree over tuples of values, and the only order it can exploit is lexicographic — which is why the first column dominates every decision about whether the index is usable.

The leftmost-prefix rule follows directly from the comparison. With a composite key (a, b, c)(a, b, c), the index is sorted by aa first, then by bb within a run of equal aa, then by cc. A search that constrains only bb cannot use the structure, because every distinct value of aa starts a fresh run and the bb values are not globally sorted. A search that constrains only aa works and returns a contiguous run. A search that constrains aa and then a range on bb works only for the run of aa being scanned, because the descent narrows at bb and then cannot resume. A search that constrains an equality on aa and then anything on bb or cc works fully, because equality fixes a single run.

The practical rule that follows — put equality-restricted columns before range-restricted columns — is a consequence, not a law. Skip scans, new in 18, relax it by allowing the descent to enumerate the distinct values of a low-cardinality leading column; the planner will use one when the leading column has few distinct values and the alternative is a full scan. The correct procedure is to write the index the way the rule says and then verify with EXP that the plan uses it and does not scan more rows than the predicate admits.

-- The canonical shape: equality column first, range column second, payload last.
CREATE INDEX CONCURRENTLY measurement_sensor_time_idx
       ON measurement (sensor_id, taken_at DESC)
       INCLUDE (value, id);

-- The same index, used two ways. Both plans are index scans; the one with the
-- range predicate is a bounded Index Cond, not a filter above the scan.
EXPLAIN (ANALYZE, BUFFERS, SETTINGS)
SELECT taken_at, value FROM measurement
 WHERE sensor_id = 17 AND taken_at >= '2026-03-01';
--  Index Scan using measurement_sensor_time_idx on measurement
--    Index Cond: (sensor_id = 17 AND taken_at >= '2026-03-01 00:00:00+00'::timestamptz)
--    Buffers: shared hit=9
--  Heap Fetches: 0        <-- INCLUDE worked: the heap was never visited

-- Reversing the order makes the range bound unservable at the descent, and the
-- planner is forced to a filter over a much larger scan of the run.
CREATE INDEX measurement_time_sensor_idx ON measurement (taken_at, sensor_id);
EXPLAIN (ANALYZE)
SELECT FROM measurement
 WHERE sensor_id = 17 AND taken_at >= '2026-01-01' AND taken_at < '2026-02-01';
--  Index Scan using measurement_time_sensor_idx
--    Index Cond: (taken_at >= ... AND taken_at < ...)
--    Filter: (sensor_id = 17)
--    Rows Removed by Filter: 41 200

-- Opclasses and sort direction are per column, and a single index may mix them.
CREATE INDEX measurement_mixed_idx ON measurement
       (sensor_id, taken_at DESC, value DESC NULLS LAST);

-- A query that mixes directions can still be served when the sort matches.
EXPLAIN (ANALYZE, BUFFERS)
SELECT sensor_id, taken_at, value FROM measurement
 WHERE sensor_id = 3
 ORDER BY taken_at DESC, value DESC NULLS LAST
 LIMIT 100;
--  Limit  (actual time=.. rows=100 loops=1)
--    ->  Index Scan using measurement_mixed_idx  (actual time=.. rows=100 loops=1)

-- Opclass inventory for one column, with the access method each belongs to.
SELECT am.amname, opc.opcname, opc.opcintype::regtype AS intype,
       opc.opcdefault, opc.opckeytype::regtype AS keytype
  FROM pg_opclass opc
  JOIN pg_am am ON am.oid = opc.opcmethod
 WHERE opc.opcintype = 'text'::regtype
 ORDER BY am.amname, opc.opcname;

-- Index size and access counts: the ledger discussed in depth in section 6.
SELECT s.relname AS index_name,
       pg_size_pretty(pg_relation_size(s.relid)) AS size,
       s.idx_scan, s.idx_tup_read, s.idx_tup_fetch
  FROM pg_stat_user_indexes s
  JOIN pg_index i ON i.indexrelid = s.indexrelid
 WHERE i.indrelid = 'measurement'::regclass
 ORDER BY pg_relation_size(s.relid) DESC;

Over-indexing a wide table is the most common structural mistake. Three columns of a wide B-tree is not three cheap indexes; it is one index whose leaf entries are large, which deepens the tree, increases the chance that a page split requires a WAL full-page image, and slows vacuum. If two of the three columns are almost always constrained together, one composite index is strictly better than two single-column ones. If they are constrained independently, two single-column indexes plus a bitmap combination is usually better than a composite. The decision requires the joint distribution of the two predicates, which is what CREATE STATISTICSCREATE STATISTICS in § 3.6 provides.

3.4 Partial indexes and the predicate requirement

A partial index stores only the rows satisfying a predicate and is usable only when the planner can prove, from the query’s own qualifiers, that the query touches only stored rows — and that proof is a syntactic implication, not a statistical guess.

The requirement is precise. The index predicate must imply the query’s restriction on the same relation, by the planner’s rules for predicate implication, which operate on operator clauses and functions that the equivalence machinery understands. In practice this means the query’s WH clause must contain the index predicate’s clauses as a subset, or a clause the implication machinery can prove implies them, and the planner must be able to evaluate that proof at plan time. Parameters are the critical exception: with a generic plan and a parameter for the predicate column, the planner cannot prove the implication, and the index is skipped. The remedy is either a query that supplies the value literally, or a custom plan, controlled by plan_cache_mode = force_custom_planplan_cache_mode = force_custom_plan for that statement.

The index predicate itself is restricted. It may reference only columns of the table, it may not contain a subquery, and its functions must be immutable — the same requirement as an expression index, for the same reason. Since PostgreSQL 17, a partial index on a partitioned table’s partition can be attached to the parent’s partial index with ALTER INDEX ... ATTACH PARTITIONALTER INDEX ... ATTACH PARTITION, which means a partition-local index is maintained automatically once the definitions match.

-- A partial index over the small fraction of rows that are actually open.
CREATE INDEX CONCURRENTLY order_line_open_idx ON order_line (order_id, placed_at)
       WHERE state = 'open';

-- The qualifying query uses it. The Index Cond is the index predicate's columns; the
-- filter is not needed because the predicate is implied.
EXPLAIN (ANALYZE, BUFFERS)
SELECT order_id, placed_at FROM order_line
 WHERE state = 'open' AND placed_at > '2026-03-01';
--  Index Scan using order_line_open_idx on order_line  (actual rows=1204 loops=1)
--    Index Cond: (order_id > 0 AND placed_at > '2026-03-01 00:00:00+00'::timestamptz)
--    Filter: (state = 'open'::text)      <-- the residual filter the planner kept

-- A query that does not imply the predicate cannot use the index at all, even
-- when the same column appears in its WHERE clause with a different operator.
EXPLAIN (ANALYZE)
SELECT order_id FROM order_line WHERE state = 'shipped';
--  Seq Scan on order_line  (cost=.. rows=..)
--    Filter: (state = 'shipped'::text)

-- OR does not imply AND. Two partial indexes do not combine for a disjunction
-- unless a btree index on the discriminating column exists to drive a bitmap OR.
CREATE INDEX order_line_state_idx ON order_line (state) WHERE state IN ('open','held');

-- Proof that the predicate was stored, and what it was normalised to.
SELECT c.relname AS idx, pg_get_expr(i.indpred, i.indrelid) AS predicate,
       pg_get_expr(i.indexprs, i.indrelid) AS expressions,
       i.indnkeyatts, i.indnatts
  FROM pg_index i
  JOIN pg_class c ON c.oid = i.indexrelid
 WHERE i.indrelid = 'order_line'::regclass
   AND i.indpred IS NOT NULL;

-- Partial index with a parameter: custom plan uses it, generic plan does not.
PREPARE q (text

A partial index is the cheapest large win available in PostgreSQL. The standard case is a large table in which most rows are terminal — delivered, closed, soft-deleted — and the queries of interest are all qualified by a status. A partial index over the live few per cent of the table is one to two orders of magnitude smaller, which means it fits in the buffer pool, which means it is read from memory rather than from storage, and it also reduces write amplification because terminal-state updates no longer touch it. The predicate must be chosen so that it is stable: a predicate that flips for most rows over time is worse than no index, because it creates dead entries that vacuum must clean.

The implication requirement is why partial indexes silently stop being used after a query refactor. A predicate of WHERE deleted_at IS NULLWHERE deleted_at IS NULL is implied by a query with the identical clause, and by a query that adds deleted_at IS NULL AND tenant_id = 5deleted_at IS NULL AND tenant_id = 5, but not by a query written as (deleted_at IS NULL OR deleted_at > now())(deleted_at IS NULL OR deleted_at > now()) nor by one that derives the condition inside a subquery or a view with a security barrier. Nothing errors; the index simply stops appearing in the plan and the query latency rises by a factor with no other symptom. When a query changes shape, re-run its EXP; when latency regresses with no code change, check plan_ca and parameter-driven plans first.

3.5 Reading EXPLAIN node by node

A plan is a tree of iterators, and reading it correctly means reading the leaves first, treating each parent’s row estimate as the product of its children, and distinguishing estimated cost from measured time at every step.

Each node reports four things: the method, the estimated cost and rows for one loop, the measured time and rows already multiplied by the loop count, and the buffer counters. The actual time=actual time= field is cumulative, so the time attributable to a node is the difference between its own reading and the sum of its children’s, adjusted for loops. A node that reports a large gap between rows=rows= and actual rowsactual rows is a statistics problem; a node whose actual time is large but whose children’s are not is a CPU problem in that node, usually a sort, a hash build, or an expensive recheck; and a node with large buffer counts and small CPU time is an I/O problem, which on PostgreSQL 18 may be asynchronous.

-- A plan worth reading, with a nested loop, an index scan, a filter, and heap fetches.
EXPLAIN (ANALYZE, BUFFERS, VERBOSE, SETTINGS)
SELECT l.sku, l.qty, o.id
  FROM "order" o
  JOIN order_line l ON l.order_id = o.id
 WHERE o.placed_at >= '2026-03-01' AND o.state = 'open'
 ORDER BY o.placed_at DESC LIMIT 10;
--
--  Limit  (cost=8.31..16.68 rows=10 width=24) (actual time=0.412..0.436 rows=10 loops=1)
--    Buffers: shared hit=15
--    ->  Nested Loop  (cost=1.06..16.68 rows=200 width=24) (actual time=0.398..0.421 rows=10 loops=1)
--          Buffers: shared hit=15
--          ->  Index Scan using order_placed_at_idx on "order" o
--                (cost=0.43..8.30 rows=200 width=16) (actual time=0.062..0.088 rows=10 loops=1)
--                Index Cond: (placed_at >= '2026-03-01 00:00:00+00'::timestamptz)
--                Filter: (state = 'open'::text)
--                Rows Removed by Filter: 340
--                Buffers: shared hit=4
--          ->  Index Scan using order_line_order_idx on order_line l
--                (cost=0.42..4.23 rows=40 width=16) (actual time=0.031..0.031 rows=8 loops=1)
--                Index Cond: (order_id = o.id)
--                Buffers: shared hit=1

The reading of that plan is: the index scan on the parent produced 10 rows for 400 examined, so the st filter removed 77 per cent of what it read and the index does not cover the predicate. The nested loop re-executed the child scan ten times, once per parent row, and each execution returned eight rows for a FilterFilter-free descent. The estimate of 200 parent rows was abandoned by the Li after ten, which is the correct behaviour and worth noting: the estimated total cost of 16.68 was never incurred. If the limit had not been there, the true cost would have been roughly 200 times the per-execution cost, and the plan choice would need re-examining.

-- The evidence that a plan is I/O bound rather than CPU bound: buffer counts,
-- read time, and the per-backend I/O attribution introduced in PostgreSQL 16.
SELECT backend_type, object, context,
       reads, read_time, writes, write_time, extends, extend_time,
       op_bytes
  FROM pg_stat_io
 WHERE backend_type = 'client backend'
   AND object = 'relation'
 ORDER BY read_time DESC LIMIT 10;

-- The 18 asynchronous I/O settings that change how those numbers behave.
SELECT name, setting, unit, short_desc FROM pg_settings
 WHERE name IN ('io_method', 'io_max_concurrency',
                  'io_workers', 'io_combine_limit',
                  'effective_io_concurrency', 'effective_cache_size',
                  'random_page_cost', 'seq_page_cost')
 ORDER BY name;

-- Sequential access pattern detection: how many distinct relations in a
-- statement, which is what large sequential scans trigger.
SELECT relname, seq_scan, seq_tup_read, idx_scan, n_live_tup, n_dead_tup,
       CASE WHEN seq_scan > 0 THEN seq_tup_read / seq_scan::numeric END AS avg_rows_per_seq_scan
  FROM pg_stat_user_tables
 WHERE n_live_tup > 10000
 ORDER BY seq_tup_read DESC LIMIT 15;

Cost units are arbitrary and only their ratios matter. The planner’s cost model is expressed in units of sequential page reads, scaled by seq_pa, cpu_tup, and cpu_oper, and it is a static model rather than a measurement. Its purpose is to rank plans, not to predict milliseconds. A plan whose estimated cost is 100 versus 90 may be either effectively identical in practice or an order of magnitude apart, and the only way to know is to run both with ANALYZEANALYZE. The one thing the model is reliable about is the relative ranking of a Nested LoopNested Loop whose inner side is re-executed thousands of times against a Hash JoinHash Join that is not.

3.6 Statistics, extended statistics and ANALYZE

The planner’s estimates come from a sampled summary of the table, and the two kinds of error that matter — a wrong row count and a wrong independence assumption — are fixed by different mechanisms.

Per-column statistics in pg_sta, exposed through the pg_s view, consist of the most common values, a histogram of up to 100 buckets, the number of distinct values, the fraction of nulls, a physical correlation coefficient, and the average width. The corre field is the one most often overlooked and the most consequential: it measures how closely the column’s physical order matches its logical order, and a value near ±1 means a range predicate can be answered by a sequential scan reading only the relevant fraction of pages, which is why a perfectly indexed ORDER BY idORDER BY id table is sometimes best served by a scan. The sampling rate is controlled by statisti per column and by default_stat per table; the target is the number of rows sampled, multiplied by 300, so the default of 100 gives roughly 30 000 rows per column and about 100 histogram buckets.

Extended statistics, added in PostgreSQL 10, address the assumption of independence. A per-column selectivity estimate for a conjunction of predicates on two different columns is the product of the individual selectivities, which is correct only if the columns are independent. Where tena and st are strongly correlated, that product is wrong by orders of magnitude. A statistics object of kind depend records functional dependencies — a value of one column determining the other — and the planner uses them to remove a redundant restriction. Statistics of kind ndis record the number of distinct combinations of the listed columns, correcting the same independence error for a union of groups. Statistics of kind m record the most frequent combinations. In PostgreSQL 18, expressions may be listed directly in the column list, so statistics can be collected on a computed value.

-- The raw, per-column numbers the planner will use, and the ones it will not.
SELECT attname, null_frac, n_distinct, correlation,
       array_length(most_common_vals::text::text[], 1) AS mcv_count,
       array_length(histogram_bounds::text::text[], 1) AS buckets,
       avg_width
  FROM pg_stats
 WHERE tablename = 'order' AND schemaname = 'public'
 ORDER BY attname;

-- Collect statistics for the correlated pair. ndistinct fixes the group count,
-- dependencies lets the planner prove state determines nothing about tenant_id.
CREATE STATISTICS order_tenant_state_stats
       (dependencies, ndistinct, mcv)
  ON tenant_id, state, order_state_reason
    FROM "order";
ANALYZE "order";

-- Raise the sampling target only where the distribution is the problem: a
-- heavily skewed column or a long IN list.
SET default_statistics_target = 500;
ANALYZE "order";
-- and put it back, so the session that raised it does not leave the
-- setting changed for everything that runs after it
RESET default_statistics_target;

-- The estimator diagnostic. run_analyze and ndistinct are per-column; the planner's
-- belief about the whole conjunction is only visible in the plan.
EXPLAIN (ANALYZE, BUFFERS, SETTINGS)
SELECT FROM "order"
 WHERE tenant_id = 7 AND state = 'open';
--  Index Only Scan using order_tenant_state_idx  (cost=.. rows=1 rows=8201 width=0)
--    Index Cond: (tenant_id = 7 AND state = 'open'::text)
--    Heap Fetches: 0
-- rows=1 against rows=8201 measured: the independence assumption produced a
-- near-zero estimate, and the scan stopped early. With extended statistics on the
-- pair, the estimate is computed from the joint cardinality instead.

-- Statistics freshness: the most recent analyse per table, and the gap.
SELECT relname, n_live_tup, n_mod_since_analyze,
       last_analyze, last_autoanalyze,
       now() - GREATEST(COALESCE(last_analyze, '-infinity'::timestamptz),
                        COALESCE(last_autoanalyze, '-infinity'::timestamptz))
                        AS since_analyze
  FROM pg_stat_all_tables
 WHERE n_live_tup > 0
 ORDER BY since_analyze DESC NULLS LAST LIMIT 15;

-- Delete-and-insert churn is the classic reason a table's statistics are
-- permanently wrong, because autovacuum only analyses a fraction at a time.
SELECT relname, n_tup_ins, n_tup_upd, n_tup_del, n_tup_hot_upd,
       n_mod_since_analyze, autovacuum_count, autoanalyze_count
  FROM pg_stat_user_tables
 WHERE n_mod_since_analyze > 10000
 ORDER BY n_mod_since_analyze DESC LIMIT 10;

Extended statistics fix one class of estimate error and no others. They apply only to selectivity estimation within a single table, only for columns listed together in the same statistics object, and only for conjunctions and unions of groups. They do not improve join selectivity across tables, do not improve the estimate for a disjunction of clauses on different columns, and do not improve the estimate of a partial index predicate. When a join’s row estimate is wrong, the answer is usually better per-column statistics on the join keys plus accurate row counts, not an extended statistics object.

Statistics can be too new, and a manual ANA immediately after a bulk load is usually wrong. After loading a table, the planner’s default behaviour is to trust the catalog’s estimate only if the table has never been analysed or if a large fraction has changed since the last analyse; a freshly loaded table reports zero rows in the catalog and the planner substitutes a heuristic. The heuristic is wrong for a table that was loaded with a non-uniform distribution. Analysing immediately is correct, but analysing immediately on a table that a background job is still filling gives statistics for a partial table, and the planner then caches a plan built on them for every prepared statement that references it. For large loads, analyse after the load, and for continuously written tables, lower autovacuum_anal rather than running manual analyses on a schedule.

Exercises 3

  1. Build an index inventory with a verdict per index. Join pg_i to pg_stat_us and pg_stat_u, and for each index on your ten busiest tables report the access method, key columns, predicate, size, idx_ count, and the write volume of the table it is attached to. Deliverable: a table with a ke, rev, or dr verdict and a stated reason for each, plus the exact size reclaimed by the drops you recommend.
  2. Measure the prefix-search boundary. For a table of 500 000 te values, build text, text_pat, COLLATE "C"COLLATE "C", and a pg_ GIN index, and time LIKE 'ab%'LIKE 'ab%', ILIKE 'ab%'ILIKE 'ab%', and LIKE '%ab’LIKE '%ab' against each. Deliverable: a 4-by-3 matrix of index size and latency with the plan used in each cell, and a written rule for choosing among the four.
  3. Demonstrate the leftmost-prefix rule empirically. Create four multicolumn indexes over a synthetic table — (a,b)(a,b), (b,a)(b,a), (a,b)(a,b) with an equality-only query, and (b,a)(b,a) with an equality-only query — and capture EXPLAIN (ANALYZE, BUFFERS)EXPLAIN (ANALYZE, BUFFERS) for a query with a range on one column. Deliverable: the four plans, the rows removed by filter in each, and the measured difference that justifies the equality-before-range ordering.
  4. Diagnose and fix a correlation-driven estimate error. Find a query in your workload whose plan estimates are off by more than two orders of magnitude, create an extended statistics object on the two or three columns involved, re-analyse, and re-measure. Deliverable: the plan before and after, the estimated versus actual rows at the join node in both, and a note on whether the chosen plan changed or merely its cost estimate.
  5. Establish the buffer-pool residency baseline for your largest indexes. Warm the cache with pg_pr, read pg_buff to compute the resident fraction, and then run your five most important queries with a cold and a warm cache. Deliverable: a table of resident percentage and query latency in both states, with a conclusion on whether shared buffers are correctly sized for the working set.

4 · Transactions, MVCC and Locking

4.1 MVCC: tuple versions, snapshots and multixacts4.2 The four isolation levels and what each permits4.3 Table-level lock modes and their conflicts4.4 Row locks, queueing and deadlock detection4.5 Savepoints and subtransaction semantics4.6 Prepared transactions and two-phase commit

This section is the concurrency contract. It explains how MVCC in PostgreSQL actually works — immutable row versions, a per-transaction snapshot, and a multixact encoding for rows updated more than once — and traces the operational consequence of that design, which is that long transactions block vacuum and hold the transaction identifier horizon open. It states precisely what each of the four isolation levels permits, why REPEATABLE READREPEATABLE READ is not the SQL standard’s REPEATABLE READREPEATABLE READ, and what SERIAL adds through snapshot serializable isolation with SIR predicate locks and the 4000140001 error. It gives the complete table-level and row-level lock conflict matrices, the diagnostic queries that resolve a live lock, the FOR UPDATEFOR UPDATE family including SKIP LOCKEDSKIP LOCKED and NOW, and the operational hazards of prepared transactions.

4.1 MVCC: tuple versions, snapshots and multixacts

Concurrency control in PostgreSQL is snapshot-based and implemented in the heap, not in locks, and the consequences for vacuum, wraparound, and bloat follow directly from that design.

Every row version is immutable. A UPD writes a new tuple version elsewhere in the heap, records the new version’s xm as the updating transaction’s identifier, and sets the old version’s xm to the same identifier. The tuple header is 24 bytes and carries xm, xm, c and xv, plus two command identifiers, cm and cm, used only to order commands within the current transaction. Each transaction takes a snapshot at its first statement, recording xm as the oldest running identifier, xm as the next identifier to be assigned, and the list of identifiers in flight between them. Visibility is a pure comparison against that snapshot, so a reader never blocks a writer and never sees a partial row.

Rows updated more than once by the same transaction cannot be represented by a single xm, which is where multixacts enter. When a transaction updates the same row version twice, the first update’s xm must record both the earlier and the later updater, so the value becomes a multixact identifier pointing into pg_mul, with an offset per member. Readers consult that structure to determine whether the row is still visible to them, and pg_vis plus heap_pa from pagei will show the raw header and the t_inf flags that indicate a multixact.

-- Raw tuple headers, read straight off the page. infomask2 flags are the
-- ones that matter: HEAP_XMAX_IS_MULTI, HEAP_XMIN_FROZEN, HEAP_ONLY_TUPLE.
--   CREATE EXTENSION pageinspect;
SELECT lp, t_xmin, t_xmax, t_field3, t_ctid, t_infomask2
  FROM heap_page_items(get_raw_page('order', 0))
 LIMIT 5;

-- Transaction identifiers and their age. A long-running transaction is the
-- single most common cause of an unwinnable vacuum and of table bloat.
SELECT pid, state, xact_start, now() - xact_start AS age,
       backend_xmin, backend_xid, left(query, 60) AS query
  FROM pg_stat_activity
 WHERE xact_start IS NOT NULL ORDER BY xact_start LIMIT 10;

-- The horizon. 32-bit xid space wraps at 4 294 967 296; autovacuum forces an
-- anti-wraparound vacuum at autovacuum_freeze_max_age (default 200 000 000).
SELECT datname, age(datfrozenxid) AS xid_age,
       mxid_age(datminmxid) AS mxid_age,
       pg_size_pretty(pg_database_size(datname))
  FROM pg_database ORDER BY age(datfrozenxid) DESC;

-- Why vacuum cannot finish: the oldest running transaction, and the
-- progress of the vacuum that is currently trying. A vacuum that reports
-- 0 percent for hours is being starved of removable tuples, not of CPU.
SELECT v.pid, v.relid::regclass AS table_name, v.phase,
       v.heap_blks_total, v.heap_blks_scanned, v.heap_blks_vacuumed,
       v.index_vacuum_count, v.max_dead_tuples,
       now() - x.xact_start AS blocked_by_oldest_xact
  FROM pg_stat_progress_vacuum v
  JOIN pg_stat_activity x ON x.pid = (
        SELECT min(pid) FROM pg_stat_activity
         WHERE backend_xmin = (SELECT min(backend_xmin) FROM pg_stat_activity
                                                 WHERE backend_xmin IS NOT NULL));
-- Bloat per table, sampled. The sampling variant is the only one that is
-- safe to run across a whole database.
--   CREATE EXTENSION pgstattuple;
SELECT relname,
       round(pgstattuple_approx(relid).dead_tuple_percent, 1) AS dead_pct,
       round(pgstattuple_approx(relid).free_percent + pgstattuple_approx(relid).dead_tuple_percent, 1) AS reclaimable_pct
  FROM pg_stat_user_tables
 ORDER BY pg_total_relation_size(relid) DESC LIMIT 10;

Dead rows are invisible, not absent, until vacuum removes them. Because xm is a committed identifier, every version whose xm is committed is retained until a vacuum pass proves no snapshot can see it, which means until every transaction that started before that commit has ended. A single read transaction open for six hours keeps six hours of updates alive in every table it has touched a snapshot of. The operational rule is that transaction duration is a storage parameter: idle_in_transacti and statemen are the two settings that bound it, and both should be set in any system where clients are not fully trusted.

4.2 The four isolation levels and what each permits

PostgreSQL implements snapshot isolation, which is stronger than read committed and weaker than the SQL standard’s serializable, and the middle level is named identically to the standard level while behaving differently.

READ UNCOMMITTEDREAD UNCOMMITTED is accepted and mapped to READ COMMITTEDREAD COMMITTED; there is no dirty read in PostgreSQL. READ COMMITTEDREAD COMMITTED, the default, takes a new snapshot at the start of every statement, so a long-running transaction sees a moving world: two statements in the same transaction may see different rows, and an UPD that finds its target row concurrently modified will re-evaluate the qualifiers against the new row version under READ COMMITTEDREAD COMMITTED and skip it if it no longer matches. That last behaviour is the source of the "my update applied to zero rows" class of report, and it is the correct semantics of this level, not a bug.

REPEATABLE READREPEATABLE READ in PostgreSQL takes one snapshot for the whole transaction, which prevents non-repeatable reads and prevents write skew between two transactions that read and then write disjoint sets of rows. It does not prevent phantoms in the strict sense: a second read in the same transaction will not see a row inserted after the snapshot, which is a stronger guarantee than the standard requires, but the standard’s REPEATABLE READREPEATABLE READ also forbids phantoms arising from other transactions' writes to rows the transaction has not read. Where the standard’s level would abort a transaction that could produce a phantom, PostgreSQL’s simply will not show the phantom.

SERIAL adds snapshot serializable isolation: on top of the same snapshot, transactions acquire SIR predicate locks on the index ranges and heap pages they read, recorded in pg_predic rather than in the ordinary lock manager, and a commit is refused with 40001 serialization_failure40001 serialization_failure when the resulting read-write dependency graph contains a dangerous structure requiring a cycle. Detection is pessimistic in the sense that it can only detect a cycle that the tracked dependencies make provable, which is why a serializable system always requires a retry loop; the alternative, ordering constraints on write transactions, is strictly more conservative.

LevelSnapshot takenNon-repeatable readWrite skewMust be retried
READ UNCOMMITTEDREAD UNCOMMITTEDPer statement, as read committedPossiblePossibleNo
READ COMMITTEDREAD COMMITTEDPer statementPossiblePossibleNo
REPEATABLE READREPEATABLE READPer transactionPreventedPrevented between transactions touching a common row setDeadlock only
SERIALPer transaction, plus SIR predicate locksPreventedPrevented; dangerous structures rejected at commitYes, on 4000140001 and on deadlock
 
-- Serializable is a per-transaction setting, not a session one, and it must be
-- declared before the first query of the transaction takes its snapshot.
BEGIN ISOLATION LEVEL SERIALIZABLE;
SELECT count(*) FROM seat_hold WHERE event_id = 9 AND seat = 'C4';
INSERT INTO seat_hold (event_id, seat) VALUES (9, 'C4');
-- If another transaction committed a conflicting hold in the meantime, COMMIT
-- fails with:  ERROR:  could not serialize access due to read/write dependencies
--                among transactions; SQLSTATE 40001. The transaction must be retried
-- from the beginning, not resumed from a savepoint.
COMMIT;

-- The retry loop is mandatory, and it belongs in the client.
--   for attempt in 1..5:
--     try:
--       with conn.transaction(isolation='serializable'):
--           ... whole unit of work ...
--       break
--     except psycopg2.errors.SerializationFailure:
--       conn.rollback(); time.sleep(random.expovariate(1/0.05))

-- Predicate locks are visible in their own catalog, keyed by page and tuple.
SELECT pid, locktype, mode, granted, classid, objid, objsubid
  FROM pg_locks WHERE locktype = 'predicate'
 UNION ALL
SELECT pid, 'advisory', mode, granted, classid::oid, objid, objsubid
  FROM pg_locks WHERE locktype = 'advisory';

-- Inspecting the snapshot a statement actually used. The xmin and xmax bounds
-- and the in-flight list are the three numbers behind every visibility test.
SELECT pg_current_snapshot()                                    AS snapshot,
       txid_snapshot_xmin(pg_current_snapshot())                  AS xmin,
       txid_snapshot_xmax(pg_current_snapshot())                  AS xmax,
       txid_snapshot_xip(pg_current_snapshot())                   AS in_flight;
-- Under read committed this value differs between two statements of one
-- transaction; under repeatable read it is fixed for the transaction's life.
BEGIN ISOLATION LEVEL REPEATABLE READ;
SELECT pg_current_snapshot() AS first;
SELECT pg_sleep(1);
SELECT pg_current_snapshot() AS second;   -- identical
COMMIT;
-- The server's own name for each level, from the same catalog the parser uses.
SELECT level, castlevel FROM unnest(enum_range(NULL::pg_isolation_level)) WITH ORDINALITY AS t(level, castlevel);

SERIAL without a retry loop is an outage. The 4000140001 error is not exceptional; in a system with genuine write contention it is a routine outcome, and a client that treats it as a failure will report errors at a rate proportional to contention, then developers will lower the isolation level, and the anomaly the level existed to prevent returns. If the retry is on the client, keep the unit of work inside the retried transaction and make it idempotent, because a retried transaction may have performed external side effects that the database did not roll back.

4.3 Table-level lock modes and their conflicts

PostgreSQL takes two independent kinds of lock: heavyweight relation locks that gate access to a table as a whole, and row-level locks stored in the tuple header, and the first is what makes DDL dangerous.

There are eight relation-level modes. ACCESS SHAREACCESS SHARE is taken by every SEL, FE, and COPY FROMCOPY FROM; it conflicts only with ACCESS EXCLUSIVEACCESS EXCLUSIVE. ROW SHAREROW SHARE is taken by SELECT FOR UPDATESELECT FOR UPDATE, SELECT FOR SHARESELECT FOR SHARE, and SELECT FOR NO KEY UPDATESELECT FOR NO KEY UPDATE. ROW EXCLUSIVEROW EXCLUSIVE is taken by UPD, DEL, ME, and INSERT ... ON CONFLICT DO UPDATEINSERT ... ON CONFLICT DO UPDATE. SHARE UPDATE EXCLUSIVESHARE UPDATE EXCLUSIVE is taken by VAC, ANA, CREATE INDEX CONCURRENTLYCREATE INDEX CONCURRENTLY, REINDEX CONCURRENTLYREINDEX CONCURRENTLY, ALTER TABLE ... SET STATISTICSALTER TABLE ... SET STATISTICS, and VALIDATE CONSTRAINTVALIDATE CONSTRAINT, and it conflicts only with itself and with the three strongest modes. SH is taken by CREATE INDEXCREATE INDEX and ALTER TABLE ... ADD FOREIGN KEYALTER TABLE ... ADD FOREIGN KEY. SHARE ROW EXCLUSIVESHARE ROW EXCLUSIVE is taken by CREATE TRIGGERCREATE TRIGGER, ALTER TABLE ... ADD CONSTRAINTALTER TABLE ... ADD CONSTRAINT generally, and EXCL by REI and VACUUM FULLVACUUM FULL. ACCESS EXCLUSIVEACCESS EXCLUSIVE is taken by almost all ALTER TABLEALTER TABLE forms, by TRUN, DR, VACUUM FULLVACUUM FULL, and ALTER TABLE ... EXCLUSIVEALTER TABLE ... EXCLUSIVE, and it conflicts with everything including itself.

Requested × HeldASRSRESUESSREEAE
ACCESS SHAREACCESS SHARE———————×
ROW SHAREROW SHARE——————××
ROW EXCLUSIVEROW EXCLUSIVE—————×××
SHARE UPDATE EXCLUSIVESHARE UPDATE EXCLUSIVE———×××××
SH———×—×××
SHARE ROW EXCLUSIVESHARE ROW EXCLUSIVE———×××××
EXCL—×××××××
ACCESS EXCLUSIVEACCESS EXCLUSIVE××××××××
 
-- The canonical diagnostic: every waiting lock, with the statement that is holding
-- it. join = true marks a granted lock that is blocking somebody.
SELECT a.pid,
       a.wait_event_type, a.wait_event,
       pg_blocking_pids(a.pid)          AS blocked_by,
       l.locktype, l.relation::regclass, l.mode, l.granted,
       left(a.query, 70)  AS query
  FROM pg_stat_activity a
  JOIN pg_locks l ON l.pid = a.pid
 WHERE a.wait_event_type = 'Lock'
    OR l.pid = ANY(pg_blocking_pids(a.pid))
 ORDER BY a.pid;

-- Deadlocks are detected after deadlock_timeout (1s) and reported with the
-- full cycle of statements. The victim is chosen by cost, not at random.
--   LOG:  deadlock detected
--   DETAIL:  Process 4711 waits for ShareLock on transaction 91234.
--           Process 4702 waits for ShareLock on transaction 91233.
--   ERROR:  deadlock detected  SQLSTATE 40P01

-- Terminate the holder rather than waiting for the client to time out.
SELECT pg_terminate_backend(4711);
-- Or make the victim selection explicit and deterministic, at the cost of
-- eliminating a large class of self-inflicted deadlocks.
--   SET deadlock_timeout = '3s';

-- The lock census: which relations are actually being locked, in which modes, and
-- how much traffic each mode carries. A relation under sustained ACCESS
-- EXCLUSIVE is a DDL that has been waiting, not a fast DDL.
SELECT l.relation::regclass              AS relation,
       l.mode,
       count(*) FILTER (WHERE l.granted)  AS held,
       count(*) FILTER (WHERE NOT l.granted) AS waiting,
       min(a.xact_start)                          AS oldest_xact
  FROM pg_locks l
  LEFT JOIN pg_stat_activity a ON a.pid = l.pid
 WHERE l.relation IS NOT NULL AND l.pid <> pg_backend_pid()
 GROUP BY 1, 2
HAVING count(*) FILTER (WHERE NOT l.granted) > 0
    OR bool_or(l.mode = 'AccessExclusiveLock')
 ORDER BY 3 DESC, 4 DESC LIMIT 20;

-- Per-mode totals across the cluster, sampled twice to get a rate.
--   SELECT mode, count(*) FROM pg_locks WHERE relation IS NOT NULL GROUP BY 1;
--   \watch 5
-- Compare the two readings: a mode whose count is climbing and not falling is
-- a lock queue building, and it is visible long before users report it.
SELECT mode, count(*) AS locks_held
  FROM pg_locks
 WHERE database = (SELECT oid FROM pg_database WHERE datname = current_database())
 GROUP BY 1 ORDER BY 2 DESC;

-- Row locks are not held in pg_locks. They live in the tuple header, so the only
-- way to see what a transaction has locked is to ask each holder, or to look
-- for the rows a statement is waiting on in pg_stat_activity.wait_event.
SELECT pid, state, wait_event_type, wait_event, xact_start, left(query, 60) AS query
  FROM pg_stat_activity
 WHERE wait_event_type = 'Tuple'
    OR wait_event_type = 'TransactionId'
 ORDER BY xact_start;
-- Tuple locks  = waiting for a row lock held by another transaction
-- TransactionId = waiting for another transaction to commit or roll back
--                  before this transaction can proceed, which is a foreign key
--                  check or a concurrent update of the same row.
SELECT a.pid, pg_blocking_pids(a.pid) AS blockers, a.query
  FROM pg_stat_activity a
 WHERE a.wait_event_type = 'Tuple';
-- Rows a transaction has locked, obtained from the transaction itself.
--   SELECT * FROM pg_locks WHERE pid = pg_backend_pid();
--   SET TRANSACTION ISOLATION LEVEL REPEATABLE READ;
--   SELECT txid_current_snapshot();   -- does not list row locks; they are not xids

Give long DDL a lock_t and retry rather than an ACCESS EXCLUSIVEACCESS EXCLUSIVE that blocks every reader. The correct pattern is SET lock_timeout = '3s’SET lock_timeout = '3s' before the DDL, and on 55P03 lock_not_available55P03 lock_not_available either retry in a loop or return the operation to a queue. Without it, a single ALTER TABLEALTER TABLE waiting behind a long-running report will block every subsequent query on that table, and those queries will in turn hold the locks that the DDL is waiting for, converting a short wait into an outage. This is the single most valuable operational habit in PostgreSQL administration.

4.4 Row locks, queueing and deadlock detection

Row-level locks live in the tuple header, are managed outside the lock manager, and differ in strength according to whether the operation can change the row’s key — which is what makes foreign key checks cheap.

Four row-level modes exist and only two can be requested explicitly. FOR KEY SHAREFOR KEY SHARE and FOR SHAREFOR SHARE block updates and deletions of the key; FOR NO KEY UPDATEFOR NO KEY UPDATE and FOR UPDATEFOR UPDATE block all updates. The distinction is that FOR NO KEY UPDATEFOR NO KEY UPDATE permits an update that leaves the key columns unchanged, so it is strictly weaker and is what PostgreSQL’s own triggers take. A SELECT FOR UPDATESELECT FOR UPDATE that can determine the target relation has no usable unique key is automatically weakened to FOR NO KEY UPDATEFOR NO KEY UPDATE, because a row that cannot be identified by a key cannot be meaningfully protected against key changes. Foreign key enforcement takes FOR KEY SHAREFOR KEY SHARE on the referenced row and FOR NO KEY UPDATEFOR NO KEY UPDATE on the referencing row, which is why a parent row with children is not blocked from having non-key columns updated.

Requested × HeldKEY SHARESHARENO KEY UPDATEUPDATE
FOR KEY SHAREFOR KEY SHARE———×
FOR SHAREFOR SHARE——××
FOR NO KEY UPDATEFOR NO KEY UPDATE—×××
FOR UPDATEFOR UPDATE××××
 
-- The queue pattern: claim work without waiting for other workers. Rows locked
-- by another transaction are returned to the result set, not blocked on.
WITH claimed AS (
  SELECT id FROM job_queue
  WHERE state = 'ready' AND run_after <= now()
  ORDER BY priority DESC, run_after
  FOR UPDATE SKIP LOCKED
  LIMIT 10)
UPDATE job_queue j
   SET state = 'running', claimed_at = now(), claimed_by = pg_backend_pid()
  FROM claimed c WHERE j.id = c.id
 RETURNING j.id, j.payload;

-- NOWAIT fails immediately with 55P03 rather than blocking; OF restricts the
-- lock to named relations in a join. Neither is permitted with GROUP BY,
-- DISTINCT, aggregates, set operations, or window functions.
SELECT * FROM account
 WHERE id = 7 FOR UPDATE OF account NOWAIT;

-- Two-key advisory locks: session-scoped and transaction-scoped, exclusive and
-- shared. They live in the same namespace as their 64-bit counterparts.
SELECT pg_try_advisory_xact_lock(4711, 1)  AS acquired;
SELECT pg_advisory_lock_shared(hashtext('inventory'));
-- Held until COMMIT or ROLLBACK for the _xact_ forms; until session end
-- for the others. Always prefer the transaction-scoped form.

Lock ordering between tables must be fixed by the application, because the server only detects the result. Two transactions that update table A then table B and table B then table A will deadlock regularly at high concurrency, and the server’s response is to abort one of them after deadlock, which means a failed transaction and a retry. This is survivable when the unit of work is small and the retry is correct, and it is a steady source of errors when it is neither. Fix the order in the code, or make the transactions genuinely independent. Raising deadlock to reduce false positives is the wrong direction: it lengthens every genuine deadlock and the lock queue behind it.

4.5 Savepoints and subtransaction semantics

A savepoint opens a subtransaction that is fully recoverable, but the guarantees are narrower than the syntax suggests and one of them surprises every application that relies on it.

SAVE and ROLLBACK TO SAVEPOINTROLLBACK TO SAVEPOINT are genuine subtransactions: they take a transaction identifier of their own, they appear in pg_stat_ as subxids of the parent, and they have the durability guarantees of the enclosing transaction. What is not rolled back is anything the client did outside the database — a message sent, a file written, an HTTP request issued. Code that assumes ROLLBACK TO SAVEPOINTROLLBACK TO SAVEPOINT undoes a partially completed unit of external work is incorrect, and this is the most common savepoint misuse.

The precise behaviour of a rollback to savepoint is worth stating in full, because it determines what code can be written on top of it. Locks acquired by the subtransaction — both relation-level and row-level — are released, even if they were acquired after the last savepoint was established, because PostgreSQL does not track which savepoint acquired which lock. Constraints deferred at the time of the rollback remain deferred and are still checked at commit. A cursor opened inside the aborted portion is closed and unusable. Volatile functions that were already evaluated in the aborted portion may be evaluated again. The subtransaction identifier is retained in the parent’s list until commit, so a long transaction with many savepoint rollbacks accumulates subxids and consumes transaction identifier space, which is the mechanism behind the subtransaction wraparound concerns in heavily batched application code.

-- Partial recovery inside one transaction: the only correct use is when every
-- side effect is database-side and reversible.
BEGIN;
SAVEPOINT sp1;
UPDATE account SET balance = balance - 100 WHERE id = 1;
-- ... conditional logic that may fail ...
ROLLBACK TO SAVEPOINT sp1;      -- balance restored, no COMMIT needed
UPDATE account SET balance = balance - 25  WHERE id = 1;
RELEASE SAVEPOINT sp1;        -- optional; commit does it anyway
COMMIT;

-- SAVEPOINT is not DDL-safe: on PostgreSQL 15 and later, a savepoint cannot
-- be established in a transaction block that has performed DDL on a
-- non-temporary table.  ERROR:  cannot establish a new savepoint for a
-- subtransaction that has operated on a relation after the last commit

-- Observing subtransactions. subxid is only populated when the parent has
-- more than 64 in flight, so absence means few, not none.
SELECT pid, xact_start, subxid, subtransaction_disabled,
       top_xid, pg_current_xact_id_if_assigned() AS current_xid
  FROM pg_stat_activity
 WHERE xact_start IS NOT NULL ORDER BY xact_start;

Prefer application-level retry to deep savepoint nesting for batch work. A loop that opens a savepoint per row, rolls it back on failure, and continues accumulates subtransaction identifiers that cannot be released until the outermost commit, and on a large batch this is a genuine wraparound risk. The alternative — abort and restart the batch with an idempotent progress marker — bounds the number of subtransactions by the retry count rather than by the row count. Savepoints are excellent for a bounded, small number of conditional recoveries inside a single logical operation, which is what they were designed for.

4.6 Prepared transactions and two-phase commit

A prepared transaction survives client disconnection and crash, holding its locks and its transaction identifier, and is the mechanism by which PostgreSQL participates in an external two-phase commit protocol.

PREPARE TRANSACTION 'gid’PREPARE TRANSACTION 'gid' converts the current transaction into a prepared one: its write-ahead log is flushed, its locks are retained, its row versions are still invisible to other transactions, and its state is recorded in a twop file in the pg_ directory. The session is disconnected, the transaction identifier remains allocated, and the prepared state survives a restart. Only COMMIT PREPAREDCOMMIT PREPARED or ROLLBACK PREPAREDROLLBACK PREPARED, each of which must name the identifier and each of which is idempotent, can resolve it. Because the locks are held indefinitely, an abandoned prepared transaction blocks vacuum across the whole database, holds the oldest transaction identifier and therefore blocks anti-wraparound progress, and blocks any conflicting DDL for as long as it exists.

The feature is enabled by setting max_prepared to a non-zero value, which by default it is not, and each prepared transaction consumes both a prepared-transaction slot and lock-manager memory proportional to its size. Where the coordinator is a foreign transaction manager such as XA or a distributed transaction coordinator, this is the correct and only available mechanism. Where the coordinator is a message broker or an application, it is usually the wrong one, and a two-phase commit against a broker that provides only at-least-once delivery does not give atomicity across the two systems in any case — it gives atomicity within PostgreSQL and a window of ambiguity outside it, which the consumer protocol must be able to resolve.

-- Configuration: slots, and the memory each prepared transaction will claim.
--   max_prepared_transactions = 100
--   max_locks_per_transaction  = 128   -- raise if a prepared txn is large
SHOW max_prepared_transactions;

-- The catalogue of prepared transactions, and the only supported way to clear
-- one out. There is no way to cancel a prepared transaction from inside SQL.
SELECT gid, prepared, owner, database, transaction_timestamp AS xact_start
  FROM pg_prepared_xacts
 ORDER BY prepared;

-- The operational symptom of an abandoned prepared transaction: the age of the
-- oldest prepared transaction equals the oldest backend_xmin across the cluster.
SELECT GREATEST(age(COALESCE((SELECT min(transaction_timestamp) FROM pg_prepared_xacts), now()))
                ) AS blocking_age,
       SELECT min(backend_xmin) FROM pg_stat_activity AS horizon;

-- Resolution. These are idempotent and must be run outside a transaction block.
COMMIT PREPARED 'xa:2f/1';
ROLLBACK PREPARED 'xa:2f/2';

-- Two-phase commit also exists as a wire-protocol feature between a cluster and
