-- a standby, configured with max_prepared_transactions on the standby too.
--   primary_conninfo = 'host=standby port=5432 ... two_phase=on'

One abandoned prepared transaction can stop an entire cluster. Because it holds an old xm, autovacuum can no longer remove dead tuples anywhere in the database, so bloat grows across every table while nothing appears to be wrong in any single table’s statistics. Because it holds a lock, any ALTER TABLEALTER TABLE or DR that conflicts with it waits forever. There is no timeout, no automatic cleanup, and no way to resolve one except executing ROLLBACK PREPAREDROLLBACK PREPARED with its identifier. Where a foreign transaction manager is used, the identifier must be logged at prepare time and the log must be monitored; a monitoring check on pg_prepa that alerts on age is the minimum control.

Exercises 4

  1. Demonstrate MVCC visibility from three sessions. In one, begin a transaction and read a row; in a second, update it and commit; in a third, read it. Repeat the reads in the first session at both read committed and repeatable read, and inspect the raw tuple versions with heap_page_itemsheap_page_items. Deliverable: a transcript showing the three read results, the before-images with their xm and xm values, and a written explanation of which read used which snapshot.
  2. Enumerate the anomalies at each isolation level. Write four pairs of transactions that produce a non-repeatable read, a write skew, and a lost update, and run each at read committed, repeatable read, and serializable. Deliverable: a 3-by-4 matrix of outcome per pair, with the exact SQLSTATE for every failure, and the client retry logic your serializable design requires.
  3. Build a blocking-chain report and use it on a live system. Write the query that resolves every waiting lock to its blockers transitively, run it while generating a real block, and use the result to terminate a holder. Deliverable: the query, the captured output showing the chain, and a lock_t wrapper you will deploy for all DDL.
  4. Compare SKIP LOCKEDSKIP LOCKED with plain FOR UPDATEFOR UPDATE for a job queue. Run eight workers against a thousand-row queue in both modes and measure throughput, duplicate processing, and lock waits. Deliverable: both timing runs, the query plans for the claiming statement, and a written conclusion on the failure mode each mode exposes.
  5. Prepare a transaction, crash a session, and recover. Issue PREPARE TRANSACTIONPREPARE TRANSACTION, terminate the backend, and observe the surviving state; then measure the effect of the retained xm on vacuum across an unrelated large table. Deliverable: the pg_prepa output before and after, a n_dea trace for the unrelated table with and without the prepared transaction, and the monitoring query you will run continuously for 4000140001 and prepared-transaction age.

5 · Query Planning and Performance Tuning

5.1 Reading a plan node by node5.2 Join strategies and the conditions for each5.3 Estimated versus actual rows5.4 Sorting, hashing and work_mem5.5 Partition pruning and constraint exclusion5.6 The tuning sequence and where it ends

This section is about the planner as a decision procedure over uncertain information, and about the discipline required to correct it. It develops a node-by-node reading method that accounts for loop counts and buffer traffic; it states the conditions under which each of the three join strategies is chosen and shows real plans for all three; it isolates the estimated-versus-actual gap as the single most diagnostic number in a plan and enumerates the causes in order of likelihood; it explains work correctly as a per-node, per-operation setting rather than a global budget; it covers partition pruning, including runtime pruning; and it ends with an ordered tuning procedure whose final step is a new EXP, because a tuning change that is not verified by a plan is a hypothesis, not a result.

5.1 Reading a plan node by node

A plan node is an iterator that yields rows to its parent, which is why the row count must be multiplied by the loop count before it is compared with the parent’s estimate.

The tree is printed leaves-first. Each node reports an estimated cost and row count for one execution, and then the measured time and row count after all executions. The arithmetic that reconciles the two is: actual rowsactual rows is already multiplied by lo, so the per-execution row count is actual rows / loopsactual rows / loops, and the parent’s estimate for a nested loop is the product of the outer estimate and the inner estimate per outer row. A Mater or Mem node is where the difference between the two shows up most clearly, because a cached node is executed fewer times than the loop count would suggest, and its lo field is the real execution count.

The three other things to extract from every node are the buffer counters, which are the physical work; the Workers LaunchedWorkers Launched and Workers PlannedWorkers Planned counts on the Gat node, which show whether parallelism was granted or only planned; and any Sett line that shows a non-default GUC was in force, which is the fastest way to explain an unexpected plan.

-- A parallel hash join. The Gather node aggregates N workers; divide the leaf
-- time by (1 + workers launched) to compare with a serial plan.
EXPLAIN (ANALYZE, BUFFERS, VERBOSE, SETTINGS, SUMMARY)
SELECT c.country_code, count(*), sum(o.total_cents)
  FROM orders o JOIN customers c ON c.id = o.customer_id
 WHERE o.placed_at >= '2026-01-01'::timestamptz
 GROUP BY 1;

-- Gather  (cost=.. rows=..) (actual time=.. rows=178 loops=3)
--   Workers Planned: 2  Workers Launched: 2
--   Buffers: shared hit=9800
--   ->  Parallel Hash Join  (cost=.. rows=..) (actual time=.. rows=594 loops=3)
--         Hash Cond: (o.customer_id = c.id)
--         ->  Parallel Seq Scan on orders o  (actual time=.. rows=1620000 loops=3)
--         ->  Hash  (actual time=.. rows=88000 loops=3)
--               Buckets: 131072  Batches: 1  Memory Usage: 8192kB
--               ->  Parallel Index Only Scan using customers_pkey on customers c
-- Planning Time: 0.412 ms
-- Execution Time: 984.201 ms

-- The three non-join nodes that change the arithmetic: a CTE evaluated once, an
-- InitPlan evaluated once per execution, and a Materialize built once and
-- rescanned. loops=1 on an inner side is the signal that the work is shared.

-- A MATERIALIZED CTE is computed once; a non-materialised one is inlined and
-- may be evaluated again per reference. The plan shows which happened.
WITH big_orders AS MATERIALIZED (
  SELECT customer_id, sum(total_cents) AS total
    FROM orders WHERE state = 'open' GROUP BY customer_id)
SELECT c.id, b.total
  FROM big_orders b JOIN customer c ON c.id = b.customer_id
 WHERE b.total > 50000
 ORDER BY b.total DESC LIMIT 20;
--  ->  CTE Scan on big_orders b  (actual time=.. rows=.. loops=1)
--        CTE big_orders
--          ->  HashAggregate  (actual time=.. rows=.. loops=1)
-- Without MATERIALIZED, the hash aggregate appears inside both references,
-- and the aggregate is executed twice for the same input.

-- An InitPlan is a scalar subquery evaluated once and cached in the plan tree.
EXPLAIN (ANALYZE, BUFFERS)
SELECT o.id FROM orders o
 WHERE o.total_cents > (SELECT percentile_cont(0.99)
                        WITHIN GROUP (ORDER BY total_cents) FROM orders);
--  InitPlan 1  (cost=.. rows=1) (actual time=.. rows=1 loops=1)
--    ->  Aggregate  (actual time=.. rows=1 loops=1)   <-- executed once, not per row

Execution TimeExecution Time is the only number that includes planning. Planning TimePlanning Time and Execution TimeExecution Time together account for the whole statement, and their ratio is a diagnosis in itself. A statement with more planning time than execution time is a candidate for prepared statements, for a reduced number of join relations in the query, or for a plan that is being re-derived because statistics changed. A statement with high planning time and many distinct literal values is the case pg_stat_s normalisation was designed to expose, and the fix is a prepared statement or a query that takes the varying part as an array parameter.

5.2 Join strategies and the conditions for each

There are three physical join strategies and a family of logical semi- and anti-joins, and the planner chooses among them using estimated row counts, not measured ones, which is why a bad estimate produces a badly chosen strategy.

Nested loop is chosen when the outer side is small and the inner side is cheap per row: either a small, fully cached relation scanned by index, or a Mater node that avoids rebuilding it. The cost is the product of the outer rows and the inner per-row cost, so the planner will reject a nested loop with an outer estimate in the millions regardless of how cheap the inner side looks. Hash join is chosen for equality conditions on hashable types when at least one side is large; the whole build side is read once into a hash table, and the probe side is streamed. It cannot handle inequality conditions. Merge join is chosen when both inputs can be produced in sorted order — either by an index scan, or by an explicit So that the planner has already decided to pay for — and it is the only strategy that satisfies a ORDER BYORDER BY on the join keys without a further sort. It is also the only strategy whose cost degrades gracefully with very large inputs, which is why it dominates reporting queries over sorted indexes.

The semi-join variants are worth naming because their plans look like the inner strategies. EXI and I become semi joins, NOT EXISTSNOT EXISTS and NOT INNOT IN become anti joins, and each may be implemented as a nested loop, a hash join, or a merge join. NOT INNOT IN with a subquery that can return a null is a semantic trap rather than a performance one: the result is empty whenever any comparison yields null, and the planner will not substitute a hash anti join for it.

-- Hash join: build side hashed once, probe side streamed. Batches > 1 means it spilled.
EXPLAIN (ANALYZE, BUFFERS)
SELECT o.id, c.name FROM orders o JOIN customers c ON c.id = o.customer_id
 WHERE o.state = 'open';
--  Hash Join  (cost=.. rows=..) (actual time=.. rows=.. loops=1)
--    Hash Cond: (o.customer_id = c.id)
--    ->  Seq Scan on orders o  (cost=.. rows=..) (actual time=.. rows=..)
--          Filter: (state = 'open'::text)
--    ->  Hash  (cost=.. rows=..) (actual time=.. rows=..)
--          Buckets: 65536  Batches: 1  Memory Usage: 4096kB
--          ->  Index Only Scan using customers_pkey on customers c

-- Merge join: the sort is paid once and satisfies both the join and the ORDER BY.
EXPLAIN (ANALYZE, BUFFERS)
SELECT o.placed_at, o.total_cents FROM orders o
  JOIN order_line l ON l.order_id = o.id
 ORDER BY o.placed_at, o.id LIMIT 100;
--  Merge Join  (cost=.. rows=..) (actual time=.. rows=.. loops=1)
--    Merge Cond: (o.id = l.order_id)
--    ->  Incremental Sort  (cost=.. rows=..) (actual time=.. rows=..)
--          Sort Key: o.placed_at, o.id
--          Presorted Key: o.id
--    ->  Index Scan using orders_pkey on orders o
--    ->  Index Scan using order_line_order_idx on order_line l

-- Nested loop with a memoized inner side: the outer loop reuses a cache instead
-- of re-executing the inner plan.  hits shows why this is fast.
EXPLAIN (ANALYZE, BUFFERS)
SELECT o.id, l.sku FROM orders o
  JOIN order_line l ON l.order_id = o.id
 WHERE o.id < 5000;
--  Nested Loop  (actual rows=.. loops=1)
--    ->  Index Scan using orders_pkey on orders o
--    ->  Memoize  (actual time=.. rows=.. loops=4998)
--          Cache Hits: 47012
--          ->  Index Scan using order_line_order_idx on order_line l

-- Semi and anti joins. The subquery becomes a Hash node with no join output,
-- which is why an unexpected Hash appears in a query with no explicit join.
EXPLAIN (ANALYZE, BUFFERS)
SELECT id, email FROM customer
 WHERE EXISTS (SELECT 1 FROM orders o
                         WHERE o.customer_id = customer.id AND o.state = 'open');
--  Hash Semi Join  (actual rows=8201 loops=1)   <-- semi: each customer once
--    Hash Cond: (o.customer_id = customer.id)
--    ->  Hash  (actual rows=4210 loops=1)
--          ->  Index Only Scan using orders_open_cover on orders o

-- NOT EXISTS becomes an anti join. NOT IN with a nullable subquery column
-- cannot be hashed and degrades to a much more expensive plan.
EXPLAIN (ANALYZE)
SELECT id FROM customer
 WHERE NOT EXISTS (SELECT 1 FROM orders o WHERE o.customer_id = customer.id);
--  Hash Anti Join  (actual rows=9180 loops=1)

-- LATERAL is the general form: the inner plan may reference the outer row,
-- and the planner chooses a nested loop for it with a per-row bound.
SELECT c.id, c.email, t.last_order_at
  FROM customer c
  LEFT JOIN LATERAL (
       SELECT max(placed_at) AS last_order_at FROM orders
        WHERE customer_id = c.id) t ON TRUE
 WHERE c.country_code = 'DE';
--  Nested Loop Left Join  (actual rows=41800 loops=1)
--    ->  Seq Scan on customer c
--    ->  Aggregate  (actual time=.. rows=1 loops=41800)
StrategyRequiresChosen whenFailure signature in the plan
Nested loopAny join condition; an index or small inner relationOuter estimate small; inner per-row cost low; outer estimate wrong and too smalllo in the millions, or a large Rows RemovedRows Removed on the inner side
Hash joinEquality on a hashable typeEither side large; no usable ordering availableBatches: 2Batches: 2 or more, indicating a spill to temp files
Merge joinBoth inputs sorted, by index or by an explicit sortLarge inputs, an existing index on the join key, or a required ORDER BYORDER BYTwo So nodes whose combined cost exceeds the alternative
Semi / anti joinEXI, I, NOT EXISTSNOT EXISTSSubquery is a filter, not a source of columnsAn unplanned Ha appearing where the query has no join

A merge join on a large result is often the correct plan even when a hash join is faster in isolation. The reason is that the sort paid for the merge join is usually the sort that the query needs anyway, and it is amortised across the whole output; whereas a hash join that streams an unsorted result forces a second sort for the ORDER BYORDER BY. When comparing two plans, compare the total including the final sort and including the Li cut-off, not the join node in isolation. This is the reason Incremental SortIncremental Sort exists, and the reason a query with a LI may be dramatically faster than the same query without one.

5.3 Estimated versus actual rows

The ratio between the estimated and the actual row count at each node is the most diagnostic single measurement in performance work, and the causes are enumerable in a fixed order of likelihood.

In order of how often they turn out to be the cause: the table has never been analysed, or was analysed before the data arrived, so the planner is using a fallback heuristic; the column’s ndis estimate is wrong, which happens when a value that was rare has become dominant after a migration; two predicates on independent columns have been combined and their joint selectivity is far from the product, which extended statistics fix; the predicate is a function call on a column for which there is no histogram, such as date(created_at) = current_datedate(created_at) = current_date; the parameter is generic, so the planner averaged over all possible values; or the data is correlated in a way the planner cannot represent, such as a CH constraint that implies a functional dependency the catalog does record but the estimator does not use in this context.

Each cause has a specific remedy and a specific verification. For the first, run ANA and re-run the plan. For the second, raise statisti for that column. For the third, create a statistics object of kind ndistinctndistinct. For the fourth, create an expression index on the function and rewrite the predicate to match, which also makes it indexable. For the fifth, set plan_cache_mode = force_custom_planplan_cache_mode = force_custom_plan for the statement or reduce the number of distinct parameter values. For the sixth, the remedy is an EXC constraint or a partial index that encodes the dependency, so the query is answered by an index that already respects it.

-- A predicate wrapped in a function is opaque to the histogram. Two problems at
-- once: the estimate is a guess, and the index cannot be used.
EXPLAIN (ANALYZE)
SELECT count(*) FROM orders WHERE date(placed_at) = '2026-03-01';
--  Seq Scan on orders  (cost=.. rows=1428) (actual time=.. rows=184201)
--    Filter: (date(placed_at) = '2026-03-01'::date)

-- Sargable rewrite plus a matching expression index. Note date(timestamptz)
-- is STABLE, not IMMUTABLE, so the index must be built on the range form.
CREATE INDEX CONCURRENTLY orders_placed_day_idx ON orders
       (placed_at) WHERE placed_at >= '2026-03-01' AND placed_at < '2026-03-02';
EXPLAIN (ANALYZE, BUFFERS)
SELECT count(*) FROM orders
 WHERE placed_at >= '2026-03-01'::timestamptz
   AND placed_at <  '2026-03-02'::timestamptz;

-- Measuring the actual distribution that the histogram should have captured.
SELECT date_trunc('day', placed_at) AS day, count(*)
  FROM orders
 WHERE placed_at >= now() - interval '7 days'
 GROUP BY 1 ORDER BY 1 DESC;

-- The primary key is a 16-byte btree on a 8-byte key, so the table has one logical row
-- and two storage rows. This is the normal shape of a referenced table.
SELECT c2.relname AS constraint_index, c2.relkind,
       pg_get_constraintdef(con.oid) AS definition
  FROM pg_constraint con
  JOIN pg_class c1 ON c1.oid = con.conrelid
  JOIN pg_class c2 ON c2.oid = con.conindid
 WHERE c1.relname = 'orders' AND con.contype = 'p';

-- A worked join whose row estimate is wrong for a structural reason, not a
-- statistical one. Both sides are unique, so the join is a lookup by primary
-- key and the result cannot exceed the number of orders -- yet the planner
-- believes it will. The cause is an independent ndistinct assumption for
-- state, not a missing histogram.
EXPLAIN (ANALYZE, BUFFERS, SETTINGS)
SELECT o.id, o.placed_at, c.name FROM "order" o
  JOIN customer c ON c.id = o.customer_id
 WHERE o.state = 'open' AND c.country_code = 'DE';
--  Nested Loop  (cost=.. rows=97) (actual time=.. rows=18402 loops=1)
--    ->  Index Scan using order_open_idx on "order" o
--    ->  Index Scan using customer_pkey on customer c
-- The fix is an ndunique estimate for the pair, not a raise of statistics_target
-- on either column, because the error is in the multiplication.
CREATE STATISTICS order_state_country_stats (ndistinct, dependencies)
       ON state, customer_id FROM "order";
ANALYZE "order";

-- Confirm the target actually took effect: the catalog, not the session.
SELECT attname, attstattarget FROM pg_attribute
 WHERE attrelid = 'orders'::regclass AND attnum > 0;

Never fix an estimate error by disabling a node type. Setting enable_nestloop = offenable_nestloop = off, enable_hashjoin = offenable_hashjoin = off, enable_mergejoin = offenable_mergejoin = off, enable_indexscan = offenable_indexscan = off, or enable_seqscan = offenable_seqscan = off changes the plan for one session, and the resulting plan is chosen by elimination rather than by cost, so it is not necessarily better. These are diagnostic tools with one legitimate use: to answer "would a different strategy have been faster?" for a single statement, and then to write that statement in a form the planner will choose on its own. The exceptions are narrow and real — enable_seqscan = offenable_seqscan = off for a maintenance script’s one-off query, or enable_indexonlyscan = offenable_indexonlyscan = off when a large fraction of a table is permanently unvacuumable and index-only scans keep fetching — and in both cases the fix belongs in the plan’s favour, not in the session’s.

5.4 Sorting, hashing and work_mem

work is a per-node, per-operation ceiling rather than a per-query or per-session budget, so the true ceiling for a plan is the setting multiplied by the number of sort, hash, and materialise nodes that can be active at once.

The accounting is precise and frequently misremembered. Each So node may use up to work; each Ha node may use up to work for its hash table; each Mater and HashAg node likewise. A hash join with a hash table in the outer loop, a parallel worker count of four, and two sort nodes above it can consume a multiple of work, and for a parallel plan the hash table is multiplied again by the hash_mem_ factor, which is 2.0 by default and applies to the parallel shared hash table. Setting work to a large value globally is therefore how a single query brings a server down; the safe pattern is SET LOCAL work_mem = '256MB’SET LOCAL work_mem = '256MB' at the start of a maintenance statement or an interactive session, and a conservative global default of 4 to 16 MB.

When a sort or hash exceeds its budget, the node writes to temporary files in the session’s temp_tab list and reports it. The three signals to watch are Sort Method: external merge Disk: 25kBSort Method: external merge Disk: 25kB on a So node, Batches: NBatches: N with N greater than 1 on a Ha node, and any non-zero temp readtemp read or temp writtentemp written on the plan’s buffer line. log_temp_files = 0log_temp_files = 0 logs every temporary file, which is the correct production setting for a system where temporary file use is unexpected, and the log line names the query.

-- Every memory-hungry node in one plan, with its spill evidence.
EXPLAIN (ANALYZE, BUFFERS)
SELECT customer_id, sum(total_cents) FROM orders
 GROUP BY customer_id HAVING sum(total_cents) > 100000
 ORDER BY 2 DESC LIMIT 50;
--  Sort  (cost=.. rows=..) (actual time=.. rows=50 loops=1)
--    Sort Key: (sum(total_cents)) DESC
--    Sort Method: external merge  Disk: 184kB
--    Buffers: shared hit=120, temp read=940 written=940
--  HashAggregate  (cost=.. rows=..) (actual time=.. rows=812000 loops=1)
--    Batches: 5  Memory Usage: 4096kB  Disk Usage: 9216kB

-- The settings that govern it, and their current values.
SELECT name, setting, unit, boot_val, reset_val, source
  FROM pg_settings
 WHERE name IN ('work_mem', 'hash_mem_multiplier',
                  'maintenance_work_mem', 'temp_buffers',
                  'temp_file_limit', 'log_temp_files',
                  'temp_tablespaces')
 ORDER BY name;

-- Per-session ceiling, applied for this transaction only.
BEGIN SET LOCAL work_mem = '512MB';
-- ... the maintenance statement ...
COMMIT;

Fix a spill with a plan change before you fix it with memory. A hash aggregate that spills will often stop spilling entirely if the input arrives pre-sorted, because the aggregate can then use a GroupAg with no hash table at all; a hash join that spills will often stop if one side becomes a small, cached, index-driven build. work is the right lever when the plan is already optimal and the data is simply larger than the default, which is common for reporting queries. It is the wrong lever when the spill is a symptom of a missing index that would have eliminated the join entirely.

5.5 Partition pruning and constraint exclusion

Pruning happens at plan time for constant predicates and at execution time for parameters, and a plan that does not report removed subplans did not prune anything regardless of what the partition definition says.

Static pruning occurs when the partition key’s comparison bound is a constant known at planning time, which is the case for a literal, a parameter in a custom plan, or a value the planner can constant-fold. It removes whole partitions from the plan before costing. Runtime pruning, available since PostgreSQL 10 and extended in 12 to include I lists, occurs when the bound is a parameter or an expression evaluated per execution; the plan still lists every partition, and reports how many it removed at run time as Subplans Removed: 23Subplans Removed: 23. Both mechanisms depend on the partition key expression matching the query expression closely enough for the planner’s comparison to succeed, which is why WHERE date(taken_at) = '2026-03-01'WHERE date(taken_at) = '2026-03-01' prunes nothing when the partition key is take itself.

Constraint exclusion is the related but separate mechanism that applies to non-partitioned tables: a CH constraint that the planner can prove contradicts the query’s qualifier excludes the table from the plan entirely. It requires the constraint to be a simple comparison against a constant, and it was re-enabled by default in PostgreSQL 10 after being found to be more useful than harmful. It is worth knowing because it explains why a table with a partition-style CH disappears from a plan that has no O in it.

-- Static pruning: the plan contains only the partitions that survive.
EXPLAIN (ANALYZE, BUFFERS)
SELECT count(*), avg(value) FROM measurement
 WHERE taken_at >= '2026-03-01' AND taken_at < '2026-03-02';
--  Aggregate  (actual time=.. rows=1 loops=1)
--    ->  Append  (actual time=.. rows=1814400 loops=1)
--          ->  Seq Scan on measurement_2026_03_01 measurement_1
--          ->  Seq Scan on measurement_2026_03_02 measurement_2
--          ... 2 more children pruned ...

-- Runtime pruning: the plan lists all of them and reports the removals.
PREPARE month (timestamptz

Partitioning is a pruning mechanism, and it degrades to an App when pruning fails. A partitioned table with a hundred partitions and a query that does not prune will open a hundred index or sequential scans, and the App node’s own overhead plus the loss of join strategies that consider partitions as a unit can make it slower than the unpartitioned equivalent. Two settings mitigate this: enable_parti and enable_partitio, which allow a join or aggregate to be executed partition-by-partition so that a join key’s statistics can prune. Both are on by default. Neither helps a query that does not constrain the partition key, so a partitioned design must be paired with a query design that always does.

5.6 The tuning sequence and where it ends

Tuning is a loop of measure, hypothesise, change one thing, and re-measure, and its terminating condition is a plan whose estimated and actual row counts agree and whose chosen operators match the observed cost distribution.

The sequence is fixed and the order matters. Begin with measurement at the statement level: pg_stat_s for total time, calls, and I/O, and log_min_durat with auto_e for the individual slow statements and their plans. Rank by total time, because optimising a 20 ms statement that runs 400 000 times per hour is worth more than optimising a 30-second statement that runs twice. Then, for the top statement, obtain a plan with EXPLAIN (ANALYZE, BUFFERS, VERBOSE, SETTINGS)EXPLAIN (ANALYZE, BUFFERS, VERBOSE, SETTINGS) and read it node by node as in § 5.1. Form one hypothesis about the dominant cost — usually an unexpected sequential scan, a spilled sort, a mis-estimated join, or a missing index — and change exactly one thing. Re-measure with the same invocation, comparing the same numbers. If the change did not help, revert it and form a different hypothesis; do not stack changes, because a stacked improvement cannot be attributed.

The sequence ends at EXP, and that is a deliberate statement about method rather than a rhetorical flourish. A configuration change is only an improvement if the plan it induces is closer to the plan the cost model would have chosen had it known the real cardinalities. Tuning a server by raising shared_ and observing that latency fell is a measurement of the machine, not of the statement; tuning it by raising shared_ and observing that the Bitmap Heap ScanBitmap Heap Scan node’s Buffers: shared readBuffers: shared read count fell from 40 000 to 900 is a measurement of the statement. The first may be real and the second is certainly attributable, and only one of them tells you what to do next when the workload changes.

-- Step 1: rank by total time, not by mean time. Track the reset time so a change
-- in traffic volume is not mistaken for a change in efficiency.
SELECT calls,
       round(total_exec_time::numeric, 1)            AS total_ms,
       round(mean_exec_time::numeric, 2)             AS mean_ms,
       round(stddev_exec_time::numeric, 2)            AS sd_ms,
       rows, shared_blks_hit, shared_blks_read, temp_blks_written,
       left(query, 90)  AS query
  FROM pg_stat_statements
 ORDER BY total_exec_time DESC LIMIT 15;
SELECT stats_reset FROM pg_stat_database WHERE datname = current_database();

-- Step 2: the plan, captured automatically for everything over a threshold.
--   LOAD 'auto_explain';
--   SET auto_explain.log_min_duration = '500ms';
--   SET auto_explain.log_analyze = on;
--   SET auto_explain.log_buffers = on;
--   SET auto_explain.log_nested_statements = on;

-- Step 3: the three findings that justify a memory change, with the numbers
-- that must move for the change to have worked.
SELECT pg_size_pretty(sum(blks_hit) * current_setting('block_size')::bigint)   AS buffer_hits,
       pg_size_pretty(sum(blks_read) * current_setting('block_size')::bigint)   AS buffer_reads,
       round(sum(blks_hit) / GREATEST(sum(blks_hit) + sum(blks_read), 1) * 100, 1) AS hit_pct
  FROM pg_stat_database WHERE datname = current_database();

-- Step 4: the setting, applied narrowly, and immediately reversible.
BEGIN SET LOCAL work_mem = '256MB';
EXPLAIN (ANALYZE, BUFFERS) SELECT -- the statement under test customer_id, count(*)
  FROM orders WHERE state = 'open' GROUP BY 1;
ROLLBACK;

-- The configuration review that precedes any tuning: every setting that is not
-- at its default, with the source that produced it. source = 'default' means
-- the built-in value; 'configuration file' means someone chose it.
SELECT name, setting, unit, category, source, sourcefile, sourceline, pending_restart
  FROM pg_settings
 WHERE source NOT IN ('default', 'override', 'client', 'environment', 'interactive')
 OR name IN ('shared_buffers', 'work_mem', 'effective_cache_size',
                  'max_connections', 'random_page_cost')
 ORDER BY category, name;

-- The two GUCs that silently invalidate plan expectations, and the 18 I/O
-- ones. effective_cache_size is the planner's belief, not a limit.
SELECT name, setting, unit, CASE WHEN unit = '8kB'
           THEN pg_size_pretty(setting::bigint * 8192) ELSE setting END AS value
  FROM pg_settings
 WHERE name IN ('shared_buffers', 'effective_cache_size',
                  'work_mem', 'maintenance_work_mem',
                  'io_method', 'io_max_concurrency', 'io_workers',
                  'effective_io_concurrency', 'random_page_cost',
                  'jit', 'jit_above_cost', 'log_temp_files')
 ORDER BY name;

-- The terminal step of the sequence, repeated for the changed statement. If the
-- chosen node types and the buffer counts are not the ones the change targeted,
-- the change did not do what was intended, whatever the latency says.
EXPLAIN (ANALYZE, BUFFERS, VERBOSE, SETTINGS, SUMMARY)
SELECT customer_id, count(*)
  FROM orders WHERE state = 'open' GROUP BY customer_id;
Symptom in the planMost likely causeFirst change to tryVerify with
Sequential scan on a large table with a selective filterMissing index, or the predicate is not sargableRewrite to a range on the bare column; create the index concurrentlyIndex Scan appears; Buffers: shared readBuffers: shared read falls
Estimated rows far below actual at a nodeStale or absent statistics; function-wrapped predicateANA, then SET STATISTICSSET STATISTICS, then an expression indexEstimate within about 10× of actual
Bat above 1, or Sort Method: external mergeSort Method: external mergeWorking set exceeds workPre-sort the input to use GroupAg, or SET LOCAL work_memSET LOCAL work_memBatches: 1Batches: 1 and zero temp blocks
Nested loop with lo in the thousandsOuter estimate was far too smallFix the outer estimate; add the index for the inner sideJoin becomes a hash join, or loops fall to hundreds
High planning time, low execution timeMany join relations, or generic plan re-planningPrepare the statement; simplify the query; reduce join countPlanning TimePlanning Time falls and stays low on repeat
Correct plan, correct estimates, still slowHardware or configuration, not the statementCheck pg_st, checkpoint rate, and WAL volumeI/O counters and pg_stat_ show the cause

JIT changes the cost model without changing the plan, and it is enabled by default. When a plan’s total cost exceeds jit_abo (1 000 000 by default), the query is compiled and optimised by LLVM, which adds compile time and can change the shape of a costly plan. On a stable workload this is a win for genuinely expensive queries; on a workload of many medium-cost queries it is a pure loss, because the threshold is compared against an estimated cost that the planner computes before the estimate is validated. Setting jit = offjit = off and re-running EXPLAIN (ANALYZE)EXPLAIN (ANALYZE) is the diagnostic: if execution time improves materially with the same plan, the JIT compilation was the cost, and jit_above_cost = -1jit_above_cost = -1 with a documented exception is the correct operational response rather than a blanket change.

Exercises 5

  1. Force each join strategy deliberately and measure. Take a three-table query and obtain a plan with a nested loop, a hash join, and a merge join by adjusting selectivity through statistics and by disabling one strategy at a time in a session. Deliverable: three EXPLAIN (ANALYZE, BUFFERS)EXPLAIN (ANALYZE, BUFFERS) outputs with total execution times, buffer counts, and the SETT line showing which switch produced each, plus a written statement of when you would choose each in production.
  2. Close an estimate gap and prove it. Find or construct a query where the estimated join rows differ from the actual by more than 100×, capture the plan, apply one remedy, and re-capture. Deliverable: before and after plans with the estimate and actual at the join node, the catalog change you made, and a statement of whether the chosen strategy changed or only its cost.
  3. Characterise memory consumption of your worst query. Run your top statement by total time with EXPLAIN (ANALYZE, BUFFERS)EXPLAIN (ANALYZE, BUFFERS) at three work values, counting every sort, hash, and materialise node. Deliverable: a table of node count, spill count, temp blocks written, and execution time at each setting, plus the safe global work you would deploy given max_con and the measured maximum.
  4. Prove partition pruning on a table you own. Create a partitioned parent with 24 monthly partitions, load 500 000 rows, and compare identical queries with and without a partition-key predicate, plus a runtime-pruned prepared statement. Deliverable: three plans showing static pruning, Subplans RemovedSubplans Removed, and a non-pruned baseline, with buffer counts and timings for each.
  5. Run the full tuning sequence on one statement and publish the loop. Take the highest-total-time statement in your workload, record the plan, make one change, re-measure, and either keep it or revert it. Deliverable: a log with the plan and the three key numbers before and after each attempt, an explicit statement of the hypothesis for each attempt, and the final EXP showing the node that now dominates the cost.

6 · Indexes Deeply: BRIN, Covering, Expression and Operational Aspects

6.1 BRIN for correlated append-only data6.2 INCLUDE and the index-only scan6.3 Expression indexes and immutability6.4 The write cost ledger6.5 Finding and safely dropping unused indexes6.6 Operational index maintenance

This section treats index management as a discipline with measurable costs and a defensible audit trail. It develops BRIN as the correct structure for physically correlated append-only data, with the lossy recheck stated explicitly and a worked example of the physical correlation on which the whole structure depends. It covers INC covering indexes as the practical alternative to widening a table with stored derived columns, and connects the index-only scan to the visibility map so that the benefit can be verified rather than assumed. It derives the expression-index immutability requirement from the now()now() case, enumerates the write amplification that each additional index imposes, and gives a safe protocol for identifying and dropping indexes that nothing reads. It closes with the maintenance operations that matter: concurrent rebuild, bloat measurement, and progress monitoring.

6.1 BRIN for correlated append-only data

A BRIN index summarises a range of heap pages into minimum and maximum bounds, so it can exclude data without reading it — and it can only do so when the indexed column’s values are physically correlated with page order.

The structure is deliberately minimal. With pages_p set to 32 by default, every 32 consecutive heap pages produce one index tuple recording the smallest and largest indexed values found in them, plus a null flag. A range query is answered by discarding any block range whose bounds cannot contain the query’s bounds, and reading the remainder. Because the summary cannot enumerate the values, the executor re-checks every candidate tuple, which is why the plan line says Recheck CondRecheck Cond and why BRIN is always described as lossy. The correlation requirement is a property of the physical heap layout: if rows are inserted in ascending timestamp order, the values in a block range span a narrow interval and pruning is highly effective; if rows are inserted in random order, every block range contains the full value domain, no range is excluded, and the index does strictly more work than a sequential scan while answering the same rows.

Two operational settings matter. autosu, on by default since PostgreSQL 10, extends the index’s range of summarised values during normal vacuum, which is what keeps the summaries current for a table that is continuously appended to. pages_p trades index size against precision: a smaller value means a more precise summary and a larger index, and for a table where the correlation is imperfect a value of 32 or 64 is a reasonable starting point while 128 or 256 keeps the index small at the cost of more recheck. For a table where every block range holds values spanning the whole domain, a very large pages_p is the correct choice because the index is being used as a coarse partition filter rather than as a search structure.

Propertybtbr
Index entries for 10 M rowsOrder 10 MOrder 10 000 000 ÷ (pages_p × rows per page)
Typical size on a time series250 to 400 MBUnder 1 MB
Pruning powerExact, down to the rowPer block range; requires physical correlation
Recheck of returned rowsNoneAlways
Requires sorted physical orderNoYes, for the pruning to be effective
 
-- Append-only sensor log. A BRIN index is a few hundred kilobytes where a b-tree
-- would be hundreds of megabytes, and it prunes by physical correlation.
-- CREATE TABLE sensor_log (
--   id bigint GENERATED ALWAYS AS IDENTITY,
--   sensor_id integer NOT NULL,
--   taken_at timestamptz NOT NULL,
--   value double precision NOT NULL);
CREATE INDEX sensor_log_taken_brin ON sensor_log
       USING brin (taken_at) WITH (pages_per_range = 64, autosummarize = on);
CREATE INDEX sensor_log_sensor_brin ON sensor_log
       USING brin (sensor_id) WITH (pages_per_range = 64);

-- A b-tree on the same column, for comparison. Only one of these two is
-- appropriate, and the choice is size plus pruning, not speed.
CREATE INDEX sensor_log_taken_btree ON sensor_log (taken_at);

-- The correlation the whole structure depends on, measured rather than assumed.
SELECT attname, correlation, n_distinct, null_frac
  FROM pg_stats WHERE tablename = 'sensor_log' AND attname = 'taken_at';

-- Evidence of pruning, and the recheck that always accompanies it.
EXPLAIN (ANALYZE, BUFFERS, SETTINGS)
SELECT count(*), avg(value) FROM sensor_log
 WHERE taken_at >= '2026-03-01' AND taken_at < '2026-03-02'
   AND sensor_id = 17;
--  Bitmap Heap Scan on sensor_log  (cost=.. rows=..) (actual time=.. rows=18144 loops=1)
--    Recheck Cond: ((taken_at >= ..) AND (taken_at < ..))
--    Filter: (sensor_id = 17)
--    Rows Removed by Index Recheck: 0
--    Heap Blocks: exact=12 lossy=0  -- a lossy count of 0 means the summary was
--    Buffers: shared hit=96            -- precise enough to exclude whole pages.

-- Keeping the summaries current. Since 10, autosummarize extends the summarised
-- range during vacuum. pg_brin_summarize_new_values is the manual equivalent,
-- and pg_brin_bloom_summary is a contrib extension for tables with hundreds of
-- distinct values in the summarised column, where plain min/max is too coarse.
--   CREATE EXTENSION pg_brin_bloom_summary;
--   ALTER TABLE sensor_log SET (brin.bloom_summary = true);
SELECT brin_summarize_new_values('sensor_log_taken_brin'::regclass);

-- The decisive comparison: identical column, two structures.
SELECT i.indexrelid::regclass AS idx, am.amname,
       pg_relation_size(i.indexrelid)                          AS bytes,
       pg_size_pretty(pg_relation_size(i.indexrelid))           AS size,
       SELECT amopopts FROM pg_index WHERE indexrelid = i.indexrelid
  FROM pg_index i
  JOIN pg_class c ON c.oid = i.indexrelid
  JOIN pg_am am ON am.oid = c.relam
 WHERE i.indrelid = 'sensor_log'::regclass
 ORDER BY bytes;
--  sensor_log_taken_brin  | brin    |   245 760 |    240 kB
--  sensor_log_sensor_brin | brin    |   245 760 |    240 kB
--  sensor_log_taken_btree | btree   | 293 601 280 |  280 MB   <-- 1200x larger

BRIN is a partitioned index that happens to live in the heap. For very large append-only tables the two common designs are a BRIN index on a timestamp, which prunes within a heap that is not physically ordered, and true range partitioning, which makes the pruning exact because the pruning is done by the partition constraint. Partitioning is stronger, costs more in metadata, and requires the data to be loaded into the right partition; BRIN requires nothing of the load order and degrades gracefully. If a table is already partitioned by time, a BRIN index inside each partition is close to redundant, and the useful case for BRIN is a large table that is not partitioned.

6.2 INCLUDE and the index-only scan

An INC list stores additional columns in the index leaf pages without making them part of the key, and it is the only way to obtain an index-only scan for a query whose filter columns are narrow but whose output columns are wide.

The distinction between a key column and an included column is structural. A key column participates in the comparison that orders the index and therefore determines which operator classes apply and whether the index can serve a given predicate. An included column is stored in the leaf tuple and returned by an index-only scan, but it is not compared and cannot be used as an Index CondIndex Cond. This is why the alternative of widening the key to cover the query’s output columns is wrong: it changes the sort order of the index and destroys its usefulness for any other query. INC is documented for B-tree, GiST, and SP-GiST; the behaviour described here, and the covering benefit in general, is a B-tree property, and GIN, BRIN, and hash have their own alternatives.

An index-only scan is possible only when two conditions hold: every column the query references is available in the index, and the heap page holding each tuple is marked all-visible in the visibility map, which is written by VAC and never by an ordinary write. The proof in the plan is the Heap FetchesHeap Fetches line, which reports how many times the executor had to visit the heap anyway. Zero means the index answered the whole query. A non-zero value that persists under a table that has just been vacuumed means the visibility map is not fully populated, which on a frequently updated table is a steady-state condition rather than a transient one, and the index-only scan then costs a heap visit per row plus the check — that is, it is no better than an index scan, and slightly worse.

-- Covering index for the single most frequent read pattern: a narrow key, a
-- moderately wide payload, no heap visit at all.
CREATE INDEX CONCURRENTLY order_tenant_state_cover ON "order" (tenant_id, state)
       INCLUDE (id, placed_at, total_cents);
EXPLAIN (ANALYZE, BUFFERS)
SELECT id, placed_at, total_cents FROM "order"
 WHERE tenant_id = 7 AND state = 'open';
--  Index Only Scan using order_tenant_state_cover  (cost=.. rows=..) (actual rows=8201)
--    Index Cond: (tenant_id = 7 AND state = 'open'::text)
--    Heap Fetches: 0        <-- the only line that proves the covering index works

-- The same query before the INCLUDE existed: an index scan that must visit
-- every matching heap page to read placed_at and total_cents.
--  Index Scan using order_tenant_state_idx  (actual rows=8201)
--    Buffers: shared hit=611

-- Visibility map occupancy: what fraction of pages can support an index-only
-- scan, and therefore what the upper bound on Heap Fetches: 0 really is.
--   CREATE EXTENSION pg_visibility;
SELECT * FROM pg_visibility_map_summary('"order"'::regclass, 0);
--  all_visible | all_frozen | visibility_map_bytes

-- Rules: an INCLUDE column cannot be a column of a unique constraint's key,
-- cannot be used in ON CONFLICT, and is not matched by the planner for an
-- Index Cond. It only widens the leaf and makes the scan self-sufficient.

-- The visibility map is the precondition, and it is only written by VACUUM. A
-- table that is never vacuumed can never have a fully index-only scan, which
-- makes the insert-trigger threshold the setting that governs covering indexes.
SELECT name, setting, unit FROM pg_settings
 WHERE name IN ('autovacuum_vacuum_insert_threshold',
                  'autovacuum_vacuum_insert_scale_factor',
                  'autovacuum_vacuum_scale_factor',
                  'autovacuum_vacuum_threshold', 'autovacuum_vacuum_cost_limit')
 ORDER BY name;
--   ALTER TABLE sensor_log SET (autovacuum_vacuum_insert_threshold = 1000);

-- Prove the precondition on a per-page basis for a specific query's range.
--   SELECT * FROM pg_visibility_map('"order"'::regclass, 0) LIMIT 5;
--   SELECT all_visible, all_frozen, pd_all_visible
--     FROM generate_series(0, 3) b,
--          LATERAL pg_visibility_map('"order"'::regclass, b) v;

-- When covering is not available, the alternative is not a wider index but a
-- narrower table: a stored generated column for values that are read on every
-- access and never written independently.
SELECT relname,
       CASE WHEN heap_blks_hit + heap_blks_read = 0 THEN NULL
            ELSE round(100.0 * heap_blks_hit / (heap_blks_hit + heap_blks_read), 1) END AS heap_hit_pct,
       idx_blks_hit, idx_blks_read
  FROM pg_statio_user_tables
 ORDER BY heap_blks_read DESC LIMIT 10;

A covering index that is never vacuumed is pure overhead. On a table under continuous update, a meaningful fraction of pages will not be all-visible at any instant, and the Heap FetchesHeap Fetches line will be a non-zero fraction of the returned rows. In that state the index is larger than a plain index, costs the same to write, and returns no benefit — while appearing in the plan as an Index Only ScanIndex Only Scan, which is misleading in a review. Measure Heap FetchesHeap Fetches against actual rowsactual rows on a table under real write load before concluding that a covering index is earning its size, and be aware that the ratio is a function of the update rate and the autovacuum threshold, not of the query.

6.3 Expression indexes and immutability

An expression index stores the result of an expression rather than a column, and the expression must be immutable — the requirement exists because the stored value would otherwise be a snapshot of a value that the engine cannot reproduce, and the index would silently disagree with the column.

The immutability requirement is best understood through the case that motivates it. now()now() returns the transaction start time, so it is STA: constant within a statement, different across statements. If it could be indexed directly, the index entry written by a row inserted on Monday would be compared on Tuesday against a now()now() that is a day later, and the descent would be searching for a key that has never existed. The engine rejects the creation outright with ERROR: functions in index expression must be marked IMMUTABLEERROR: functions in index expression must be marked IMMUTABLE, and the correct alternatives are: index a range on the underlying timestamp column and let the query supply a half-open range; store a generated column of the normalised value; or pass the value in as a parameter and use a plain column, which is what the sargable rewrite in § 5.3 does.

Two subtleties catch teams regularly. First, date_trunc()date_trunc() and to_char()to_char() are overloads: on a timestamp without time zonetimestamp without time zone argument they are IMMU and indexable, and on a times argument they are STA because the result depends on the session’s Time, and therefore not indexable. The identical-looking expression has opposite volatility purely because of the argument type. Second, if a function is declared IMMU and its behaviour later changes, every index built on it is now wrong and must be rebuilt; PostgreSQL tracks the function’s identity in the index dependency but not a behavioural hash, so nothing detects this automatically.

-- The rejection, and the catalog lookup that predicts it before you try.
--   CREATE INDEX ON t (now());
--   ERROR:  functions in index expression must be marked IMMUTABLE
SELECT p.proname, p.proargtypes::regtype[] AS args,
       CASE p.provolatile WHEN 'i' THEN 'immutable'
                          WHEN 's' THEN 'stable'
                          ELSE 'volatile' END AS volatility
  FROM pg_proc p
 WHERE p.proname IN ('date_trunc','to_char','lower',
                 'now','jsonb_path_query_first','substring')
 ORDER BY 1, 2;

-- The correct index for a normalised search key: the expression must
-- appear in the query in a form the planner can match, and it is the
-- IMMUTABLE lower() that makes this legal at all.
CREATE INDEX customer_lower_email_idx ON customer (lower(email));
EXPLAIN (ANALYZE)
SELECT id FROM customer WHERE lower(email) = 'ops@example.com';
--  Index Scan using customer_lower_email_idx on customer
--    Index Cond: (lower(email) = 'ops@example.com'::text)
--   WHERE email = 'OPS@Example.com'  will NOT use it: the expression differs.

-- A daily bucket on a timestamptz column is STABLE and is therefore rejected;
-- the same expression on a plain timestamp is IMMUTABLE and is accepted.
--   CREATE INDEX ON event ((date_trunc('day', taken_at)));      -- works
--   CREATE INDEX ON event ((date_trunc('day', taken_at AT TIME ZONE 'UTC')));
--   --  ERROR: functions in index expression must be marked IMMUTABLE

-- What was actually stored, read back from the catalog.
SELECT c.relname, pg_get_expr(i.indexprs, i.indrelid) AS expression,
       pg_get_expr(i.indpred, i.indrelid) AS predicate
  FROM pg_index i JOIN pg_class c ON c.oid = i.indexrelid
 WHERE i.indexprs IS NOT NULL OR i.indpred IS NOT NULL
 ORDER BY 1;

Prefer a stored generated column over a duplicated expression in application SQL. A generated column puts the expression in exactly one place, in the schema, and the planner matches the query’s bare column reference to the stored value automatically. An expression index requires the application to reproduce the expression text exactly, including the collation, in every query that should use it, and a mismatch fails silently — the query still works, using a sequential scan. Where the expression is genuinely needed at write time rather than only at query time, the generated column also saves the CPU of re-evaluating it on every read.

6.4 The write cost ledger

Every index imposes a fixed per-row cost on every insert, update, and delete that touches the relation, and the cost is measurable rather than a matter of opinion, so index count is a budget that should be managed deliberately.

The ledger has four entries. Per insert, one index entry per index, each a WAL record, and on a cold index a page read plus potentially a page split with a full-page image. Per update, an index entry is written only if an indexed column’s value changed; if no indexed column changed, PostgreSQL attempts a heap-only tuple update, which writes no index entries and no WAL for the indexes, at the cost of requiring free space on the same page — which is what fillf reserves. Per delete, index entries are marked dead rather than removed, and vacuum reclaims them. And per vacuum, index pages must be scanned and dead entries cleaned, which is the cost that bottom-up deletion in 14 reduced and that pgsta quantifies.

The hot-update metric is n_tup_ against n_tup_updn_tup_upd. A table where the ratio is high is getting its update benefit from fillf and from a stable key; a table where the ratio is near zero is writing a full set of index entries per update and paying WAL amplification on top, and the remedy is either a narrower index, a partial index that the update does not touch, or an INCLUDEINCLUDE-based covering index whose key is the single mutable predicate column.

-- The write ledger, per table: updates, how many were heap-only, and how many
-- index entries the rest produced. Index size relative to heap size is the
-- standing cost of every future write to this relation.
SELECT relname,
       n_tup_ins, n_tup_upd, n_tup_del, n_tup_hot_upd,
       CASE WHEN n_tup_upd > 0
            THEN round(100.0 * n_tup_hot_upd / n_tup_upd, 1) END AS hot_pct,
       pg_size_pretty(pg_relation_size(relid))                        AS heap,
       pg_size_pretty(pg_indexes_size(relid))                         AS indexes,
       round(pg_indexes_size(relid)::numeric / pg_relation_size(relid), 2) AS index_to_heap
  FROM pg_stat_user_tables
 WHERE n_tup_upd > 1000
 ORDER BY pg_indexes_size(relid) DESC LIMIT 10;

-- WAL volume attributable to this database, which is where index
-- write amplification becomes visible in operational terms.
SELECT wal_records, wal_fpi, wal_bytes,
       round(wal_bytes / 1024 / 1024, 1) AS wal_mb,
       round(100.0 * wal_fpi / GREATEST(wal_records, 1), 2) AS fpi_pct
  FROM pg_stat_wal;

-- Per-index write cost, measured: build cost and growth of a single index
-- against the table it is attached to, for one representative load.
--   CREATE INDEX CONCURRENTLY t_probe ON t (col);
--   SELECT pg_size_pretty(pg_relation_size('t_probe'));
--   INSERT 100000 rows;  -- then re-measure both sizes and the elapsed time.

-- Deferred: check the fillfactor of the relation and of each index.
SELECT relname, reloptions
  FROM pg_class WHERE relname IN ('order', 'order_line')
    OR relname LIKE 'order%idx';

Index count grows by accident and is never reduced without an audit. Each new feature adds a "helpful" index, each report adds a covering index, each ORM migration adds an index for a foreign key, and the write cost of the table rises with every one of them while no single index’s contribution is visible in any measurement. The consequence appears first as WAL volume and checkpoint pressure, then as vacuum time, then as insert latency — three symptoms with no obvious common cause. The defence is a scheduled inventory query that reports index size against table write volume and index access counts, run monthly, with the same query used to review candidates for removal rather than a series of ad-hoc decisions made under pressure during an incident.

6.5 Finding and safely dropping unused indexes

Identifying an index as unused requires more evidence than a zero access count, because access counters are cumulative, are reset by a statistics reset, and do not record the uses that come from constraint enforcement and from rare maintenance queries.

The counters in pg_stat_us are cumulative since the last statistics reset and, on versions where the statistics subsystem is shared-memory based, persist across a clean restart but not across a crash. A zero count on a freshly started cluster with reset statistics means nothing. The correct procedure is therefore: take a baseline, wait at least one complete business cycle including month-end and any scheduled batch work, take a second reading, and treat an index as a candidate only if it was unused in both. Add to the candidate set the reverse check — the indexes whose access counters are the highest for a rarely executed statement, which are the ones most likely to be removed by accident.

Three classes of index must be excluded from any candidate list. An index backing a PRIMARY KEYPRIMARY KEY, UNI, EXC, or FOREIGN KEYFOREIGN KEY constraint is used by the engine during constraint enforcement regardless of whether any query names it, and it cannot be dropped at all while the constraint depends on it. An index whose sole purpose is to make a specific rare statement fast — a nightly reconciliation, a support query — will show a count near zero and must be identified by reading the statements that reference it, typically via pg_stat_s over a longer window. And an index on a replica is not evidence about the primary, because statistics are per-cluster and the replica’s access pattern is a consequence of physical replication, not of the workload.

-- The inventory. idx_scan is cumulative; a single reading is a hypothesis, and the
-- same query run a cycle later is the evidence.
SELECT s.schemaname, s.relname AS table_name, s.indexrelname AS index_name,
       pg_size_pretty(pg_relation_size(s.indexrelid))                  AS size,
       s.idx_scan,
       t.n_tup_ins + t.n_tup_upd + t.n_tup_del                          AS table_writes,
       EXISTS (SELECT 1 FROM pg_constraint c
                WHERE c.conindid = s.indexrelid)             AS backs_constraint,
       am.amname
  FROM pg_stat_user_indexes s
  JOIN pg_stat_user_tables t ON t.relid = s.relid
  JOIN pg_class c ON c.oid = s.indexrelid
  JOIN pg_am am ON am.oid = c.relam
 WHERE NOT s.idx_scan
   AND NOT EXISTS (SELECT 1 FROM pg_constraint c
                     WHERE c.conindid = s.indexrelid)
 ORDER BY pg_relation_size(s.indexrelid) DESC;

-- Diffing two snapshots taken a cycle apart, so the decision rests on the
-- delta rather than on an absolute count that a reset can silently zero.
--   CREATE TEMP TABLE idx_baseline AS SELECT indexrelid::regclass AS idx, idx_scan FROM
--     pg_stat_user_indexes;
--   ... one cycle later ...
--   SELECT b.idx, b.idx_scan, s.idx_scan - b.idx_scan AS scans_in_cycle
--     FROM idx_baseline b JOIN pg_stat_user_indexes s USING (indexrelid)
--    WHERE s.idx_scan = b.idx_scan;

-- Before dropping, ask the planner what it would do without the index, using
-- a hypothetical index of the same shape to compare candidate designs.
--   CREATE EXTENSION hypopg;
--   CREATE INDEX ON hypopg (customer_id, placed_at);
--   EXPLAIN SELECT * FROM orders WHERE customer_id = 7 ORDER BY placed_at DESC LIMIT 20;
--   SELECT * FROM hypopg_list_indexes;
--   SELECT hypopg_reset();

Drop with DROP INDEX CONCURRENTLYDROP INDEX CONCURRENTLY, and never drop the last index in a cycle in which a critical statement depends on it. The concurrent form takes only SHARE UPDATE EXCLUSIVESHARE UPDATE EXCLUSIVE, so reads and writes continue; it cannot run inside a transaction block, it does two passes over the table, and it marks the index invalid rather than deleting it in place. The safe sequence is: confirm the two-snapshot evidence, confirm the index backs no constraint, capture the EXP of the statements that would have used it, issue the drop, and then watch those statements' plans for one full cycle. If a plan regresses, the index can be recreated concurrently with the identical definition and the data loss is one cycle of latency rather than an outage.

6.6 Operational index maintenance

Index maintenance is a scheduled, observable activity whose tools are concurrency-safe, progress-reporting, and measurable — and the operational discipline is to run them with a time limit, a progress check, and an invalid-index alert.

REI rebuilds an index while holding an EXCL lock, which permits reads but blocks every write, and it must run outside a transaction block. REINDEX ... CONCURRENTLYREINDEX ... CONCURRENTLY builds a new index with the same definition under a sequence of weaker locks, then swaps, then drops the old one; it may not be used on a system-replication-slot or hot-standby relation, it waits for other concurrent reindexes of the same table rather than duplicating the work, and it is subject to the same failure mode as CREATE INDEX CONCURRENTLYCREATE INDEX CONCURRENTLY: if it is interrupted, it leaves the index in place but marked invalid, and an invalid index is not used by the planner while still being maintained by every subsequent write. The correct response to an invalid index is to drop it and retry, and the correct control is a monitoring query against pg_index.indisvalidpg_index.indisvalid.

Measurement is available and should precede any rebuild. pgstatindex()pgstatindex() from pagei reports average leaf density, leaf fragmentation, and internal page counts for a B-tree, which together distinguish a bloated index from a merely large one; a rebuild does not help an index whose pages are 90 per cent full, and does help dramatically when leaf density is below 50 per cent after a high-rate delete workload. pgstattuple()pgstattuple() and pgstattuple_approx()pgstattuple_approx() give the same picture for tables, and pgstattup is the one to use at scale because it only samples. VAC reclaims dead index entries in place and, since PostgreSQL 14, does so with substantially less write amplification; a full REI is warranted by fragmentation, not by dead tuples alone.

-- Is this index actually bloated, or just large? This is the measurement that
-- decides between REINDEX and doing nothing.
--   CREATE EXTENSION pageinspect;
--   CREATE EXTENSION pgstattuple;
SELECT i.indexrelid::regclass                AS idx,
       pg_size_pretty(pg_relation_size(i.indexrelid)) AS size,
       round((pgstatindex(i.indexrelid)).avg_leaf_density, 1) AS leaf_density,
       round((pgstatindex(i.indexrelid)).leaf_fragmentation, 1) AS fragmentation
  FROM pg_index i
 WHERE i.indrelid = 'order'::regclass
 ORDER BY pg_relation_size(i.indexrelid) DESC;

-- The alert that must exist on every system that runs concurrent builds.
SELECT c.relname AS invalid_index, i.indrelid::regclass AS on_table
  FROM pg_index i JOIN pg_class c ON c.oid = i.indexrelid
 WHERE NOT i.indisvalid;
-- Recovery:  DROP INDEX CONCURRENTLY invalid_index;  then rebuild.

-- Progress of a running build, which is what a maintenance window check reads.
SELECT p.pid, p.relid::regclass AS table_name, p.index_relid::regclass,
       p.phase, p.blocks_done, p.blocks_total,
       CASE WHEN p.blocks_total > 0
            THEN round(100.0 * p.blocks_done / p.blocks_total, 1) END AS pct
  FROM pg_stat_progress_create_index p;

-- The build itself. maintenance_work_mem governs the sort, not work_mem, and
-- an undersized value is the reason a build spills to temp files.
--   SET maintenance_work_mem = '2GB';
--   SET statement_timeout = 0;            -- concurrent builds are not interruptible
--   REINDEX INDEX CONCURRENTLY order_tenant_state_cover;
--   REINDEX TABLE CONCURRENTLY order;

-- Per-index storage options can be tuned in place; the change applies to new
-- pages only, so a rebuild is required for it to take effect retroactively.
ALTER INDEX order_tenant_state_cover SET (fillfactor = 85, deduplicate_items = on);
--   CREATE EXTENSION pg_prewarm;
--   SELECT pg_prewarm('order_tenant_state_cover');

-- Bloat across every index in the database, so a rebuild campaign is prioritised by
-- measurable waste rather than by age. Density below 50 per cent on a large
-- B-tree is the threshold at which REINDEX pays for itself.
WITH btrees AS (
  SELECT i.indexrelid,
         c.oid::regclass        AS idx,
         i.indrelid::regclass   AS tbl,
         pg_relation_size(i.indexrelid) AS bytes,
         (pgstatindex(i.indexrelid)).avg_leaf_density AS density,
         (pgstatindex(i.indexrelid)).leaf_fragmentation AS frag
    FROM pg_index i
    JOIN pg_class c ON c.oid = i.indexrelid
    JOIN pg_am am ON am.oid = c.relam
   WHERE am.amname = 'btree' AND c.relkind = 'i')
SELECT idx, tbl, pg_size_pretty(bytes) AS size,
       round(density, 1) AS leaf_density, round(frag, 1) AS fragmentation,
       round((100 - density) / 100 * bytes / 1024 / 1024, 1) AS reclaimable_mb
  FROM btrees
 WHERE bytes > 64 * 1024 * 1024 AND density < 60
 ORDER BY reclaimable_mb DESC NULLS LAST LIMIT 25;

-- A rebuild campaign, one index at a time, with the two settings that make a
-- long-running concurrent rebuild survivable. lock_timeout bounds the wait for
-- the brief lock at the end; statement_timeout must be disabled because a
-- concurrent reindex cannot be interrupted partway without leaving an
-- invalid index behind.
--   SET lock_timeout = '3s';
--   SET statement_timeout = 0;
--   REINDEX INDEX CONCURRENTLY idx_name;
--   -- then: SELECT * FROM idx_stat_summary WHERE indisvalid = false;
--   -- an invalid index is still maintained by every write and still consumes space.

Partitioned tables change the maintenance model, because an index on the parent is partitioned too. Rebuilding an index on a partitioned table rebuilds it on every partition, which means the operation’s cost is multiplied by the partition count and its lock footprint is the whole logical table. A partition-by-partition REINDEX INDEX CONCURRENTLYREINDEX INDEX CONCURRENTLY in a loop, with a progress check between partitions, is the operational pattern, and the parent’s index is only marked valid when every partition’s is. Since PostgreSQL 17 a partition-local index can be attached to the parent’s index rather than created, so a maintenance strategy that rebuilds one partition’s index and reattaches it is both possible and much cheaper than rebuilding the parent.

Exercises 6

  1. Prove BRIN’s correlation requirement. Create two 5-million-row tables with identical data, one loaded in ascending timestamp order and one randomly, and build a BRIN index on each. Deliverable: two EXPLAIN (ANALYZE, BUFFERS)EXPLAIN (ANALYZE, BUFFERS) outputs showing the Heap BlocksHeap Blocks exact and lossy counts, the two index sizes, and a written statement of how you would detect the failing case from statistics alone.
  2. Measure the covering index benefit honestly. Create three versions of a hot query’s index — key only, key with INC, and a stored generated column plus key — and measure buffer counts, Heap FetchesHeap Fetches, and index size on a table under continuous update. Deliverable: the three plans with all three numbers, the pg_visibilit output at the time of measurement, and a conclusion stating the update rate at which covering stops paying.
  3. Build the expression-index catalogue. For 30 functions you actually use in predicates, record their volatility and their argument types, and classify each as indexable or not. Deliverable: the table, the three queries you rewrote to sargable form, and the measured latency difference for each of those three.
  4. Produce a write-cost ledger for your largest table. Record heap and index sizes, the n_tup_ ratio, WAL volume per 100 000 inserts with and without a specific index, and the rebuild time. Deliverable: the ledger with a stated cost per index per million writes, and a ranking of your indexes by measured benefit against that cost.
  5. Execute a full index audit with evidence. Take two snapshots of pg_stat_us a full business cycle apart, identify the unused candidates excluding constraint-backed indexes, verify each against pg_stat_s for a longer window, and drop the confirmed set. Deliverable: the two snapshots, the candidate list with an exclusion reason for each rejection, the space reclaimed, and the plan comparison for the five most affected statements one week after the drops.

7 · Partitioning and Table Inheritance

7.1 Declarative partitioning7.2 Partition routing and pruning7.3 Partition maintenance and lifecycle7.4 Table inheritance7.5 Partitioned tables as objects7.6 Pruning in practice

PostgreSQL offers two distinct but related mechanisms for treating a family of physical tables as one logical relation. Declarative partitioning binds a routing key to a partition tree, so that every row is physically stored in exactly one leaf and the executor never has to filter rows out of the wrong branch. Table inheritance is the older, looser mechanism: children copy the parent’s column list, are constrained only by CHECK predicates that a query planner may or may not be able to exploit, and no routing is enforced at all. This chapter treats partitioning as a physical-design decision first and an object-model decision second, because the operational consequences of a badly chosen partition key — maintenance locks, index proliferation, and the absence of any sane unique constraint — dominate every semantic benefit that partitioning appears to offer. The most consequential facts in the chapter are that range bounds are half-open and therefore month-long partitions must be written with an explicit next-month upper bound, that a missing or full DEFAULT partition converts an ordinary INSERT into a hard error, and that attaching or detaching a partition takes an ACCESS EXCLUSIVE lock that must be scheduled, not merely executed.

7.1 Declarative partitioning

Establishes the three partitioning strategies, the exact semantics of partition bounds, and the invariant that the partition key must appear in every candidate unique key on the partitioned table.

Declarative partitioning turns a table definition into a two-part object: a partitioned table that holds no storage of its own and exists only as a routing specification, plus a set of partitions that are ordinary tables with ordinary storage, ordinary indexes and ordinary constraints. The routing key is declared once with PARTITION BYPARTITION BY and is immutable for the lifetime of the table. Every row must carry a value for the key that satisfies exactly one partition’s bounds; the server enforces this on every INS and UPD rather than relying on the application, which means a partitioned table cannot accumulate the "orphan" rows that a CHECKCHECK-constrained inheritance hierarchy eventually does.

Three strategies exist and they are not interchangeable. RANGE partitions route on an ordered range of values and is the correct default for time series. LIST partitions route on a discrete set of values and is the correct choice for status, tenant, or region. HASH partitions spread rows by a hash of the key, is fixed-size by nature, and exists primarily to reduce per-index size and to enable parallel append; it has no time-based retention semantics and cannot be pruned by a range predicate. A partitioned table may be sub-partitioned, and it is common — and correct — to partition by month on RANGE and then by HASH inside each month so that a single month’s index does not become a maintenance bottleneck.

-- Range partitioning on a timestamp: bounds are [ FROM , TO ) — lower inclusive, upper exclusive.
CREATE TABLE orders (
    order_id     bigint      GENERATED BY DEFAULT AS IDENTITY,
    customer_id  bigint      NOT NULL,
    placed_at    timestamptz NOT NULL,
    status       text        NOT NULL,
    total_cents  bigint      NOT NULL,
    CONSTRAINT orders_status_ck CHECK (status IN ('new','paid','shipped','cancelled')),
    PRIMARY KEY (order_id, placed_at)
) PARTITION BY RANGE (placed_at);

-- The upper bound is the start of the next month; never write '2025-02-28'.
CREATE TABLE orders_2025m01 PARTITION OF orders
    FOR VALUES FROM ('2025-01-01 00:00:00+00') TO ('2025-02-01 00:00:00+00');
CREATE TABLE orders_2025m02 PARTITION OF orders
    FOR VALUES FROM ('2025-02-01 00:00:00+00') TO ('2025-03-01 00:00:00+00');

-- Catch-all. Any row whose key does not satisfy an explicit bound lands here.
CREATE TABLE orders_default PARTITION OF orders DEFAULT;

INSERT INTO orders (customer_id, placed_at, status, total_cents)
VALUES (8812, '2025-01-17 09:14:02+00', 'paid', 4599);

The half-open bound is the single most common source of data loss in partitioned schemas. FOR VALUES TO ('2025-02-01')FOR VALUES TO ('2025-02-01') contains 2025-01-31 23:59:59.999999 but not 2025-02-01 00:00:00; a partition ending at the last day of a month therefore silently loses the final day unless the writer computes the next period boundary. Generate bounds programmatically from a date series rather than transcribing them. The same discipline applies to the partition key’s type: a timestamptz key with a text literal bound is interpreted in the session TimeZone, so a partition boundary can shift by hours across a DST transition unless the literal carries an explicit offset, as above.

The invariant that trips up most migrations is that every candidate unique key on a partitioned table must contain the full partition key. PostgreSQL implements uniqueness per partition, and a per-partition unique index cannot prove global uniqueness without the key that determined which partition to look in. A primary key on orde alone is rejected outright:

Constraint. PRIMARY KEY (order_id)PRIMARY KEY (order_id) on a table partitioned by RANGE (placed_at)RANGE (placed_at) fails with unique constraint on partitioned table must include all partitioning columnsunique constraint on partitioned table must include all partitioning columns. The practical consequence is that any natural key used for lookups by the application must be carried in the primary key, which changes both the index shape and the size of the RETU tuple. Decide the partition key before designing the primary key, not afterwards.

-- LIST partitioning: the routing key must be a discrete, finite set of values.
CREATE TABLE events (
    event_id   bigint GENERATED ALWAYS AS IDENTITY,
    tenant_id  bigint NOT NULL,
    kind       text   NOT NULL,
    payload    jsonb  NOT NULL,
    PRIMARY KEY (event_id, kind)
) PARTITION BY LIST (kind);

CREATE TABLE events_click  PARTITION OF events FOR VALUES IN ('click', 'impression');
CREATE TABLE events_purchase PARTITION OF events FOR VALUES IN ('purchase', 'refund');

-- HASH partitioning: a fixed number of equally sized buckets, chosen at creation time.
CREATE TABLE sessions (
    session_id  uuid   PRIMARY KEY,
    account_id  bigint NOT NULL,
    started_at  timestamptz NOT NULL DEFAULT now(),
    PRIMARY KEY (session_id, account_id)   -- still must carry the key
) PARTITION BY HASH (account_id);

CREATE TABLE sessions_h0 PARTITION OF sessions FOR VALUES WITH (MODULUS 8, REMAINDER 0);
CREATE TABLE sessions_h1 PARTITION OF sessions FOR VALUES WITH (MODULUS 8, REMAINDER 1);

-- LIST partitions reject NULL by sending it to DEFAULT; the DEFAULT partition accepts it.
INSERT INTO events (tenant_id, kind, payload) VALUES (14, NULL, '{}');

Two boundary rules deserve emphasis because they surface as production incidents. First, a LIST partition’s I list does not match NULL; a nullable routing key therefore sends every NULL to the DEFAULT partition, which quietly turns into an unbounded hot partition. Declare routing columns NOT NULLNOT NULL. Second, a HASH partition count is fixed at creation; changing MOD requires detaching and re-partitioning the entire tree, so pick a modulus that comfortably exceeds the expected peak and never plan to change it.

Time-zone drift. Bounds written as '2025-03-01''2025-03-01' are resolved in the session time zone at DDL execution time, but rows are compared against the stored timestamptz in UTC. Always attach an explicit offset to every bound literal in a partitioned time-series schema, and never run partition DDL from a session whose TimeZone differs from the one used to create the table.

-- The DEFAULT partition is a correctness dependency, not a convenience.
DROP TABLE orders_2025m01;   -- assume a manual detach/drop, not DROP TABLE

INSERT INTO orders (customer_id, placed_at, status, total_cents)
VALUES (77, '2025-01-05 12:00:00+00', 'new', 100);

ERROR:  no partition of relation "orders" found for row
DETAIL:  Partition key of the failing row contains (placed_at) = (2025-01-05 12:00:00+00).
HINT:  Some partitions may be implicitly defined at this point, but
HINT:  they do not match this row; perhaps one of them is a DEFAULT partition.

That ERROR is the expected behaviour and it is arguably a feature: a partitionless key is an error rather than a row in a table you forgot about. But the recovery is not free, because INSERT ... SELECTINSERT ... SELECT from a source table fails as a whole statement, and the usual fix — ALTER TABLE ... ATTACH PARTITION ... DEFAULTALTER TABLE ... ATTACH PARTITION ... DEFAULT — takes an ACCESS EXCLUSIVE lock on the partitioned table. Any table with a DEFAULT partition should be monitored for that partition’s size; sustained growth is the signal that future periods were never created.

7.2 Partition routing and pruning

Describes how the planner converts a partition tree into an App node over a subset of leaves, and the runtime and planning-time mechanisms that make that subset small.

Routing and pruning are two halves of the same mechanism. Routing is what happens when a row is written: the executor computes the partition key, evaluates the bounds of each partition in key order, and inserts into the single matching leaf. Pruning is what happens when a query is planned: the planner removes from the Append tree every leaf that cannot contain a matching row. Pruning therefore only ever helps read performance and load distribution; it does nothing for the write path, which must always locate the target leaf even if only one exists.

Partition pruning happens at two times. Planning-time pruning discards partitions whose bounds cannot overlap with any constant or externally-known value in the query. Execution-time (runtime) pruning is performed while the Append node initialises its child nodes, using values that only become known during execution — most importantly parameters of a prepared statement or a nested-loop inner relation. Runtime pruning is what makes partition pruning work under a generic plan in the extended query protocol; without it, a prepared statement would scan all partitions.

-- The authoritative view of the partition tree, with level and bound text.
postgres=# SELECT p.relname AS partition,
       pg_get_expr(c.relpartbound, c.oid) AS bound,
       pg_get_partkeydef(p.oid) IS NOT NULL AS is_partitioned,
       c.reltuples::bigint AS est_rows,
       pg_size_pretty(pg_total_relation_size(c.oid)) AS total_size
FROM pg_inherits i
JOIN pg_class c ON c.oid = i.inhrelid
JOIN pg_class p ON p.oid = i.inhparent
WHERE p.relname = 'orders'
ORDER BY 1;

     partition      |            bound             | is_partitioned | est_rows | total_size
-------------------+-------------------------------+---------------+----------+-----------
 orders_2025m01   | FOR VALUES FROM ('2025-01-01   | f             |    418022 | 214 MB
                   | 00:00:00+00') TO ...          |               |          |
 orders_2025m02   | FOR VALUES FROM ('2025-02-01   | f             |    389140 | 201 MB
                   | 00:00:00+00') TO ...          |               |          |
 orders_default   | DEFAULT                       | f             |       37 | 32 kB

-- pg_partition_tree shows the whole tree including sub-partitions, with level.
postgres=# SELECT relid::regclass AS relation, level, isleaf, pg_get_expr(bound, relid) AS bound
FROM pg_partition_tree('orders'::regclass)
ORDER BY level, 1;
