A useful production discipline is to treat partition size and row count as a first-class metric, because a tree with a thousand partitions of four rows each costs more in planning and catalog time than it saves. The planning overhead of a partitioned table scales with the number of leaves and is not negligible above a few hundred; when it becomes material, sub-partitioning to a HASH strategy inside each month keeps the total leaf count bounded by months times modulus rather than growing without limit.

-- Only one leaf survives pruning: the Append node has exactly one child.
postgres=# EXPLAIN (ANALYZE, BUFFERS)
SELECT sum(total_cents) FROM orders
WHERE placed_at >= '2025-02-03' AND placed_at < '2025-02-20';

 Append  (cost=0.42..24518.77 rows=512 width=8) (actual time=0.031..18044.912 rows=189344 loops=1)
   ->  Seq Scan on orders_2025m02 orders_2025m02  (cost=0.00..24518.00 rows=512 width=8)
         (actual time=0.030..18041.223 rows=189344 loops=1)
         Filter: (placed_at >= '2025-02-03 00:00:00+00'::timestamptz AND
                  placed_at < '2025-02-20 00:00:00+00'::timestamptz)
         Rows Removed by Filter: 199796
         Buffers: shared hit=188 read=2104
 Planning Time: 0.412 ms
 Execution Time: 18052.774 ms

Note the residual filter inside the surviving leaf. Partition pruning reduces the number of relations scanned, never the number of rows scanned within a leaf; a coarse partition key with fine-grained queries still pays the full per-row cost. This is the basis for the common advice to keep partitions small enough that a sequential scan of one leaf is itself cheap — roughly, under a few hundred thousand rows and a few hundred megabytes.

Two GUCs extend pruning into the join and aggregate phases. enable_parti allows the planner to consider partition-by-partition joins when both sides of a join are identically partitioned, which replaces one large hash of the entire dataset with many smaller ones. enable_partitio allows a GROUP BY whose grouping key is compatible with the partition key to be computed per partition and combined, again converting memory-resident work into partition-sized chunks. Both are off by default because they can only help when the partitioning actually aligns with the query, and a misaligned partition key makes them unusable.

-- Partition-wise aggregate: GroupAggregate per partition plus a final Aggregate.
postgres=# SET enable_partitionwise_aggregate = on;
postgres=# EXPLAIN (ANALYZE, BUFFERS)
SELECT customer_id, count(*) FROM orders GROUP BY customer_id;

 Finalize GroupAggregate  (cost=65110.40..65210.40 rows=200 width=16)
   ->  Gather Merge  (cost=65110.40..67743.55 rows=600 width=16)
         ->  Partial GroupAggregate  (cost=65010.40..65310.40 rows=600 width=16)
               ->  Parallel Append  (cost=0.00..64210.40 rows=900 width=16)
                     ->  Parallel Seq Scan on orders_2025m01 ...
                     ->  Parallel Seq Scan on orders_2025m02 ...
                     ->  Parallel Seq Scan on orders_default ...

-- Memory: work_mem is applied per partition, not per query, when partition-wise runs.
postgres=# SELECT name, setting, unit, short_desc FROM pg_settings
WHERE name IN ('enable_partition_pruning', 'enable_partitionwise_join',
                 'enable_partitionwise_aggregate', 'constraint_exclusion')
ORDER BY name;

             name              | setting | short_desc
------------------------------+---------+--------------------------------------
 constraint_exclusion         | partition| on = check all constraints...
 enable_partition_pruning     | on      | allow planner to remove irrelevant partitions
 enable_partitionwise_aggregate| off     | enable partition-wise aggregation
 enable_partitionwise_join    | off     | enable partition-wise join

Enable narrowly. Set enable_partitio and enable_parti on the specific reporting roles or sessions that run aggregate and join workloads, rather than globally in postgresql.conf. Both are safe defaults-off precisely because their benefit depends on query shape; a session-level enable lets you prove the win with EXPLAIN (ANALYZE) before committing.

7.3 Partition maintenance and lifecycle

Covers attaching and detaching partitions without long locks, the two-staging pattern for zero-downtime migrations, and sub-partitioning for index-size control.

Every partition lifecycle operation acquires an ACCESS EXCLUSIVE lock on the partitioned table, because the operation changes the mapping between logical and physical rows. The difference between an outage and a blip is entirely in how much work happens before the lock is taken. CREATE TABLE ... PARTITION OFCREATE TABLE ... PARTITION OF scans the new partition to validate its contents against the bounds and, in older releases, was executed as a single long transaction; the two-staging pattern below exists to reduce the window to the milliseconds required to take a lock and rewrite a partition’s metadata.

The pattern is: create the partition as a standalone table (no lock on the parent), load and index it at leisure, then issue ALTER TABLE parent ATTACH PARTITION child FOR VALUES ...ALTER TABLE parent ATTACH PARTITION child FOR VALUES .... The attach itself still requires ACCESS EXCLUSIVE on the parent, but it does not need to scan the child if the child carries a constraint that the server can prove implies the partition bounds — for example a CHECK clause matching the range exactly. Without such a constraint the attach performs a full sequential scan of the new partition while holding the lock, which for a large backfill is unacceptable.

-- Stage 1: create and populate outside any lock on the parent.
CREATE TABLE orders_2025m03 (LIKE orders INCLUDING DEFAULTS INCLUDING IDENTITY);
INSERT INTO orders_2025m03
SELECT * FROM legacy_orders WHERE placed_at >= '2025-03-01 00:00+00'
                                 AND placed_at <  '2025-04-01 00:00+00';

-- A constraint that implies the bounds lets ATTACH skip the validation scan.
ALTER TABLE orders_2025m03 ADD CONSTRAINT orders_2025m03_bounds_ck
    CHECK (placed_at >= '2025-03-01 00:00:00+00' AND placed_at < '2025-04-01 00:00:00+00');
CREATE INDEX orders_2025m03_customer_idx ON orders_2025m03 (customer_id, placed_at);

-- Stage 2: brief ACCESS EXCLUSIVE window. Set a short lock_timeout first.
BEGIN;
SET LOCAL lock_timeout = '3s';
ALTER TABLE orders ATTACH PARTITION orders_2025m03
    FOR VALUES FROM ('2025-03-01 00:00:00+00') TO ('2025-04-01 00:00:00+00');
COMMIT;

Setting lock_t locally before the attach is not optional practice. Without it, the statement queues behind whatever long-running transaction is holding a conflicting lock, and because every subsequent statement in the same connection queues behind it, a single maintenance command can turn into a full connection-level stall. With a timeout, the attach either succeeds immediately or fails cleanly and is retried.

Retention. DROP TABLE partitionDROP TABLE partition is fast but crude: it takes an ACCESS EXCLUSIVE lock and takes it while the drop is queued behind all existing readers of that partition. DETACH PARTITION ... CONCURRENTLYDETACH PARTITION ... CONCURRENTLY takes only SHARE UPDATE EXCLUSIVE, which does not block reads or writes, at the cost of a two-step protocol whose second half must be run later and which cannot run inside a transaction block. Prefer detaching, then dropping the detached table asynchronously.

-- Fast detach: cannot run inside BEGIN/COMMIT, cannot be combined with FINALIZE.
ALTER TABLE orders DETACH PARTITION orders_2024m11 CONCURRENTLY;
-- After all traffic against the partition has drained, finish the operation:
ALTER TABLE orders DETACH PARTITION orders_2024m11 FINALIZE;

-- Routing new rows to a landing partition during a migration.
CREATE TABLE orders_2026m01 (LIKE orders);
ALTER TABLE orders ATTACH PARTITION orders_2026m01 DEFAULT;

-- Rows for March 2026 now land in orders_2026m01 with no error and no gap.
INSERT INTO orders (customer_id, placed_at, status, total_cents)
VALUES (91, now(), 'new', 2500);

-- The real partition cannot be attached while a DEFAULT exists and holds rows.
CREATE TABLE orders_2026m03 (LIKE orders);
ALTER TABLE orders_2026m03 ADD CONSTRAINT bnd
    CHECK (placed_at >= '2026-03-01' AND placed_at < '2026-04-01');
ALTER TABLE orders ATTACH PARTITION orders_2026m03
    FOR VALUES FROM ('2026-03-01 00:00+00') TO ('2026-04-01 00:00+00');
ERROR:  updated partition constraint for default partition "orders_default"
      would be violated by some row

That final error is the correct and important behaviour: the server refuses to attach a real partition while rows that belong to it are sitting in the DEFAULT partition. The remedy is to move the offending rows out first — detach the default, migrate the rows, attach the real partition, then re-attach the default — or to accept that they stay and let the attach fail until the window in which those rows exist is closed. Attempting to work around it by dropping the DEFAULT partition leaves the table with no catch-all and reintroduces the hard-error failure mode of 7.1.

Sub-partitioning solves the problem of a single large partition and is worth the extra leaf count when one period’s data exceeds the size at which a sequential scan of a leaf stops being acceptable.

-- Month on the outer level, 8 hash buckets inside each month: 24 leaves per year.
CREATE TABLE orders_2025m04 PARTITION OF orders
    FOR VALUES FROM ('2025-04-01 00:00+00') TO ('2025-05-01 00:00+00')
    PARTITION BY HASH (order_id);

CREATE TABLE orders_2025m04_p0 PARTITION OF orders_2025m04
    FOR VALUES WITH (MODULUS 8, REMAINDER 0);
CREATE TABLE orders_2025m04_p1 PARTITION OF orders_2025m04
    FOR VALUES WITH (MODULUS 8, REMAINDER 1);
-- ... p2 through p7

-- In practice this is driven by pg_partman, not by hand.
CREATE EXTENSION IF NOT EXISTS pg_partman;
SELECT * FROM partman.show_partitioned_table AS p
WHERE p.part_table = 'orders'::regclass;

 part_table | partition_interval | premake | retention | retention_keep_table
------------+--------------------+--------+-----------+---------------------
 orders     | 1 month             | 7      | 6 months  | t

Retaining a dropped partition as an empty table (retention_keep_table in pg_partman) is a deliberate operational choice: it preserves the table’s grants and lets a mistaken drop be reversed with an attach, at the cost of the empty relation and its indexes still occupying catalog slots. Whether that trade is acceptable is a decision about how often partitions are dropped and how quickly an operator may need to undo one.

7.4 Table inheritance

Distinguishes CHECK-constrained inheritance from declarative partitioning and explains why inheritance remains the right tool for a small number of heterogeneous children.

Inheritance predates declarative partitioning and solves a different problem. A child declared with INHE copies the parent’s column list and inherits its CHECK and NOT NULL constraints, but nothing forces the child’s rows to satisfy those constraints beyond what its own definition enforces, and — crucially — nothing routes rows. A query against the parent is rewritten by the planner into a union over the parent and every descendant, so a child that violates its parent’s CHECK predicate simply contributes rows the parent query had no reason to expect. That absence of enforcement, not the query rewrite, is the practical reason inheritance is not a partitioning mechanism.

What inheritance does offer is schema evolution of an existing hierarchy: ALTER TABLE parent ADD COLUMNALTER TABLE parent ADD COLUMN recurses to all children, adding the column to the physical storage of every one of them. Where that is worth an ACCESS EXCLUSIVE sweep over the whole hierarchy depends entirely on how many children exist and how wide they are. It also offers ONLY semantics for commands, and NO INHERIT on a CHECK constraint to keep a constraint local to a parent that is used as a base type.

-- Inheritance: children share a column list, constrained only by CHECK.
CREATE TABLE measurement (
    sensor_id   bigint      NOT NULL,
    recorded_at timestamptz NOT NULL,
    value       double precision NOT NULL,
    PRIMARY KEY (sensor_id, recorded_at)
);

CREATE TABLE measurement_temperature (
    celsius double precision,
    CONSTRAINT measurement_temperature_ck CHECK (celsius > -273.15 AND celsius < 2000)
) INHERITS (measurement);

CREATE TABLE measurement_pressure INHERITS (measurement);

-- Reading the parent expands to the parent plus every descendant, at any depth.
postgres=# EXPLAIN
SELECT * FROM measurement WHERE recorded_at > now() - INTERVAL '1 day';
Append  (cost=0.00..1200.00 rows=200 width=40)
  ->  Seq Scan on measurement  (cost=0.00..500.00 rows=100 width=40)
  ->  Seq Scan on measurement_pressure measurement_1  (cost=0.00..500.00 rows=100 width=40)
  ->  Seq Scan on measurement_temperature measurement_2  (cost=0.00..500.00 rows=100 width=40)

-- ONLY restricts a command to the named table.
postgres=# SELECT * FROM ONLY measurement;
Seq Scan on measurement  (cost=0.00..500.00 rows=100 width=40)

Not a partition key. Because a LIST partition enforces membership and an inheritance child does not, a child table can be empty, can hold rows outside its parent’s predicate, and can hold rows that a later query against the parent would have to filter. If the intent is horizontal scaling by a key, use declarative partitioning; if the intent is a heterogeneous extension of a base type with a small, stable number of children, inheritance is the appropriate tool.

The catalog exposes the hierarchy directly in pg_inherits, and the inheritance tree is not limited to a single parent — a table may inherit from several — although overlapping column types then have to be merge-compatible. Dynamic alteration of the link is possible with ALTER TABLE ... NO INHERITSALTER TABLE ... NO INHERITS and ALTER TABLE ... INHERITSALTER TABLE ... INHERITS, which is why the older documented technique of implementing partitioning by repeatedly attaching and detaching children is possible at all.

7.5 Partitioned tables as objects

Explains what a partitioned table is in the catalogs, how partitioned unique constraints and foreign keys are validated, and why identity sequences on a partitioned table are a single shared object.

A partitioned table has a catalog row in pg_class with a non-null relkind of 'p' and zero pages. Its partition key, strategy and expression list live in pg_partitioned_table, joined by partrelid; its leaves are ordinary 'r' relations reachable through pg_inherits exactly as inheritance children are. Because the two features share catalog machinery, tooling must check relkind rather than assume that "has children" means "is partitioned".

Constraints on partitioned tables are validated across the whole tree, which is why adding one can be expensive and why NOT VALID matters more here than on ordinary tables. A unique or primary key constraint on a partitioned table creates an index on every leaf and validates the constraint in every leaf within the same transaction. Adding a CHECK or NOT NULL constraint requires scanning every leaf, and in PostgreSQL 18 the NOT NULL revalidation is one of the operations the new nullability work makes cheaper, but the scan is still a scan.

-- The partition key, verbatim from the catalog.
postgres=# SELECT partrelid::regclass AS parent, partstrat, partnatts,
       pg_get_partkeydef(partrelid) AS definition
FROM pg_partitioned_table;

    parent    | partstrat | partnatts | definition
--------------+----------+-----------+---------------------------
 orders      | r        |         1 | RANGE (placed_at)
 events      | l        |         1 | LIST (kind)
 sessions    | h        |         1 | HASH (account_id)

-- Identity sequences are declared on the parent and are NOT per partition.
postgres=# SELECT pg_get_serial_sequence('orders', 'order_id');
      pg_get_serial_sequence
-------------------------------
 public.orders_order_id_seq

-- Adding a constraint across every leaf. NOT VALID skips the scan; validate later.
ALTER TABLE orders ADD CONSTRAINT total_positive_ck CHECK (total_cents > 0) NOT VALID;
ALTER TABLE orders VALIDATE CONSTRAINT total_positive_ck;  -- SHARE UPDATE EXCLUSIVE only

-- A foreign key may reference a partitioned table since PostgreSQL 12.
ALTER TABLE shipments ADD CONSTRAINT shipments_order_fk
    FOREIGN KEY (order_id, order_placed_at) REFERENCES orders (order_id, placed_at);

Sequences are shared, not per partition. orde on a RANGE-partitioned table comes from one sequence on the parent. That is correct for uniqueness but wrong for locality: all inserts contend on a single sequence relation, and sequence cache loss on failover produces gaps that are proportional to the connection count. If insert throughput on the hot partition is the limiting factor, prefer an application-supplied key — for example uuidv7() in PostgreSQL 18, which is monotonically ordered enough to index well — over tuning sequence_cachesequence_cache.

-- One view that reports the shape of every partitioned table in the cluster.
CREATE VIEW admin.partition_health AS
SELECT n.nspname                                        AS schema,
       c.relname                                        AS parent_table,
       pg_get_partkeydef(c.oid)                          AS partition_key,
       count(*)                                         AS leaves,
       sum(cnt.est_rows)::bigint                        AS est_rows,
       pg_size_pretty(sum(cnt.total_bytes)::bigint)      AS total_size,
       max(cnt.total_bytes)::bigint                      AS largest_leaf_bytes
FROM pg_class c
JOIN pg_namespace n ON n.oid = c.relnamespace
JOIN pg_inherits i      ON i.inhparent = c.oid
JOIN pg_class leaf      ON leaf.oid = i.inhrelid
CROSS JOIN LATERAL (SELECT leaf.reltuples AS est_rows,
                             pg_total_relation_size(leaf.oid) AS total_bytes) cnt
WHERE c.relkind = 'p'
GROUP BY 1, 2, 3;

-- Leaf-size spread is the metric that tells you whether the key is well chosen.
postgres=# SELECT parent_table, partition_key, leaves,
       pg_size_pretty(largest_leaf_bytes) AS largest,
       round(largest_leaf_bytes / (est_rows / leaves)) AS skew
FROM admin.partition_health ORDER BY largest_leaf_bytes DESC LIMIT 5;

7.6 Pruning in practice

Demonstrates the observable effects of pruning at plan and execution time, the settings that disable it, and the cases where pruning fails to engage.

Pruning failures are usually diagnosed by comparing an EXPLAIN with pruning enabled against one with it disabled, and reading the Subplans Removed counter that the planner reports for the Append node. When the number of subplans matches the number of leaves, pruning did not engage; the reasons are almost always a partition key wrapped in an expression the planner cannot match against bounds, a parameter used as a prepared-statement generic-plan value, a misaligned sub-partition key, or an unanalysed partition whose relpages and reltuples are both zero.

That last case is the one that surprises operators most often. A freshly created or freshly attached partition has no statistics until ANALYZE runs, and until then the planner estimates it at a single page and a handful of rows. Pruning still works — pruning depends on bounds, not statistics — but costing becomes wildly wrong and a good index on a new partition may be judged not worth using. Autovacuum analyses new tables automatically; COPYCOPY-loaded staging partitions in a migration script do not get that benefit until you ask.

-- Append reports how many subplans the planner eliminated at plan time.
postgres=# EXPLAIN
SELECT * FROM orders WHERE status = 'shipped' AND placed_at >= '2025-01-01';

 Append  (cost=0.00..29455.00 rows=1200 width=64) (actual rows=...)
   ->  Seq Scan on orders_2025m01 ...
   ->  Seq Scan on orders_2025m02 ...
   ->  Seq Scan on orders_default ...
 Planning Time: 0.318 ms
-- Status has no partition affinity here: all three leaves survive. Correct behaviour.

-- Compare with pruning disabled to see the baseline cost.
postgres=# SET enable_partition_pruning = off;
postgres=# EXPLAIN (ANALYZE)
SELECT count(*) FROM orders
WHERE placed_at >= '2025-02-01' AND placed_at < '2025-02-08';

 Append  (cost=0.00..39210.55 rows=256 width=0) (actual time=... rows=42110 loops=1)
   ->  Seq Scan on orders_2025m01  (actual rows=407221 loops=1)
   ->  Seq Scan on orders_2025m02  (actual rows=189344 loops=1)
   ->  Seq Scan on orders_default  (actual rows=37 loops=1)
 Planning Time: 0.402 ms
 Execution Time: 5118.220 ms

postgres=# RESET enable_partition_pruning;
-- Runtime pruning under a prepared statement: subplans are dropped after execution starts.
postgres=# PREPARE monthly (timestamptz) AS
          SELECT count(*), sum(total_cents) FROM orders
          WHERE placed_at >= $1 AND placed_at < $1 + INTERVAL '1 month';
postgres=# EXPLAIN (ANALYZE) EXECUTE monthly('2025-02-01 00:00+00');

 Append  (cost=0.00..24518.00 rows=1024 width=16) (actual time=0.031..18044.9 rows=1 loops=1)
   ->  Seq Scan on orders_2025m02  (actual time=0.030..18041.2 rows=189344 loops=1)
 Planning: Subplans Removed: 2
 Execution: Subplans Removed: 2
 Execution Time: 18052.774 ms

-- Statistics that matter for partitioning specifically.
postgres=# ALTER TABLE orders ALTER COLUMN placed_at SET STATISTICS 500;
ALTER TABLE orders ALTER COLUMN status     SET STATISTICS 1000;
postgres=# ANALYZE orders;   -- cascades to every leaf, including DEFAULT

-- Bound metadata the planner uses, straight from pg_stats on a partition key.
postgres=# SELECT attname, n_distinct, most_common_vals, histogram_bounds IS NOT NULL AS has_histogram
FROM pg_stats WHERE schemaname = 'public' AND tablename = 'orders_2025m02'
  AND attname = 'placed_at';

Verify, do not assume. Add (ANALYZE, BUFFERS)(ANALYZE, BUFFERS) to every EXPLAIN you use to justify a partitioning decision and record the number of leaves scanned alongside the buffers read. A plan that touches one partition but reads 200 MB of heap is telling you the partition key is right and the indexes are wrong — a different fix from the opposite case.

Exercises 7

  1. Build a RANGE-partitioned eve table on a timestamptz key with one partition per month for a quarter plus a DEFAULT partition, then prove the bound semantics by inserting rows at exactly '2025-04-01 00:00:00+00''2025-04-01 00:00:00+00', one microsecond before it, and with a NULL key; record which partition each row lands in using tableoid::regclasstableoid::regclass.
  2. Demonstrate the missing-partition failure: detach a month so its key range is uncovered, capture the exact ERROR and HINT text for a failing INSERT, then re-attach and confirm the statement succeeds. Submit the console transcript, not a paraphrase.
  3. Convert a 50 GB unpartitioned table to a range-partitioned one using the two-staging pattern. Record the wall-clock duration of the ATTACH PARTITION itself (not the load) and prove it stayed under one second by wrapping it with SET LOCAL lock_timeoutSET LOCAL lock_timeout and capturing pg_l from a second session during the attach.
  4. Measure pruning. For ten representative queries against the partitioned table, capture EXPLAIN (ANALYZE, BUFFERS) with enable_parti on and off, and produce a table of leaves scanned, execution time, and shared block reads. Identify at least one query where pruning fails to engage and explain why in terms of the partition key expression.
  5. Build a hierarchy of 12 inheritance children with heterogeneous columns and demonstrate the four differences that rule inheritance out as a partitioning mechanism: no routing, per-child CHECK enforcement, non-inherited unique indexes, and query-time expansion. Convert the same data to declarative partitioning and record the EXPLAIN difference for a parent-table aggregate.

8 · Extensions, Types and Procedural Programming

8.1 Extensions and the extension catalog8.2 Composite types8.3 Domains8.4 Enums8.5 PL/pgSQL8.6 SQL functions and dynamic SQL

PostgreSQL’s extensibility is a catalog contract rather than a plugin API: an extension is a control file, a SQL script, and optional shared libraries, and installing one is a single CREATE EXTENSIONCREATE EXTENSION that may run as a non-superuser when the extension is marked trusted. Alongside extensions, the server offers four user-definable type constructors — composite, domain, enum, and the base types created only in C — and two procedural languages that matter operationally. The engineering discipline this chapter insists on is versioning: every object created by an extension or a function body is an ordinary catalog object that pg_ serialises as text, so a restore into a cluster whose extension is a different version silently changes behaviour unless the version is pinned and asserted. The other recurring theme is privilege: functions default to invoker rights with the caller’s searc, which makes an unqualified call inside a function a security defect rather than a style question.

8.1 Extensions and the extension catalog

Explains how extension control files map to catalog objects, how to assert the installed version, and which extensions carry operational consequences.

Every extension ships a control file declaring its name, default version, relocatability, schema and whether it is trusted. CREATE EXTENSIONCREATE EXTENSION looks the file up by name, runs the SQL script for the chosen version, and records the result in pg_extension; the objects it creates are ordinary tables, types, functions and operators that happen to be marked as belonging to the extension for the benefit of ALTER EXTENSION ... DROPALTER EXTENSION ... DROP and pg_dumppg_dump. A trusted extension may be installed by a database owner without superuser rights, which is the mechanism that lets managed PostgreSQL services allow pg_trgm or citext but not arbitrary C code.

-- What the server could install, and at which versions.
postgres=# SELECT name, default_version, installed_version,
       trusted, relocatable, schema, comment
FROM pg_available_extensions ORDER BY name LIMIT 8;

     name     | default_version | installed_version | trusted | relocatable | schema
-------------+-----------------+-------------------+---------+--------------+--------
 btree_gin   | 1.3             |                   | f       | t            |
 citext      | 1.6             | 1.6               | t       | t            | public
 hstore      | 1.8             | 1.8               | t       | t            | public
 intarray    | 1.5             |                   | f       | f            |
 jsonb_plaintext| 1.0           |                   | f       | f            |
 lo          | 1.1             |                   | f       | t            |
 ltree       | 1.3             | 1.3               | t       | t            | public
 pgcrypto    | 1.3             | 1.3               | t       | t            | public

-- Assert the version in the deploy script; never rely on default_version.
postgres=# SELECT extname, extversion, extnamespace::regnamespace AS schema
FROM pg_extension ORDER BY 1;

   extname   | extversion | schema
-------------+------------+---------
 citext      | 1.6        | public
 hstore      | 1.8        | public
 ltree       | 1.3        | public
 pgcrypto    | 1.3        | public
 plpgsql     | 1.0        | pg_catalog

-- Install into a dedicated schema, never public, then pin explicitly.
CREATE EXTENSION pgcrypto WITH SCHEMA extensions VERSION '1.3';

Dump behaviour. pg_ emits CREATE EXTENSION IF NOT EXISTSCREATE EXTENSION IF NOT EXISTS without a version clause and then dumps the extension’s objects as ordinary catalog DDL. Restoring into a cluster where a newer version is the default does not fail; it produces a cluster whose objects came from the dump and whose extension bookkeeping says the newer version. Assert extve after restore rather than assuming the versions matched.

-- Version drift detection across a fleet.
SELECT current_setting('server_version') AS server_version, e.extname, e.extversion,
       a.default_version,
       (a.default_version <> e.extversion) AS behind_default
FROM pg_extension e
JOIN pg_available_extensions a ON a.name = e.extname
WHERE e.extname NOT IN ('plpgsql')
ORDER BY behind_default DESC, e.extname;

-- Upgrade in place, then re-assert. Some extensions need REINDEX of their indexes.
ALTER EXTENSION pg_trgm UPDATE TO '1.7';
SELECT extname, extversion FROM pg_extension WHERE extname = 'pg_trgm';

-- Objects an extension owns, with their kinds.
postgres=# SELECT d.classid::regclass AS catalog, a.attname
FROM pg_depend d JOIN pg_extension e ON e.oid = d.refobjid
JOIN pg_attribute a ON a.attrelid = d.objid
WHERE e.extname = 'hstore'
LIMIT 5;

-- pgcrypto: prefer the built-in uuidv7() in PostgreSQL 18 for ordered keys.
postgres=# SELECT gen_random_uuid()::text          AS v4,
                 uuidv7()::text                  AS v7,
                 encode(digest('tenant-42', 'sha256'), 'hex') AS hmac;
                  v4                                  |                v7
--------------------------------------+------------------------------------
 d1f4a3b0-9d21-4a1c-b6b4-9d1f4a3b09d2 | 0192f3e1-8a4b-7c1e-9a55-6d0b2f4e8c11
(1 row)

The three text-search extensions are worth treating as a unit because they compose. pg_trgm supplies similarity scoring and a GIN index over trigrams that turns LIKE '%term%', ILIKE and % similarity operators from sequential scans into index scans; unaccent supplies a dictionary that folds diacritics so unaccent is a non-deterministic function with no IMMUTABLE marker and therefore cannot be indexed directly without a functional index; and citext supplies a case-insensitive text type with an equality operator that is genuinely indexable rather than a lower() convention applied inconsistently.

CREATE EXTENSION IF NOT EXISTS pg_trgm WITH SCHEMA extensions;
CREATE EXTENSION IF NOT EXISTS unaccent WITH SCHEMA extensions;

-- Trigram GIN: supports LIKE '%..%', ILIKE, similarity() and word_similarity().
CREATE INDEX customers_name_trgm_idx ON customers
    USING GIN (name gin_trgm_ops);

postgres=# EXPLAIN
SELECT customer_id, name, similarity(name, 'Jon Smyth') AS score
FROM customers
WHERE name ILIKE '%smth%' ORDER BY score DESC LIMIT 10;

-- Index                        Bitmap Index Scan on customers_name_trgm_idx
--   Recheck Cond: (name ~~* '%smth%')      Rows: 6  Buffers: shared hit=91

-- Case-insensitive equality, with a real operator rather than lower() convention.
CREATE EXTENSION IF NOT EXISTS citext WITH SCHEMA extensions;
ALTER TABLE customers ALTER COLUMN email TYPE citext;
CREATE UNIQUE INDEX customers_email_uniq ON customers (email);

-- unaccent is STABLE, not IMMUTABLE: index it through an explicit wrapper.
CREATE FUNCTION extensions.f_norm(text) RETURNS text
LANGUAGE sql IMMUTABLE STRICT PARALLEL SAFE AS
SELECT unaccent('public.unaccent', $1);

-- Hierarchical labels, enforced by ltree's own cast validation.
CREATE EXTENSION IF NOT EXISTS ltree WITH SCHEMA extensions;
CREATE TABLE org_units (
    unit_path  ltree PRIMARY KEY,
    unit_name  text NOT NULL,
    manager_id bigint REFERENCES org_units (unit_path)
) PARTITION BY LIST (left(unit_path, 1));

CREATE INDEX org_units_path_gist ON org_units USING GIST (unit_path);

Contention and immutability. A GIN trigram index over a low-cardinality column produces a posting list that is effectively the whole table, so trigram indexes that look cheap in a staging environment can behave as table scans in production. Separately, marking a wrapper around unaccent as IMMUTABLE is a promise about the dictionary file’s contents; if the dictionary is replaced by ALTER EXTENSION ... UPDATE or by a change of unaccent_rules_file, every index built on that function is silently stale.

The choice between hstore and jsonb is not a preference but a set of measurable differences, and the decision should be made per column rather than per database.

Dimensionhstorejsonb
Value modelFlat text → text map onlyArbitrary JSON, nested arrays and objects
OrderingUnordered; output is normalised and not stableDuplicate keys retained, last value wins on lookup, binary form preserves key order
Numeric handlingEverything is text; '7''7' and '7.0''7.0' are distinctNative num, no loss of precision or exponent form
Indexable operatorsFull GiST/GIN over the whole map plus per-key @> containmentDefault GIN on json; expression indexes on (data->>'k')(data->>'k') needed for scalar lookups
Typing disciplineNone; wrong types are undetectableParsed and validated on write; malformed input is rejected
Operational profileSmaller per key, cheaper @> containmentStandard-compliant, interoperable, larger per key

PostgreSQL 18 additionally allows stored generated columns to be logically replicated and adds OLD/NEW qualifiers in RETURNING; both matter for extensions and CDC consumers that previously had to recompute derived values subscriber-side. Virtual generated columns — the default in 18 — are computed on read, which makes them a natural fit for jsonb projections where the derived value is only occasionally read and never indexed.

8.2 Composite types

Covers user-defined composite types, the implicit row type of every table, and the boundary between a composite and a document column.

A composite type is a named row: an ordered set of named, typed attributes with exactly the semantics of an anonymous RECORD, but with a name that survives in the catalog, in function signatures and in casts. Every table also has an implicit composite type of the same name, so ord as a type is SELECT * FROM orders LIMIT 1 shaped. Declaring a composite explicitly is worthwhile when the type is passed between functions, when a function must accept either of two shapes, or when a table and its row type must be documented as one contract.

CREATE TYPE money_amount AS (
    amount_cents bigint        NOT NULL,
    currency     char(3)       NOT NULL,
    captured_at  timestamptz   DEFAULT now()
);

CREATE TABLE invoices (
    invoice_id  bigint GENERATED ALWAYS AS IDENTITY PRIMARY KEY,
    billed      money_amount NOT NULL,
    -- Field access, whole-row assignment, and expansion both work.
    CONSTRAINT billed_positive_ck CHECK ((billed).amount_cents > 0)
);

INSERT INTO invoices (billed)
VALUES (ROW(459900, 'EUR')::money_amount);

postgres=# SELECT (billed).currency, (billed).amount_cents, billed FROM invoices;
 currency | amount_cents |          billed
----------+--------------+--------------------------
 EUR      |       459900 | (459900,EUR,2026-01-08 09:12:44.118+00)

-- A function may accept several composite types, or anyrow.
CREATE FUNCTION billing.total(money_amount) RETURNS numeric
LANGUAGE sql IMMUTABLE AS $$ SELECT order_id FROM sales_order WHERE tenant_id = $1 $$;

Two operational properties follow from the composite being an ordinary row type. Equality is structural and not orderable by default, so a composite column cannot be the target of an ordered sort without an explicit operator, and a UNIQUE constraint over a whole composite needs a b-tree operator class that is not supplied by default. Comparison is possible on the individual attributes, which is almost always what is wanted, and it is the reason composite types remain useful as function arguments even though jsonb has become the default choice for stored documents.

8.3 Domains

Explains domains as reusable constraints over base types, their propagation semantics, and the catalog effects that make them a poor partitioning key.

A domain is a base type plus a named constraint set. Every column declared with the domain type inherits the domain’s NOT NULL, CHECK and DEFAULT behaviour, and the domain’s constraints are re-validated against existing rows whenever the domain is altered. Domains exist for reuse of a constraint that must mean the same thing in many tables; they are not an encapsulation mechanism and confer no privacy, since the underlying base type’s operators remain available to anyone with column privilege.

-- A domain is a base type plus constraints; it adds no storage and no dispatch cost.
CREATE DOMAIN email_address AS text
    NOT NULL
    CHECK (value ~ '^[^@[:space:]]+@[^@[:space:]]+\.[^@[:space:]]+$')
    CHECK (length(value) <= 254);

CREATE DOMAIN bcp47_tag AS text
    NOT NULL
    DEFAULT 'en'
    CHECK (value ~ '^[a-z]{2,3}(-[A-Za-z0-9]{2,8})*$');

CREATE TABLE subscribers (
    subscriber_id bigint GENERATED ALWAYS AS IDENTITY PRIMARY KEY,
    email         email_address NOT NULL,
    locale        bcp47_tag NOT NULL   -- default 'en' applies
);

-- Domain constraints live in pg_constraint against pg_type, not against each table.
postgres=# SELECT t.typname, con.conname, pg_get_constraintdef(con.oid) AS definition
FROM pg_constraint con JOIN pg_type t ON t.oid = con.contypid
WHERE t.typname IN ('email_address', 'bcp47_tag');

  typname      |    conname     |                definition
---------------+----------------+-----------------------------------------------
 email_address | email_address_check | CHECK ((value ~ '...'::text) AND ...)
 email_address | email_address_check1| CHECK ((length(value) <= 254))
 bcp47_tag     | bcp47_tag_check| CHECK ((value ~ '^[a-z]{2,3}'::text))

Domain checks are invisible to the planner’s statistics. ANA collects statistics for the underlying base type, not for the domain’s constraint, so a CHECK (value IN (...)) domain on a low-cardinality enumerated string is invisible to selectivity estimation. A domain is also not permitted as a partition key unless the domain’s base type is itself one of the permitted partition key types, and a domain over an array or a composite is never valid. Treat domains as documentation with teeth, not as a schema-enforcement layer.

Altering a domain is a whole-database operation. ALTER DOMAIN ... ADD CONSTRAINTALTER DOMAIN ... ADD CONSTRAINT validates against every column that uses the domain across every table in the database, taking ACCESS EXCLUSIVE on each. Add it NOT VALID and run VALIDATE CONSTRAINTVALIDATE CONSTRAINT separately, per table, so that the blocking window is bounded and the operation is restartable.

8.4 Enums

Documents the ordering semantics of enum labels, the transactional restriction on adding values, and the migration cost of a value that must be renamed or removed.

An enum type is an ordered list of labels. Ordering follows the order of declaration, not collation, which is the property that makes an enum useful as a sort key for workflow stages and dangerous as a sort key for user-facing text. The Btree and Hash operator classes for enums are sparse: an index is built over the declared labels and needs an ALTER TYPE ... ADD VALUE plus a rebuild before a newly added label is indexable on systems older than the reindex path introduced for hash indexes.

CREATE TYPE ticket_state AS ENUM ('new', 'triaged', 'in_progress', 'blocked', 'resolved', 'closed');

CREATE TABLE tickets (
    ticket_id bigint GENERATED ALWAYS AS IDENTITY PRIMARY KEY,
    state     ticket_state NOT NULL DEFAULT 'new',
    opened_at timestamptz NOT NULL DEFAULT now(),
    closed_at timestamptz
);

CREATE INDEX tickets_state_idx ON tickets (state);
CREATE INDEX tickets_state_open_idx ON tickets (state, opened_at DESC)
    WHERE closed_at IS NULL;

-- Ordering is by enum position, not by collation.
postgres=# SELECT state, count(*) FROM tickets GROUP BY 1 ORDER BY 1;
     state    | count
---------------+-------
 new          |  1204
 triaged      |   880
 in_progress  |   311

-- Postgres 16 and later allow the label to be added outside a transaction block.
ALTER TYPE ticket_state ADD VALUE IF NOT EXISTS 'wont_fix' BEFORE 'closed';
-- The new value cannot be used in the same transaction that added it.
--   ERROR:  unsafe use of new value "wont_fix" of enum type ticket_state
--   HINT:  New values must be committed before they can be used.

Adding a value is a lock, not a DDL no-op. ALTER TYPE ... ADD VALUEALTER TYPE ... ADD VALUE takes an ACCESS EXCLUSIVE lock on the enum type and blocks queries that read columns of that type while it runs, even though the operation itself is a catalog insert. Insert the label in a dedicated transaction, outside a migration transaction that will also do DDL, so that the lock window is milliseconds rather than seconds.

-- Renaming a label is metadata-only and preserves stored row versions.
ALTER TYPE ticket_state RENAME VALUE 'wont_fix' TO 'not_planned';

-- Removing a value is a full table rewrite of every dependent column.
ALTER TYPE ticket_state RENAME ticket_state_v1 TO ticket_state_v2;
-- create new type, migrate rows, swap names, drop old. Never DROP a value in place.

-- Mapping an enum to application text: the cast is the contract, not the label.
CREATE FUNCTION ticket_state_to_wire(ticket_state) RETURNS text
LANGUAGE sql IMMUTABLE PARALLEL SAFE AS
SELECT CASE $1::text
    WHEN 'new'         THEN RETURN 'NEW'
    WHEN 'triaged'     THEN RETURN 'TRIAGED'
    WHEN 'in_progress' THEN RETURN 'IN_PROGRESS'
    WHEN 'blocked'     THEN RETURN 'BLOCKED'
    WHEN 'resolved'    THEN RETURN 'RESOLVED'
    WHEN 'not_planned' THEN RETURN 'NOT_PLANNED'
    WHEN 'closed'      THEN RETURN 'CLOSED'
  END;

-- Filter on the enum directly: the cast to text defeats the index.
postgres=# EXPLAIN (ANALYZE)
SELECT count(*) FROM tickets WHERE state = 'in_progress';
Index Scan using tickets_state_idx on tickets  (rows=311)  Execution Time: 0.214 ms

8.5 PL/pgSQL

Sets out the structure of a PL/pgSQL routine — declaration block, statements, exception block — and the three facilities that carry most of the operational risk.

PL/pgSQL is an extension of SQL with a block structure: an optional declaration section, a sequence of statements, and an optional exception section that catches errors raised by any statement in the block. Its value is not that it can express what SQL cannot, but that it provides control flow, error trapping and multi-row result assembly without a client-side round trip per row. Its cost is that every routine is a compiled object whose plan cache behaviour is invisible unless it is inspected, and that a routine which loops row by row will be slower than the equivalent set-based SQL by orders of magnitude.

CREATE FUNCTION billing.reprice_invoice(
    p_invoice_id  bigint,
    p_currency    char(3),
    p_rate        numeric
) RETURNS numeric
LANGUAGE plpgsql
VOLATILE
SECURITY INVOKER
SET search_path = billing, pg_temp
AS $fn$
DECLARE
    v_lines  int;
    v_total  numeric;
    v_new    numeric;
BEGIN
    -- Lock the invoice rows first, in a deterministic order, to avoid deadlocks.
    SELECT count(*), coalesce(sum(line_total_cents), 0) / 100.0
      INTO v_lines, v_total
      FROM invoice_lines
     WHERE invoice_id = p_invoice_id
     ORDER BY invoice_id
     FOR UPDATE;

    IF v_lines = 0 THEN
        RAISE EXCEPTION 'invoice % has no lines', p_invoice_id
            USING ERRCODE = 'no_data_found', HINT = 'import lines before repricing';
    END IF;

    v_new := round(v_total * p_rate, 2);

    UPDATE invoices
       SET total_cents = (v_new * 100)::bigint,
           repriced_at = now(),
           repriced_by = current_user
     WHERE invoice_id = p_invoice_id;

    RAISE NOTICE 'repriced invoice %: % -> %', p_invoice_id, v_total, v_new;
    RETURN v_new;
EXCEPTION
    WHEN unique_violation THEN
        RAISE EXCEPTION 'invoice % already repriced', p_invoice_id
            USING ERRCODE = 'object_not_in_prerequisite_state';
    WHEN query_canceled THEN
        RAISE 'repricing of % cancelled by statement_timeout', p_invoice_id;
    END;
$fn$;

An exception block is a subtransaction boundary. Every statement inside a block with an EXCEPTION section runs in an implicit subtransaction, which means that INSERT/UPDATE/DELETE performed earlier in the same block are rolled back to the block’s savepoint when the handler fires. Code that "logs the failure and continues" using an exception block silently discards the partial work above it. When partial progress must survive, either structure the routine as several top-level calls or use an explicit savepoint pattern with the error handled by the caller.

-- RETURNS TABLE: output columns are OUT parameters; the body must assign or RETURN QUERY.
CREATE FUNCTION billing.top_customers(p_from date, p_to date, p_limit int DEFAULT 20)
RETURNS TABLE (
    customer_id bigint,
    full_name   text,
    gross_cents bigint,
    order_count bigint
)
LANGUAGE plpgsql STABLE PARALLEL SAFE
AS $fn$
BEGIN
    RETURN QUERY
    SELECT c.customer_id, c.full_name,
           sum(l.line_total_cents)::bigint, count(DISTINCT i.invoice_id)::bigint
      FROM invoices i
      JOIN invoice_lines l ON l.invoice_id = i.invoice_id
      JOIN customers c   ON c.customer_id = i.customer_id
     WHERE i.issued_at >= p_from AND i.issued_at < p_to
     GROUP BY 1, 2
     ORDER BY 3 DESC
     LIMIT p_limit;
END;
$fn$;

-- GET DIAGNOSTICS replaces the historical FOUND variable.
CREATE FUNCTION billing.archive_invoice(p_id bigint) RETURNS boolean
LANGUAGE plpgsql VOLATILE AS $fn$
DECLARE v_rows int; v_sqlstate text; v_msg text;
BEGIN
    UPDATE invoices SET archived_at = now() WHERE invoice_id = p_id;
    GET DIAGNOSTICS v_rows = ROW_COUNT;
    IF v_rows = 0 THEN
        GET STACKED DIAGNOSTICS v_msg = MESSAGE_TEXT;
        RAISE 'invoice % not archived (% rows)', p_id, v_rows;
        RETURN false;
    END IF;
    RETURN true;
END;
$fn$;

-- Counterfactual: a row-at-a-time loop over the same result set.
-- FOR r IN SELECT ... LOOP  PERFORM billing.reprice_invoice(r.id, 'EUR', 1.0); END LOOP;
-- One plan, one snapshot, but ~N round trips through the executor and per-row overhead.

Three plpgsql settings in postgresql.conf affect safety more than performance. plpgsql.ch and plpgsql.ext instrument ASSERT statements and common mistakes for development environments only. plpgsql.ex reports suspicious constructs — a bare SELECT with no destination inside a TRIGGER function, for instance — and is the cheapest available defence against a class of PL/pgSQL bugs that only manifests under concurrent load.

8.6 SQL functions and dynamic SQL

Contrasts LANGUAGE sqlLANGUAGE sql with LANGUAGE plpgsqlLANGUAGE plpgsql, describes inlining, and shows the correct construction of dynamic SQL with EXECUTE ... USINGEXECUTE ... USING.

A LANGUAGE sql function is a parseable, plannable object in the same sense as a view. Its body is parsed at creation time by the validator, so a syntax or reference error is a CREATE FUNCTION failure rather than a runtime surprise, and the planner will inline a simple single-statement STABLE or IMMUTABLE function into the calling query, eliminating the call overhead entirely and allowing the function’s tables to participate in join selection. That inlining condition is narrow and worth knowing: the body must be a single SELECT, the function must not be declared SECURITY DEFINER, must not be VOLATILE, and must not set search_pathsearch_path. Any deviation turns it back into an opaque call.

-- SQL-standard body (PostgreSQL 14+): parsed at creation, dependency-tracked, inlinable.
CREATE FUNCTION billing.line_tax(p_amount_cents bigint, p_country char(3))
RETURNS bigint
LANGUAGE sql
STABLE
PARALLEL SAFE
AS SELECT round(p_amount_cents * r.rate)::bigint
       FROM tax_rates r WHERE r.country = p_country;

-- Inlining proof: no Function Scan appears in the plan.
postgres=# EXPLAIN (ANALYZE)
SELECT billing.line_tax(l.line_total_cents, 'DE')
  FROM invoice_lines l WHERE l.invoice_id = 4417;

Seq Scan on invoice_lines l  (cost=0.00..1520.00 rows=61 width=8)
    Filter: (invoice_id = 4417)
    Planning Time: 0.092 ms    Execution Time: 0.318 ms

-- A set-returning SQL function is still an opaque node unless it is inlined.
CREATE FUNCTION billing.customers_with_open_balance()
RETURNS SETOF customers
LANGUAGE sql STABLE
AS SELECT * FROM customers WHERE balance_cents > 0;
AspectLANGUAGE sqlLANGUAGE plpgsql
ParsingValidated at creation; standard-body form also dependency-trackedBody parsed at first execution, then cached per session
InlineableYes, when a single SEL and not SECURITY DEFINERSECURITY DEFINERNo
Row-by-row control flowNot availableAvailable; the reason to choose it
Error handlingNone; the statement abortsEXCEPTION section per block, at subtransaction cost
Per-call overheadNear zero when inlined; one call otherwiseOne call plus per-statement SPI dispatch
Dynamic SQLRequires EXECUTE or a RETURNS-typed helperNative, with EXECUTE ... USING
Default volatilityVOLATILE unless statedVOLATILE unless stated

Declaring volatility correctly is the single highest-leverage annotation on any function. A function marked STABLE or IMMUTABLE is a candidate for constant folding, for reordering around other relations, and for use inside an index expression; the same function marked VOLATILE forces a per-row evaluation and blocks both. IMMUTABLE additionally permits use in generated columns, partial indexes and check constraints, so a mislabelled IMMUTABLE function containing now() is a correctness bug, not merely a performance one.

-- Dynamic SQL: build identifiers with format() %I and values with USING, never concatenation.
CREATE FUNCTION reporting.count_where(p_table text, p_where text, p_args text[])
RETURNS bigint
LANGUAGE plpgsql STABLE PARALLEL SAFE
AS $fn$
DECLARE v_sql text; v_n bigint;
BEGIN
    v_sql := format('SELECT count(*) FROM %I WHERE %s', p_table, p_where);
    EXECUTE v_sql INTO v_n USING p_args['1']::timestamptz,
                                            p_args['2']::timestamptz;
    RETURN v_n;
END;
$fn$;

-- USING parameters bind out of band: no quoting, no injection surface.
CREATE FUNCTION audit.audit_bulk(p_event text, p_ids bigint[])
RETURNS int
LANGUAGE plpgsql VOLATILE
AS $fn$
DECLARE v_n int; v_id bigint;
BEGIN
    FOREACH v_id IN ARRAY p_ids LOOP
        EXECUTE 'INSERT INTO audit.log(obj_id, event) VALUES ($1, $2)'
        USING v_id, p_event;
        v_n := v_n + 1;
    END LOOP;
    RETURN v_n;
END;
$fn$;

-- The security-definer trap: unpinned search_path inside a privileged routine.
CREATE FUNCTION app_secret.rotate_token() RETURNS text
LANGUAGE plpgsql SECURITY DEFINER AS $fn$
DECLARE v_token text;
BEGIN
    -- UNSAFE: resolved against the caller's search_path. Any writable schema
    -- earlier in the path can shadow operator_tokens and hijack the call.
    UPDATE operator_tokens SET revoked_at = now();
    INSERT INTO operator_tokens (token)
    VALUES (encode(gen_random_bytes(32), 'hex'))
    RETURNING token INTO v_token;
    RETURN v_token;
END;
$fn$;

-- Fixed form: pin the path, put pg_temp last so temp objects cannot shadow.
CREATE OR REPLACE FUNCTION app_secret.rotate_token() RETURNS text
LANGUAGE plpgsql SECURITY DEFINER
SET search_path = app_secret, pg_catalog, pg_temp
AS $fn$
DECLARE v_token text;
BEGIN
    UPDATE app_secret.operator_tokens SET revoked_at = now();
    INSERT INTO app_secret.operator_tokens (token)
    VALUES (encode(pg_catalog.gen_random_bytes(32), 'hex'))
    RETURNING token INTO v_token;
    RETURN v_token;
END;
$fn$;

The dynamic-SQL discipline has three parts, and all three matter. Build the statement with format()format() using %I for identifiers and %L for literals, or bind values through USING; %s inserts text verbatim and is only correct when the caller has already guaranteed validity. Restrict the identifier argument against a catalog lookup rather than trusting a string. And give every SECURITY DEFINER function an explicit SET search_pathSET search_path that names only trusted schemas and ends with pg_temp, which is the placement that makes temporary objects unable to shadow anything.

Exercises 8

  1. Write a deploy script that installs pg_trgm, citext, unaccent, ltree and pgcrypto into a dedicated exten schema with explicit versions, then produce a query that reports every installed extension whose extve differs from default_versiondefault_version. Demonstrate that the same script run twice is idempotent.
  2. Build a fuzzy-match feature on a 2-million-row customer table: a trigram GIN index, a similarity() threshold, and an unaccent-normalised functional index. Record EXPLAIN (ANALYZE, BUFFERS) before and after each index, and measure the throughput difference between an accent-sensitive and accent-insensitive query on a 5 % miss rate.
  3. Define a money_amount composite type and a bcp47_tag domain, then demonstrate three documented limitations: domain checks invisible to pg_stats, a composite column unusable for ordered sorts without an operator class, and ALTER DOMAIN ... ADD CONSTRAINTALTER DOMAIN ... ADD CONSTRAINT locking every dependent table. Measure the lock window on a table with 50 columns of the domain type.
  4. Convert a ticket_state enum from text-with-CHECK to a real enum type on a live table, then perform a label rename and a value insertion. Capture the exact error text produced by using a newly added label in the same transaction, and the ALTER TYPEALTER TYPE lock behaviour observed from a concurrent reader.
  5. Write the same routine twice — once LANGUAGE sql, once LANGUAGE plpgsql — and prove inlining by comparing EXPLAIN (ANALYZE) plans for a 10-million-row scan. Then build a SECURITY DEFINER function both with and without a pinned searc and demonstrate the shadowing hijack with a malicious operator table in a writable schema.

9 · The Write Path, WAL and Durability

9.1 Write-ahead logging9.2 Checkpoints9.3 Durability and synchronous commit9.4 The vacuum horizon9.5 Bloat and bloat measurement9.6 Autovacuum and its thresholds

Every committed transaction in PostgreSQL is a sequence of byte ranges appended to a write-ahead log before it is acknowledged, and the relationship between that log, the operating system’s page cache and the durability guarantee is the whole subject of this chapter. The three wal_ values determine what may be decoded from that log and therefore what kinds of replica and point-in-time recovery are available at all. Checkpoints convert the log into on-disk pages and are the dominant source of I/O bursts on a write-heavy system. Vacuum is the mechanism that makes MVCC bounded, and it is the mechanism most often defeated by a single long-running transaction that a monitoring dashboard never surfaces. The chapter’s central operational claim is that fsync=offfsync=off is not a tuning knob but a corruption switch, and that synchronous_commit=offsynchronous_commit=off is a bounded and measurable trade rather than a free performance win.

9.1 Write-ahead logging

Defines the WAL record format, the pg_wal directory lifecycle, and the three wal_ settings that govern decodability.

The write-ahead log is a ring of 16 MB segments in pg_wal containing physical and logical change records, one per page touched, each carrying the page’s LSN. Before a modified data page is written back to the relation file, the WAL records describing it must be flushed; before the transaction is acknowledged, the WAL up to its commit LSN must be flushed. That ordering is the entire guarantee. A crash therefore loses at most transactions whose WAL had not yet been flushed, and never leaves a data page ahead of the log that describes it.

The log is written into the operating system’s page cache by default and flushed by the database, which means the server’s notion of durability depends on the kernel’s willingness to honour fdatasync(). Three wal_ settings exist. minimal omits most WAL for the primary’s own crash recovery and exists only so that a COPY-modified table in the default tablespace can skip WAL entirely; it makes the database unrecoverable after a crash and is never appropriate except in a throwaway instance. replica adds enough information for physical replicas and for continuous archiving. logical adds the logical decoding output required for logical replication and change data capture. replica is the correct setting for any database that must survive a crash.

-- WAL is a ring of 16 MB segments, recycled rather than deleted.
postgres=# SELECT pg_walfile_name(pg_current_wal_lsn()) AS current_segment,
       pg_current_wal_lsn()                       AS insert_lsn,
       pg_current_wal_insert_lsn()                 AS insert_pos,
       pg_current_wal_flush_lsn()                  AS flush_lsn,
       pg_size_pretty(pg_wal_lsn_diff(pg_current_wal_insert_lsn(),
                                      pg_current_wal_flush_lsn())) AS unflushed;

 current_segment | insert_lsn      | insert_pos     | flush_lsn       | unflushed
-----------------+----------------+----------------+----------------+-----------
 0000000100000A | 0/A5312B8      | 0/A5312B8      | 0/A5312B0      | 8 kB

-- Segment-level view of the ring.
postgres=# SELECT pg_walfile_name(lsn) AS segment,
       pg_size_pretty(pg_wal_lsn_diff(lsn, pg_current_wal_lsn())) AS behind
FROM pg_ls_waldir()
ORDER BY mod(name, 2^32) DESC LIMIT 8;

    segment    | behind
---------------+----------
 0000000100000A00000000 | 14 MB
 00000001000009A00000000 | 30 MB
 00000001000009900000000 | 46 MB

-- wal_level determines decodability, not just replica capability.
postgres=# SHOW wal_level;                     -- replica
postgres=# SELECT name, setting, unit, source FROM pg_settings
WHERE name IN ('wal_level', 'wal_compression',
                 'full_page_writes', 'wal_buffers', 'wal_writer_delay')
ORDER BY name;

             name              | setting | unit  |  source
------------------------------+---------+-------+------------
 full_page_writes             | on      |       | configuration file
 wal_buffers                  | 16      | MB    | configuration file
 wal_compression              | off     |       | configuration file
 wal_level                    | replica |       | configuration file

Three WAL settings carry most of the tuning weight. full_page_writes, on by default, forces a full page image after any checkpoint, so that a torn page can be reconstructed; disabling it is safe only on hardware with atomic 8 KB writes. wal_compression, added in PostgreSQL 15 and accepting off, pglz and lz4 in later versions, compresses full-page images; on write-heavy workloads it trades CPU for substantially less WAL traffic, and it is usually a clear win whenever the write device is the bottleneck. wal_buffers sizes the shared staging area for unflushed WAL and defaults to 1/32 of shared_buffers; raising it past that is rarely useful and only matters when a single commit generates more WAL than the buffer holds.

WAL segments and replication slots. A replication slot pins an LSN, and therefore pins every WAL segment from that LSN forward, so that pg_wal cannot be recycled past it. A slot belonging to a subscriber that has been offline for a week will fill the WAL volume of the primary and take it down. pg_replication_ and the safe_w field — which estimates the volume remaining before the slot becomes a problem — should be on the same dashboard as disk.

9.2 Checkpoints

Explains when checkpoints are triggered, how checkpoint_completion_target spreads the writes, and what the burst looks like in pg_stat_bgwriter.

A checkpoint flushes all dirty buffers to disk and writes a checkpoint record so that recovery can begin from a known point. Checkpoints are triggered when either checkpoin has elapsed since the last one, default 5 minutes, or the WAL generated since the last checkpoint exceeds max_wa, default 1 GB. Time-based checkpoints keep the redo point short and recovery fast; size-based checkpoints keep WAL volume bounded under heavy write load. The defaults are a compromise that is wrong for both OLTP and data-warehouse workloads, and the correct direction depends on the ratio of write volume to recovery-time tolerance.

PostgreSQL does not write all dirty pages at once. After the checkpoint record is written it continues writing for up to checkpoint_com of the estimated interval, default 0.9. Within that window the checkpoint uses a maintenance I/O budget proportional to the estimated duration, spreading the writes behind foreground traffic; at the end of the window it abandons the spread and writes whatever remains, which is the burst operators see as periodic I/O saturation. Setting the target to 1.0 completes almost everything smoothly but makes checkpoints overlap more heavily and increases I/O contention.

-- The two triggers, made explicit. OLTP: short timeout, generous WAL budget.
checkpoint_timeout              = 5min
checkpoint_completion_target   = 0.9
max_wal_size                    = 8GB
min_wal_size                    = 2GB
-- Warehouse: infrequent checkpoints, small WAL, recovery time is acceptable.
checkpoint_timeout              = 30min
max_wal_size                    = 2GB

-- Checkpoint counters, sampled.
postgres=# SELECT checkpoints_timed, checkpoints_req, checkpoint_write_time,
       checkpoint_sync_time, buffers_checkpoint, stats_reset FROM pg_stat_bgwriter;

 checkpoints_timed | checkpoints_req | checkpoint_write_time | checkpoint_sync_time
-------------------+-----------------+-----------------------+----------------------
              1441 |              318 |              412_883 ms |           9_112 ms

-- A req-dominated ratio means WAL pressure, not time, is driving checkpoints.
postgres=# SELECT round(100.0 * checkpoints_req /
       (checkpoints_timed + checkpoints_req), 1) AS pct_request_driven
FROM pg_stat_bgwriter;

 pct_request_driven
------------------
             18.1

Never zero it. checkpoint_timeout = 0checkpoint_timeout = 0 is a legal value that requests no timed checkpoints. On a write-heavy system it does not mean max_wal_size governs; it means recovery time becomes unbounded and a crash requires replaying an entire WAL archive. It is occasionally recommended as a performance experiment and should be reverted immediately.

-- Replay checkpoint records from the log without starting a cluster.
-- pg_waldump is a contrib binary, present in every server installation.
postgres=# pg_waldump -p ./pg_wal -s 0/5000060 -e 0/5000200 -t CHECKPOINT

rmgr: XLOG   len (rec/tot):    42/    42, tx:        0, lsn: 0/05000258, prev 0/05000140
  -- CHECKPOINT_ONLINE, redo 0/05000060, tli 1, prev_tli 1
rmgr: XLOG   len (rec/tot):    42/    42, tx:        0, lsn: 0/050002A8, prev 0/05000258
  -- CHECKPOINT_ONLINE, redo 0/05000060, tli 1, prev_tli 1

-- Record-type mix of the current segment, via pg_waldump piped to awk.
postgres=# pg_waldump -p ./pg_wal 000000010000000A00000000 2>/dev/null \
          | awk '{print $5}' | sort | uniq -c | sort -rn | head -6
   41208 COMMIT
    8820 FPI_FOR_HINT
    4410 FPI
    2210 INSERT
     118 UPDATE
       14 CHECKPOINT_SHUTDOWN

postgres=# SELECT backend_type, object, context,
       writes, write_time, fsyncs, fsync_time
FROM pg_stat_io WHERE writes > 0
ORDER BY write_time DESC LIMIT 6;

   backend_type    |  object   |  context  | writes | write_time | fsyncs
-------------------+-----------+-----------+--------+------------+--------
 checkpointer     | relation  | normal    | 18204  |  182.441 s |    812
 checkpointer     | relation  | bulk      |  41022 |   61.002 s |    214
 autovacuum worker| relation  | normal    |   8810 |   14.220 s |     88
 background writer| relation  | normal    |  2201 |    3.118 s |      0

The bulk context in that last query is the extension of pg_stat_io introduced in PostgreSQL 16, which distinguishes index-build and vacuum writes from ordinary background-writer writes. On a write-heavy deployment the bu context is where the volume is, and it is largely unaffected by background_writer tuning — which is why the historical advice to raise bgwriter_delay for write-heavy workloads has largely stopped being effective. PostgreSQL 18 routes more of these operations through the new asynchronous I/O subsystem, so the distribution between foreground backends, the checkpointer and bulk workers shifts again; re-baseline the counters after upgrading rather than comparing them across versions.

9.3 Durability and synchronous commit

Quantifies what synchronous_commit buys and costs, and explains why fsync=off is categorically different in kind.

The commit protocol has two independent flush points. The first is the WAL flush of the commit record: setting synchronous_commit to on or remote_apply means the server does not return from COMMIT until that record is on stable storage. Setting it to off returns immediately and lets the WAL accumulate in the page cache; a crash can then lose recently acknowledged transactions, up to the amount the operating system had not yet written. Setting it to remote_write or remote_apply additionally waits for synchronous standbys and is what a zero-data-loss failover requires.

The second flush point is the transaction’s data pages, and its default behaviour is the reason fsync=off is not in the same category. With fsync=off the server does not ask the kernel to write a page before reusing it for a different purpose, so a page can be modified in memory, left unflushed, and written back out with mixed content — producing a corrupted page rather than a lost transaction. Corruption is not recoverable by restoring an archive; a lost transaction is. The setting exists for benchmarking and for the rare case of a database that is genuinely rebuilt from source on every boot.

SettingLocal commit waits forLoss window on crashUse when
synchronous_commit = onsynchronous_commit = onLocal WAL flushNothing acknowledgedDefault; any system of record
loLocal WAL flush, ignores standbysNothing acknowledged locallyAsync standby acceptable
remoteStandby write acknowledgmentNothing acknowledged locallyStandby may lag but must survive failover
remoteStandby apply acknowledgmentNothing acknowledged, anywhereTrue zero-data-loss synchronous replication
oNothingAll commits since last WAL flushBenchmarks; derived, rebuildable data
fsync = offfsync = offNothing, anywhereCorrupted pages, whole-database rebuildNever, in production
 
-- Measure the actual cost of the commit flush at your storage layer.
postgres=# SELECT name, setting, unit FROM pg_settings
WHERE name IN ('synchronous_commit', 'fsync',
                 'full_page_writes', 'commit_delay', 'commit_siblings')
ORDER BY name;

             name             | setting
------------------------------+---------
 commit_delay                  | 0
 commit_siblings               | 5
 fsync                         | on
 full_page_writes              | on
 synchronous_commit            | on

-- Per-checkpoint fsync counts, from the 16+ I/O statistics.
postgres=# SELECT backend_type, object, context, fsyncs, fsync_time,
       round(fsync_time::numeric / NULLIF(fsyncs, 0), 2) AS avg_ms
FROM pg_stat_io
WHERE fsyncs > 0 AND object = 'relation'
ORDER BY fsync_time DESC LIMIT 5;

   backend_type    | object  | context | fsyncs | fsync_time | avg_ms
-------------------+---------+---------+--------+------------+--------
 checkpointer     | relation | normal  |  18204 |   91.004 s |  5.00
 background writer| relation | normal  |   2201 |    0.882 s |  0.40
 checkpointer     | relation | bulk    |   41022 |  204.110 s |  4.98

-- commit_siblings groups concurrent commits behind one flush. Group commit is real:
-- a batch of 20 commits on one device costs roughly one fsync, not twenty.
postgres=# SHOW commit_delay;   -- 0: no artificial delay, group commit is purely opportunistic

Group commit makes the setting cheaper than it looks. With five or more concurrent committers, synchronous_commit=onsynchronous_commit=on amortises the flush across the batch and the per-transaction cost falls sharply. Measure the cost at your real concurrency rather than from a single-session benchmark; pg_stat_ showing 300 idle-in-transaction or active sessions is a stronger signal of group-commit benefit than any setting on the primary.

If the flush latency is dominated by the device rather than by PostgreSQL, the correct remedy is a device that acknowledges writes — a battery-backed cache with a power-loss-protected cache, or a write-through controller — not a weaker commit guarantee. On cloud block storage, sizing the instance I/O credits above the measured write rate plus checkpoint bursts is the equivalent intervention.

9.4 The vacuum horizon

Defines the xmin horizon, explains why a long transaction blocks reclamation, and gives the queries that find the blocker.

MVCC makes an UPDATE write a new tuple version and mark the old one dead rather than overwriting it. Reclaiming the space requires knowing that no running or future transaction can still see the dead version, which requires a lower bound on the age of every transaction that exists or will exist. That bound is the xmin horizon, and it is the minimum over all running transactions' snapshots, all prepared transactions, all replication slots' confirmed flush positions, and two cluster-wide safety values. A dead tuple whose xmin precedes the horizon is removable; one whose xmin is at or above it is not, and is not scanned again until the horizon advances past it.

Everything about vacuum difficulty follows from that minimum. A transaction opened on Monday and idle over the weekend pins the horizon at Monday’s snapshot, and no amount of vacuuming on Tuesday can reclaim a single tuple updated since Monday. On a high-churn table that produces unbounded growth. Worse, prepared transactions and replication slots can pin the horizon indefinitely without any session to point at, which is why max_prepared_transactions should be left at zero unless prepared transactions are genuinely required, and why slot lag must be monitored as a first-class metric.

-- The horizon and its age. Anything above pg_snapshot_xmin(xmin) cannot be removed.
postgres=# SELECT pg_snapshot_xmin(xmin) AS xmin,
       age(pg_snapshot_xmin(xmin))               AS horizon_age,
       mxid_current()                            AS current_xid,
       pg_current_wal_lsn()
FROM (SELECT txid_current_snapshot() AS xmin) s;

     xmin     | horizon_age | current_xid | pg_current_wal_lsn
--------------+-------------+-------------+------------------
 112004288    |        1842 |  112009441  | 0/B1C4A20

-- Sessions that are holding the horizon back, longest first.
postgres=# SELECT pid, usename, application_name, state,
       now() - xact_start                          AS xact_age,
       now() - state_change                        AS in_state,
       backend_xmin,
       left(query, 90)                             AS query
FROM pg_stat_activity
WHERE backend_xmin IS NOT NULL
ORDER BY backend_xmin DESC LIMIT 5;

  pid  | usename | application_name |       state       | xact_age  | in_state | backend_xmin
-------+---------+------------------+------------------+-----------+----------+-------------
 44102 | etl     | airflow-scheduler | idle in transaction| 6 days 04: | 6 days   | 112004288
 51903 | app     | orders-api       | active            | 00:00:04 | 00:00:03 | 112009001
-- Slots and prepared transactions pin the horizon with no session to attribute it to.
postgres=# SELECT slot_name, slot_type, active, restart_lsn,
       pg_size_pretty(pg_wal_lsn_diff(pg_current_wal_lsn(), restart_lsn)) AS retained,
       pg_size_pretty(safe_wal_size) AS safe_wal_size, wal_status
FROM pg_replication_slots;

    slot_name    | slot_type | active |  retained  | safe_wal_size | wal_status
----------------+-----------+--------+------------+--------------+-----------
 cdc_orders    | logical   | t      | 12 GB      | 48 GB         | ok

postgres=# SELECT gid, prepared, owner, database, now() - prepared AS age
FROM pg_prepared_xacts;
-- Empty result is the desired steady state.

-- The single most valuable vacuum dashboard query.
postgres=# SELECT schemaname, relname,
       n_dead_tup, n_live_tup,
       round(100.0 * n_dead_tup / NULLIF(n_live_tup + n_dead_tup, 0), 1) AS dead_pct,
       last_autovacuum, autovacuum_count, vacuum_count
FROM pg_stat_user_tables
WHERE n_dead_tup > 10000
ORDER BY dead_pct DESC LIMIT 5;

  schemaname |   relname    | n_dead_tup | n_live_tup | dead_pct | last_autovacuum
------------+--------------+------------+------------+----------+----------------
 public      | events_2025m01|    842019 |     21044  |    97.6 | 2025-04-02 03:11
 public      | sessions      |     91233 |   1002888  |     8.3 | 2025-04-02 03:04

Idle in transaction is a vacuum outage. A connection sitting idle in transactionidle in transaction holds a snapshot and pins the horizon for the entire duration of the idle period, not the duration of its statements. Setting idle_in_transacti is the single most effective vacuum protection available, and application-side pooling libraries must be configured not to hold a transaction open between pool checkouts.

-- VACUUM modes and the lock each one takes.
VACUUM events_2025m01;                  -- ShareUpdateExclusive: DML continues
VACUUM (ANALYZE, VERBOSE) events_2025m01;   -- statistics refresh only
VACUUM (PARALLEL 4) events_2025m01;        -- PostgreSQL 13+, parallel index vacuum
VACUUM (TRUNCATE, ANALYZE) events_old;  -- returns the tail space to the OS

-- Locks as observed from a second session while VACUUM FULL runs.
postgres=# SELECT l.mode, l.granted, l.relation::regclass
FROM pg_locks l WHERE l.relation = 'events'::regclass;

   mode       | granted | relation
--------------+---------+----------
 ShareUpdateExclusiveLock | t | events   -- VACUUM
 AccessExclusiveLock      | f | events   -- queued behind VACUUM

-- Lock-strength comparison. This is the part that belongs on a wall.
BEGIN;
VACUUM FULL events;    -- AccessExclusive: every DML statement queues
ERROR:  canceling statement due to lock timeout
ROLLBACK;

-- REINDEX CONCURRENTLY does not block writes, but does not shrink the file.
REINDEX TABLE CONCURRENTLY events;
REINDEX INDEX CONCURRENTLY events_2025m01_pkey;
OperationLock on the tableBlocks DMLReclaims to the OSRewrites
VACShareUpdateExclusiveNoNo — space stays in the relationNo
VACUUM (TRUNCATE)VACUUM (TRUNCATE)ShareUpdateExclusiveNoOnly the empty tailNo
VACUUM FULLVACUUM FULLAccessExclusiveYes, fullyYesYes, full copy
pg_rBrief AccessExclusive at the endMinimalYesYes, online

9.5 Bloat and bloat measurement

Distinguishes free space that PostgreSQL can reuse from space that is lost, and shows how to measure each with pgstattuple.

Bloat has two distinct forms and conflating them leads to the wrong remedy. Free space inside a page is space left by dead tuples that a future insert may reuse; it costs nothing but query selectivity, because the planner’s relp reflects the high-water mark and so does not distinguish a full page from a sparse one. Space at the end of the relation cannot be reused by ordinary inserts and inflates every sequential scan, every index scan that touches the heap, and every backup. Only a rewrite returns the latter to the operating system.

The correction for the first form is more vacuuming, since the visibility map marks all-free pages and the planner already discounts them. The correction for the second form is a rewrite: VACUUM FULLVACUUM FULL online and blocking, or pg_repack online with a brief lock. Note also that bloat on the heap is the least of it — indexes bloat far more often, because index entries are not reordered by vacuum and an index whose page occupancy has fallen below 90 % may still not be repacked by REINDEX CONCURRENTLYREINDEX CONCURRENTLY, which produces a new file of the same size.

-- pgstattuple: page-level detail for one relation. Full table, no locks beyond ShareUpdateExclusive.
postgres=# CREATE EXTENSION IF NOT EXISTS pgstattuple;
postgres=# SELECT table_len, tuple_count, tuple_len, tuple_percent,
       dead_tuple_count, dead_tuple_len, dead_tuple_percent,
       free_space, free_percent
FROM pgstattuple('events_2025m01');

  table_len | tuple_count | tuple_percent | dead_tuple_count | dead_tuple_percent | free_space | free_percent
------------+-------------+----------------+-----------------+--------------------+------------+--------------
 8.6 GB     |    21044    |          4.2 |          842019  |              95.6 |  12 MB     |       0.1

-- pgstattuple_approx: sampling, usable on a live table under load.
postgres=# SELECT table_len, approx_tuple_count, approx_tuple_percent,
       approx_dead_tuple_count, approx_free_space, approx_free_percent
FROM pgstattuple_approx('events_2025m01');

  table_len | approx_tuple_count | approx_dead_tuple_count | approx_free_space | approx_free_percent
------------+--------------------+-------------------------+-------------------+--------------------
 8.6 GB     |             410000 |                  820000 |           24 MB   |              0.2

-- Index-level bloat. pgstatindex reports leaf fragmentation, not tuple bloat.
postgres=# SELECT i.indexrelname,
       pg_size_pretty(pg_relation_size(s.indexrelid)) AS size,
       round(s.leaf_fragmentation, 1) AS leaf_fragmentation,
       round(s.leaf_count * (1 - s.leaf_fragmentation / 100.0))::int AS wasted_pages
FROM pg_index s
JOIN pg_class t ON t.oid = s.indrelid
JOIN pg_indexes i ON i.indexrelid = s.indexrelid
WHERE t.relname = 'events_2025m01'
  AND s.leaf_fragmentation > 20;

   indexrelname      |  indexsize  | leaf_fragmentation | wasted_pages
---------------------+-------------+--------------------+---------------
 events_pkey         | 1.2 GB      |              31.8   |         31204
 events_tenant_idx   | 4.4 GB      |              44.2   |        148022

-- Vacuum does not defragment an index. Only a REINDEX or pg_repack rewrites it,
-- and REINDEX CONCURRENTLY usually produces a file of identical size.
postgres=# SELECT pg_size_pretty(pg_relation_size('events_tenant_idx'));
 pg_size_pretty
----------------
 4.4 GB

Version note. PostgreSQL 16 reworked the vacuum visibility map so that it stores heap blocks in two levels and records all-visible and all-frozen status per block, which makes both index-only scans and the visibility checks inside vacuum substantially cheaper on large heaps. PostgreSQL 18 then routes the same operations through the asynchronous I/O subsystem. Both changes reduce the time vacuum takes; neither reduces the space a bloated relation occupies, so re-baseline after an upgrade but do not expect bloat to have fixed itself.

-- Heap bloat estimated from live-row statistics, over the whole database.
WITH t AS (
  SELECT s.relid, s.n_live_tup, s.n_dead_tup,
         pg_table_size(s.relid) AS bytes
  FROM pg_stat_user_tables s
  JOIN pg_index i ON i.indrelid = s.relid AND i.indisprimary
)
SELECT relid::regclass AS table_name,
       pg_size_pretty(bytes) AS total,
       n_live_tup, n_dead_tup,
       round(100.0 * bytes / GREATEST(n_live_tup, 1), 1) AS bytes_per_live_row,
       -- 80 bytes/row is a reasonable heuristic floor for a narrow row
       pg_size_pretty(greatest(bytes - (n_live_tup * 80)::bigint, 0)) AS est_bloat
FROM t WHERE bytes > 100 * 1024 * 1024
ORDER BY bytes DESC LIMIT 5;

   table_name    |  total  | bytes_per_live_row | est_bloat
-----------------+---------+--------------------+------------
 events_2025m01 | 8.6 GB  |             427.4   | 7.9 GB
 events_2025m02 | 8.1 GB  |             218.6   | 6.5 GB

-- Estimate only. Confirm with pgstattuple before scheduling a rewrite.
postgres=# SELECT * FROM pgstattuple('events_2025m02');

9.6 Autovacuum and its thresholds

Explains the trigger arithmetic, cost-based throttling, per-table overrides, and the correct way to respond to a table vacuuming too slowly.

Autovacuum starts a worker for a table when the number of dead tuples since the last vacuum exceeds autovacuum_vacuum_threshold + autovacuum_vacuum_scale_factor × reltuplesautovacuum_vacuum_threshold + autovacuum_vacuum_scale_factor × reltuples, default 50 plus 20 % of the table.
