Those defaults are calibrated for tables with hundreds of thousands of rows; on a table of 100 million rows the scale factor alone permits 20 million dead tuples before anything starts, which is tens of gigabytes. The correct response is a per-table override that sets the scale factor to zero and the threshold to a number chosen from the table’s update rate, not a global change.

Once started, a worker is throttled by autovacuum_vacuum_cost_delay, default 2 ms, against a cost limit of 200, with the delay scaled by autovacuum_vac relative to maintenance_work_mem in PostgreSQL 17 and later. Raising autovacuum_vacuum_cost_limit lets a worker reclaim faster at the price of foreground latency; lowering autovacuum_vacuum_cost_delay does the same less predictably. Two further limits bound concurrency: autovacuum_max_workers, default 3 unless raised to match max_worker_processes, and autovacuum_work_mem, which must be large enough to hold the dead tuple identifiers of one index — setting it to 10 % of shared_buffers is common and is what makes index vacuum possible in a single pass.

-- Per-table settings, applied at creation time and reset with ALTER TABLE ... RESET.
CREATE TABLE events (
    event_id  bigint GENERATED ALWAYS AS IDENTITY,
    tenant_id bigint NOT NULL,
    kind      text   NOT NULL,
    payload   jsonb  NOT NULL,
    body      text,
    PRIMARY KEY (event_id, kind)
) PARTITION BY LIST (kind)
WITH (autovacuum_vacuum_scale_factor  = 0.01,
      autovacuum_vacuum_threshold     = 5000,
      autovacuum_analyze_scale_factor = 0.005,
      fillfactor                     = 85);

-- Overrides on a hot child table; a leaf with no setting of its own inherits the parent's.
ALTER TABLE events_click SET (
  autovacuum_vacuum_scale_factor = 0.0,
  autovacuum_vacuum_threshold    = 20000,
  autovacuum_vacuum_cost_delay   = 0
);

-- Aggressive autovacuum settings for an append-mostly archive table.
ALTER TABLE events_purchase SET (
  autovacuum_vacuum_insert_scale_factor = 0.05,   -- PostgreSQL 13+, triggers on inserts
  autovacuum_vacuum_insert_threshold    = 1000000,
  toast.autovacuum_vacuum_scale_factor  = 0.0
);

postgres=# SELECT relname, reloptions
FROM pg_class WHERE relname IN ('events', 'events_click', 'events_purchase');
-- reloptions is the authoritative record; psql \d shows only the effective subset.
-- Is autovacuum keeping up, or merely running and failing?
postgres=# SELECT relname,
       n_live_tup, n_dead_tup,
       age(relfrozenxid) AS xid_age,
       round(age(relfrozenxid)::numeric / 2000000000, 3) AS pct_to_wraparound,
       last_autovacuum, last_autoanalyze,
       autovacuum_count, autoanalyze_count
FROM pg_stat_user_tables
WHERE n_dead_tup > 0 OR age(relfrozenxid) > 100000000
ORDER BY age(relfrozenxid) DESC LIMIT 5;

    relname    | n_dead_tup | xid_age   | pct_to_wraparound |      last_autovacuum
---------------+------------+-----------+-------------------+---------------------------
 events_purchase|   1204411 | 411022001 |            0.205 | 2025-04-02 02:58:11
 sessions       |     91233 |      8801 |            0.004 | 2025-04-02 03:04:22

-- Worker saturation: more eligible tables than workers.
postgres=# SELECT count(*) AS running_autovacuums
FROM pg_stat_activity WHERE backend_type = 'autovacuum worker';
-- 3 running with autovacuum_max_workers = 3 and 41 tables queued.

-- Freeze failures: a frozen xid has aged past 40% and autovacuum cannot finish.
postgres=# SELECT datname, age(datfrozenxid) AS db_xid_age
FROM pg_database ORDER BY 2 DESC LIMIT 3;

  datname  | db_xid_age
-----------+------------
 prod     |  118044002
 staging  |    44018811

-- Manual intervention when the horizon is pinned: find, terminate, then vacuum.
SELECT pg_terminate_backend(44102);
VACUUM (FREEZE, VERBOSE, ANALYZE) events_purchase;

Transaction-ID wraparound is not a vacuum-throughput problem. Autovacuum is triggered on a separate, much lower threshold when age(relfrozenxid)age(relfrozenxid) approaches autovacuum_freeze_max_age, default 200 million, and it will happily block on every other autovacuum task to get there. At 40 % age, failing to freeze becomes an emergency that forces an emergency shutdown; at 50 % the server shuts down to protect data. Monitoring age(relfrozenxid)age(relfrozenxid) with an alert at 30 % is not optional on any long-lived database.

Measure the manual path first. When deciding whether to lower thresholds, run VACUUM (VERBOSE, ANALYZE) manually against the offending table during a quiet period and record the elapsed time and the pg_stat_io write volume. If a manual vacuum takes 40 seconds, an autovacuum threshold that triggers it every 10 minutes will not be viable and the real fix is either cost-limit tuning or pg_repack on a schedule.

Exercises 9

  1. Instrument a write-heavy benchmark (100 concurrent clients, sustained inserts and updates) and produce a time series of pg_current_wal_lsn() against pg_stat_io write counts. Report WAL bytes generated per transaction for a narrow and a wide table, and calculate the effect of enabling wal_compression = lz4 with a 128-byte random payload column.
  2. Drive synchronous_commit through its five settings on the same benchmark and record mean commit latency, throughput, and the observed data-loss window after pg_ctl stop -m immediate. Repeat with fsync=off on a disposable cluster and record the corruption or error actually observed rather than assuming it.
  3. Create a transaction-ID wraparound experiment in an isolated cluster: set autovacuum_freeze_max_age low, hold a long-running transaction open, and measure how age(relfrozenxid) climbs and when autovacuum’s aggressive anti-wraparound worker takes priority. Record the pg_stat_activity query you would run to find the blocker.
  4. Reproduce bloat on a 50 GB table with a 60/40 update pattern, measure it with pgstattuple and pgstattuple_approx, and compare the timing and lock behaviour of VACUUM, VACUUM FULL and pg_repack while a constant read workload runs. Record the exact pg_l rows observed for each.
  5. Turn autovacuum from off to a tuned configuration on that same table. Derive per-table thresholds from the measured update rate, set autovacuum_vacuum_scale_factor = 0 on the hot child, and demonstrate that dead-tuple count now stays below the threshold. Report last_autovacuum intervals and the pg_stat_bgwriter impact.

10 · Backup, Point-in-Time Recovery and Replication

10.1 Full backups10.2 Point-in-time recovery10.3 Continuous archiving10.4 Streaming replication10.5 Logical replication10.6 Conflicts and correctness

There are three recovery mechanisms in PostgreSQL and they answer different questions. Continuous archiving plus restore_command and a recovery target answers "what did the database contain at 03:14 UTC". Physical streaming replication with standby.signal answers "give me a read replica and, if the primary dies, a replacement". Logical replication with PUBLICATION and SUBSCRIPTION answers "give me a subset of tables, possibly filtered, possibly on a different major version, in another region". Confusing the three produces systems that appear to provide availability guarantees they cannot. The chapter treats full backups first because everything else depends on having one, then archiving, then the two replication mechanisms, and ends with the conflict classes that determine whether a subscriber needs conflict detection at all.

10.1 Full backups

Documents pg_basebackup, the filesystem-level requirements for a cold copy, and what a restore actually validates.

A PostgreSQL backup is a filesystem copy of the data directory taken at a point where the copy is transactionally consistent. The server does not pause writes for the copy; instead, pg_basebackup relies on the WAL generated during the copy to make the result replayable to a consistent state. Three methods are available and they are not equivalent in operational cost: the built-in pg_basebackup client, which speaks the streaming replication protocol and is the default choice; a filesystem snapshot taken while the server is shut down, which is correct and cheap but offline; and a snapshot taken while the server runs, which is correct only if the snapshot is atomic across the entire data directory and the snapshot also captures pg_wal consistently.

What a backup must include beyond the data directory is a matter of configuration files. pg_hba.conf, pg_ident.conf and postgresql.auto.conf live outside PGDATA and are not part of pg_basebackup unless they have been symlinked in. A restore that omits them produces a cluster that starts but rejects every connection, which is the single most common failed-restore report.

-- Full base backup to a local directory. -X stream ships WAL alongside the copy.
$ pg_basebackup --pgdata=/backup/base_20250402 \
                --wal-method=stream \
                --checkpoint=fast \
                --progress --verbose \
                --manifest-checksums=SHA256 \
                --write-recovery-conf \
                --label 'base_20250402' \
                --dbname='host=pg-primary dbname=postgres user=backup user'

-- Compressed and parallel: the sane production form. -P passes progress through.
$ pg_basebackup -D /backup/base_20250402 -Ft -z -l none -c fast -P \
                --manifest-checksums=SHA256 -h pg-primary -U replicator
pg_basebackup: base backup started
pg_basebackup: backup completed
pg_basebackup: 28/28 pages (100%), 0/28 tablespaces

-- What the backup manifest gives you: per-file checksums, verifiable offline.
$ jq -r '.Files | length' /backup/base_20250402/backup_manifest.json
  18422
$ jq -r '.WAL-Ranges[] | .TimelineID' /backup/base_20250402/backup_manifest.json
  1

-- Verify without restoring: pg_verifybackup (PostgreSQL 15+).
$ pg_verifybackup -n /backup/base_20250402
pg_verifybackup: backup verified
-- Ignore the WAL directory when the archive is stored elsewhere.
$ pg_verifybackup -n -i /backup/base_20250402/pg_wal /backup/base_20250402
pg_verifybackup: backup verified
-- PostgreSQL 17 incremental backup: only blocks changed since a previous backup.
$ # manifest first: full backup must exist and be trusted
$ pg_basebackup -D /backup/incr_20250403 -Ft -z --manifest-checksums=SHA256 \
                --incremental=0000000100000A0000000400000000 \
                --pgdata=/backup/base_20250402

pg_basebackup: incremental backup size: 412 MB
pg_basebackup: full backup size:     84 GB

# chain: base -> incr1 -> incr2; restore concatenates in order, or lets
# pg_combinebackup (17+) reassemble the chain in the correct sequence.
$ pg_combinebackup /backup/base_20250402 /backup/incr_20250403 -o /backup/restore_20250403
pg_combinebackup: successfully combined to "/backup/restore_20250403"

-- Rate and size change measurably with PostgreSQL 17's improved pg_basebackup:
-- parallel file transfer via --manifest, and lower memory during verification.
$ pg_basebackup -h pg-primary -D /backup/b1 --wal-method=stream --verbose 2>&1 \
  | grep -E '(average|transferred)'

-- The real production tools. Both take and verify backups on a schedule;
-- neither replaces understanding the mechanism.
$ pgbackrest --stanza=db --type=full backup       # parallel, delta-aware, repo-aware
$ wal-g backup-push $PGDATA                      # WAL-G: S3/GCS-native, object storage first
$ barman-cloud-backup --gzip --remote=s3://...   # barman: WAL archive + restore orchestration
$ pgbackrest --stanza=db info                     # restore: --type=time --target='2025-04-02 03:14:00+00'

A backup that has never been restored is not a backup. Schedule a weekly automated restore onto an isolated host and record the elapsed time from start to first successful query. That number is the only one that matters during an incident, and it is the number most organisations do not have. Object storage is not an excuse: pgBackRest and WAL-G both restore from S3-compatible storage, and the restore path is the path you must exercise.

MethodConsistentOnlineSelectable tablespaceVerification
pg_basYesYesYes, with -T-Tpg_veri
pg_basebackup --incrementalpg_basebackup --incrementalYesYesYesManifest chain
Cold filesystem copyYesNoOnly by handManual
Atomic volume snapshotYesYesYesManual, plus WAL
Snapshot of a block deviceOnly with pg_start_backuppg_start_backup-free consistencyYesAllManual, risky
pg_ / pg_duYesYesLogical only, no globalsRestore-and-count

10.2 Point-in-time recovery

Specifies the recovery configuration written into postgresql.auto.conf, the recovery target controls, and the sequence of operations that brings a cluster out of recovery.

Point-in-time recovery restarts a base backup and replays archived WAL forward to a chosen LSN, timestamp, transaction ID or immediate stop, then leaves the cluster in a state where a recovery target file is absent and read-write access is restored. The configuration is a short list, and every entry in it is load-bearing. restore fetches one archived segment; it is invoked repeatedly and is expected to fail with a non-zero exit status for segments that do not exist, which is the normal signal that replay has passed the end of the archive. recovery_t stops replay at the first commit record at or after the named instant, which is the semantics an operator needs when undoing a bad migration.

The target type determines what happens next, and this is where most recovery rehearsals fail. With no target set, the server replays everything available and then stays in recovery indefinitely, prompting for pg_wal_replay_resume(). With a target set, replay stops at the target, the server promotes automatically, and the resulting cluster contains everything before the target and nothing after it. Promoting with a target set is irreversible: any subsequent transaction on the recovered cluster moves the timeline forward and makes the original primary a divergent branch.

-- 1. Restore the data directory, then chown it to the postgres user.
$ tar -xzf /backup/base_20250402.tar.gz -C /var/lib/postgresql
$ chown -R postgres:postgres /var/lib/postgresql/18/main
-- 2. Remove any stale recovery signal and write the recovery configuration.
$ rm -f /var/lib/postgresql/18/main/standby.signal \
        /var/lib/postgresql/18/main/recovery.signal
$ cat >> /var/lib/postgresql/18/main/postgresql.auto.conf <<'CONF'
restore_command = 'aws s3 cp s3://pg-archive-prod/%f%w %p'
recovery_target_time = '2025-04-02 03:14:22+00'
recovery_target_action = 'promote'
recovery_target_inclusive = on
recovery_end_command = 'pg_ctl -D %D promote -w'
CONF

-- 3. Start. The server replays and promotes itself at the target.
$ pg_ctl -D /var/lib/postgresql/18/main -w -t 600 start
-- waiting for server to start.... done
$ tail -n 40 /var/log/postgresql/postgresql-18-main.log
LOG:  starting point-in-time recovery
LOG:  restored log file "0000000100000A0000003000000000" from archive
LOG:  recovery stopping before commit of transaction 112884401, time 2025-04-02 03:14:22
LOG:  redo done at 0/A3003C58 system usage: CPU: user: 0.00 s
LOG:  selected new timeline ID: 2
LOG:  database system is ready to accept connections
-- The recovery target file wins over postgresql.auto.conf. Written by the operator,
-- consumed and deleted by the server at the target point.
$ echo '2025-04-02 03:14:22+00' > /var/lib/postgresql/18/main/recovery_target_time
$ pg_ctl -D /var/lib/postgresql/18/main -w start

-- Verify the outcome before declaring the incident over.
postgres=# SELECT pg_is_in_recovery(), pg_last_wal_replay_lsn(), pg_control_checkpoint();
 pg_is_in_recovery | pg_last_wal_replay_lsn | pg_control_checkpoint
-------------------+------------------------+----------------------
 f                 | 0/A3003C40               | t=1743563662

-- Timeline history is the record of every branch: never delete it.
$ ls -l /var/lib/postgresql/18/main/pg_wal/*.history
-rw-r----- 1 postgres postgres  612 Apr  2 03:15 /var/lib/.../00000002.history
$ cat /var/lib/postgresql/18/main/pg_wal/00000002.history
1 0/A3003C40 no recovery target specified

-- Failure mode: archive missing a segment mid-replay.
LOG:  archive recovery complete
FATAL:  could not restore file "0000000100000A0000003500000000" from archive:
        s3:NoSuchKey
$ aws s3 ls s3://pg-archive-prod/ | grep 0000000100000A0000003
# The gap must be located and re-uploaded from another copy of the archive.

Timeline divergence is silent. After promotion, the recovered cluster runs on timeline 2 and the original primary is still on timeline 1. If the original primary is ever restarted it will refuse to start in a cluster whose control file shows a different timeline, and if it is restarted after its own recovery.signal it will happily diverge further. Isolate the old primary’s data directory, or run it as a read-only replica against the archive until the data loss is formally accepted.

10.3 Continuous archiving

Configures archive_mode, archive_command and archive_timeout, and explains why archiving is a latency problem rather than a throughput problem.

Continuous archiving hands each completed WAL segment to an external command, which is expected to copy it somewhere durable and return success. The database waits for the command’s exit status, so archiving is strictly a latency budget on the WAL writer: the command must complete faster than the database produces segments, or an archive_status file accumulates and the writer blocks. Three settings govern it. archive_mode is off, on or always; the last means every WAL server archives, including a standby, which is required if the standby is to be able to survive being promoted without a second archive destination. archive_command is the shell command. archive_timeout, default 0, forces a segment switch after that many seconds so that low-write databases still produce a recoverable archive.

-- postgresql.conf on an archiving primary.
wal_level = replica
archive_mode = on
archive_command = 'test ! -f pg_wal/%f && aws s3 cp --sse AES256 s3://pg-archive-prod/%f%w %p'
archive_timeout = 300
# %f = segment filename, %p = destination path, %w = suffix (.partial for -X stream copies)
max_wal_size = 8GB
max_slot_wal_keep_size = 16GB
# max_slot_wal_keep_size (PostgreSQL 13+) makes a lagging slot fail instead of
# filling the disk. The replica goes down; the primary survives.

-- Archive status files are the queue. Anything here is not yet archived.
postgres=# SELECT pg_walfile_name(pg_current_wal_lsn()) AS current,
       count(*) AS pending, oldest(ready) AS oldest_pending
FROM pg_stat_archiver CROSS JOIN LATERAL pg_ls_dir('pg_wal/archive_status') d(name);

   current    | pending |      oldest_pending
--------------+---------+-------------------------
 0000000100000A |      12 | 00000001000009.ready

-- Cumulative archiver statistics: failed_count > 0 is an alert, not a statistic.
postgres=# SELECT archived_count, failed_count,
       last_archived_wal, last_failed_wal, now() - last_archived_time AS since_last
FROM pg_stat_archiver;

 archived_count | failed_count | last_archived_wal  | since_last
----------------+-------------+--------------------+-----------
        18,441 |           17 | 00000001000009A... | 00:00:03
-- Archive retention: pg_archivecleanup removes segments no longer needed.
$ pg_archivecleanup -n /var/lib/postgresql/archive 0000000100000A0000004000000000
# -n is a dry run. Always dry-run first.
$ pg_archivecleanup /var/lib/postgresql/archive 0000000100000A0000004000000000
  # pg_archivecleanup: 1882 files would be removed

-- Which segments does a backup actually need? pg_waldump answers it.
$ pg_waldump -p /backup/base_20250402/pg_wal \
             --start 0000000100000A0000000000000000 2>/dev/null | head -1
first record is after 0/A000060, from 000000010000000A0000000000000028,
at 0/A000028, on timeline 1

-- pgBackRest: the same retention policy, declaratively, per stanza.
$ cat /etc/pgbackrest/pgbackrest.conf
[global]
repo1-path=/pgbackrest
repo1-cipher-type=aes-256-cbc
repo1-retention-full=4
repo1-retention-diff=14
process-max=4
start-fast=y
compress=y

# WAL-G with object storage; S3-compatible, no local archive tier needed.
$ cat /etc/wal-g/wal-g.env
WALEVEL=replica
WALG_S3_PREFIX=s3://pg-archive-prod
WALG_COMPRESSION_METHOD=lz4
WALG_DELTA_MAX_STEPS=7

An archive command that silently succeeds on failure is worse than no archive. Returning exit status 0 without having written the segment leaves the database believing the segment is archived; recovery later discovers the gap and stops, but the gap may be beyond the point anyone checks. Make archive fail loudly — let the copy utility’s own exit status propagate, and use test ! -ftest ! -f plus a checksum verification before returning success. Monitor pg_stat_archiv as a hard alert with a page.

10.4 Streaming replication

Documents standby.signal, synchronous_standby_names, the feedback settings, and pg_receivewal as a lightweight log consumer.

Streaming replication is a physical replica: byte-for-byte identical data directory, kept current by the primary’s WAL sender streaming over the replication protocol. Creating one requires three things: a non-superuser role with REPLICATION, a pg_hb entry using the replication database, and a restore of a base backup followed by standby.signalstandby.signal. The standby must run on the same architecture and a compatible major version; PostgreSQL supports replication from an older major to a newer one for limited windows, but the supported combination for most operators is same major version on both sides.

Two feedback settings govern whether the replica can hold the primary back. hot_standb tells the primary how far in the past the standby’s queries have reached, so that vacuum cannot remove tuples the standby still needs; a standby running a long report with this on will eventually cause bloat on the primary, and the fix is to give the standby max_standby_streaming_delaymax_standby_streaming_delay. max_standby_s bounds how long replay may lag before the standby cancels its own queries, trading replay freshness for query availability.

-- Primary: replication role, pg_hba entry, and a slot so WAL is never discarded early.
CREATE ROLE replicator WITH REPLICATION LOGIN PASSWORD '...generated...';
SELECT * FROM pg_create_physical_replication_slot('standby_prod');
 slot_name    | lsn
--------------+-----------
 standby_prod | 0/B1C4A20

-- pg_hba.conf: hostssl, scram-sha-256, replication database, no trust.
hostssl  replicator  10.40.0.0/24  scram-sha-256
hostssl  all         all         10.40.0.0/24  scram-sha-256

-- postgresql.auto.conf on the primary.
wal_level = replica
wal_keep_size = 4GB
synchronous_standby_names = 'ANY 1 (standby_prod)'
synchronous_commit = remote_apply
max_slot_wal_keep_size = 16GB

-- postgresql.auto.conf on the standby. recovery.signal is standby.signal.
primary_conninfo = 'host=pg-primary port=5432 user=replicator password=... application_name=standby_prod'
primary_slot_name = 'standby_prod'
hot_standby_feedback = on
max_standby_streaming_delay = 30s
recovery_target_timeline = latest
hot_standby = on

-- Promote the standby: it becomes a primary and the old one is fenced.
$ pg_ctl -D /var/lib/postgresql/18/standby promote -w
server promoting
2025-04-02 03:15:11.882 UTC LOG:  pg_ctl: server promoting
2025-04-02 03:15:11.884 UTC LOG:  redo done at 0/A3003C40 system usage: CPU: user: 0.00 s
2025-04-02 03:15:11.886 UTC LOG:  selected new timeline ID: 2
-- The replication view, from the primary. Byte lag and flush lag are different things.
postgres=# SELECT s.pid, s.application_name, s.state, s.sync_state,
       client_addr, sent_lsn, write_lsn, flush_lsn, replay_lsn,
       pg_size_pretty(pg_wal_lsn_diff(pg_current_wal_lsn(), replay_lsn)) AS replay_lag
FROM pg_stat_replication s;
--     pid  | application_name |   state   | sync_state | sent_lsn | replay_lag
--    44102 | standby_prod     | streaming | sync       | 0/B1D1A40 | 8 kB

-- On the standby itself.
postgres=# SELECT pg_is_in_recovery(),
       pg_last_wal_receive_lsn()  AS received,
       pg_last_wal_replay_lsn()   AS replayed,
       pg_last_xact_replay_timestamp() AS last_replayed_tx;

-- pg_receivewal: a physical standby for log shipping only. No data directory.
$ pg_receivewal --directory=/archive/shipped \
                --no-loop --no-remotepgpass --endpos 0/A3003C40 \
                --dbname='host=pg-primary dbname=replication user=replicator'
# pg_receivewal writes a .history/.partial when --endpos or --endpos is reached,
# which lets a separate archive server take over archiving at that LSN.
sync_stateMeaningCommit latency on primaryZero data loss on failover
asWAL streamed, no acknowledgment requiredLocal flush onlyNo — recent commits may be lost
poteEligible under A, not currently in priority listLocal flush onlyNo
quoNamed under a quorum policyLocal flush onlyNo
syNamed first under FIAwaits standby flushYes if the standby’s storage is durable
remoteFirst under A, with remoteAwaits standby applyYes, including standby-side queries

Always use a replication slot, and bound it. wal_ke is a fixed allowance that fails in both directions — too small when the standby is briefly down, too large when it wastes archive volume. A physical slot pins exactly the LSN the standby needs; setting max_slot_wa caps the damage when a standby disappears, converting a primary outage into a replica outage. That is the correct trade for every environment except one that cannot tolerate a replica restart.

10.5 Logical replication

Documents PUBLICATION, SUBSCRIPTION, the pgoutput plugin, and the schema features logical replication cannot carry.

Logical replication ships a stream of row-level changes derived from the WAL, filtered by publication, applied by the subscriber into tables that already exist with the same shape. The publishing side needs wal_level = logicalwal_level = logical and a logical replication slot; the subscribing side needs a SUBSCRIPTION, which creates the slot, the replication worker and the apply process. The subscriber’s tables are created by the operator with pg_dump --schema-only or a migration tool — replication does not carry DDL.

The set of things logical replication does not carry is long and every one of them has caused a production incident. Sequences are not replicated, so serial columns on the subscriber advance independently and eventually collide with replicated values. TRUNCATE is not propagated by default. Schema changes are not propagated, so a column added on the publisher is silently ignored by the subscriber until someone replicates the DDL. Large statements are not atomic from the subscriber’s point of view. And REPLICA IDENTITYREPLICA IDENTITY governs whether updates and deletes can be applied at all: with the default identity the whole row must be sent, and with REPLICA IDENTITY FULL every update and delete carries the entire before-image, which is roughly double the payload for wide rows and defeats any attempt to keep the slot’s WAL retention low.

-- Publisher: wal_level must be logical; requires a restart.
wal_level = logical
max_replication_slots = 16
max_wal_senders = 16

-- A table published FOR ALL TABLES needs REPLICA IDENTITY to do updates/deletes.
ALTER TABLE public.customers REPLICA IDENTITY FULL;
-- Prefer a named unique index over FULL: only the index columns are shipped.
CREATE UNIQUE INDEX customers_email_uniq ON customers (email);
ALTER TABLE public.customers REPLICA IDENTITY USING INDEX customers_email_uniq;

CREATE PUBLICATION cdc_all FOR ALL TABLES;
-- Or a filtered, explicitly enumerated publication, which is far cheaper to decode.
CREATE PUBLICATION cdc_orders
    FOR TABLE orders, order_lines, customers
    WITH (publish = 'insert, update, delete', publish_via_partition_root = true);

-- Subscriber. The schema must already match.
$ pg_dump -h pg-primary --schema-only -t orders -t order_lines -t customers \
       | psql -h pg-analytics -d analytics

CREATE SUBSCRIPTION orders_sub
    CONNECTION 'host=pg-primary port=5432 dbname=prod user=cdc_user password=... application_name=orders_sub'
    PUBLICATION cdc_orders
    WITH (copy_data = true, create_slot = true, enabled = true, slot_name = 'orders_sub');
-- pgoutput options, set at subscription creation or altered later.
postgres=# SELECT subname, subpublications, subslotname, subenabled
FROM pg_subscription;
   subname    |    subpublications     |  subslotname  | subenabled
--------------+------------------------+---------------+------------
 orders_sub   | {cdc_orders}           | orders_sub    | t

-- Sequences are NOT replicated. Reset them on the subscriber after a bulk load.
postgres=# SELECT setval(pg_get_serial_sequence('orders', 'order_id'),
                    (SELECT max(order_id) FROM orders));
   setval
-----------
 91004422

-- Initial sync is a COPY per table; watch it from the subscriber.
postgres=# SELECT pid, application_name, state, sent_lsn, write_lsn, flush_lsn
FROM pg_stat_replication WHERE application_name LIKE '%orders_sub%';
-- state=streaming means the COPY finished and catch-up is in progress.

-- To ship everything but the noise: publish_via_partition_root and column lists.
CREATE PUBLICATION cdc_orders_colonly
    FOR TABLE (orders COLUMNS (order_id, customer_id, status, total_cents))
    WITH (publish_via_partition_root = true);

-- PostgreSQL 18: stored generated columns are now publishable to subscribers
-- that did not compute them. Virtual generated columns remain subscriber-local.
ALTER TABLE orders ADD COLUMN total_with_tax numeric
    GENERATED ALWAYS AS (total_cents * 1.21) STORED;
CapabilityPhysical streamingLogical replication
Schema and DDL carriedYes, identicalNo — replicate DDL out of band
SequencesYes, identical stateNo — reset manually on the subscriber
Unlogged tablesSkipped on standbyNot replicable
Large objectsYesNo
Row filtering / column subsetNoYes, per publication
Cross major versionSame major (limited windows)Yes, subscriber may be newer
Conflict handlingNone needed; replica is read-onlyManual; subscriber tables must be read-only to the app
Round trip costBytes on the wireDecoding CPU on both ends

10.6 Conflicts and correctness

Classifies replication conflicts, states the mitigations that exist in core, and explains why conflict-free replication is an architectural constraint.

A physical replica has no conflicts because nothing writes to it. A logical subscriber can have writes — the danger of a CDC or analytics target that accepts them — and every such write is a potential conflict that the apply worker resolves by doing the wrong thing quietly or by stopping. The classes are worth enumerating because each has a different mitigation, and only one of them is solved by configuration.

-- Detection: conflicts surface as errors in the apply worker's log, not as SQL errors
-- returned to any session. There is no per-subscription conflict counter; use this.
postgres=# SELECT subname, subenabled,
       (SELECT count(*) FROM pg_stat_replication
         WHERE application_name = s.subname) AS sender_up
FROM pg_subscription s;

-- An apply worker that has stalled on a conflict, from the server log:
--   ERROR:  55006: canceling statement due to conflicting lock on replicated relation
--   DETAIL:  Process 8812 waits for ShareLock on relation 16384.

-- The four conflict classes and their real mitigations.
-- 1. UPDATE on a row the subscriber also updated  -> do not allow writes to subscriber tables
-- 2. INSERT of a key that already exists          -> dedupe, or rebuild the target
-- 3. DELETE of a row the subscriber modified    -> same as 1
-- 4. DDL that does not match the subscriber's   -> freeze DDL or replicate it explicitly

-- Practical pattern: subscriber is strictly read-only for the application.
ALTER TABLE orders OWNER TO analytics_owner;
REVOKE INSERT, UPDATE, DELETE, TRUNCATE ON ALL TABLES IN SCHEMA public
FROM PUBLIC, app_writer;

-- Version guard: major version skew on the subscriber side.
postgres=# SELECT slot_name, plugin, slot_type, database,
       active, confirmed_flush_lsn
FROM pg_replication_slots WHERE slot_type = 'logical';

   slot_name  |  plugin   | slot_type | database | active | confirmed_flush_lsn
--------------+-----------+-----------+----------+--------+---------------------
 orders_sub   | pgoutput  | logical   | prod     | t      | 0/B1D2C88

PostgreSQL 17 and 18 improvements. PostgreSQL 17 added logical replication support for REPLICA IDENTITY FULL on tables with generated columns plus publish_via_p refinements that reduce the number of per-partition publications. PostgreSQL 18 continues that line: stored generated columns are now included in the replication stream, so CDC consumers such as Debezium and pgstream receive derived values directly instead of recomputing them, and MERGE statements can participate in logical decoding. None of these remove the fundamental restrictions on DDL, sequences and truncate.

Exercises 10

  1. Take a full pg_basebackup of a 100 GB database with --wal-method=stream, verify it with pg_verifybackup, then restore it onto a second host and record elapsed time to first successful query. Repeat the whole cycle as a PostgreSQL 17 incremental backup with pg_combinebackup and report the size and duration difference.
  2. Configure continuous archiving to object storage with archive_timeout = 60. Then recover the base backup to a timestamp 20 minutes in the past, capturing the full postgresql.auto.conf, the resulting timeline ID, and proof from pg_last_wal_replay_lsn() that replay stopped at the intended point. Document what you did with the original primary afterwards.
  3. Build a synchronous physical standby using a replication slot with max_slot_wal_keep_size set, and a second asynchronous standby for reporting with hot_standby_feedback = on. Run a 30-minute report on the async standby and measure the resulting bloat on the primary. Then configure max_standby_streaming_delay and repeat.
  4. Set up logical replication from a partitioned primary to a subscriber running one major version newer, using pgoutput with a column list. Demonstrate and document each of the four things that do not replicate: DDL, sequences, TRUNCATE, and large objects. Produce the compensating script for each.
  5. Force a replication conflict by writing directly to a subscribed table on the subscriber, and capture the exact server log message, the pg_stat_replication state at the moment of failure, and the recovery procedure. Compare the REPLICA IDENTITY FULL and USING INDEXUSING INDEX slot retention footprints on a wide table.

11 · Connection Management, Pooling and High Availability

11.1 The connection lifecycle11.2 PgBouncer11.3 Pool sizing and the saturation model11.4 Availability architecture11.5 Failover mechanics11.6 Monitoring the pool

PostgreSQL’s process-per-connection model means that connections are expensive in a specific way: each backend is a forked operating-system process with its own memory arenas, and the number that can coexist is bounded by both max_con and the operating system’s process limit. That budget must be reserved for the components that must always be able to connect — replication, autovacuum, the checkpointer, monitoring agents and the operator holding a session during an incident. This chapter derives the sizing arithmetic, describes PgBouncer’s three pooling modes and the semantics each one changes, and lays out the availability topologies that PostgreSQL natively supports, together with the tooling that implements them. The recurring lesson is that the most common database outage is not a crash but exhaustion of the connection budget by an application that opens a connection per request.

11.1 The connection lifecycle

Describes the cost of a backend process, the authentication handshake, and the settings that bound connection count.

Every client connection forks a backend process. The fork itself is cheap on a copy-on-write operating system, but the backend then allocates its own work_mem per sort or hash node, per parallel worker, and per active cursor, and it holds at least max_sta of stack. Two hundred idle connections are not expensive; two hundred connections each running three parallel workers with four hash nodes are a memory incident. The relevant budget is therefore not connection count but the product of connection count, per-backend memory settings, and query shape, and it must be reconciled against shared_buffers so that the operating system has memory left for everything else.

Authentication adds a measurable round trip per connection. scram-sha-256 is the default from PostgreSQL 14 and requires no cleartext password in pg_hba.conf; md5 is still accepted for compatibility but should be removed. TLS negotiation adds a further handshake unless the session is resumed, which is why connection-level caching in a pooler is worth configuring. Every connection also runs a set of startup-time queries for default privileges and role settings, and those are cheap individually and significant in aggregate.

-- The connection budget, stated as an accounting identity.
postgres=# SELECT max_connections, superuser_reserved_connections,
       reserved_connections AS pg18_reserved,
       max_connections - superuser_reserved_connections AS normal_conns
FROM pg_settings CROSS JOIN (SELECT 0 AS reserved_connections) r
WHERE name = 'max_connections';

 max_connections | superuser_reserved_connections | normal_conns
-----------------+-------------------------------+--------------
 200             | 3                             | 197

-- postgresql.conf: leave headroom for the components that must always connect.
max_connections = 200
superuser_reserved_connections = 5
reserved_connections = 5          -- PostgreSQL 18, for background workers
shared_buffers = 32GB
work_mem = 16MB                  -- x 2 x parallel workers x active nodes, not x sessions
maintenance_work_mem = 2GB
max_worker_processes = 24
max_parallel_workers = 8
max_parallel_workers_per_gather = 4

-- Current usage and, more importantly, what is currently in each state.
postgres=# SELECT state, count(*), count(*) FILTER (WHERE wait_event_type = 'Lock') AS waiting_on_lock
FROM pg_stat_activity GROUP BY 1 ORDER BY 2 DESC;

       state       | count | waiting_on_lock
-------------------+-------+----------------
 active             |    42 |             12
 idle in transaction |     7 |              0
 idle               |   118 |              0
-- One connection per HTTP request: the pattern that exhausts a database.
postgres=# SELECT count(*) AS client_conns,
       round(100.0 * count(*) /
             current_setting('max_connections')::int, 1) AS pct_of_budget,
       count(DISTINCT application_name)                      AS distinct_clients,
       round(avg(now() - backend_start)::numeric, 0)         AS avg_conn_age_s
FROM pg_stat_activity
WHERE backend_type = 'client backend';

-- Terminating what is over budget, oldest first, without touching replication.
postgres=# SELECT pg_terminate_backend(pid)
FROM (
  SELECT pid, row_number() OVER (ORDER BY backend_start)
  FROM pg_stat_activity
  WHERE datname = 'prod'
    AND backend_type = 'client backend'
    AND pid <> pg_backend_pid()
    AND state <> 'idle in transaction (failed)'
) s WHERE rn > 150;

-- Timeouts, in order of preference per workload class.
idle_in_transaction_session_timeout = 60s   -- the vacuum-protecting one
statement_timeout = 30s                   -- interactive role
lock_timeout = 5s                        -- inside application transactions
deadlock_timeout = 1s                    -- deadlock detection interval

postgres=# ALTER ROLE app_reports SET statement_timeout = '15min';
postgres=# ALTER ROLE app_reports SET idle_in_transaction_session_timeout = '300s';

Role-level timeouts beat global ones. A reporting role that legitimately runs for twelve minutes and an interactive role that should never exceed thirty seconds cannot share a statement_timeoutstatement_timeout. Set the global value to the strictest requirement and override it upward with ALTER ROLE ... SET for the roles that need longer. The same split applies to lock_t, which should be set inside every application transaction rather than globally because it interacts with waiting on the application’s own locks.

11.2 PgBouncer

Explains transaction, session and statement pooling modes, and the semantics each one breaks for the application.

PgBouncer is a lightweight connection pooler that maintains a fixed number of PostgreSQL connections and multiplexes many client connections over them. Its efficiency comes from the pooling mode, and each mode changes the contract the application must satisfy. In transaction mode, a server connection is assigned for the duration of a transaction and returned afterwards; prepared statements survive only if max_prepared_statements is non-zero, because the server-side prepared statement belongs to the server connection that created it and must therefore be tracked and re-bound on reuse. In session mode, the server connection is held for the lifetime of the client connection, which preserves every session-level feature but gives up most of the multiplexing benefit. In statement mode, the server connection is returned after every statement, which forbids multi-statement transactions and is rarely appropriate.

Transaction mode also breaks LIS/NOT delivery to the pooled connection, because the notification is delivered to whichever server connection is holding the listening socket at the time. PgBouncer 1.21 and later route notifications to the correct client connection, but applications that depend on LIS for cache invalidation should be verified against the deployed version rather than assumed.

-- /etc/pgbouncer/pgbouncer.ini
[databases]
prod       = host=10.40.0.11 port=5432 dbname=prod      pool_size=40
analytics  = host=10.40.0.12 port=5432 dbname=analytics pool_size=8
# Every non-pooled setting must be given its own [databases] entry with a distinct
# pool_size, otherwise the global value is silently reused.

[pgbouncer]
listen_addr = 0.0.0.0
listen_port = 6432
auth_type = scram-sha-256
auth_file = /etc/pgbouncer/userlist.txt
pool_mode = transaction
max_client_conn = 1000
default_pool_size = 40
min_pool_size = 10
reserve_pool_size = 10
reserve_pool_timeout = 3
max_db_connections = 60
max_user_connections = 60
server_lifetime = 3600
server_idle_timeout = 600
query_wait_timeout = 120
max_prepared_statements = 200     -- required for transaction mode + prepared stmts
ignore_startup_parameters = extra_float_digits
log_connections = 0
log_disconnections = 0
log_pooler_errors = 1

-- SHOW POOLS: the table that answers "is the pool the bottleneck?"
$ psql -p 6432 -U pgbouncer pgbouncer
pgbouncer# SHOW POOLS;
   database   |   user   | cl_active | cl_waiting | sv_active | sv_idle | sv_used | maxwait
--------------+----------+-----------+------------+-----------+----------+---------+---------
 prod         | app_prod |       142 |          38 |        40 |        0 |       0 | 11.4
 prod         | reporting|         2 |           0 |         1 |        1 |       0 | 0.0
 analytics    | app_prod |         4 |           0 |         3 |        2 |       0 | 0.0

-- cl_waiting > 0 sustained means the pool is the constraint, not the database.

Session state is not portable across transactions. In transaction mode, S, LIS, advisory locks held across statements, cursors spanning statements, and server-side prepared statements are all connection-scoped state that the pooler may hand to a different client. Advisory locks held with pg_advis inside a transaction are fine; held outside one they are a correctness bug under pooling. The failures are intermittent and appear only under load, which is exactly the profile of a bug that reaches production.

-- PgHero-style health query, run against the database rather than the pooler.
postgres=# SELECT now() AS checked_at,
       (SELECT count(*) FROM pg_stat_activity
         WHERE state <> 'idle')                        AS busy,
       (SELECT count(*) FROM pg_stat_activity)                            AS total,
       (SELECT max(now() - query_start) FROM pg_stat_activity
         WHERE state = 'active')                             AS longest_query,
       (SELECT count(*) FROM pg_replication_slots
         WHERE wal_status <> 'ok')                       AS bad_slots,
       (SELECT count(*) FROM pg_stat_activity
         WHERE state = 'idle in transaction'
           AND now() - state_change > '1 min')              AS stuck_tx;

-- PgHero checks live on the /var/lib/pgsql/data/pghero or in pghero_procs:
$ pghero check --once
PASS  DB Version           PostgreSQL 18.0 on x86_64-pc-linux-gnu
PASS  Server Load          2.1%  ·  0.71  ·  0.92
FAIL  Connections (2%)     5 / 200
PASS  Repl                1 standby · 8 kB lag · sync
PASS  DNS                  0.4ms
PASS  Write Latency        0.4ms
FAIL  Replication Slots    cdc_orders retained 41 GB
WARN  autovac             2 / 3 running, 41 queued

-- pghero_procs entry for an external check such as a replication slot guard.
/usr/lib/postgresql/18/bin/psql -Atqc \
  "SELECT 1 FROM pg_replication_slots WHERE wal_status <> 'ok' LIMIT 1" \
  -h localhost -U postgres postgres

11.3 Pool sizing and the saturation model

Derives pool size from measured throughput and latency, and identifies the failure mode where a small pool improves average latency and destroys tail latency.

Pool size should be derived, not chosen. Throughput against a database saturates at a concurrency level N for a given workload; beyond N, adding concurrent sessions adds queueing delay without adding throughput. The right pool size is that N plus enough to cover the variance in request duration, and the classic failure is sizing the pool well below N, which artificially serialises a workload that could have run in parallel and makes the average look good while the ninety-ninth percentile is dominated by pool waits.

The measurement to run is a concurrency sweep: hold the request rate constant, vary client concurrency from one to four times the database’s saturation point, and record throughput, mean latency and the ninety-ninth percentile at each step. The knee of the throughput curve is the database’s N; the point at which p99 begins to rise faster than p50 is where the pool should sit. For a CPU-bound OLTP workload N is close to the core count; for an I/O-bound workload it is roughly the ratio of device queue depth to request size, and is usually much larger.

-- Saturation point from the server side: where CPU stops tracking active count.
postgres=# SELECT date_trunc('minute', a.sample) AS minute,
       round(avg(active))                        AS avg_active,
       round(100 * avg(cpu_user + cpu_system) / 100, 1) AS avg_cpu_pct,
       round(avg(reads))                          AS avg_buffers,
       round(avg(write_time) / NULLIF(avg(writes), 0) * 1000, 2) AS avg_write_ms
FROM admin.conn_samples a GROUP BY 1 ORDER BY 1 DESC LIMIT 10;

      minute       | avg_active | avg_cpu_pct | avg_buffers | avg_write_ms
-------------------+------------+-------------+-------------+---------------
 2025-04-02 03:41 |       31.4 |        96.2 |       88412 |        0.42
 2025-04-02 03:40 |       28.1 |        88.4 |       71020 |        0.44

-- Pool-side view: cl_waiting is the leading indicator of a too-small pool.
pgbouncer# SHOW STATS;
 total_xact_count | total_query_count | total_received | total_sent | total_xact_time | total_wait_time
-----------------+-------------------+----------------+------------+-----------------+------------------
         4820114 |          19280456 |      918221044 | 4129088111 |        412882.21 |           8812.44

-- total_wait_time is time spent queued inside the pool. It is not zero in a
-- healthy system; it is small relative to total_xact_time. Ratio here: 2.1%.

pgbouncer# SHOW CLIENTS;
   type |  user  |   database |   state   |   addr   | port |     link
-------+--------+------------+-----------+----------+------+----------------
 C     | app    | prod       | waiting   | 10.8.0.4 | 51422| p
 C     | app    | prod       | active    | 10.8.0.4 | 51890| p
LayerKnobEffect of increasingSymptom when wrong
ApplicationPool size per instanceMore concurrency to PgBouncerN × instances > PgBouncer max_cli work
PgBouncerdefault_More server backends, more memoryUnreachable CPU saturation, p99 collapse
PgBouncerreserve_Headroom for bursts above the poolNever used if reserve_po is too high
Servermax_conRoom for poolers and agentsProcess/memory exhaustion, no room to connect during an incident
Servershared_More cache, slower startupPostgreSQL 18 with AIO prefers a larger working set than a huge one
ServerworkFaster sorts, OOM riskEstimated work_mem × active nodes × parallel workers × connections
OSulimit -nulimit -nMore accepted sockets"could not accept connection: Too many open files"

Size for the incident, not the average. A pool tuned to 40 per pooler is a pool that cannot absorb the reconnect stampede after a failover, a deployment or a connection loss. Compute the pool so that the total across all poolers plus the reserved connections leaves at least 20 % of max_con for operators, monitoring and replication — and verify it by actually pulling a cable during a rehearsal.

11.4 Availability architecture

Compares single primary with replicas, synchronous replication, multi-primary via logical replication, and quorum consensus, and states what each guarantees.

PostgreSQL has no multi-primary write capability at the physical level. The replication cluster is one writer with any number of read replicas, and automatic failover is provided by the surrounding system rather than by the database. Everything else in this section is a consequence of that fact, and the selection between them is a selection between data-loss windows, write latency and operational complexity.

-- Synchronous replication, priority 1: at least one of the named standbys acknowledges.
synchronous_standby_names = 'FIRST 2 (standby_a, standby_b, standby_c)'
synchronous_commit = remote_write

-- Quorum: any 2 of 3. Tolerates the loss of any one standby.
synchronous_standby_names = 'ANY 2 (standby_a, standby_b, standby_c)'
synchronous_commit = remote_apply

-- Verify who is actually being waited for, from the primary.
postgres=# SELECT application_name, sync_state, sync_priority,
       pg_size_pretty(pg_wal_lsn_diff(pg_current_wal_lsn(), replay_lsn)) AS lag
FROM pg_stat_replication ORDER BY sync_priority;

   application_name | sync_state | sync_priority |  lag
-------------------+------------+--------------+-------
 standby_a         | sync       |            1 |  0 kB
 standby_b         | potential  |            2 | 12 kB
 standby_c         | quorum     |            3 |  4 kB

-- Degraded synchronous state: the primary is waiting on nobody and still commits.
postgres=# SELECT pg_is_in_recovery(),
       (SELECT count(*) FROM pg_stat_replication WHERE state = 'streaming') AS streaming;
 pg_is_in_recovery | streaming
-------------------+-----------
 f                 |         0

-- Quorum consensus: the primary steps down when it loses the vote.
-- etcd/Consul members are the evidence that a node may serve as primary.
$ export PGTARGET_MEMBER='postgres-1,postgres-2,postgres-3'
$ patronictl -c /etc/patroni/patroni.yml list
+ Cluster: batman (7306221093447065600) ---+----+-----------+
| Member   | Host           | Role    | State     | TL | Lag in MB |
+----------+----------------+---------+-----------+----+-----------+
| postgres-1| 10.40.0.11     | Leader  | running   |  4 |          0 |
| postgres-2| 10.40.0.12     | Replica | streaming |  4 |          0 |
| postgres-3| 10.40.0.13     | Replica | streaming |  4 |          1 |
+----------+----------------+---------+-----------+----+-----------+
TopologyRPORTOWrite latencyComplexity
Single primary, local backupBackup interval (hours)Manual restore (hours)None addedLow
Primary + async replicasReplication lag (seconds)Manual promotion (minutes)None addedLow
Primary + synchronous replicaZeroManual promotion (minutes)One network RTTMedium
Patroni / repmgrd, sync or quorumZero15–60 s automaticOne RTT, or none if asyncHigh
Multi-primary via logical replicationSeconds, per channelApplication-dependentConflict handling requiredVery high
Managed service (RDS/Aurora/Cloud SQL)Zero with sync groups30–120 s automaticVaries by regionLowest

Synchronous replication with one standby and no A policy is a availability trap. If the synchronous standby becomes unreachable, the primary either blocks every commit until synchronous_c expires or, with synchronous_standby_names emptied, silently falls back to asynchronous behaviour and loses its zero-loss guarantee. Configure at least two named standbys under ANY, set synchronous_commit_timeout deliberately, and alert on the moment the number of synchronous standbys drops below the configured quorum.

11.5 Failover mechanics

Walks through promotion, the fencing problem, DNS or virtual-IP handover, and the checks that must be run before traffic is admitted.

Failover is a sequence with a hazardous middle. A standby becomes primary only when the old primary is guaranteed not to still be accepting writes, because two writable primaries on the same timeline produce divergent history that neither can recover from. Physical replication cannot detect this: a disconnected primary does not know it has been demoted and continues to accept writes if clients can still reach it. Fencing is therefore the responsibility of the orchestrator — a STONITH mechanism at the hypervisor or cloud API level, a storage-level fence, or a routing change that removes the old primary’s address before the new one is advertised.

After promotion the new primary needs a sequence of state repairs before it is safe to serve: reset the archived recovery configuration, re-point logical subscriptions, verify that the old primary’s writes are gone, and confirm that no replication slot is holding the old timeline. pg_rewind exists for the case where the old primary can be brought back — pg_rewind scans the diverged relation files and copies only the blocks that differ, which is far cheaper than a re-base backup and is the standard Patroni repair procedure.

-- Manual promotion of a standby, and the checks that follow.
$ pg_ctl -D /var/lib/postgresql/18/standby promote -w
server promoting
$ psql -c 'SELECT pg_is_in_recovery()'
 pg_is_in_recovery
------------------
 f

-- 1. Remove the recovery configuration so a restart cannot re-enter recovery.
ALTER SYSTEM RESET primary_conninfo;
ALTER SYSTEM RESET primary_slot_name;
ALTER SYSTEM RESET restore_command;
ALTER SYSTEM RESET recovery_target_time;
$ rm -f $PGDATA/standby.signal
# Note: ALTER SYSTEM RESET on restore_command when archive_mode=always is required,
# otherwise the promoted primary silently stops archiving.

-- 2. Confirm the timeline advanced and the old one is history.
postgres=# SELECT timeline_id FROM pg_control_checkpoint();
 timeline_id
------------
           2
$ cat $PGDATA/pg_wal/00000002.history
1 0/A3003C40 no recovery target specified

-- 3. Repair the old primary rather than rebuilding it.
$ pg_ctl -D /var/lib/postgresql/18/old_primary stop -m immediate
$ pg_rewind --target-pgdata=/var/lib/postgresql/18/old_primary \
            --source-server='host=10.40.0.12 user=postgres dbname=postgres'
servers diverged at WAL location 0/A3003C40 on timeline 1
no rewind required
-- then reconfigure it as a standby and restart; it rejoins at timeline 2.
-- Post-failover verification, run before any traffic is admitted.
postgres=# SELECT now() AS promoted_at,
       pg_is_in_recovery()                                     AS is_primary,
       (SELECT count(*) FROM pg_replication_slots
         WHERE slot_type = 'physical' AND active) AS active_slots,
       (SELECT count(*) FROM pg_replication
         WHERE state = 'streaming')                 AS standbys,
       (SELECT count(*) FROM pg_stat_archiver
         WHERE last_archived_wal IS NOT NULL)        AS archiving_ok;

-- Confirm sequence health: a promoted primary loses cached sequence values.
postgres=# SELECT sequencename, last_value, cache_size
FROM pg_sequences
WHERE schemaname = 'public' ORDER BY last_value DESC LIMIT 5;

-- Patroli/HAProxy topology handover. VIP or DNS is the client-visible switch.
$ curl -sf http://10.40.0.11:8000/primary | jq .
{
  "role": "master",
  "conn_url": "postgres://10.40.0.12:5432/postgres",
  "timeline": 2,
  "xlog_position": 2214567816
}

-- The fencing check that must exist before promotion is allowed.
$ patronictl -c /etc/patroni/patroni.yml switchover --leader postgres-2 --force
# Patroni verifies the old leader is stopped (or fenced) before it promotes.
No standby found for switchover, continuing with 'promote' operation
Successfully switched over to "postgres-2"

Split brain is unrecoverable by design. If a split brain did occur, the only correct repair is pg_rewind of the discarded primary, which discards every transaction it accepted after the divergence point. Decide before the incident who is authorised to choose which primary survives, because that decision will be made under time pressure by someone who does not have the information needed to make it.

11.6 Monitoring the pool

Assembles the connection and availability metrics that matter, the alert thresholds that are defensible, and the queries that produce them.

The metric set for connection management is small and should be exported at high resolution — one-minute granularity, no averaging beyond the maximum. numba against max_con with a threshold at 80 %. cl_wa from PgBouncer, with a threshold at zero sustained for five minutes. synchronous_ against the configured quorum. pg_replication_ and safe_wal_sizesafe_wal_size. age(relfrozenxid)age(relfrozenxid) maximum across user tables. failed from pg_stat_archiver, with a threshold of zero.

-- A single-row snapshot suitable for a pushgateway or exporter.
postgres=# SELECT
  (SELECT count(*) FROM pg_stat_activity
    WHERE backend_type = 'client backend')                       AS client_backends,
  (SELECT count(*) FROM pg_stat_activity
    WHERE state = 'idle in transaction')                          AS idle_in_txn,
  (SELECT count(*) FROM pg_stat_activity
    WHERE wait_event_type = 'Lock')                                  AS lock_waits,
  (SELECT max(now() - xact_start) FROM pg_stat_activity
    WHERE state = 'idle in transaction')                          AS longest_idle_txn,
  (SELECT count(*) FROM pg_replication
    WHERE state = 'streaming')                                   AS streaming_replicas,
  (SELECT count(*) FROM pg_replication
    WHERE state = 'streaming' AND sync_state IN ('sync','quorum')) AS sync_replicas,
  (SELECT count(*) FROM pg_replication_slots
    WHERE wal_status <> 'ok')                                 AS slot_pressure,
  (SELECT max(age(relfrozenxid)) FROM pg_stat_user_tables)                                AS max_xid_age,
  (SELECT failed_count FROM pg_stat_archiver)                                          AS archive_failures,
  (SELECT count(*) FROM pg_stat_activity
    WHERE backend_type = 'autovacuum worker')                       AS autovac_workers;

-- Lock-wait detail, joined to the blocker. The standard incident query.
postgres=# SELECT blocked.pid AS blocked_pid,
       blocked.usename AS blocked_user,
       now() - blocked.query_start AS blocked_for,
       left(blocked.query, 120)     AS blocked_query,
       blocker.pid                   AS blocker_pid,
       blocker.state                AS blocker_state,
       left(blocker.query, 120)     AS blocker_query
FROM pg_stat_activity blocked
JOIN pg_locks bl ON bl.pid = blocked.pid AND bl.locktype = 'transactionid'
JOIN pg_locks kl ON kl.locktype = bl.locktype AND kl.transactionid = bl.transactionid
JOIN pg_stat_activity blocker ON blocker.pid = kl.pid AND blocker.pid <> blocked.pid
WHERE NOT bl.granted AND NOT kl.granted;

Alert on ratios, not absolutes. numbackends > 150numbackends > 150 means nothing on a 200-connection cluster and everything on a 40-connection one. Alert on numbackends / max_connections > 0.8numbackends / max_connections > 0.8, on cl_waiting > 0cl_waiting > 0 sustained, and on sync_replicas < configured_quorumsync_replicas < configured_quorum. Those three conditions predict the incidents that actually happen, and they do not require anyone to remember what the absolute numbers were.

Exercises 11

  1. Derive max_con for a service with 6 application instances, each running a pool of 30, plus 4 PgBouncer instances, 3 monitoring agents, 2 standbys and 3 operator sessions. Show the arithmetic against superuser_reserved_connections and reserved_connections, then load-test to find the actual saturation point.
  2. Run a concurrency sweep from 5 to 200 clients at constant offered load, recording throughput, mean, p95 and p99 latency at each step. Identify the database’s saturation concurrency, size the pool from the p99 knee, and demonstrate the queueing collapse caused by deliberately undersizing the pool by half.
  3. Deploy PgBouncer in transaction mode against an application that uses prepared statements, cursors, advisory locks and LISTEN/NOTIFY. For each feature, either make it work with the correct PgBouncer configuration or document the failure and the required application change, with the log evidence.
  4. Build a two-node synchronous replication setup with ANY 1, kill the standby, and measure what happens to commit latency under synchronous_c of 5 seconds. Then repeat with ANY 1ANY 1 over two standbys and show that commit latency is unaffected by the loss of one.
  5. Perform a full failover rehearsal: fence the primary, promote the standby, verify the timeline, repair the old primary with pg_rewind, and rejoin it. Record the measured RTO for each step and identify which step dominates, then write the runbook entry that makes the whole sequence a single documented procedure.

12 · Security, Roles and Row-Level Security

12.1 Roles and attributes12.2 Privileges and default privileges12.3 Row-level security12.4 Transport security and authentication12.5 Auditing12.6 Hardening

PostgreSQL’s authorization model is layered and, in order, is connection authentication, role membership, schema and table privileges, column privileges, row-level security policies, and finally the rights that a SECURITY DEFINER routine holds on the caller’s behalf. Every layer must be configured deliberately because the defaults are permissive in one specific and consequential way: a freshly created database grants USAGE and CREATE on the pub schema to the PUBLIC pseudo-role, which means every role that can connect can create objects there. This chapter works from the role definition outwards, establishes the privilege baseline that must be revoked on day one, then covers row-level security as the multi-tenant control, TLS and authentication as the transport control, auditing as the detective control, and a hardening checklist that ties them together.

12.1 Roles and attributes

Documents the CREATE ROLE attributes, group roles, and the privilege boundaries that superuser bypasses.

PostgreSQL has one kind of principal: the role. A role with LOGIN can connect; a role without it exists only to be granted privileges and assumed through SET ROLE. There is no separate user/group distinction, which makes the group-role pattern natural: define app_re and app_w with NOLOGIN, grant them the table privileges, and grant those group roles to as many login roles as required. Changing what the application can do is then a GRANT or REVOKE on one object rather than a change to every user.

The role attributes are independent flags and the safe combination is worth stating explicitly. NOSUPERUSER is non-negotiable: a superuser bypasses every privilege check, every row-level policy, and can read and write any table in any database. NOCREATEDB and NOCREATEROLE bound the blast radius of a compromised application credential. NOINHERIT means the role does not automatically receive the privileges of roles it is a member of, which is the setting to use for a role that must explicitly SET ROLE to escalate. VALID UNTIL sets an expiry, which is the mechanism for time-boxed contractor and break-glass access.

-- The role baseline. Nothing here should be granted to an application.
CREATE ROLE app_rw
    WITH LOGIN NOSUPERUSER NOCREATEDB NOCREATEROLE NOINHERIT
         CONNECTION LIMIT 200
         VALID UNTIL '2027-01-01 00:00:00+00';
CREATE ROLE app_readonly      WITH NOLOGIN NOSUPERUSER;
CREATE ROLE analytics_ro       WITH NOLOGIN NOSUPERUSER;
CREATE ROLE break_glass        WITH LOGIN BYPASSRLS VALID UNTIL '2026-03-31 00:00:00+00';

-- Login role with NOINHERIT: privileges arrive only through an explicit SET ROLE.
CREATE ROLE svc_orders
    WITH LOGIN PASSWORD '...' NOSUPERUSER NOCREATEDB NOCREATEROLE NOINHERIT;
GRANT app_rw TO svc_orders;

-- Scram-SHA-256 verifier is stored; the cleartext is not, after this runs.
SET password_encryption = 'scram-sha-256';
ALTER ROLE svc_orders PASSWORD 'correct-horse-battery-staple';

postgres=# SELECT rolname, rolsuper, rolinherit, rolcreaterole, rolcreatedb,
       rolcanlogin, rolreplication, rolbypassrls, rolconnlimit, rolvaliduntil
FROM pg_roles WHERE rolname IN ('app_rw', 'svc_orders', 'break_glass');

   rolname    | rolsuper | rolinherit | rolcanlogin | rolbypassrls | rolconnlimit |   rolvaliduntil
--------------+----------+------------+-------------+--------------+--------------+--------------------
 svc_orders   | f        | f          | t           | f            |           -1 |
 app_rw       | f        | t          | f           | f            |           -1 |
 break_glass  | f        | t          | t           | t            |            3 | 2026-03-31 00:00:00+00
-- Every role that can log in, with its effective superuser/replication/RLS bypass status.
postgres=# SELECT r.rolname, r.rolconnlimit, m.member,
       g.rolname AS member_of,
       array_to_string(r.rolconfig, ',') AS role_settings
FROM pg_roles r
LEFT JOIN pg_auth_members am ON am.member = r.oid
LEFT JOIN pg_roles g                ON g.oid = am.roleid
WHERE r.rolcanlogin
ORDER BY r.rolname;

-- Privileges held transitively, resolving one level of membership.
postgres=# SELECT member.rolname AS role,
       grantee.rolname AS granted_to,
       table_schema, table_name, privilege_type
FROM information_schema.role_table_grants g
JOIN pg_roles member  ON member.rolname = g.grantor
JOIN pg_roles grantee ON grantee.rolname = g.grantee
ORDER BY 1, 4, 5 LIMIT 12;

      role      |  granted_to  | table_schema | table_name | privilege_type
---------------+--------------+--------------+------------+----------------
 svc_orders    | app_rw       | public       | orders     | INSERT
 svc_orders    | app_rw       | public       | orders     | SELECT
 app_rw        | PUBLIC       | public       | sessions   | SELECT

-- Lock down the session attributes an application cannot be trusted to set itself.
ALTER ROLE svc_orders SET search_path = billing, pg_catalog, pg_temp;
ALTER ROLE svc_orders SET statement_timeout = '30s';
ALTER ROLE svc_orders SET idle_in_transaction_session_timeout = '60s';
ALTER DATABASE prod REVOKE ALL ON SCHEMA public FROM svc_orders;

NOINHERIT plus SET ROLE is not a security boundary. A role with INHERIT — or a role that can SET ROLE into a privileged role — can exercise that role’s privileges for the remainder of the session. NOINHERIT delays escalation until the application explicitly asks for it, which is useful for auditing and useless against a determined attacker with a valid credential. Real boundaries come from separate databases, separate roles with distinct credentials, and row-level security.

12.2 Privileges and default privileges

Revokes the default PUBLIC grants, documents ALTER DEFAULT PRIVILEGES, and shows column-level grants.

Object creation is not privilege; the ability to create objects in a schema is. The public schema is created with USAGE and CREATE granted to PUBLIC, which means that in a database that has not been hardened, any role with CONNECT can create a table, a function or an operator class in a schema everyone else uses. A function created by an attacker in that schema is an immediate route to privilege escalation through a search-path hijack. Removing that grant is the first action of any hardening runbook, and it must be done in every database, not just in template1, because it is per-database state.

-- 1. Record what exists before changing it.
postgres=# SELECT table_schema, table_name, privilege_type, grantee
FROM information_schema.role_table_grants
WHERE grantee = 'PUBLIC' ORDER BY 1, 2, 3;

-- 2. Take CREATE away from PUBLIC; keep USAGE so existing code resolves.
postgres=# REVOKE CREATE ON SCHEMA public FROM PUBLIC;
-- PostgreSQL 15+: apply the change to new databases as well.
postgres=# ALTER DATABASE prod REVOKE CREATE ON SCHEMA public FROM PUBLIC;

-- 3. Application objects live in their own schema, owned by a non-login owner role.
CREATE SCHEMA billing AUTHORIZATION app_owner;
REVOKE ALL ON SCHEMA billing FROM PUBLIC;
GRANT USAGE ON SCHEMA billing TO app_rw, analytics_ro;

-- 4. Default privileges for objects created later, by the owner role only.
ALTER DEFAULT PRIVILEGES FOR ROLE app_owner IN SCHEMA billing
    REVOKE ALL ON TABLES FROM PUBLIC;
ALTER DEFAULT PRIVILEGES FOR ROLE app_owner IN SCHEMA billing
    GRANT SELECT ON TABLES TO analytics_ro;
ALTER DEFAULT PRIVILEGES FOR ROLE app_owner IN SCHEMA billing
    GRANT INSERT, UPDATE ON TABLES TO app_rw;
-- Note the asymmetry: app_rw gets no DELETE and no TRUNCATE by default.

-- 5. Column-level grants: read most columns, write only the writable ones.
GRANT SELECT ON billing.customers TO analytics_ro;
GRANT SELECT (customer_id, display_name, created_at) ON billing.customers TO analytics_ro;
REVOKE SELECT (national_id, date_of_birth, email) ON billing.customers FROM app_rw;

-- 6. Privileges never cascade through views by default in modern PostgreSQL.
CREATE VIEW billing.customer_names WITH (security_invoker = true) AS
SELECT customer_id, display_name FROM billing.customers;
-- security_invoker=true makes the view run with the caller's privileges, so the
-- column grants above are enforced. The default grants the view owner access.
-- Audit: every privilege the application role holds, resolved.
postgres=# SELECT table_schema, table_name,
       string_agg(DISTINCT privilege_type, ', ' ORDER BY privilege_type) AS privileges
FROM information_schema.role_table_grants
WHERE grantee = 'app_rw' OR (grantee = 'PUBLIC' AND table_schema = 'billing')
GROUP BY 1, 2 ORDER BY 1, 2;

 table_schema | table_name   |              privileges
--------------+--------------+---------------------------------------
 billing      | customers    | INSERT, SELECT, UPDATE
 billing      | invoices     | INSERT, SELECT, UPDATE
 billing      | audit_log    | INSERT, SELECT

-- Anything granted to PUBLIC is a finding, not a style issue.
postgres=# SELECT nspname, obj_type, count(*) AS public_grants
FROM (
  SELECT n.nspname, 'table' AS obj_type, c.relname
  FROM pg_class c JOIN pg_namespace n ON n.oid = c.relnamespace
  CROSS JOIN LATERAL aclexplode(c.relacl) a
  WHERE a.grantee = 0 AND c.relkind = 'r'
  UNION ALL
  SELECT n.nspname, 'function', p.proname
  FROM pg_proc p JOIN pg_namespace n ON n.oid = p.pronamespace
  CROSS JOIN LATERAL aclexplode(p.proacl) a WHERE a.grantee = 0
) s GROUP BY 1, 2 ORDER BY 3 DESC;

  nspname   | obj_type  | public_grants
------------+-----------+---------------
 billing    | table     |             0
 public     | table     |             1   -- audit_log, from an older deployment

ALTER DEFAULT PRIVILEGES only affects objects created after it runs, by the named role. It does not retroactively change existing tables, and it is scoped to the role whose objects are being created — the migration tool’s role, not the table owner’s role, unless you say FOR ROLE. Run it from the same role that performs migrations, or the defaults will silently not apply to production objects.

12.3 Row-level security

Documents ENABLE ROW LEVEL SECURITY versus FORCE, permissive and restrictive policies, and the USING and WITH CHECK split.

Row-level security filters rows by a policy rather than a predicate applied by the query. It is enforced by the executor, not by the planner, so a policy cannot be bypassed by writing a different query — including one written by someone who has read the schema. A policy has two clauses with different jobs. USING filters which existing rows are visible to SELECT, UPDATE and DELETE. WITH CHECK validates rows being written by INSERT and UPDATE, and defaults to the USING expression when omitted — which is a trap, because a USING-only policy that permits reading a tenant’s rows but not moving a row into that tenant will silently accept the move if no separate check is written.

The second trap is ENABLE versus FORCE. ALTER TABLE ... ENABLE ROW LEVEL SECURITY makes policies apply to every role except the table owner and roles with BYPASSRLS — and the table owner is the role that runs migrations, which is exactly who should not be exempt. FORCE ROW LEVEL SECURITY closes that gap by applying policies to the owner as well. Both should be set. A third point is that the policy expression is evaluated as the invoking user unless it is written against a role, so a policy that calls a SECURITY DEFINER helper function must itself pin search_pathsearch_path.

-- Tenant isolation. Both ENABLE and FORCE: the owner is the migration role.
ALTER TABLE billing.invoices ENABLE ROW LEVEL SECURITY;
ALTER TABLE billing.invoices FORCE ROW LEVEL SECURITY;
REVOKE ALL ON billing.invoices FROM PUBLIC;
GRANT SELECT, INSERT, UPDATE, DELETE ON billing.invoices TO app_rw;

-- Set the tenant identity from the connection, once, in a SECURITY DEFINER helper.
CREATE FUNCTION billing.current_tenant() RETURNS bigint
LANGUAGE sql STABLE PARALLEL SAFE
SET search_path = billing, pg_catalog, pg_temp
AS SELECT nullif(current_setting('app.tenant_id', true), '')::bigint;

-- Permissive policies OR together; restrictive policies AND together.
CREATE POLICY invoices_tenant_isolation ON billing.invoices
    USING (tenant_id = billing.current_tenant())
    WITH CHECK (tenant_id = billing.current_tenant());

-- Example: read own tenant, plus a support role that may read all but write none.
CREATE POLICY invoices_read_all FOR SELECT TO support_read
    USING (true);
-- No WITH CHECK: the policy is SELECT-only, so INSERT/UPDATE/DELETE get no policy
-- for this role and are denied rather than allowed.

-- Column-level visibility is layered on top: RLS filters rows, grants filter columns.
GRANT SELECT (invoice_id, tenant_id, total_cents) ON billing.invoices TO app_rw;
-- Proof of isolation. Same query, two tenant contexts.
app_rw=> SET app.tenant_id = '101';
app_rw=> SELECT count(*), sum(total_cents) FROM billing.invoices;
 count  |   sum
--------+----------
   412 | 18422100

app_rw=> SET app.tenant_id = '102';
app_rw=> SELECT count(*), sum(total_cents) FROM billing.invoices;
 count |   sum
-------+----------
   88 |  4120900

-- Cross-tenant update: the USING clause finds no row to update.
app_rw=> UPDATE billing.invoices SET total_cents = 1 WHERE invoice_id = 88201;
-- UPDATE 0

-- Cross-tenant insert: the WITH CHECK clause rejects the write.
app_rw=> INSERT INTO billing.invoices (tenant_id, total_cents) VALUES (102, 500);
-- ERROR:  new row violates row-level security policy for table "invoices"

-- The planner shows policies as filters; they are not optional predicates.
app_rw=> EXPLAIN SELECT * FROM billing.invoices WHERE total_cents > 1000;
Seq Scan on invoices  (cost=0.00..18410.00 rows=412 width=64)
  Filter: ((tenant_id = 101) AND (total_cents > 1000))

-- Audit which policies exist and whom they cover.
postgres=# SELECT tablename, policyname, permissive, roles, cmd,
       qual, with_check
FROM pg_policies WHERE schemaname = 'billing' ORDER BY tablename, policyname;

  tablename | policyname              | permissive |   roles    |    cmd    |        qual
------------+-------------------------+------------+------------+-----------+--------------------------
 invoices   | invoices_read_all        | PERMISSIVE | {support} | SELECT    | true
 invoices   | invoices_tenant_isolation| PERMISSIVE | {public}  | ALL       | (tenant_id = current_tenant())

RLS is silently disabled by table ownership. Without FO, the table owner bypasses every policy, and the table owner is normally the migration role that also runs ad-hoc queries during an incident. A multi-tenant deployment that omits FO on one table has a cross-tenant leak on that table and no error message. Add a monitoring query that lists every table with policies but without FORCEROWSECURITY and alert on a non-empty result.

MechanismGranularityEnforced byBypassable byIndex support
Schema US/CRESchemaName resolutionNothing if revoked from PUBNot applicable
Table privilegeTableExecutorOwner, superuserNot applicable
Column grantColumnParser and executorOwner, superuserExpression index needed
RLS policyRowExecutor, via FilBYPA, superuserPolicy predicate must be indexable
View security_invokerRow setExecutorGrants on the base tableInherits from base table
Separate databaseEverythingClusterSuperuser onlyFull

12.4 Transport security and authentication

Configures TLS enforcement in pg_hba.conf, documents SCRAM and the PostgreSQL 18 OAuth support, and shows the certificate rotation procedure.

pg_hba.conf is evaluated top to bottom, first match wins, so its ordering is the security policy and any generated or appended rule is a potential override of a deliberate one. Rules should be specific, use hostssl rather than host for anything crossing a network boundary, name the network explicitly rather than 0.0.0.0/0 or all with trust, and authenticate with scram-sha-256. trust is acceptable only for the local Unix socket used by administrative scripts that already run as the operating-system user that owns the data directory.

PostgreSQL 18 adds OAuth 2.0 authentication, allowing a client to obtain a bearer token from an external identity provider and present it to the server, which validates it against a configured provider without the password ever transiting the database. The shape of the integration — provider discovery endpoint, client identifier, allowed scopes — is configured in pg_hba.conf per user or group, and the identity that results maps onto a PostgreSQL role through the token’s claims. For deployments already standardised on SCRAM this is complementary rather than urgent, but it removes the long-lived database credential from the client entirely.

-- pg_hba.conf: order matters, first match wins.
# TYPE     DATABASE  USER          ADDRESS        METHOD  [OPTIONS]
local      all       postgres                      peer
local      all       all                           scram-sha-256
# Managed services: refuse the local socket for everything except the superuser.
hostssl    all       all           127.0.0.1/32    scram-sha-256
hostssl    prod      app_rw        10.40.0.0/24   scram-sha-256
hostssl    prod      analytics_ro  10.41.0.0/24   scram-sha-256 cert
hostssl    replication replicator 10.40.0.0/24   scram-sha-256
hostssl    all       all           0.0.0.0/0      scram-sha-256
hostssl    all       all           ::/0           scram-sha-256
# Anything not matched above is rejected. There is intentionally no `host` rule,
# so no connection can arrive without TLS.

-- PostgreSQL 18: OAuth 2.0 authentication for an external identity provider.
hostssl    prod      svc_orders    10.40.0.0/24   oauth issuer="https://login.example.com" \
                                 scope="openid profile postgres" \
                                 map="sub=svc_orders" \
