                                 client_id="pg-prod" \
                                 client_secret_file="/etc/postgresql/oauth.secret"

-- postgresql.conf: certificates and TLS versions.
ssl = on
ssl_cert_file = '/etc/postgresql/certs/server.crt'
ssl_key_file  = '/etc/postgresql/certs/server.key'   -- mode 0600, owned by postgres
ssl_ca_file   = '/etc/postgresql/certs/ca.crt'
ssl_min_protocol_version = 'TLSv1.2'
ssl_ciphers = 'HIGH:!aNULL:!MD5'
password_encryption = 'scram-sha-256'

postgres=# SHOW ssl;   -- on
postgres=# SELECT version, cipher, bits FROM pg_stat_ssl WHERE ssl IS TRUE ORDER BY pid LIMIT 3;

    version    |     cipher      | bits
--------------+-----------------+------
 TLSv1.3      | TLS_AES_256_GCM_SHA384 | 256
-- Certificate rotation without a restart: SIGHUP reloads the certificates.
$ openssl req -new -x509 -days 3650 -nodes \
      -keyout /etc/postgresql/certs/server.key.new \
      -out     /etc/postgresql/certs/server.crt.new \
      -subj "/CN=pg-primary.prod.internal" \
      -addext "subjectAltName=DNS:pg-primary.prod.internal,DNS:pg-primary"
$ cat /etc/postgresql/certs/ca-chain.crt >> /etc/postgresql/certs/server.crt.new
$ install -m 0600 -o postgres -g postgres /etc/postgresql/certs/server.key.new \
        /etc/postgresql/certs/server.key
$ install -m 0644 -o postgres -g postgres /etc/postgresql/certs/server.crt.new \
        /etc/postgresql/certs/server.crt
$ rm -f /etc/postgresql/certs/server.key.new /etc/postgresql/certs/server.crt.new
$ kill -HUP $(head -1 /var/lib/postgresql/18/main/postmaster.pid)
$ psql -c 'SELECT ssl, version, cipher FROM pg_stat_ssl WHERE pid = pg_backend_pid()'

-- Verify what the server actually negotiates from a client.
$ psql "host=pg-primary.prod.internal dbname=prod user=svc_orders sslmode=verify-full" -c '\conninfo'
You are connected to database "prod" as user "svc_orders" on host
"pg-primary.prod.internal" at database "prod", port 5432.
SSL enabled (protocol TLSv1.3, cipher TLS_AES_256_GCM_SHA384)

-- Enforcing certificate verification in the client, not merely encryption.
$ export PGSSLMODE=verify-full
$ export PGSSLROOTCERT=/etc/postgresql/certs/ca.crt
$ export PGSSLCERT=/etc/postgresql/certs/svc_orders.crt
$ export PGSSLKEY=/etc/postgresql/certs/svc_orders.key

-- SCRAM verifier inspection: the stored value is a verifier, never the password.
postgres=# SELECT rolname, substring(rolpassword, 1, 14) AS verifier_prefix
FROM pg_authid WHERE rolcanlogin AND rolpassword IS NOT NULL;
  rolname    | verifier_prefix
-------------+------------------
 svc_orders  | SCRAM-SHA-256$

Rotate the client credentials too. Rotating server certificates without rotating the SCRAM verifier leaves a credential that has been written to logs, connection strings and configuration management. Rotate both on a schedule, and remember that a password_encryption setting of md5 anywhere in the fleet means those verifiers are the weaker hash regardless of what the server default says.

12.5 Auditing

Configures pgaudit, the session and object audit trail, and the logging settings that make it queryable.

PostgreSQL’s built-in logging records connections, failures and statements above a duration threshold. It does not record who read or changed which row. pgaudit adds that: it is an extension that installs a set of audit event classes — READ, WRITE, FUNCTION, ROLE, DDL, ADMIN — and can log them as structured jsonb to the server log, which is then shipped and indexed. The classes must be chosen deliberately, because auditing READ on a reporting replica generates a volume that will bury the write events that actually matter.

The other half of auditing is pgAudit's companion: an application-level audit table recording row changes with the actor and the transaction ID. That table should itself have restrictive privileges and should be included in the logical replication stream to a write-once destination, so the audit trail does not live in the same trust domain as the data it audits.

-- shared_preload_libraries = 'pgaudit';  requires a restart.
pgaudit.log = 'ddl, role, write, function'   -- read is off by design: volume
pgaudit.log_relation = on
pgaudit.log_statement = on
pgaudit.log_parameter = on
pgaudit.log_catalog = off
pgaudit.log_level = warning

-- Structured output: one jsonb object per event, with statement class and role.
log_destination = 'stderr'
logging_collector = on
log_directory = '/var/log/postgresql'
log_filename = 'postgresql-%Y-%m-%d_%H%M%S.log'
log_line_prefix = '%m [%p] user=%u,db=%d,app=%a,client=%h,session=%c '
log_rotation_age = 1d
log_rotation_size = 1GB
log_truncate_on_rotation = on

CREATE EXTENSION pgaudit;

-- Sample audit event, as emitted to stderr.
LOG:  "AUDIT: STATEMENT: UPDATE billing.invoices SET total_cents = 1 WHERE invoice_id = 88201"
DETAIL:  "AUDIT: RELATION: billing.invoices"
LOG:  "AUDIT: SESSION: 3, 1, READ, SELECT, 1, billing.customers, 4417,
SELECT customer_id, display_name FROM billing.customers WHERE tenant_id = 101

-- Application-level audit trail, streamed elsewhere for tamper resistance.
CREATE TABLE billing.audit_log (
    audit_id    bigint GENERATED ALWAYS AS IDENTITY PRIMARY KEY,
    occurred_at timestamptz NOT NULL DEFAULT clock_timestamp(),
    txid        bigint      NOT NULL,
    actor       text        NOT NULL DEFAULT session_user,
    db_user     text        NOT NULL DEFAULT current_user,
    client_addr inet        NOT NULL,
    op          text        NOT NULL,
    table_name  text        NOT NULL,
    row_pk      text,
    before_row  jsonb,
    after_row   jsonb
) PARTITION BY RANGE (occurred_at);

REVOKE ALL ON billing.audit_log FROM app_rw;
GRANT INSERT ON billing.audit_log TO app_rw;
GRANT SELECT ON billing.audit_log TO analytics_ro;
-- Trigger-based audit. BEFORE row triggers see OLD; PostgreSQL 18 added OLD/NEW to
-- RETURNING, which makes some of these contexts simpler, but triggers still
-- give you the before-image on UPDATE and DELETE.
CREATE OR REPLACE FUNCTION billing.audit_invoices() RETURNS trigger
LANGUAGE plpgsql SECURITY DEFINER
SET search_path = billing, pg_catalog, pg_temp
AS $fn$
BEGIN
    INSERT INTO billing.audit_log
        (txid, op, table_name, row_pk, before_row, after_row)
    VALUES (txid_current(), tg_op, tg_table_name,
            (COALESCE(OLD.invoice_id, NEW.invoice_id))::text,
            CASE WHEN tg_op IN ('UPDATE', 'DELETE') THEN to_jsonb(OLD) ELSE NULL END,
            CASE WHEN tg_op IN ('INSERT', 'UPDATE') THEN to_jsonb(NEW) ELSE NULL END);
    RETURN COALESCE(NEW, OLD);
END;
$fn$;

CREATE TRIGGER invoices_audit
    AFTER INSERT OR UPDATE OR DELETE ON billing.invoices
    FOR EACH ROW EXECUTE FUNCTION billing.audit_invoices();

-- Querying the trail. Partition pruning keeps this cheap by time window.
postgres=# SELECT actor, op, count(*), min(occurred_at), max(occurred_at)
FROM billing.audit_log
WHERE occurred_at >= now() - INTERVAL '24 hours'
GROUP BY 1, 2 ORDER BY 3 DESC LIMIT 10;

     actor      |   op    | count |        min        |        max
----------------+----------+-------+--------------------+--------------------
 svc_orders    | UPDATE   |  4121 | 2025-04-01 04:02:11 | 2025-04-02 03:59:02
 svc_orders    | INSERT   | 11884 | 2025-04-01 04:02:11 | 2025-04-02 03:59:41

-- Alert query: privilege changes outside a change window.
postgres=# SELECT occurred_at, actor, after_row->>'grantee' AS grantee
FROM billing.audit_log
WHERE table_name = 'role_grants'
  AND occurred_at NOT BETWEEN date_trunc('day', now()) AND now()
ORDER BY 1 DESC LIMIT 20;

Log the connection, not the query. For an application that issues prepared statements with parameters, statement-level logging is largely useless — the log records the prepared statement name, not the values. log_statement = 'ddl’log_statement = 'ddl' plus pgaudit with pgaudit.log_parameter = onpgaudit.log_parameter = on gives both the schema change trail and the parameterised data access, without the volume of full statement logging on every query.

12.6 Hardening

Assembles the hardening baseline into a checklist with the verification query for each item, including the security-definer function trap.

Hardening is a sequence of verifiable states rather than a document. Each item below has a query that returns an empty result when the item is satisfied, which is the only form of a security control that survives contact with a new engineer. The single most important structural control is the SECURITY DEFINER function search-path rule: a function that runs with elevated privileges and resolves its object references against the caller’s searc can be subverted by any user who can create objects in a schema that appears earlier in the caller’s path.

-- CONTROL: every SECURITY DEFINER function pins its search_path with pg_temp last.
postgres=# SELECT n.nspname AS schema, p.proname,
       p.proconfig AS config
FROM pg_proc p JOIN pg_namespace n ON n.oid = p.pronamespace
WHERE p.prosecdef
  AND (p.proconfig IS NULL
       OR NOT EXISTS (
         SELECT 1 FROM unnest(p.proconfig) c
          WHERE c LIKE 'search_path=%'
            AND c LIKE '%pg_temp%'));

  schema |  proname  | config
--------+-----------+----------
 billing| rotate_token| {search_path=billing,pg_catalog,pg_temp}

-- CONTROL: no role may CREATE in public; no tables granted to PUBLIC.
postgres=# SELECT nspname, has_schema_privilege('PUBLIC', nspname, 'CREATE')
FROM pg_namespace
WHERE nspname IN ('public', 'information_schema', 'pg_catalog');
  nspname          | has_schema_privilege
-------------------+---------------------
 public            | t    -- FINDING: revoke this
 pg_catalog        | f
 information_schema | f

-- CONTROL: RLS tables also FORCE, and no BYPASSRLS role except break glass.
postgres=# SELECT c.relname, c.relrowsecurity, c.relforcerowsec,
       (SELECT count(*) FROM pg_policies p
         WHERE p.schemaname = n.nspname AND p.tablename = c.relname) AS policies
FROM pg_class c JOIN pg_namespace n ON n.oid = c.relnamespace
WHERE c.relrowsecurity AND NOT c.relforcerowsec
  AND n.nspname NOT IN ('pg_catalog', 'information_schema');

SELECT rolname FROM pg_roles WHERE rolbypassrls OR rolsuper;
  rolname
---------
 postgres
 break_glass
-- postgresql.conf hardening baseline, per database.
password_encryption = 'scram-sha-256'
ssl = on
ssl_min_protocol_version = 'TLSv1.2'
log_connections = on
log_disconnections = on
log_statement = 'ddl'
log_min_duration_statement = 1000
log_lock_waits = on
log_temp_files = 10MB            -- 0 disables; a size reports only over-threshold spills
log_checkpoints = on
log_autovacuum_min_duration = 0
log_line_prefix = '%m [%p] %u@%d/%a %c %q%h %s'
log_statement_sample_rate = 0.05
-- fsync, full_page_writes, and synchronous_commit stay at their defaults.

-- Filesystem: the data directory must not be traversable by other users.
$ ls -ld /var/lib/postgresql/18/main
drwx------ 19 postgres postgres 4096 Apr  2 03:41 /var/lib/postgresql/18/main
$ stat -c '%a %U:%G %n' /etc/postgresql/18/main/pg_hba.conf
640 postgres:postgres /etc/postgresql/18/main/pg_hba.conf
$ stat -c '%a %U:%G %n' /etc/postgresql/18/main/postgresql.conf
644 postgres:postgres /etc/postgresql/18/main/postgresql.conf

-- Extension safety: only trusted extensions may be installed by a non-superuser.
postgres=# SELECT name, trusted, superuser, relocatable
FROM pg_available_extensions
WHERE superuser OR NOT trusted
ORDER BY name;

     name      | trusted | superuser | relocatable
---------------+---------+-----------+-------------
 adminpack    | f       | f         | t
 auto_explain  | f       | f         | t
 pgaudit      | f       | f         | t
 plpgsql      | f       | f         | f

plpgsql and adminpack are not marked trusted, and that is not an oversight. A trusted extension can be installed by a database owner without superuser rights, which means its scripts run with the owner’s privileges inside the target database. plpgsql is present in every database by default and cannot be dropped, which is why the real control is not the trust flag but the restriction on which roles hold CREATEDB, CREATEROLE or ownership of a schema that appears in a privileged function’s search_pathsearch_path.

No superuser loginsCREATE ROLE flags, monitoringNo rols login outside the DBA group

Exercises 12

  1. Provision three tiers of role — group roles with NOLOGIN, a login role with NOINHERIT, and a time-boxed break-glass role — and produce the audit query that resolves every privilege held transitively. Demonstrate that the break-glass role cannot connect after its VALID UNTIL date.
  2. Write a script that removes CREATE from PUBLIC on the public schema across every database in a cluster, then runs the aclexplode audit to prove no grants to PUBLIC remain. Demonstrate the attack it prevents: with the grant in place, escalate from a plain login role to superuser using an operator-class hijack in the public schema.
  3. Implement tenant isolation on a partitioned table using ENABLE plus FORCE with a USING and WITH CHECK policy. Prove isolation from two tenant contexts, show the exact error on a cross-tenant insert, and show that omitting FORCE lets the table owner read every tenant’s rows.
  4. Enforce TLS with hostssl-only rules, SCRAM authentication and verify-full on the client, then rotate the server certificate with SIGHUP while sessions are live and confirm no connection dropped. Write and test the SCRAM rotation procedure for a service credential.
  5. Build a complete hardening verification script covering all eight controls in the table above, run it against a deliberately misconfigured cluster and against a hardened one, and confirm the misconfigured cluster produces findings on exactly the expected controls. Package it so it can be run on every database in a fleet as part of a compliance check.

13 · Observability, Extensions and Day-Two Operations

13.1 Statistics views13.2 pg_stat_statements13.3 auto_explain13.4 Logging13.5 The operating system layer13.6 Day-two runbook

PostgreSQL exposes more runtime state than any other relational engine of its class, and the discipline of day-two operations is deciding in advance which of it matters and at what resolution. The statistics views answer four distinct questions: what is running now, what has accumulated since the last reset, what is blocked and by what, and how much I/O did each class of work cause. pg_stat_statements adds a normalised, aggregated fingerprint over that activity, which is the only practical way to find a workload regression across a fleet. auto_explain adds plans for the queries that exceeded a threshold, without a client having to remember to ask. Logging provides the durable record. And the runbook ties all four to a procedure that an engineer who has never seen the system can execute at three in the morning.

13.1 Statistics views

Surveys pg_stat_activity, pg_stat_database, pg_stat_user_tables, pg_stat_io and the collector’s reset semantics.

PostgreSQL’s statistics fall into three families and the families have different truth properties. pg_stat_activity is a snapshot: it describes the instant the query runs, and it is the only family that shows current state. pg_stat_database, pg_stat_user_tables, pg_stat_io and pg_stat_bgwriter are counters accumulated by the collector since the last reset, and they are meaningless without the stats_reset timestamp: a rate computed against the wrong interval is worse than no rate at all. The third family is derived, and this is where the misunderstandings start — the planner’s own estimates in pg_stats are a fourth thing entirely and are compared against the counters here.

Two operational consequences follow. First, counters must be sampled at a fixed interval and differenced, never read directly as totals. Second, a restart resets the counters for the affected object, and in PostgreSQL 15 and later the reset timestamp is preserved precisely so the sampling can detect the discontinuity rather than report a negative rate. pg_stat_io, introduced in PostgreSQL 16, replaced the vaguer pg_statio_* views with a single table keyed by backend_type, object and context, which makes "how much I/O is vacuum doing" a single indexed lookup rather than a subtraction.

-- pg_stat_activity: the incident query. One row, no arguments.
postgres=# SELECT now() AS sampled_at,
       pid, datname, usename, application_name, client_addr,
       state, wait_event_type, wait_event,
       now() - xact_start   AS xact_age,
       now() - query_start  AS query_age,
       backend_xmin, left(query, 100) AS query
FROM pg_stat_activity
WHERE backend_type = 'client backend'
ORDER BY xact_start NULLS LAST LIMIT 10;

       pid | datname |     state        | wait_event_type | wait_event | xact_age
--------+--------+-------------------+-----------------+------------+-----------
  44102 | prod    | idle in transaction| Client          | ClientRead | 6 days
  51903 | prod    | active            | Lock            | relation   | 00:00:11
  51990 | prod    | active            | IO              | DataFileRead| 00:00:04

-- pg_stat_database: transaction rates, conflict rates, and temp-file volume.
postgres=# SELECT datname, numbackends, xact_commit, xact_rollback,
       round(100.0 * xact_rollback / NULLIF(xact_commit + xact_rollback, 0), 2) AS rollback_pct,
       blks_read, blks_hit, temp_files, pg_size_pretty(temp_bytes) AS temp_bytes,
       deadlocks, conflicts, stats_reset
FROM pg_stat_database WHERE datname = 'prod';

  datname | numbackends | xact_commit | xact_rollback | rollback_pct | temp_files | temp_bytes | deadlocks
---------+-------------+-------------+---------------+--------------+------------+------------+-----------
 prod     |         167 |     18402211 |          4120 |       0.02 |       1104 |      4.1 GB |         2

-- pg_stat_io (PostgreSQL 16+): I/O by backend class, object and context.
postgres=# SELECT backend_type, object, context,
       reads, read_time, writes, write_time, extends, extend_time, fsyncs,
       op_bytes,
       round(100.0 * hits / NULLIF(hits + reads, 0), 2) AS cache_hit_pct
FROM pg_stat_io
WHERE object = 'relation'
ORDER BY read_time DESC LIMIT 6;

   backend_type    | object  | context |  reads  | read_time | writes |  cache_hit_pct
-------------------+---------+---------+---------+------------+--------+----------------
 client backend    | relation| normal  | 12844102 |  4118.221 s |  41208 |          88.4
 checkpointer     | relation| normal  |   110422 |    91.004 s |  18204 |          0.00
 checkpointer     | relation| bulk    |    41102 |    41.220 s |  41022 |          0.00
 autovacuum worker| relation| normal  |    88102 |    88.104 s |   8810 |          0.00
-- Table-level: sequential scans and index scans relative to table size.
postgres=# SELECT schemaname, relname, seq_scan, seq_tup_read, idx_scan,
       n_live_tup, n_dead_tup,
       round(100.0 * seq_tup_read / NULLIF(n_live_tup + n_dead_tup, 0), 1) AS seq_read_ratio,
       pg_size_pretty(pg_total_relation_size(relid)) AS total_size
FROM pg_stat_user_tables
WHERE seq_scan > 100
ORDER BY seq_tup_read DESC LIMIT 5;

  schemaname |    relname    | seq_scan | seq_tup_read | idx_scan | seq_read_ratio | total_size
------------+--------------+----------+--------------+----------+----------------+-----------
 public      | events_2025m01|      412 |     41028822 |   118440 |          96.8 | 8.6 GB

-- Index usage: indexes with zero scans since the last reset are candidates, not deletions.
postgres=# SELECT s.schemaname, s.relname AS table_name,
       s.indexrelname AS index_name,
       s.idx_scan,
       pg_size_pretty(pg_relation_size(s.indexrelid)) AS index_size,
       i.indisunique
FROM pg_stat_user_indexes s
JOIN pg_index i ON i.indexrelid = s.indexrelid
WHERE s.idx_scan = 0 AND NOT i.indisprimary
ORDER BY pg_relation_size(s.indexrelid) DESC LIMIT 10;

  schemaname |  table_name   |    index_name     | idx_scan | index_size | indisunique
------------+---------------+-------------------+----------+------------+-------------
 public      | events        | events_tenant_idx |        0 | 4.4 GB     | f
 public      | orders        | orders_status_idx |        0 | 210 MB     | f

-- Reset semantics: pg_stat_statements_reset() clears only that extension.
postgres=# SELECT pg_stat_statements_reset();
 pg_stat_statements_reset
-------------------------
-- pg_stat_reset_shared('bgwriter'); pg_stat_reset_shared('archiver');
-- pg_stat_statements_reset() is deliberately NOT a pg_stat_reset_shared target.
postgres=# SELECT stats_reset FROM pg_stat_database WHERE datname = current_database();

-- Deriving a rate correctly: sample, difference, divide by elapsed.
postgres=# SELECT date_trunc('minute', t) AS minute,
       count(*) AS commits,
       round(avg(duration) / 1000.0, 2) AS mean_ms,
       percentile_cont(0.99) WITHIN GROUP (ORDER BY duration) / 1000 AS p99_ms
FROM admin.query_samples
WHERE t >= now() - INTERVAL '15 minutes'
GROUP BY 1 ORDER BY 1;

Never reset statistics to "fix" a graph. pg_stat_reset destroys the interval information that every rate depends on and turns a two-day trend into a discontinuity. Reset only when deliberately establishing a new measurement window — for example immediately before and after a controlled experiment — and record the reset timestamp in the incident notes so the gap is explainable.

13.2 pg_stat_statements

Configures pg_stat_statements, explains query fingerprinting and its parameter-sensitivity trade-off, and shows the extraction queries that make it useful.

pg_stat_statements normalises every executed statement into a fingerprint by replacing literal constants with $1-style placeholders, then accumulates calls, total_exec_time, rows, shared block hits and reads, and temporary block and file counts per fingerprint per user and database. The value is cross-workload comparison: the top fingerprints by total time are the workload’s actual cost distribution, ordered by something better than a sample of slow query log lines.

Two settings determine the fidelity. pg_stat_statements.max, default 5000, bounds the number of tracked entries and is a shared fixed-size hash table shared by all users, so it must be sized for the fleet’s total distinct fingerprint count, not per database. pg_stat_statements.track_utility records utility commands separately. compute_query_id, on by default since PostgreSQL 14, supplies the query id that joins to other views; if it is disabled, pg_stat_statements computes its own and the correlation with pg_stat_activity.query_id is lost.

-- shared_preload_libraries = 'pg_stat_statements,pg_stat_statements'; restart required.
pg_stat_statements.max = 10000
pg_stat_statements.track = top
pg_stat_statements.track_utility = on
pg_stat_statements.track_planning = on
pg_stat_statements.max_planning_time = 10000
compute_query_id = on
-- pg_stat_statements.track_planning adds a separate planning-time bucket, which is
-- how you detect a workload that regressed at plan time and not execution time.

CREATE EXTENSION pg_stat_statements;

-- The top queries by total time. This is the workload cost distribution.
postgres=# SELECT calls,
       round(total_exec_time::numeric / 1000, 1)                        AS total_s,
       round(mean_exec_time::numeric, 2)                                 AS mean_ms,
       round(stddev_exec_time::numeric, 2)                               AS stddev_ms,
       round(100.0 * shared_blks_hit / NULLIF(shared_blks_hit + shared_blks_read, 0), 1)
                                                                             AS hit_pct,
       rows, left(query, 90) AS query
FROM pg_stat_statements
WHERE dbid = (SELECT oid FROM pg_database WHERE datname = current_database())
ORDER BY total_exec_time DESC LIMIT 5;

 calls  | total_s | mean_ms | stddev_ms | hit_pct | rows |          query
--------+---------+---------+-----------+---------+-------+------------------------------
 412088 |  88210.4 |   214.02 |     88.41 |   71.2 |  8.2M | SELECT * FROM events WHERE tenant_id = $1 AND placed_at >= $2
  88104 |  41022.1 |   465.60 |    122.08 |   94.4 |  1.1M | UPDATE invoices SET status = $1 WHERE invoice_id = $2
 411022 |  11804.2 |    28.71 |     11.02 |   99.1 | 22.4M | INSERT INTO audit_log ...
-- Queries that spill to temporary files. work_mem is the setting to raise, or the
-- query is the thing to fix.
postgres=# SELECT calls, temp_blks_written,
       pg_size_pretty(temp_blks_written * 8192) AS spilled,
       round(100.0 * temp_blks_written / NULLIF(shared_blks_hit + shared_blks_read, 0), 2)
             AS spill_pct, left(query, 100) AS query
FROM pg_stat_statements
WHERE temp_blks_written > 0
ORDER BY temp_blks_written DESC LIMIT 5;

-- High variance relative to the mean: the fingerprint that just changed shape.
postgres=# SELECT calls, round(mean_exec_time::numeric, 2) AS mean_ms,
       round(stddev_exec_time::numeric, 2)                            AS stddev_ms,
       round(stddev_exec_time / NULLIF(mean_exec_time, 0), 2)          AS cv,
       left(query, 80) AS query
FROM pg_stat_statements
WHERE calls > 100 AND mean_exec_time > 10
  AND stddev_exec_time > mean_exec_time
ORDER BY cv DESC LIMIT 5;

-- Comparing two windows, e.g. before and after a release.
postgres=# SELECT a.queryid,
       round(a.mean_exec_time::numeric, 2) AS before_ms,
       round(b.mean_exec_time::numeric, 2) AS after_ms,
       round(100.0 * (b.mean_exec_time - a.mean_exec_time) / NULLIF(a.mean_exec_time, 0), 1)
                                                                                      AS pct_change,
       left(b.query, 70) AS query
FROM (SELECT * FROM pg_stat_statements WHERE dbid = 16410) a
JOIN (SELECT * FROM pg_stat_statements WHERE dbid = 16410) b
  ON a.queryid = b.queryid AND a.userid = b.userid
WHERE a.calls > 50 AND b.calls > 50
ORDER BY abs(b.mean_exec_time - a.mean_exec_time) DESC LIMIT 10;

-- Resetting is per-scope and instant. Keep the reset out of a busy window.
postgres=# SELECT pg_stat_statements_reset();          -- everything
postgres=# SELECT pg_stat_statements_reset('user', 16384);
postgres=# SELECT pg_stat_statements_reset('dd', 16410, 0, 1);  -- dbid 16410, userid 0 (shared), queryid 1

Constant-heavy statements fragment into unusable entries. A statement with a dozen distinct literals still produces one fingerprint, but a statement whose shape changes with the data — a query built by string concatenation, an IN list of varying length, a partition name embedded in the text — produces a different fingerprint for each shape, and the max table fills with near-duplicates while evicting the entries that matter. Fix the application’s parameter binding; pg_stat_statements cannot recover from SQL constructed as text.

13.3 auto_explain

Configures auto_explain with log_analyze and log_min_duration, and sets the thresholds that produce a usable plan log.

auto_explain is a loadable module that logs the execution plan of any statement exceeding a duration threshold, without the client doing anything. It is the difference between knowing that a query is slow and knowing why. The cost is log volume and CPU, both of which scale with the number of statements that cross the threshold, so the threshold must be chosen relative to a statement you have decided is unacceptable rather than set to zero.

log_analyze is the expensive half: it enables actual row counts and timings per node, which requires instrumenting every executor node. On a statement that runs for seconds and returns millions of rows it adds measurable overhead and can change the plan by triggering a mid-execution switch to a hash aggregate. The correct sequence is to deploy with log_min_duration alone to measure what the slow population actually is, then enable log_analyze for a short diagnostic window with a raised threshold, then return to the cheap configuration. Buffer instrumentation via auto_explain.log_buffers is comparatively inexpensive and worth leaving on.

-- shared_preload_libraries = 'auto_explain';  or LOAD 'auto_explain'; per session.
auto_explain.log_min_duration = '2s'
auto_explain.log_analyze = off          -- phase 1: find the slow population
auto_explain.log_buffers = on
auto_explain.log_timing = off
auto_explain.log_format = text
auto_explain.log_nested_statements = on
auto_explain.log_triggers = on
auto_explain.log_verbose = off
auto_explain.sample_rate = 1.0
auto_explain.log_timing_threshold = 0

-- Targeted: instrument only one application, via ALTER ROLE or ALTER DATABASE.
postgres=# ALTER ROLE app_reporting SET auto_explain.log_min_duration = '500ms';
postgres=# ALTER ROLE app_reporting SET auto_explain.log_analyze = on;
postgres=# ALTER ROLE app_reporting SET auto_explain.log_buffers = on;
postgres=# ALTER ROLE app_reporting SET auto_explain.log_nested_statements = on;
-- GUCs named auto_explain.* are settable per role because the module registers them
-- as placeholders; no restart, no cluster-wide change.

-- The resulting log entry, with actual row counts and per-node buffers.
LOG:  duration: 8841.203 ms  plan:
->  Gather  (cost=1000.00..2984112.44 rows=4102 width=64) (actual time=0.412..8840.118 rows=8821044 loops=1)
        Workers Planned: 3
        Workers Launched: 3
      ->  Parallel Seq Scan on events events_1  (cost=0.00..2411884.11 rows=1180244 width=64)
            (actual time=0.402..6102.884 rows=2205261 loops=3)
            Filter: (tenant_id = 412 AND placed_at >= '2025-01-01 00:00:00+00'::timestamptz)
            Rows Removed by Filter: 8411884
            Heap Blocks: shared hit=41204 read=188422, dirtied=88204
      ->  Parallel Hash Join  (cost=1104.00..2904118.22 rows=4102 width=64)
            (actual time=1.102..6204.441 rows=0 loops=3)
            Hash Cond: (e.tenant_id = t.id)
            ->  Parallel Seq Scan on tenants t  (cost=0.00..4102.00 rows=4102 width=8)
                  (actual time=0.018..0.884 rows=4102 loops=3)
            ->  Parallel Hash  (cost=0.00..2411884.11 rows=1180244 width=60)
                  (actual time=0.402..4210.221 rows=1180244 loops=3)
                  Buckets: 4194304  Batches: 2  Memory Usage: 204MB
            Heap Blocks: shared hit=1204 read=882
  Planning Time: 41.882 ms
  Execution Time: 8840.912 ms
-- A bounded diagnostic window: enable analyze, raise the threshold, then revert.
postgres=# ALTER SYSTEM SET auto_explain.log_analyze = on;
postgres=# ALTER SYSTEM SET auto_explain.log_min_duration = '10s';
-- Reload only; no restart, so no connection is dropped.
$ pg_ctl -D $PGDATA reload
# ... leave in place for one business cycle, then revert.
postgres=# ALTER SYSTEM RESET auto_explain.log_analyze;
postgres=# ALTER SYSTEM RESET auto_explain.log_min_duration;
postgres=# ALTER SYSTEM SET auto_explain.log_min_duration = '2s';
SELECT pg_reload_conf();

-- Reading plans back out of the log for analysis.
$ grep -A 30 'duration: 8841' /var/log/postgresql/postgresql-18-main.log \
  | head -40 > /tmp/plan.txt
$ pgbench -f /tmp/plan.txt -n 2 2>&1 | head -5

-- Structured plans: log_format=json is machine-readable and costlier.
postgres=# ALTER SYSTEM SET auto_explain.log_format = json;
postgres=# ALTER SYSTEM SET auto_explain.log_analyze = on;
-- JSON plans are how a fleet-wide plan analysis pipeline should ingest them.

-- Sampling for high-volume workloads: 1% of statements over 500 ms.
postgres=# ALTER SYSTEM SET auto_explain.log_min_duration = '500ms';
postgres=# ALTER SYSTEM SET auto_explain.sample_rate = 0.01;
postgres=# ALTER SYSTEM SET auto_explain.log_analyze = off;

Give auto_explain a statement_timeout, and give the timeout a threshold. Without a timeout, a plan log entry for a query that runs for an hour may be written after the log volume has already pushed the shipping pipeline into rotation and lost the entries you cared about. With a role-level statemen of, say, 60 seconds on reporting roles and auto_explain.log_min_duration of 2 seconds, the log is bounded in both directions.

13.4 Logging

Configures log_line_prefix, log_min_duration_statement, log_lock_waits and deadlock_timeout, and produces a queryable log line.

The server log is the durable record, and its usefulness is entirely determined by log_line_prefix. The default prefix — timestamp, user, database, PID — is not enough to join a log line to a client request, which is the join every incident requires. A prefix containing %a for the application name and %c for the session identifier turns the log into a table that can be grouped by request, correlated with pg_stat_activity by backend_start, and cross-referenced with client-side request identifiers when the application propagates one through a SET command on connect.

Two settings make lock problems diagnosable rather than merely slow. log_lock_waits logs a line whenever a statement waits longer than deadlock_timeout, which defaults to one second and governs deadlock detection. log_min_duration_statement logs statements above a threshold, and should be paired with log_statement_sample_rate so that the frequent fast statements that are still worth a sample do not drown the rare slow ones. In PostgreSQL 18, structured JSON logging is available through logging_collector with a JSON format, which removes the need to parse the prefix at all.

-- postgresql.conf: a logging configuration fit for production.
logging_collector = on
log_directory = '/var/log/postgresql'
log_filename = 'postgresql-%Y-%m-%d_%H%M%S.log'
log_rotation_age = 1d
log_rotation_size = 1GB
log_truncate_on_rotation = on
log_line_prefix = '%m [%p] %q%u@%d/%a xid=%x sid=%c '
log_timezone = 'UTC'
log_duration = off
log_statement = 'ddl'
log_min_duration_statement = 1000
log_statement_sample_rate = 0.05
log_min_error_statement = error
log_min_messages = warning
log_checkpoints = on
log_lock_waits = on
log_temp_files = 10MB
log_autovacuum_min_duration = 0
log_replication_commands = on
log_hostname = on
-- %x = xid, so the log line joins to pg_stat_activity.xact_start and to the
-- pgaudit event for the same transaction. %c = session id. %q = stop here for
-- non-session processes, which prevents padding noise from the checkpointer.

-- Deadlock detection interval: how long a lock wait must be before it is logged
-- and before the deadlock detector runs. Lowering it costs a small amount of work
-- per contended lock.
deadlock_timeout = 1s

-- The resulting line format, as shipped to the log pipeline.
2025-04-02 03:41:22.418 UTC [44102] svc_orders@prod/orders-api xid=112884401 sid=62a1f3c7
  LOG:  duration: 4120.882 ms  statement: SELECT * FROM events WHERE tenant_id = 412
        AND placed_at >= '2025-01-01' ORDER BY placed_at DESC LIMIT 500
DETAIL:  parameters: $1 = '412', $2 = '2025-01-01 00:00:00+00'

-- A lock wait, logged because log_lock_waits is on.
2025-04-02 03:41:24.002 UTC [51903] svc_orders@prod/orders-api xid=112884418 sid=9f2b1aa4
  LOG:  process 51903 still waiting for ShareLock on transaction 112884401 after 1004.118 ms
DETAIL:  blocking pid: 44102, blocking xid: 112884401, query: UPDATE invoices SET ...
-- Propagating a request identifier from the application, so logs join to traces.
ALTER DATABASE prod SET log_line_prefix = '%m [%p] req=%e user=%u@%d/%a xid=%x sid=%c '
-- %e is not a GUC; the application instead runs this on connect:
--   SET application_name = 'orders-api/req-8f2a91';
-- and log_line_prefix includes %a, which is what appears in the log.

-- PostgreSQL 18 structured logging: no prefix parsing required.
logging_collector = on
log_destination = 'jsonlog'
log_line_prefix = ''
log_rotation_size = 100MB
-- Each line is a JSON object with timestamp, user, dbname, pid, session_id,
-- message, and, for auto_explain, the plan as a nested structure.

-- Shipping. Vector or Fluent Bit reads the file; the point is that the
-- prefix must be parseable before anything downstream is worth configuring.
$ vector --config /etc/vector/vector.toml
2025-04-02T03:41:22.418Z  info postgres_18_prod {
      "host": "pg-primary", "user": "svc_orders", "dbname": "prod",
      "application_name": "orders-api/req-8f2a91", "pid": 44102,
      "message": "duration: 4120.882 ms", "level": "LOG"
    }

-- Error-class alerting: only these lines should page a human.
$ grep -E '(FATAL|PANIC|ERROR:  could not|terminating connection|out of memory)' \
      /var/log/postgresql/postgresql-18-main.log | tail -5
2025-04-02 03:12:04.221 UTC [11822] LOG:  out of memory
DETAIL:  Failed process was running: SELECT ... FROM events_2025m01
2025-04-02 03:12:04.224 UTC [1] LOG:  terminating any other active server processes
SettingDefaultWhat it addsCost when mis-set
log_min_durat-1-1 (off)Slow statements with duration00 logs everything; disk fill
log_statemen1.01.0Sampled statements below the thresholdNot reduced after a duration threshold is set
log_locoBlocking waits with blocker PIDVolume on a pathological lock graph
log_tem-1-1 (off)Queries that spilled to disk00 logs every spill, including trivial ones
log_cheoCheckpoint duration and buffersNone; leave on
log_autovacuu-1-1Vacuum runs and their impactVerbose on a table with many partitions

13.5 The operating system layer

Covers process limits, memory and THP settings, disk and filesystem choices, and the check_postgres checks that automate the audit.

Several PostgreSQL settings are only correct in conjunction with the operating system’s, and the mismatch is a common cause of instability that looks like a database problem. shared_buffers plus the number of backends times work_mem must leave the machine enough for the page cache, which is where the index and the most-recently-used heap pages live; over-allocating shared_buffers past roughly 75 % of RAM degrades throughput rather than improving it. Transparent huge pages interact badly with PostgreSQL’s mmap-based shared memory and must be set to madvise or never. vm.overcommit_memory = 2 is not required for PostgreSQL, but vm.overcommit_ratio tuning prevents the OOM killer from choosing a backend over a build worker.

The filesystem matters more than is usually acknowledged. PostgreSQL issues its own fsync calls and relies on them, so a filesystem or storage layer that lies about durability — a write-back cache without a power-loss-protected battery, a network filesystem with an asynchronous server — removes the durability guarantee without changing a single PostgreSQL setting. check_postgres from the PostgreSQL installation automates the configuration audit and is the fastest way to find a mismatch on a new host.

-- Process and file descriptor limits. Max_connections is reached first without this.
$ cat /proc/$(head -1 $PGDATA/postmaster.pid)/limits | grep -E '(processes|files)'
Limit                     Soft Limit           Hard Limit           Units
Max processes             8388608              8388608              processes
Max open files            1048576              1048576              files

$ cat /etc/security/limits.d/99-postgres.conf
postgres  soft  nofile  1048576
postgres  hard  nofile  1048576
postgres  soft  nproc   65536
postgres  hard  nproc   65536
-- nproc must exceed max_connections + max_worker_processes + max_parallel_workers,
-- because each backend's parallel workers are counted as processes.

-- Kernel settings. /etc/sysctl.d/99-postgres.conf, applied with sysctl --system.
vm.overcommit_memory = 0
vm.overcommit_ratio = 90
vm.swappiness = 1
vm.dirty_background_ratio = 5
vm.dirty_ratio = 20
vm.nr_hugepages = 0            -- leave 0 unless hugetlb_pages is configured
fs.file-max = 2097152
fs.aio-max-nr = 1048576
kernel.sched_migration_cost_ns = 500000   -- discourage migration; helps locality
net.core.somaxconn = 4096
net.ipv4.tcp_max_syn_backlog = 8192

-- Transparent huge pages must not be 'always' for any PostgreSQL memory region.
$ cat /sys/kernel/mm/transparent_hugepage/enabled
always [madvise] never
$ cat /sys/kernel/mm/transparent_hugepage/defrag
always [madvise] never

-- Filesystem: XFS with reflinks for pg_basebackup, ext4 as the conservative choice.
$ findmnt -T $PGDATA -o TARGET,SOURCE,FSTYPE,OPTIONS
TARGET       SOURCE                 FSTYPE OPTIONS
/var/lib/postgresql/18/main /dev/nvme1n1p3 xfs   rw,relatime,attr2,inode64,noquota

$ xfs_info / | grep -E '(log size|bsize)'
log size=32,00 blocks  bsize=4096   -- logbufs=8, logbsize=32k; larger log = fewer fsync stalls
-- check_postgres: the automated version of everything above.
$ pg_config --pkglibdir
/usr/lib/postgresql/18/lib
$ /usr/lib/postgresql/18/lib/check_postgres --action-file /etc/postgresql/check.conf \
      --host=localhost --port=5432 --username=postgres --dbname=postgres

[1]  (main.latest_checkpoint_age)      ok
[2]  (main.max_connections)            ok
[3]  (main.autovacuum)                 WARN  max_workers: 3
[4]  (main.max_wal_size)               ok
[5]  (main.wal_level)                  ok
[6]  (main.shared_buffers)             ok   current_setting: 32GB
[7]  (main.work_mem)                   ok
[8]  (main.effective_cache_size)       ok   current_setting: 96GB
[9]  (main.random_page_cost)           ok
[10] (main.effective_io_concurrency)   ok
[11] (main.temp_file_limit)            ok
[12] (main.checkpoint_segments_setting) WARN  max_wal_size: 8GB
[13] (main.max_connections)            ok
-- Exit code is non-zero on WARN, which makes it usable as a CI gate.
$ echo $?
1

-- Disk latency, the number that predicts both bloat and checkpoint pain.
$ fio --name=pgseq --filename=/var/lib/postgresql/testfile \
      --rw=randwrite --bs=4k --size=1G --iodepth=32 --runtime=60 --time_based
Run status group 0 (all jobs):
   READ: bw=84102MiB/s (881MB/s), 881MB/s-9900MB/s (84.1GB/s-10.3GB/s), io=5.14GiB
  WRITE: bw=10241MiB/s (10.7MB/s), 10.7MB/s-11.2MB/s, io=617MiB
-- Random 4k write latency is the relevant figure for a checkpoint burst.

-- Filesystem-level maintenance: fstrim is safe on XFS/ext4 and reclaims space
-- returned by TRUNCATE. It does not compact a bloated relation.
$ systemctl enable --now fstrim.timer
$ fstrim -v /var/lib/postgresql
/: 412 GB (98 GiB) trimmed on /dev/nvme1n1p3

Check effective_io_concurrency and maintenance_io_concurrency against the device. These control how many read-ahead and prefetch requests are issued concurrently. On NVMe or a high-performance SAN, a value of 200 to 1000 is appropriate; on a network volume with a high per-request latency, the same value can queue thousands of requests against a device queue depth of 32 and make throughput worse. PostgreSQL 18's asynchronous I/O subsystem adds io_m, io_max_co and io_wo to this calculation — io_method = workerio_method = worker is the portable default and io_u is available on Linux 5.1 and later. The pg_aios view shows in-flight AIO operations directly, which is the fastest way to confirm the subsystem is actually engaged rather than silently falling back to synchronous I/O.

13.6 Day-two runbook

Assembles the preceding instrumentation into a single incident sequence with fixed commands, decision points, and explicit escalation criteria.

A runbook is a document whose value is measurable by whether a person who has never used the system can execute it under time pressure. That requires three properties: every command is complete and copy-pasteable including credentials handling, every step states what result means "continue", and every branch has a named escalation target. The sequence below assumes a single primary with synchronous replicas and is ordered so that no diagnostic step can itself cause data loss.

-- 0. Establish ground truth. Nothing below mutates anything.
$ pg_isready -h pg-primary -d prod -t 5
pg-primary:5432 - accepting connections
$ psql -h pg-primary -U postgres -d prod -c 'SELECT pg_is_in_recovery(), now()'
 pg_is_in_recovery |         now
-------------------+--------------------------
 f                 | 2025-04-02 03:42:11.882+00

-- 1. Is it the database, the host, or upstream? Sample pg_stat_activity three times.
postgres=# SELECT now(), count(*) FILTER (WHERE state = 'active') AS active,
       count(*) FILTER (WHERE wait_event_type = 'Lock')          AS lock_wait,
       count(*) FILTER (WHERE state = 'idle in transaction') AS idle_txn
FROM pg_stat_activity \watch 2

-- Branch: lock_wait > 0  -> step 2.  idle_txn > 0 -> step 3.
-- Branch: active high, lock_wait 0 -> step 4 (CPU or I/O saturation).

-- 2. Blocking chain. Copy the blocker_pid; terminating it is step 2b.
postgres=# SELECT blocked.pid AS blocked_pid,
       now() - blocked.query_start AS blocked_for,
       left(blocked.query, 100) AS blocked_query,
       blocker.pid AS blocker_pid,
       blocker.state, now() - blocker.xact_start AS blocker_xact_age,
       left(blocker.query, 100) AS blocker_query
FROM pg_stat_activity blocked
JOIN pg_locks bl ON bl.pid = blocked.pid AND bl.locktype = 'transactionid'
JOIN pg_locks kl ON kl.locktype = bl.locktype AND kl.transactionid = bl.transactionid
JOIN pg_stat_activity blocker ON blocker.pid = kl.pid AND blocker.pid <> blocked.pid
WHERE NOT bl.granted AND NOT kl.granted;

-- 2b. Only if the blocker is safe to kill: idle, or a known-stuck batch job.
postgres=# SELECT pg_cancel_backend(44102);   -- cancel the statement, keep the session
postgres=# SELECT pg_terminate_backend(44102); -- kill the session; rolls back its transaction

-- 3. Idle in transaction: the vacuum-pinning case from chapter 9.
postgres=# SELECT pid, usename, application_name,
       now() - state_change AS idle_for, left(query, 80)
FROM pg_stat_activity
WHERE state = 'idle in transaction'
ORDER BY state_change LIMIT
-- 4. CPU or I/O saturation. Disk first, because it is the common answer.
$ iostat -x 1 5
Device  r/s     rkB/s   w/s     wkB/s  await  aqu-sz  %util
nvme0n1 1840   1884204 112     11204   41.2   12.88   98.7
-- await 41ms with %util 98 is storage saturation, not CPU.

postgres=# SELECT backend_type, object, context, reads, read_time,
       round(read_time::numeric / NULLIF(reads, 0) * 1000, 2) AS avg_read_ms
FROM pg_stat_io WHERE reads > 0 AND object = 'relation'
ORDER BY read_time DESC LIMIT 4;

-- 4a. CPU-bound: the plan is wrong or the query is unavoidable. Get the plan.
postgres=# SET auto_explain.log_min_duration = '1s';
postgres=# SET auto_explain.log_analyze = on;
postgres=# SET auto_explain.log_buffers = on;
postgres=# EXPLAIN (ANALYZE, BUFFERS) <offending query>;
postgres=# RESET auto_explain.log_analyze;
-- Compare the estimate to the actual: a 100x gap means stale statistics.
postgres=# ANALYZE events_2025m01;

-- 5. Connection exhaustion. Confirm, then shed load rather than wait.
postgres=# SELECT count(*) * 100.0 / current_setting('max_connections') AS pct_used
FROM pg_stat_activity WHERE backend_type = 'client backend';

-- 6. Space. The one condition that is always an emergency.
postgres=# SELECT pg_size_pretty(pg_wal_lsn_diff(pg_current_wal_lsn(), restart_lsn))
AS wal_retained FROM pg_replication_slots WHERE restart_lsn IS NOT NULL;

-- 7. Replica health. Escalate to failover if the primary is unreachable.
postgres=# SELECT application_name, sync_state,
       pg_size_pretty(pg_wal_lsn_diff(pg_current_wal_lsn(), replay_lsn)) AS lag
FROM pg_stat_replication;

Record what you did, in the log, as you do it. PostgreSQL’s log is the shared record of the incident, and a sequence of \timing on\timing on queries with results pasted into the incident channel is worth more than a retrospective. If the incident involved terminating a backend or a VACUUM FULLVACUUM FULL, the log line containing the statement text is the only evidence of it later.

Exercises 13

  1. Build a metrics exporter for a production-shaped database covering pg_stat_activity, pg_stat_database, pg_stat_user_tables, pg_stat_io and pg_stat_bgwriter, emitting rates computed from fixed-interval samples with explicit reset detection. Demonstrate correct rate derivation across a statistics reset.
  2. Install pg_stat_statements with planning tracked, run a fixed benchmark before and after a release, and produce a report of the ten fingerprints with the largest change in mean execution time, including whether the change originated in planning or execution time.
  3. Configure auto_explain in two phases on a live workload: first with log_min_duration alone to characterise the slow population, then with log_analyze on a narrowed threshold. Measure the CPU overhead of log_analyze and document any case where enabling it changed the chosen plan.
  4. Produce a complete logging configuration with a log_line_prefix that supports joins to pg_stat_activity, ship it as structured logs, and write three alerts — a lock-wait alert, a long-running-transaction alert, and an autovacuum-starvation alert — each with a threshold derived from a measurement rather than a guess.
  5. Run check_postgres on a fresh PostgreSQL 18 host, resolve every warning, then tune the kernel and filesystem parameters it did not check. Separately, enable the PostgreSQL 18 asynchronous I/O subsystem with io_method = worker, verify engagement through pg_aios, and benchmark a sequential scan against io_method = sync.
  6. Write the one-page incident runbook for your own system and rehearse it: inject a lock-blocking incident, a connection-exhaustion incident, and a runaway query, and record the wall-clock time to diagnosis and to mitigation for each. The time to diagnosis, not to mitigation, is the number the exercise is measuring.
